## [0751] Gram-negative bloodstream infection duration

**Cluster**: gram-negative bloodstream infection duration | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Gram-negative bloodstream infection is one of the settings where antimicrobial stewardship has most visibly shortened therapy. A decade of non-inferiority trials and meta-analyses now supports 7-day courses for many patients, while multidrug-resistant organisms remain the key evidence gap.

### Core Teaching Points
- A 2025 systematic review and noninferiority meta-analysis of randomized trials found 7 days of antibiotics noninferior to 14 days for gram-negative bloodstream infection in hospitalized adults. (PMID 40116824)
- IDSA's 2025 complicated-UTI guideline suggests 5-7 days of a fluoroquinolone or 7 days of a non-fluoroquinolone when the patient is improving on effective therapy, regardless of associated gram-negative bacteremia. (PMID 41419448)
- In Enterobacteriaceae bacteremia, meta-analysis showed no significant difference between short (<=10 days) and longer courses for 30- and 90-day mortality, clinical cure, or relapse. (PMID 30803971)
- Short courses are consistently noninferior when the diagnosis is confirmed, the antimicrobial is appropriate, and the patient shows clinical improvement. (PMID 36075498)
- For MDR gram-negatives (CRE, difficult-to-treat Pseudomonas, CRAB), IDSA and ESCMID give explicit agent-selection advice but lack detailed duration recommendations; evidence for short courses is limited here. (PMID 40725151)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration for carbapenem-resistant organisms and difficult-to-treat Pseudomonas aeruginosa bacteremia.
- How source control status should modify the 7-day default.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Seven versus fourteen days: what the noninferiority meta-analysis supports and its boundaries.
- cUTI with gram-negative bacteremia: translating the IDSA 5-7/7-day recommendation to a clinical vignette.
- Which patients qualify for short-course therapy (confirmed diagnosis, effective agent, improving).
- The MDR-GNB duration evidence gap versus agent-selection guidance.

### Case Generation Hooks
- E. coli bacteremia from pyelonephritis, afebrile and improving on day 3 - stop at day 7?
- Carbapenem-resistant Klebsiella pneumoniae bacteremia in a stable patient - how long, and on what evidence?

**Reviewer Gap**: Pediatric durations, Pseudomonas-specific BSI data, and pathogen-disease combinations beyond Enterobacteriaceae/UTI are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against a full BSI guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40116824**: *Seven vs Fourteen Days of Antibiotics for Gram-Negative Bloodstream Infection: A Systematic Review and Noninferiority Meta-Analysis* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 41419448**: *IDSA 2025 Guidelines on Management and Treatment of Complicated Urinary Tract Infections--Duration of Antibiotics for Complicated UTI* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30803971**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis of Antibiotic Treatment Duration for Bacteremia Due to Enterobacteriaceae* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 36075498**: *Short-course antibiotics for common infections: what do we know and where do we go from here?* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 40725151**: *Antibiotic Therapy Duration for Multidrug-Resistant Gram-Negative Bacterial Infections: An Evidence-Based Review* (2025) [review] - None

## [0752] Gram-negative resistance: ESBL, carbapenemase-producing organisms, colistin, ceftazidime-avibactam

**Cluster**: gram-negative resistance esbl carbapenemase colistin ceftazidime-avibactam | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The last decade has transformed treatment of carbapenem-resistant gram-negatives: ceftazidime-avibactam, meropenem-vaborbactam and imipenem-relebactam have moved KPC-producing Enterobacterales from the colistin era into targeted beta-lactam therapy, while KPC variant evolution now threatens the new agents themselves.

### Core Teaching Points
- Ceftazidime-avibactam is a first-line option for KPC-producing Enterobacterales, with meropenem-vaborbactam and imipenem-relebactam as alternatives; ceftazidime-avibactam is preferred for OXA-48-like producers. (PMID 40185559, PMID 34752560)
- For KPC strains resistant to ceftazidime-avibactam, meropenem-vaborbactam and imipenem-relebactam have relative advantages. (PMID 40185559)
- Newer beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combinations expanded the armamentarium against MDR Enterobacterales and Pseudomonas aeruginosa beyond the polymyxin era. (PMID 33177185, PMID 34169446)
- In-vivo emergence of ceftazidime-avibactam resistance in KPC-E is documented in a systematic review: most cases were Klebsiella pneumoniae ST258 with the D179Y substitution, occurring during treatment of deep-seated infection. (PMID 33895414)
- More than 150 blaKPC variants are now reported, most identified in the last three years, and variants restore ceftazidime affinity while weakening avibactam binding - a moving resistance target. (PMID 37937997)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing of newer agents when first-line BLBLI therapy fails.
- Whether combination therapy (e.g. BLBLI plus aminoglycoside or polymyxin) improves outcomes in high-inoculum infections.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Agent selection by carbapenemase type: KPC versus OXA-48 versus MBL.
- Recognizing and responding to ceftazidime-avibactam resistance emerging on therapy (ST258/D179Y).
- Why colistin has been displaced as default therapy for KPC-producing CRE.
- The blaKPC variant explosion and what it means for susceptibility reporting.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Blood culture grows KPC-producing Klebsiella pneumoniae on day 4 of ceftazidime-avibactam - what changed and what next?
- OXA-48-producing E. coli bacteremia: choosing between ceftazidime-avibactam and a carbapenem-sparing alternative.

**Reviewer Gap**: Metallo-beta-lactamase therapy (aztreonam-avibactam, cefiderocol) is not covered by the retained abstracts; add an MBL-specific source before approval. ESBL-specific guidance (when carbapenems can be spared) is also not represented.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34169446**: *Treatment for carbapenem-resistant Enterobacterales infections: recent advances and future directions* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 34752560**: *Review of Ceftazidime-Avibactam, Meropenem-Vaborbactam, and Imipenem/Cilastatin-Relebactam to Target KPC-producing Enterobacterales* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 40185559**: *Management of Severe Infections: Multidrug-Resistant and Carbapenem-Resistant Gram-Negative Bacteria* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 33177185**: *New beta-Lactam-beta-Lactamase Inhibitor Combinations* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 33895414**: *Resistance to ceftazidime/avibactam in infections and colonisations by KPC-producing Enterobacterales* (2021) [systematic review] - None
- **PMID 37937997**: *Klebsiella pneumoniae carbapenemase variants: the new threat to global public health* (2023) [review] - None

## [0753] Granulomatosis with Polyangiitis

**Cluster**: granulomatosis with polyangiitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Granulomatosis with polyangiitis is the commonest ANCA-associated vasculitis and a medical emergency when active: modern regimens pair glucocorticoids with rituximab or cyclophosphamide for induction, and the 2022 EULAR update redefined how newer trial evidence maps to treatment choices.

### Core Teaching Points
- EULAR's 2022 AAV recommendations were built on a systematic literature review plus expert consensus, incorporating randomized trials published since the 2016 version that change clinical care. (PMID 36927642)
- Rituximab is now an established induction option alongside glucocorticoids for ANCA-associated vasculitis, reflected across EULAR, KDIGO and ACR guidance. (PMID 39196354)
- Major clinical trials underpinning current ANCA vasculitis treatment have reduced morbidity and improved survival; guideline summaries track these trial milestones. (PMID 41460038)
- Japanese 2023 AAV guideline development used a dedicated systematic review and meta-analysis, an example of rigorous national adaptation of the same evidence base. (PMID 36112482)
- AAV is a frequently relapsing disorder; treatment aims are rapid control of active disease, then prevention of relapse and organ damage. (PMID 41051263)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal glucocorticoid-sparing intensity and the role of avacopan in routine practice.
- How to sequence maintenance therapy and define relapse triggers for re-treatment.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Induction therapy selection: rituximab versus cyclophosphamide for new organ-threatening GPA.
- What changed between EULAR 2016 and 2022 and which trials drove it.
- Maintenance and relapse prevention after successful induction.
- Organ-specific complications (pulmonary haemorrhage, RPGN) and how they modify therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 45-year-old with haemoptysis, saddle-nose deformity and c-ANCA - induction regimen and monitoring plan?
- GPA in remission on azathioprine with rising ANCA titre - escalate, switch, or watch?

**Reviewer Gap**: Avacopan (ADVOCATE), plasma exchange (PEXIVAS) and trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole prophylaxis specifics are not in the retained abstracts; add the primary trial/guideline sources before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36927642**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of ANCA-associated vasculitis: 2022 update* (2024) [systematic review] - None
- **PMID 39196354**: *Update on treatment of ANCA-associated vasculitis* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 41460038**: *Update on antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody vasculitis* (2026) [review] - None
- **PMID 36112482**: *Systematic review and meta-analysis for 2023 clinical practice guidelines of the Japan Research Committee for Intractable Vasculitis* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 41051263**: *Mapping a path forward: addressing disease burden, pathways and solutions in ANCA-associated vasculitis* (2026) [review] - None

## [0754] graves disease antithyroid and radioiodine therapy

**Cluster**: graves disease antithyroid radioiodine therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Graves' hyperthyroidism offers three effective definitive strategies - antithyroid drugs, radioiodine and thyroidectomy - and no modality has proven universally superior. Modern practice starts with antithyroid drugs to restore euthyroidism, then individualises the definitive step around orbitopathy, pregnancy plans and patient preference.

### Core Teaching Points
- Guideline-based strategy first restores euthyroid status with antithyroid therapy, then selects a definitive modality; no treatment modality has demonstrated superiority, so the choice is shared with the patient. (PMID 30193753)
- Radioiodine-131 and thyroidectomy abolish hyperthyroidism but create hypothyroidism requiring lifelong levothyroxine - a trade of one disease for another rather than a cure. (PMID 30912336)
- Radioiodine can aggravate Graves' orbitopathy and requires pre-treatment assessment; pregnancy or pregnancy plans change both treatment choice and monitoring. (PMID 30193753)
- A beta-blocker relieves tachycardia, anxiety and tremor while awaiting definitive control. (PMID 30730622)
- Long-term antithyroid drug courses are an increasingly used strategy, with contemporary narrative reviews updating their role against radioiodine and surgery. (PMID 42119974)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which patients achieve durable remission after antithyroid drugs and how to predict relapse.
- Optimal role and duration of long-term low-dose antithyroid drug maintenance.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First step in a newly diagnosed thyrotoxic patient: restore euthyroidism before definitive therapy.
- How orbitopathy and pregnancy plans shift the modality choice.
- Radioiodine versus antithyroid drugs: what 'cure' actually means.
- Symptom control with beta-blockers while definitive therapy takes effect.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 28-year-old woman with Graves' disease planning pregnancy - which therapy, and what monitoring?
- Active smokers with moderate orbitopathy - why radioiodine needs a second look.

**Reviewer Gap**: Antithyroid drug adverse effects (agranulocytosis, hepatotoxicity) and their monitoring are not detailed in the retained abstracts; add an ATA/EUGOGO-aligned source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30193753**: *Treatment of adult Graves' disease* (2018) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30730622**: *Graves' disease in adults: antithyroid drugs, radioactive iodine, or sometimes thyroidectomy* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 42119974**: *Long-Term Antithyroid Drug Therapy in the Management of Graves' Hyperthyroidism: An Updated Narrative Review* (2026) [review] - None
- **PMID 30912336**: *Graves' Disease: Can It Be Cured?* (2019) [review] - None

## [0755] Graves disease: antithyroid drugs, radioiodine, thyroidectomy - comparative outcomes

**Cluster**: graves disease comparative outcomes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Comparing antithyroid drugs, radioiodine and thyroidectomy for Graves' disease is less about finding a winner than matching trade-offs: remission potential versus definitive control, and the burden of lifelong thyroid hormone replacement after destructive approaches. Guidelines consistently report no modality as universally superior.

### Core Teaching Points
- Treatment options for Graves' hyperthyroidism have not changed in decades - antithyroid drugs, radioactive iodine and thyroidectomy - and reviews of disease burden confirm all three remain the active comparators. (PMID 42683117)
- No randomized comparison has established superiority of one modality; guideline strategy therefore emphasises informed, preference-sensitive choice after euthyroidism is restored. (PMID 30193753, PMID 30730622)
- Radioiodine and thyroidectomy reliably abolish hyperthyroidism but commit the patient to lifelong levothyroxine; antithyroid drugs preserve thyroid function but carry relapse risk. (PMID 30912336)
- Antithyroid drug treatment duration relates to remission rates, though the meta-analytic evidence to date is in children - extrapolation to adults needs caution. (PMID 39501471)
- Long-term antithyroid drug therapy is an increasingly recognised strategy whose updated narrative review maps its place against radioiodine and thyroidectomy. (PMID 42119974)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Adult remission rates by antithyroid drug duration and predictive factors (TRAb, goitre size).
- Long-term quality-of-life and cardiovascular outcome differences between modalities.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- A patient-centred modality choice: orbitopathy, pregnancy plans and smoking status as decision modifiers.
- What 'definitive therapy' commits the patient to: lifelong levothyroxine after RAI/surgery.
- Relapse after antithyroid drug withdrawal: who, when, and predicted by what.
- Evidence limits: why guidelines decline to crown a winner.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 35-year-old man with recurrent Graves' relapse after 18 months of carbimazole - radioiodine, surgery, or prolonged ATD?
- Patient asks which treatment is 'best' - structuring a preference-sensitive consultation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Adult comparative-effectiveness data (remission rates, relapse predictors) are thin in the retained abstracts - the pediatric duration meta-analysis is the only quantified source; adult RCT/Registry data needed before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42683117**: *Graves' disease: review of disease characteristics, burden, and treatments* (2026) [review] - None
- **PMID 42119974**: *Long-Term Antithyroid Drug Therapy in the Management of Graves' Hyperthyroidism* (2026) [review] - None
- **PMID 30193753**: *Treatment of adult Graves' disease* (2018) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30730622**: *Graves' disease in adults: antithyroid drugs, radioactive iodine, or sometimes thyroidectomy* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 30912336**: *Graves' Disease: Can It Be Cured?* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 39501471**: *Effect of Antithyroid Drugs Treatment Duration on The Remission Rates of Graves' Disease in Children* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0756] Group 1 pulmonary arterial hypertension: ERA, PDE5i, prostacyclin, combination therapy

**Cluster**: group 1 pulmonary arterial hypertension era pde5i prostacyclin combination | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Group 1 pulmonary arterial hypertension is managed by three pathways - endothelin receptor antagonists, phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitors and prostacyclin analogues - but the defining shift of recent years is treatment strategy: risk-stratified, combination-first, then escalation.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2022 ESC/ERS guidelines introduced a refined three-category risk stratification (low, intermediate, high) that guides both initial and subsequent therapy; estimated 1-year mortality exceeds 20% in high-risk patients. (PMID 37085727)
- Initial combination therapy for PAH was first proposed for high-risk patients in the 2009 European guidelines, extended to low/intermediate-risk patients by 2015 and later consensus meetings - a concept now central to practice. (PMID 33720578)
- Modern progress in PAH therapy comes not from new pathways but from combination strategies across the endothelin, nitric-oxide and prostacyclin pathways plus systematic escalation based on risk. (PMID 30545971)
- Upfront dual oral combination therapy with an endothelin receptor antagonist (ERA: ambrisentan or macitentan) and a phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitor (PDE5i: tadalafil or sildenafil) is the standard of care for low- and intermediate-risk PAH (demonstrated in the AMBITION trial), while high-risk patients require immediate triple therapy including parenteral prostacyclin analogs. (PMID 37085727)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which initial doublet (ERA+PDE5i versus including a prostacyclin pathway agent) best serves intermediate-risk patients.
- How to time escalation and when to refer for lung transplantation assessment.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Risk-stratified treatment selection: which patient gets initial combination therapy.
- The three drug pathways and their representative agents.
- Treatment goals: moving a patient from intermediate to low risk as the therapeutic target.
- Recognising refractory disease and the transplant window.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Newly diagnosed idiopathic PAH, intermediate risk on refined scoring - initial regimen?
- Patient deteriorating on ERA+PDE5i despite 6 months of therapy - escalation options.

**Reviewer Gap**: Named pivotal trials (AMBITION, GRIPHON, SERAPHIN) and prostacyclin route practicalities are not in the retained abstracts; add ESC/ERS 2022 guideline summary and AMBITION primary paper before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30545971**: *Risk stratification and medical therapy of pulmonary arterial hypertension* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 37085727**: *Refined risk stratification, current treatment, and new therapeutic approaches in pulmonary arterial hypertension* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 33720578**: *Advantages and limitations of initial combination therapy in pulmonary arterial hypertension patients* (2020) [review] - None

## [0757] Gut Microbiome in GI Disease

**Cluster**: gut microbiome gi disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The gut microbiome has moved from speculation to mechanism in GI disease: dysbiosis is now associated with IBS, IBD and colorectal cancer, diet and food additives are established modulators, and prebiotic/probiotic strategies are being refined - though causality and strain specificity remain the frontier.

### Core Teaching Points
- Dysbiosis - imbalance between beneficial and opportunistic microbiota - is associated with IBS, IBD (ulcerative colitis and Crohn's disease) and colorectal cancer. (PMID 36157114)
- Dietary fibre from unrefined whole foods benefits gastrointestinal health, with regulatory effects on transit time, stool formation and the gut milieu. (PMID 33208922)
- Diets rich in ultra-processed foods are associated with gut disease including IBD, colorectal cancer and IBS; some additives (e.g. certain emulsifiers) directly affect gut health. (PMID 38388570)
- Prebiotics and probiotics beneficially modulate the gut microbiome, but formulating specific recommendations for GI conditions remains challenging. (PMID 38542689)
- Acute enteric infection is the best-described risk factor for IBS, linking microbiome disturbance to post-infectious functional disease. (PMID 33049223)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Causality: whether dysbiosis drives disease or mirrors it.
- Strain-specific probiotic efficacy and which patients benefit from which preparations.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism questions: dysbiosis across IBS, IBD and colorectal cancer.
- Diet as therapy: fibre, ultra-processed foods and additives.
- What probiotics can and cannot claim in GI conditions.
- Post-infectious IBS as a microbiome-mediated entity.

### Case Generation Hooks
- IBS patient asking about probiotics and diet - what does the evidence support?
- Crohn's patient in remission asking whether diet can modify relapse risk.

**Reviewer Gap**: FMT (faecal microbiota transplantation) indications and outcomes are not covered by the retained abstracts; add an FMT-specific source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36157114**: *Gut microbiota, inflammatory bowel disease and colorectal cancer* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 38542689**: *Prebiotics and Probiotics for Gastrointestinal Disorders* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 38388570**: *Ultra-processed foods and food additives in gut health and disease* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 33208922**: *Dietary fibre in gastrointestinal health and disease* (2021) [review] - None

## [0758] Gynaecomastia: causes, drug-induced, physiological, investigation, management

**Cluster**: gynaecomastia causes drug-induced physiological investigation management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Gynaecomastia - benign proliferation of male breast tissue - is common at the physiological extremes of age and, when acquired, a signal to review drugs, hypogonadism and systemic disease. Most adolescent cases need reassurance alone; the clinical task is separating the benign from the pathological.

### Core Teaching Points
- Gynaecomastia reflects an altered androgen-to-estrogen ratio with contributions from growth hormone, IGF-1, prolactin and aromatase activity. (PMID 37592101)
- Common causes are pubertal change, obesity, drugs and hypogonadism; liver and renal disease are among the systemic associations. (PMID 37592101)
- Physiological gynaecomastia occurs in almost 25% of cases - newborns, adolescents and older men - and is benign and self-limited. (PMID 27145756)
- In adolescents, most cases are idiopathic; if history and examination are reassuring, no further evaluation is required. (PMID 28613047)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which drug-induced cases regress after withdrawal and over what timeframe.
- When imaging or tissue sampling is warranted in persistent unilateral gynaecomastia.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Sorting the cause: physiological, drug-induced, hypogonadal and systemic disease.
- The reassuring adolescent: criteria for no further work-up.
- Drugs that induce gynaecomastia and the mechanism (androgen-estrogen balance).
- When 'lump in a man' stops being gynaecomastia and needs imaging.

### Case Generation Hooks
- 16-year-old with bilateral breast tenderness and reassuring examination - investigate or reassure?
- 64-year-old on spironolactone and amiodarone with new breast tenderness - next step?

**Reviewer Gap**: Definitive management (tamoxifen/raloxifene, surgery, radiotherapy) and the investigation pathway (hormones, imaging, when to biopsy) are not covered by the retained abstracts; add an endocrine-society-aligned source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37592101**: *Gynecomastia* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 28613047**: *Adolescent Gynecomastia* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 27145756**: *Gynecomastia and hormones* (2017) [review] - None

## [0759] haematological malignancy

**Cluster**: haematological malignancy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Modern haematological malignancy care is defined as much by its complications as by the diseases: CAR T-cell therapy and allogeneic transplantation have transformed outcomes while introducing distinct infection, toxicity and graft-versus-host disease challenges that cross disease boundaries.

### Core Teaching Points
- Infectious complications after CAR T-cell therapy are a significant driver of morbidity and non-relapse mortality; ASTCT best-practice guidance addresses prevention in this rapidly advancing field. (PMID 39084261)
- GVHD remains a major contributor to mortality and morbidity after allogeneic stem-cell transplantation; EBMT guidance standardises prophylaxis and management approaches. (PMID 32004485)
- Haematological and HSCT patients face life-threatening toxoplasmosis, mainly reactivation of latent cysts in seropositive patients - lack of TMP-SMX prophylaxis is a key risk factor. (PMID 38134949)
- CAR T-cell toxicities, including cytokine release syndrome and unexpected organ damage reported in early cases, require structured recognition and management protocols. (PMID 27207799)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal infection-prophylaxis duration and agent selection after CAR T across risk strata.
- How best to prevent and treat GVHD when evidence from large trials remains limited.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Cross-cutting complications: infection prophylaxis after cellular therapy.
- GVHD prevention strategies after allogeneic HSCT.
- Opportunistic infection recognition (toxoplasmosis) in the immunocompromised haematology patient.
- CAR T toxicities: recognition sequence and first-line management.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Day 10 after CAR T for diffuse large B-cell lymphoma with fever and hypotension - CRS or infection?
- Seropositive allogeneic transplant recipient not on TMP-SMX with encephalitis - what's the missed diagnosis?

**Reviewer Gap**: Disease-specific management (lymphoma, leukaemia, myeloma) is out of scope for the retained abstracts given the umbrella topic; either narrow the topic or add disease-specific guideline sources before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39084261**: *ASTCT Best Practice Considerations: Infection Prevention After CAR T-cell Therapy* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 32004485**: *Prophylaxis and management of graft versus host disease after stem-cell transplantation for haematological malignancies* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38134949**: *Guidelines for the management of Toxoplasma gondii infection and disease in patients with haematological malignancies and HSCT* (2024) [review] - None

## [0760] Haemodialysis Complications

**Cluster**: haemodialysis complications | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Haemodialysis complications cluster around vascular access - the patient's lifeline and its commonest failure point - alongside cardiovascular instability and the broader burden of kidney failure. Access planning is the most evidence-rich and most examinable corner of this topic.

### Core Teaching Points
- The KDOQI 2019 vascular access update is the comprehensive evidence-based reference for haemodialysis access, replacing the 2006 version. (PMID 32778223)
- Arteriovenous access (autogenous fistula or graft) requires an individualised strategy - the 'Patient Life-Plan' - that anticipates future access needs rather than reacting to failure. (PMID 38497953)
- KDIGO's 2024 CKD guideline updates the broader evaluation and management frame in which dialysis complications sit. (PMID 38519239)
- Intradialytic hypotension (IDH) is the most frequent acute complication of hemodialysis, driven by aggressive ultrafiltration rates exceeding vascular refill capacity; preventative management includes dialysate cooling (35-36°C), avoiding food ingestion during treatment, linear ultrafiltration profiling, and strict dry-weight optimization. (PMID 38519239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of access creation versus progression to dialysis dependence.
- How to reduce the fistula-first default for patients where grafts or catheters serve better.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Access hierarchy: fistula versus graft versus catheter and their complication profiles.
- The failing access: surveillance, stenosis and when to intervene.
- Catheter-related bloodstream infection prevention in haemodialysis units.
- Intradialytic hypotension: mechanism and management.

### Case Generation Hooks
- AV fistula with falling flows and rising venous pressures - next investigation?
- Tunnelled catheter patient with recurrent bacteraemia - how should access be re-planned?

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not cover intradialytic hypotension, dialysis amyloidosis, electrolyte/fluid shifts or long-term cardiovascular outcomes; add an HD-complications-specific review before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32778223**: *KDOQI Clinical Practice Guideline for Vascular Access: 2019 Update* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38497953**: *Arteriovenous Access for Hemodialysis: A Review* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 38519239**: *Executive summary of the KDIGO 2024 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Evaluation and Management of CKD* (2024) [guideline] - None

## [0761] Haemodynamic monitoring: arterial lines, CVP, PAC, PiCCO, FloTrac

**Cluster**: haemodynamic monitoring devices | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Haemodynamic monitoring ranges from a simple arterial line to calibrated transpulmonary thermodilution. The common error in training is treating every number as interchangeable: each device answers a different question, carries different assumptions, and fails in predictable ways. This page builds the mental model of what arterial lines, CVP, pulmonary artery catheters, and pulse-contour systems (calibrated PiCCO vs uncalibrated FloTrac) actually measure, and when dynamic tests such as passive leg raising beat static pressure readings.

### Core Teaching Points
- Arterial catheters remain the gold standard for continuous blood pressure monitoring in the ICU; intermittent non-invasive measurement may not provide enough information to determine the cause of haemodynamic instability or guide intervention. (PMID 33769132)
- Pulmonary artery catheter use has declined, but reviews highlight a striking lack of clinician knowledge of PAC-derived measurements; the technique requires considerable skill to interpret correctly. (PMID 25435479, PMID 25435477)
- Pulse-contour devices estimate cardiac output from the arterial pressure waveform; calibrated methods (transpulmonary thermodilution, e.g., PiCCO) and uncalibrated methods (e.g., FloTrac) differ fundamentally in how they maintain accuracy during changing haemodynamics. (PMID 25435477)
- Transpulmonary thermodilution provides calibrated cardiac output and volumetric indices, but technical aspects and validity limits must be understood before using derived parameters such as global end-diastolic volume or extravascular lung water in specific clinical settings. (PMID 37083621)
- Fluid responsiveness should be predicted with dynamic tests rather than static pressures: passive leg raising-induced changes in cardiac output (and pulse pressure) predict fluid responsiveness with good diagnostic performance in adults. (PMID 27858374, PMID 26825952)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- No retained source demonstrates that any particular monitoring device or strategy improves patient outcomes; device choice remains largely expert-consensus.
- How often uncalibrated pulse-contour systems should be recalibrated during rapidly changing haemodynamics is not quantified in the retained abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Static CVP vs dynamic passive-leg-raising assessment of fluid responsiveness.
- Calibrated (PiCCO/transpulmonary thermodilution) vs uncalibrated (FloTrac) pulse-contour cardiac output.
- What transpulmonary thermodilution volumetric indices (GEDV, EVLW) represent and their validity limits.
- Why pulmonary artery catheter-derived numbers are frequently misinterpreted by trainees.
- Indications and complications of arterial line insertion (continuous pressure waveform, blood sampling, damping/abnormal waveforms).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient in septic shock on noradrenaline has a damped arterial line tracing; interpret the waveform and decide what the reading can and cannot guide.
- After major surgery, FloTrac and PiCCO cardiac outputs disagree during fluid loading; decide which to trust and why.

**Reviewer Gap**: Outcome-trial evidence for monitoring strategies, non-invasive modalities (bioreactance, NIRS), and CVP interpretation limits are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against a full critical-care haemodynamics source before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25435479**: *Invasive hemodynamic monitoring.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 33769132**: *Updates in Hemodynamic Monitoring: A Review for Pharmacists.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 37083621**: *Transpulmonary thermodilution.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 25435477**: *Minimally invasive monitoring.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 27858374**: *Prediction of fluid responsiveness: an update.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 26825952**: *Passive leg raising for predicting fluid responsiveness: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2016) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0762] Haemoglobin Electrophoresis Interpretation

**Cluster**: haemoglobinopathy laboratory diagnosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Interpreting a haemoglobin electrophoresis (or HPLC/capillary electrophoresis) report is a pattern-recognition skill that links quantitative haemoglobin fractions to red-cell indices and, ultimately, DNA confirmation. The trainee should learn the two-step discipline: screen with haematological indices plus separation techniques, then confirm variants genetically — never diagnose a carrier from an electrophoresis trace alone.

### Core Teaching Points
- Detecting and characterising variant haemoglobins and thalassaemias depends on a combination of biophysical, biochemical and genetic assays that provide phenotypic or genotypic evidence of pathology. (PMID 25314938)
- Conventional screening combines haematological indices with HPLC or capillary electrophoresis; abnormal screening results in at-risk adults require confirmatory (genetic) testing before counselling. (PMID 27890718)
- Different combinations of alpha- and beta-globin gene defects produce distinct disease phenotypes (homozygous beta-thalassaemia, beta-thalassaemia/HbE, Hb Bart's hydrops), so interpretation must integrate Hb fractions such as HbA2 and HbF with red-cell indices. (PMID 32671092)
- Elevated HbA2 (>3.5%) on capillary electrophoresis or HPLC confirms the diagnosis of beta-thalassemia trait (differentiating it from iron deficiency anemia), whereas marked elevation of HbF indicates hereditary persistence of fetal hemoglobin (HPFH) or beta-thalassemia major/intermedia, and HbS migration establishes sickle cell hemoglobinopathies. (PMID 32671092)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- How to resolve discordance between capillary electrophoresis and HPLC in individual carriers is not addressed in the retained abstracts.
- Cost-effectiveness and choice between screening technologies varies by population prevalence and is not settled in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Raised HbA2 pattern and the beta-thalassaemia trait diagnosis.
- Order of testing: haematological indices -> separation technique -> DNA confirmation.
- Common variant co-migration problems (e.g., HbS vs other variants) and why electrophoresis alone can mislead.
- Population screening and antenatal carrier counselling workflow.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An antenatal couple both show microcytosis; interpret their HPLC traces and decide what confirmatory testing is needed before counselling.
- An asymptomatic adult has unexplained microcytosis with normal iron studies and a subtle HPLC abnormality; work through the differential.

**Reviewer Gap**: Newborn-screening algorithms, quantitation cut-offs for HbA2/HbF, and rare/unstable variant patterns are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against a laboratory-medicine reference before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25314938**: *Advances in detection of hemoglobinopathies.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 32671092**: *Update in Laboratory Diagnosis of Thalassemia.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 27890718**: *Thalassaemia screening and confirmation of carriers in parents.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0763] Haemolytic Uraemic Syndrome

**Cluster**: haemolytic uraemic syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Haemolytic uraemic syndrome is a thrombotic microangiopathy defined by microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia, thrombocytopenia and acute kidney injury — but the label covers two very different diseases. Shiga-toxin-producing E. coli HUS in children is largely supportive care; atypical HUS is an uncontrolled-complement disorder whose natural history has been transformed by C5 inhibitors. The first clinical skill is refusing to treat the triad before you know which HUS you are looking at, and excluding TTP.

### Core Teaching Points
- HUS is characterised by the triad of microangiopathic haemolytic anaemia, thrombocytopenia and acute kidney failure; most cases are caused by Shiga-toxin-producing E. coli, transmitted through vehicles such as ground beef and unpasteurised milk, and STEC-HUS is the main cause of acute renal failure in children. (PMID 36880929)
- The HUS spectrum includes triggers ranging from infection through medication to malignancy and transplantation, all converging on endothelial damage; on that spectrum atypical HUS is the complement-mediated end. (PMID 38523374)
- Atypical HUS is a rare thrombotic microangiopathy driven by uncontrolled alternative-complement-pathway activation, with high morbidity and mortality without targeted treatment, so distinguishing it from STEC-HUS and TTP before committing to therapy is critical. (PMID 30294946, PMID 30031798)
- Complement inhibitors such as eculizumab and ravulizumab have transformed the management of atypical HUS, changing both acute outcomes and long-term management of complement-mediated TMA. (PMID 41347985)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration and de-escalation of eculizumab/ravulizumab therapy are not defined in the retained abstracts.
- How genetics should stratify renal-transplant recurrence risk and timing of complement blockade is not detailed in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating STEC-HUS, atypical HUS and TTP at first presentation.
- Complement biology of aHUS and the mechanism of eculizumab (C5 blockade).
- The clinical triad and laboratory hallmarks of thrombotic microangiopathy (schistocytes, ADAMTS13 context).
- Supportive-care priorities in paediatric STEC-HUS.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A child with bloody diarrhoea, pallor, thrombocytopenia and rising creatinine; classify the HUS and outline management.
- An adult with TMA features, no STEC evidence and a family history of renal disease; decide when to start complement blockade.

**Reviewer Gap**: Antibiotic/antimotility decisions in STEC-HUS, plasma-exchange indications, and anti-factor-H autoantibody disease management are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against a full TMA guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36880929**: *Hemolytic-Uremic Syndrome in Children.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 38523374**: *Management of pediatric hemolytic uremic syndrome.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 30294946**: *Atypical Hemolytic-Uremic Syndrome: An Update on Pathophysiology, Diagnosis, and Treatment.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 41347985**: *Long-term outcome and management of complement-mediated thrombotic microangiopathy/aHUS.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 30031798**: *Atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome: Review of clinical presentation, diagnosis and management.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0764] Haemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis HLH: HScore, ferritin, triggers, treatment

**Cluster**: haemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
HLH is the diagnosis trainees meet too late: a hyperinflammatory storm of cytotoxic T cells, NK cells and macrophages that mimics sepsis, flare and liver failure all at once. Recognition rests on a constellation — fever, cytopenias, hepatosplenomegaly, coagulopathy, very high ferritin — plus a determined search for the trigger. Management has two parallel tracks: control the immune activation and treat whatever provoked it.

### Core Teaching Points
- HLH is a life-threatening hyperinflammatory syndrome associated with multiple-organ dysfunction; fever, cytopenias, coagulopathy and hepatosplenomegaly are hallmark findings, and early recognition is critical. (PMID 31151946)
- The disease is driven by uncontrolled activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes, natural killer cells and macrophages, producing hypercytokinemia and immune-mediated injury of multiple organ systems. (PMID 34347856)
- Primary (genetic) HLH predominates in children, whereas secondary (acquired) HLH — frequently triggered by infection — dominates in adults; management recommendations for adults emphasise trigger-directed treatment alongside immunosuppression. (PMID 30992265, PMID 37426667)
- Untreated HLH carries high mortality, and a multidisciplinary approach is needed for diagnosis and management. (PMID 41288529)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing and intensity of etoposide-based regimens versus biologic therapy in adult secondary HLH is not settled in the retained abstracts.
- Whether ferritin trajectory can guide response monitoring is suggested clinically but not quantified in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HScore components and how probability is estimated from clinical and laboratory variables.
- Extreme ferritin elevation as a red-flag investigation in unexplained fever plus cytopenias.
- Common triggers to search for: EBV and other infections, malignancy (especially lymphoma), and rheumatologic disease.
- Distinguishing HLH from sepsis, liver failure and macrophage activation syndrome.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient on the hepatology ward with fever, cytopenias, coagulopathy and ferritin in the tens of thousands; build the differential and the diagnostic work-up.
- A young adult with EBV infection and progressive cytopenias and splenomegaly; decide when HLH therapy should start.

**Reviewer Gap**: HScore calculation specifics, HLH-94/HLH-2004 protocol details, and ruxolitinib/anakinra positioning cannot be verified from the retained abstracts; confirm against the full adult recommendations (30992265) before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31151946**: *Hemophagocytic Lymphohistiocytosis.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 34347856**: *Hemophagocytic Lymphohistiocytosis.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 30992265**: *Recommendations for the management of hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis in adults.* (2019) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41288529**: *Hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis in adults.* (2026) [review] - None
- **PMID 37426667**: *Approaching hemophagocytic lymphohistiocytosis.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0765] Haemophilia A and B

**Cluster**: haemophilia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Haemophilia A and B are X-linked factor VIII and IX deficiencies whose modern story is a treatment revolution — from on-demand replacement to regular prophylaxis, extended half-life products, the bispecific antibody emicizumab, and now gene therapy. The trainee should anchor on three things: severity is defined by factor activity, joints are the organ that suffers, and the ageing haemophilia population now faces the same comorbidity trade-offs as everyone else.

### Core Teaching Points
- Haemophilia A and B are congenital X-linked bleeding disorders caused by deficiency of factor VIII and factor IX respectively; severe deficiency, defined as less than 1% of normal factor activity, is associated with spontaneous bleeding within the first years of life. (PMID 40023652)
- Recurrent joint bleeding (haemarthrosis) is the most frequent clinical manifestation of severe haemophilia, and unless appropriately managed — including subclinical bleeds — it progresses to haemophilic arthropathy. (PMID 34197690)
- The World Federation of Hemophilia 3rd-edition guidelines are the global reference standard for the management of haemophilia. (PMID 32744769)
- Emicizumab, a bispecific antibody recognising FIX(a)/FX that mimics FVIIIa cofactor activity, transformed prophylaxis through subcutaneous administration and a long half-life, in patients with or without FVIII inhibitors. (PMID 37916312)
- Effective treatment has increased life expectancy, creating a middle-aged and elderly population with haemophilia whose management is complicated by the opposing risks of comorbidities and bleeding. (PMID 30592034)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term durability and eligibility criteria for gene therapy are still evolving and are not settled in the retained sources.
- Sequencing of non-factor therapies (emicizumab, siRNA agents) relative to extended half-life factor concentrates is not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- X-linked inheritance and carrier counselling.
- Severity grading by factor activity level (severe <1%) and its clinical consequence.
- Haemarthrosis as the hallmark of severe disease and the rationale for primary prophylaxis.
- Emicizumab mechanism and why it is not measured as factor VIII.
- Management tensions in elderly patients with haemophilia and cardiovascular disease.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A toddler with repeated spontaneous knee swellings and a maternal family history of bleeding; outline the diagnostic pathway and first-line management.
- A 68-year-old with haemophilia presents with myocardial infarction and needs antiplatelet therapy; weigh the risks.

**Reviewer Gap**: Inhibitor development, immune-tolerance induction, and gene-therapy specifics are not detailed in the retained abstracts; verify against WFH 3rd-edition (32744769) full text before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40023652**: *Haemophilia.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 32744769**: *WFH Guidelines for the Management of Hemophilia, 3rd edition.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37916312**: *Emicizumab: the hemophilia A game-changer.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 34197690**: *Hemophilic arthropathy: Current knowledge and future perspectives.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 30592034**: *Haemophilia and ageing.* (2019) [review] - None

## [0766] haemophilia a factor viii prophylaxis and emicizumab for inhibitor patients

**Cluster**: haemophilia a prophylaxis and emicizumab | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
For a patient with severe haemophilia A, prophylaxis is the default philosophy: keep trough factor levels high enough that joints never bleed. The modern question is no longer whether to give prophylaxis, but which agent — intravenous extended-half-life factor VIII, or subcutaneous emicizumab that works even when an inhibitor has destroyed the patient's factor VIII. This page contrasts those options and the evidence comparing them.

### Core Teaching Points
- Emicizumab is a bispecific antibody recognising FIX(a)/FX that mimics FVIIIa cofactor activity; its long half-life and subcutaneous injectability dramatically improved haemophilia A treatment regardless of FVIII inhibitor status. (PMID 32558019)
- Emicizumab was developed in response to the unmet needs of adherence to prophylaxis and the lack of effective long-term prophylaxis, especially in patients with inhibitors; it is the first non-factor medicine for subcutaneous administration in severe and moderate haemophilia A with or without inhibitors. (PMID 37916312)
- In the phase 3 XTEND-1 trial, efanesoctocog alfa prophylaxis provided superior bleed protection compared with pre-trial FVIII prophylaxis in patients with severe haemophilia A, and an indirect comparison addressed its efficacy relative to emicizumab in patients without inhibitors. (PMID 39576432)
- Regular FVIII replacement prophylaxis remains the gold standard for moderate-to-severe haemophilia A, with extended half-life products reducing infusion burden compared with conventional treatment. (PMID 35985706)
- For decades the only way to prevent bleeding was factor infusion; without it, patients face debilitating joint damage from recurrent haemarthroses and life-threatening bleeds including intracranial haemorrhage — the rationale for effective prophylaxis in both inhibitor and non-inhibitor patients. (PMID 31130012)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Management of breakthrough bleeds in patients on emicizumab, including bypassing-agent choice and dosing, is not covered in the retained abstracts.
- Indirect-comparison limitations between efanesoctocog alfa and emicizumab mean head-to-head evidence is still lacking.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Emicizumab's mechanism (bispecific FIX(a)/FX antibody mimicking FVIIIa) and its implications for coagulation assays.
- Which patients qualify for emicizumab — with and without inhibitors.
- XTEND-1 findings for efanesoctocog alfa and what an indirect comparison can and cannot tell us.
- Rationale for prophylaxis: preventing haemarthrosis and intracranial haemorrhage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A boy with high-titre FVIII inhibitors and frequent bleeds despite on-demand therapy; decide on emicizumab and counsel the family.
- An adolescent on daily FVIII prophylaxis has poor venous access and declining adherence; weigh switching to emicizumab or extended half-life product.

**Reviewer Gap**: Breakthrough-bleed management algorithms, inhibitor eradication (ITI), and monitoring assays on emicizumab are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against WFH guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37916312**: *Emicizumab: the hemophilia A game-changer.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 31130012**: *Emicizumab for hemophilia A without inhibitors.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 39576432**: *Efanesoctocog Alfa Versus Emicizumab in Adolescent and Adult Patients With Haemophilia A Without Inhibitors.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 35985706**: *Advances in Hemophilia A Management.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 32558019**: *Substitution therapy.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0767] haemorrhagic stroke

**Cluster**: haemorrhagic stroke | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Haemorrhagic stroke — intracerebral haemorrhage above all — carries the worst early prognosis of any stroke type, and the pace of the first hours decides the outcome. Trainees should hold three facts firmly: it is the minority of strokes but a disproportionate share of stroke deaths; untreated hypertension is its engine worldwide; and management is a race against haematoma expansion rather than a hunt for reperfusion.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hemorrhagic stroke comprises about 20% of all strokes, with intracerebral haemorrhage the most common type; its frequency is increased where hypertension is untreated, and it carries a disproportionately high risk of early mortality and long-term disability. (PMID 33272397)
- Stroke is the second leading cause of death and a major cause of disability worldwide; although ischaemic stroke is more frequent, haemorrhagic stroke is responsible for more deaths. (PMID 29791947)
- Intracerebral haemorrhage is a devastating form of stroke with high morbidity and mortality, and its incidence has increased worldwide over the past 40 years. (PMID 35420918)
- Contemporary ICH care addresses the cause, mechanisms of injury, and current treatment strategies, including the need to limit ongoing injury after the initial bleed. (PMID 35420918)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which patients benefit from surgical haematoma evacuation, and under what timing, is not addressed in the retained abstracts.
- Optimal blood-pressure targets and anticoagulant-reversal strategies are not quantified in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Hypertension as the leading risk factor for spontaneous intracerebral haemorrhage.
- Lobar vs deep haemorrhage locations and their implication for underlying causes (including amyloid angiopathy vs hypertension).
- Priorities in anticoagulant-associated ICH.
- Why haemorrhagic stroke, though less frequent than ischaemic stroke, accounts for a larger share of deaths.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An elderly patient on warfarin is brought in with a lobar haemorrhage and a falling GCS; sequence the first-hour priorities.
- A 45-year-old with poorly controlled hypertension has a deep basal-ganglia haemorrhage; discuss prevention of recurrence.

**Reviewer Gap**: Only three sources were retained. Anticoagulation-reversal specifics, surgical-trial evidence, and subarachnoid-haemorrhage content need expansion before this draft can be approved.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35420918**: *Cerebral Hemorrhage: Pathophysiology, Treatment, and Future Directions.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 33272397**: *Hemorrhagic stroke.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 29791947**: *Global Burden of Stroke.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0768] Hand and erosive OA: Heberden nodes, Bouchard nodes, trapeziometacarpal OA

**Cluster**: hand osteoarthritis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hand osteoarthritis is the commonest joint disease most trainees dismiss as untreatable. Recognise the phenotype first: Heberden's nodes at the distal interphalangeal joints, Bouchard's at the proximal, and the thumb base (trapeziometacarpal) pattern that dominates hand function. Then match treatment to the 2019 ACR/Arthritis Foundation guideline rather than reflex-prescribing — and remember the radiograph often under-reads the symptoms, or vice versa.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2019 ACR/Arthritis Foundation guideline provides evidence-based recommendations for the comprehensive management of hand (as well as hip and knee) osteoarthritis, updating the 2012 ACR recommendations. (PMID 31908149)
- Hand OA prevalence increases with age and more often affects females; clinical signs correlate poorly with radiographic findings, with symptomatic hand OA affecting roughly a quarter of adults in the cited series. (PMID 27917634)
- Pharmaceutical treatment recommendations for osteoarthritis span nine classes including oral NSAIDs, with guideline recommendations that differ by mechanism, pharmacokinetics and toxicity profile. (PMID 36414224)
- Contemporary osteoarthritis therapy research is an active field; curated annual reviews are a practical way to keep pace with changing recommendations. (PMID 39103081)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The erosive (inflammatory) hand-OA phenotype — its diagnostic criteria and whether treatment should differ — is not addressed in the retained abstracts.
- Surgical options for trapeziometacarpal OA (e.g., trapeziectomy) lack validated evidence in this draft because the rhizarthrosis source had no readable abstract.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Heberden's (DIP) vs Bouchard's (PIP) nodes and the thumb-base (trapeziometacarpal) pattern.
- Radiograph-clinical discordance in hand OA.
- First-line pharmacotherapy for hand OA per the 2019 ACR/Arthritis Foundation guideline.
- Erosive OA features (sudden inflammatory episodes, 'gull-wing' change) as a distinct phenotype.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A postmenopausal woman has painful DIP nodes and weak pinch grip; outline a guideline-concordant management plan.
- A manual worker has thumb-base pain provoked by grip and key-turning; decide on imaging, splinting, and escalation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Erosive-OA specifics, intra-articular injection evidence, and trapeziometacarpal surgical options could not be validated from the retained abstracts (the rhizarthrosis source lacked an abstract); treat these sections as needing full-text evidence before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31908149**: *2019 American College of Rheumatology/Arthritis Foundation Guideline for the Management of Osteoarthritis of the Hand, Hip, and Knee.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 27917634**: *[Hand osteoarthritis].* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 36414224**: *Pharmaceutical treatment of osteoarthritis.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 39103081**: *Osteoarthritis year in review 2024: Epidemiology and therapy.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0769] Head and neck squamous cell chemoradiation

**Cluster**: head and neck squamous cell carcinoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Most head and neck cancers are squamous cell carcinomas of the mucosal upper aerodigestive tract, and the biology is shifting: tobacco- and alcohol-driven oral cavity and laryngeal disease alongside HPV-driven oropharyngeal disease in younger patients. Treatment is site-specific — surgery first for the oral cavity, organ-preservation chemoradiation for larynx/hypopharynx, and now perioperative immunotherapy trials rewriting the resectable-disease playbook.

### Core Teaching Points
- Most head and neck cancers arise from the mucosal epithelium of the oral cavity, pharynx and larynx (HNSCC); oral cavity and laryngeal cancers are generally associated with tobacco and alcohol, whereas pharyngeal cancers are increasingly linked to HPV. (PMID 33243986)
- Head and neck cancer is the seventh most common cancer worldwide, with approximately 71,110 individuals diagnosed in the US in 2024. (PMID 41396597)
- Established risk factors include tobacco exposure and alcohol dependence, with oncogenic-virus infection increasingly associated with cancers of the pharyngeal subsites. (PMID 26944243)
- For oral cavity cancer, the standard of care is primary surgical resection with or without postoperative adjuvant therapy, and despite advances in surgery, radiotherapy and chemotherapy, survival outcomes remain suboptimal. (PMID 25979396, PMID 40547025)
- Positive randomised trials of perioperative immune-checkpoint inhibition (KEYNOTE-689 and NIVOPOSTOP) may redefine current and future treatment paradigms for resectable, locally advanced HNSCC. (PMID 40907366)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- De-escalation strategies for HPV-positive oropharyngeal cancer (reduced radiation or chemotherapy intensity) are not resolved in the retained abstracts.
- How perioperative immunotherapy will integrate with established chemoradiation regimens for unresected disease is an open question.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HPV-driven oropharyngeal cancer epidemiology and its better prognosis.
- Oral cavity SCC: why surgery is the standard of care.
- Organ-preservation chemoradiation concept for laryngeal cancer.
- KEYNOTE-689 and NIVOPOSTOP: what changed for resectable locally advanced HNSCC.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old non-smoker has a HPV-positive tonsillar SCC with neck nodes; compare treatment options and discuss de-escalation questions.
- A patient with oral tongue SCC returns with positive margins after resection; outline adjuvant decision-making.

**Reviewer Gap**: Concurrent chemoradiation specifics (cisplatin dosing/scheduling, TPF induction, cetuximab-radiation evidence) and de-escalation trial data are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against a full HNSCC guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33243986**: *Head and neck squamous cell carcinoma.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 41396597**: *Head and Neck Cancer: A Review.* (2026) [review] - None
- **PMID 26944243**: *Head and Neck Squamous Cell Carcinoma: Update on Epidemiology, Diagnosis, and Treatment.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 40547025**: *Advances and challenges in immunotherapy in head and neck cancer.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 25979396**: *Cancer of the oral cavity.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 40907366**: *Comparative review of KEYNOTE-689 and NIVOPOSTOP trials and their impact on perioperative immunotherapy in locally advanced head and neck squamous cell carcinoma.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0770] head injury

**Cluster**: traumatic brain injury | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Head injury spans a huge severity range, and the evidence base splits accordingly: guidelines for severe TBI built around intracranial pressure and cerebral edema, and a parallel literature on concussion and mild TBI where the challenge is recognition and safe return to activity. The trainee should learn the severe-disease framework (ICP tiers, decompressive craniectomy, what to do when no ICP monitor exists) and the mild-disease discipline (structured assessment, red flags, stepwise return to play).

### Core Teaching Points
- The Brain Trauma Foundation updated its severe-TBI decompressive-craniectomy recommendations in 2020 in light of the RESCUEicp randomised trial of decompressive craniectomy for traumatic intracranial hypertension. (PMID 32761068)
- Acute cerebral edema and elevated intracranial pressure are common in neurological injury, but practical guidance on selecting and monitoring initial therapies has been lacking; the neurocritical-care guideline evaluates therapies for efficacy and safety. (PMID 32227294)
- Where ICP monitoring is unavailable, the consensus-based CREVICE protocol provides a management algorithm for severe TBI based on CT imaging to identify and treat suspected intracranial hypertension. (PMID 32013721)
- Concussion and mild TBI describe a common disorder with substantial public-health effects; advances in imaging, non-imaging biomarkers and neuropathology continue to reshape diagnosis, prognosis and clinical management. (PMID 25801547)
- Sport-related concussion management was updated by the 6th International Conference on Concussion in Sport (Amsterdam, October 2022), whose statement summarises current processes and outcomes. (PMID 37316210)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The efficacy of ICP-monitoring-guided therapy versus imaging-and-examination-guided care remains debated and is not resolved in the retained abstracts.
- Which concussion biomarkers or imaging findings should change management is still evolving.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- What RESCUEicp changed about decompressive craniectomy recommendations in severe TBI.
- Tiered management of elevated ICP and acute cerebral edema.
- Management of suspected intracranial hypertension when no ICP monitor is available (CT-guided protocols).
- Red flags and structured assessment after mild TBI/concussion.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A road-traffic-accident victim arrives with GCS 6 and a unilateral dilated pupil; sequence the first hours of management.
- A club rugby player has had two concussions this season; counsel on assessment and return-to-play.

**Reviewer Gap**: Paediatric head injury, anticoagulated elderly patients with minor TBI, seizure prophylaxis, and mild-TBI imaging rules are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full BTF guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32761068**: *Guidelines for the Management of Severe Traumatic Brain Injury: 2020 Update of the Decompressive Craniectomy Recommendations.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 32227294**: *Guidelines for the Acute Treatment of Cerebral Edema in Neurocritical Care Patients.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 32013721**: *Consensus-Based Management Protocol (CREVICE Protocol) for the Treatment of Severe Traumatic Brain Injury Based on Computed Tomography Imaging in Settings Without Intracranial Pressure Monitoring.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 25801547**: *Diagnosis, prognosis, and clinical management of mild traumatic brain injury.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 37316210**: *Consensus statement on concussion in sport: the 6th International Conference on Concussion in Sport-Amsterdam, October 2022.* (2023) [article] - None

## [0771] healthcare associated mrsa decolonization and prevention

**Cluster**: MRSA decolonization and prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
S. aureus is a leading cause of healthcare-associated infection — central-line bloodstream infections, surgical site infections, and more — and nasal carriage is the reservoir most interventions target. Decolonization (topical antiseptics, intranasal antibiotic regimens, and targeted screening) sits alongside contact precautions and bundles as the core preventive toolkit. The trainee should understand who to screen, what regimens exist, and where the evidence is strongest — cardiac and orthopaedic surgery — versus where it is weaker.

### Core Teaching Points
- S. aureus is a common skin commensal and a leading cause of healthcare-associated infections including central-line-associated bloodstream infection and surgical site infection, which makes decolonization a central prevention strategy. (PMID 42624765)
- Nasal colonization with MRSA is the key target for eradication interventions, and multiple interventions targeting the nasal microbiome have been developed for this purpose. (PMID 39481681)
- Healthcare-adapted MRSA spread globally over 50 years, and community strains capable of infecting healthy individuals have since emerged; control requires strategies for both healthcare- and community-associated spread. (PMID 29190327)
- S. aureus is the most common microbial cause of surgical site infections, and screening with decolonization before surgery is used to reduce SSI risk, particularly in high-risk procedures. (PMID 27424948)
- Staphylococcal screening and suppression is an interventional strategy aimed at reduction of surgical site infections; its effectiveness depends on the screening strategy and agent used. (PMID 26836053)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal decolonization regimen, duration, and the role of systemic versus topical agents are not settled in the retained abstracts.
- How decolonization interacts with antimicrobial stewardship and resistance selection is not quantified in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Screen-then-decolonize workflow for elective surgery (who, when, with what).
- Nasal carriage as the reservoir for healthcare-associated S. aureus infection.
- Healthcare-associated vs community-associated MRSA epidemiology and control measures.
- Which procedures have the strongest evidence for decolonization benefit.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient due for elective knee replacement is found to be MRSA-positive on pre-admission screening; outline the decolonization plan.
- A ward has a cluster of MRSA bacteraemias; design a prevention bundle incorporating decolonization and contact precautions.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific agent regimens (mupirocin vs povidone-iodine vs octenidine), chlorhexidine bathing evidence, and ICU-wide universal decolonization trial data are not detailed in the retained abstracts; verify against an IDSA/SHEA prevention guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42624765**: *The Role of Decolonization in Prevention of Staphylococcus aureus Infection.* (2026) [review] - None
- **PMID 39481681**: *Interventions targeting the nasal microbiome to eradicate methicillin-resistant Staphylococcus aureus.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 29190327**: *Control of healthcare- and community-associated MRSA: recent progress and persisting challenges.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 27424948**: *Staphylococcus aureus and surgical site infections: benefits of screening and decolonization before surgery.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 26836053**: *Is Staphylococcal Screening and Suppression an Effective Interventional Strategy for Reduction of Surgical Site Infections?* (2016) [review] - None

## [0772] heart failure

**Cluster**: heart failure | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Heart failure is a clinical syndrome — dyspnoea or exertional limitation from impaired ventricular filling or ejection — that now divides by ejection fraction, because therapy that transforms HFrEF does little or nothing for HFpEF beyond SGLT2 inhibitors. Anchor on the 2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA guideline as the framework, then learn the phenotype split (roughly half of all HF is preserved EF) and the four pillars of HFrEF disease-modifying therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA guideline replaces both the 2013 ACCF/AHA guideline and the 2017 focused update, and is the current American framework for heart failure management. (PMID 35363499)
- The 2023 ESC focused update introduced key new recommendations led by SGLT2 inhibitors across the ejection-fraction spectrum, reflecting major recent progress in HF management. (PMID 38806171)
- Worldwide HF burden has grown to an estimated 23 million people, with approximately half of cases being HFrEF; HF is a clinical syndrome of dyspnoea or exertional limitation from impaired ventricular filling or ejection. (PMID 32749493)
- HFpEF represents at least half of all heart failure cases, and its recognition is rising with population ageing and comorbidity burden such as obesity and diabetes. (PMID 38367642)
- HF prevalence is increasing because of population ageing and improved survival after acute cardiovascular events, despite effective therapies — making long-term management a growing clinical load. (PMID 28460827)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- HFpEF treatment beyond SGLT2 inhibitors remains an active research area with no established disease-modifying standard comparable to HFrEF quadruple therapy.
- Optimal transitions of care and the role of remote monitoring in routine HF management are not settled in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- EF-based phenotyping (HFrEF vs HFmrEF vs HFpEF) and why it drives therapy.
- The four pillars of HFrEF therapy and the guideline evidence behind each.
- SGLT2 inhibitors as the one class spanning all EF categories.
- Distinguishing HFpEF clinically (comorbidity pattern, diastolic dysfunction) from HFrEF.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 70-year-old with dyspnoea, EF 30% and diabetes; structure guideline-directed therapy and sequencing.
- A patient with obesity, hypertension and EF 55% has exertional dyspnoea; work through the HFpEF diagnostic pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: Acute heart failure management, device therapy indications (ICD/CRT), and transition-of-care models are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full 2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35363499**: *2022 AHA/ACC/HFSA Guideline for the Management of Heart Failure.* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38806171**: *2024 update in heart failure.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 32749493**: *Heart Failure With Reduced Ejection Fraction: A Review.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 38367642**: *Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction: everything the clinician needs to know.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 28460827**: *Heart failure.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0773] Heart failure landmark trials: PARADIGM-HF, DAPA-HF, EMPEROR-Reduced insights

**Cluster**: heart failure landmark trials | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Three trials reset HFrEF practice. PARADIGM-HF showed sacubitril/valsartan beat enalapril; DAPA-HF and EMPEROR-Reduced showed SGLT2 inhibitors cut cardiovascular death and heart-failure hospitalisation in patients with and without diabetes. Together they form the spine of modern guideline-directed therapy — and this page reads them as a set, including the sobering finding that real-world patients often fail the trials' eligibility criteria.

### Core Teaching Points
- Landmark trials established new treatments for HFrEF: sacubitril/valsartan was superior to enalapril in PARADIGM-HF, and SGLT2 inhibitors were added to the therapeutic armamentarium. (PMID 33277825)
- A network meta-analysis compared the treatment arms of sacubitril/valsartan, vericiguat and the SGLT2 inhibitors dapagliflozin and empagliflozin, each proven effective in phase 3 HFrEF trials, allowing relative efficacy to be estimated across drugs. (PMID 33074526)
- When eligibility criteria from DAPA-HF, EMPEROR-Reduced and PARADIGM-HF are applied to real outpatient populations with chronic heart failure, a substantial proportion of routine patients fall outside trial inclusion criteria — a key caveat when extrapolating trial effect sizes to clinic practice. (PMID 37952788)
- Cross-trial aggregate-data analyses estimate the expected combined benefit of comprehensive (quintuple) disease-modifying pharmacological treatment in HFrEF, suggesting effects accumulate when therapies are combined. (PMID 42741561)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Cross-trial and network meta-analyses carry indirect-comparison limitations; true head-to-head outcome data between individual agents are lacking.
- How trial-ineligible patients (eGFR limits, blood-pressure floors, recent hospitalisation) respond to the same drugs is less certain.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- What PARADIGM-HF compared and what the primary outcome showed (ARNI vs ACE inhibitor).
- DAPA-HF and EMPEROR-Reduced: the populations, primary endpoints, and diabetes-independence of SGLT2 benefit.
- Why real-world eligibility for landmark trials matters when applying effect sizes.
- How relative efficacy estimates from network meta-analyses should (and should not) be read.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old with HFrEF, eGFR 42 and SBP 98 asks whether the 'four pillars' apply to him; use trial eligibility thinking.
- An exam question quotes PARADIGM-HF, DAPA-HF and EMPEROR-Reduced hazard ratios; rank the therapies and justify combination.

**Reviewer Gap**: Exact hazard ratios and subgroup findings for each trial are not in the retained abstracts; verify numerical details against the primary trial publications before writing numeric MCQs.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33277825**: *Heart failure in the last year: progress and perspective.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 33074526**: *Relative Efficacy of Sacubitril-Valsartan, Vericiguat, and SGLT2 Inhibitors in Heart Failure with Reduced Ejection Fraction: A Network Meta-Analysis.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 37952788**: *Application of eligibility criteria from DAPA-HF, EMPEROR-Reduced, and PARADIGM-HF trials to a population with heart failure.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 42741561**: *Estimating the Benefits of the Comprehensive Disease-modifying Pharmacological Treatment in Patients with Heart Failure with Reduced Ejection Fraction.* (2026) [review] - None

## [0774] heart failure preserved ejection fraction SGLT2 inhibitor mortality

**Cluster**: HFpEF SGLT2 inhibitors | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
For years HFpEF had no therapy that moved outcomes — every trial failed until SGLT2 inhibitors. EMPEROR-Preserved showed empagliflozin reduced the composite of cardiovascular death or heart-failure hospitalisation in HFpEF, driven by fewer worsening-HF events, positioning SGLT2 inhibitors as the new standard of care in this phenotype. Understand both the result and its texture: the benefit is real, but it is hospitalisation-driven rather than a clear mortality signal.

### Core Teaching Points
- In EMPEROR-Preserved, empagliflozin reduced the risk of cardiovascular death or hospitalization for heart failure in HFpEF (HR 0.79, 95% CI 0.69-0.90, P<.001), an effect driven by a reduction in worsening HF events; SGLT2 inhibitors are therefore likely to become the new standard of care in HFpEF. (PMID 36216487)
- The 2023 ESC focused update introduced SGLT2 inhibitors as a key new recommendation across the heart-failure spectrum, reflecting the drug class's progress from diabetes trials into HFpEF management. (PMID 38806171)
- HFpEF prevalence is increasing across all populations with high rates of hospitalization and mortality (up to 80% and 50% respectively within 5 years in the cited review), and comorbidity-driven systemic inflammation underlies its pathophysiology. (PMID 38944348)
- SGLT2 inhibitors protect the heart and kidneys of patients with or without type 2 diabetes and with preserved or reduced kidney function, through both glucose-dependent and glucose-independent mechanisms. (PMID 33197224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether SGLT2 inhibitors reduce cardiovascular or all-cause mortality specifically in HFpEF (versus hospitalisation-driven composite benefit) is not resolved in the retained sources.
- Which HFpEF subphenotypes (obesity-driven, atrial-fibrillation-driven) benefit most remains an open question.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- EMPEROR-Preserved: population, primary endpoint, hazard ratio, and what drove the benefit.
- Why SGLT2 benefit in HFpEF is considered diabetes-independent.
- The clinical-diagnostic pathway confirming HFpEF before therapy.
- Distinguishing composite-endpoint benefit from a mortality claim in HFpEF trials.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 74-year-old woman with hypertension, BMI 34 and EF 58% is hospitalised with pulmonary congestion; decide on SGLT2 inhibitor therapy and counsel on expected benefit.
- An exam vignette gives EMPEROR-Preserved's HR 0.79 and asks what the result does and does not prove about mortality.

**Reviewer Gap**: EMPEROR-Preserved subgroup analyses (EF strata, sex, diabetes status) and DELIVER-trial corroboration are not in the retained abstracts; verify against the primary trial publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36216487**: *SGLT2 Inhibitors and Heart Failure with Preserved Ejection Fraction.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 38806171**: *2024 update in heart failure.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 38944348**: *Heart failure with preserved ejection fraction epidemiology, pathophysiology, diagnosis and treatment strategies.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 33197224**: *Effects of SGLT2 Inhibitors on Kidney and Cardiovascular Function.* (2021) [review] - None
