## [0798] Hepatitis C virus direct-acting antiviral therapy

**Cluster**: HCV DAA therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Direct-acting antivirals target specific HCV proteins — NS3/4A protease, NS5A, NS5B polymerase — and combining agents from different classes produced the cure rates that define modern hepatology. This page focuses on the therapy itself: how DAAs transformed the natural history, how well they work in special populations (the elderly, patients with HCC), and the uncomfortable gap between efficacy and delivery, since global treatment rates remain far below need.

### Core Teaching Points
- HCV is a hepatotropic RNA virus causing progressive liver damage resulting in cirrhosis, decompensated liver disease and hepatocellular carcinoma; WHO called for HCV elimination in 2016, made conceivable by DAA therapy. (PMID 37741678)
- HCV remains a major cause of chronic liver disease and premature mortality worldwide, and WHO and the US Department of Health and Human Services have committed to eliminating HCV as a public health problem. (PMID 40971900)
- A systematic review and meta-analysis quantified global DAA treatment rates and identified barriers to treatment in the DAA era, showing efficacy has not translated into universal access. (PMID 36825358)
- Meta-analysis of patients aged 60 and older confirms DAA efficacy and safety in elderly HCV patients. (PMID 35357774)
- The effect of hepatocellular carcinoma on response to interferon-free DAA therapy in chronic HCV has been evaluated in systematic review and meta-analysis, informing treatment of patients with HCC history. (PMID 31096005)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether DAAs should be given before or after HCC treatment, and their impact on HCC recurrence, remain debated and are not resolved in the retained sources.
- Barriers to DAA access (cost, infrastructure, stigma) persist despite high efficacy, and solutions vary by health system.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- DAA targets: NS3/4A protease inhibitors, NS5A inhibitors, NS5B polymerase inhibitors and why they are combined.
- Treatment access gap: efficacy versus global treatment rates.
- DAAs in special populations: elderly, prior HCC, advanced fibrosis.
- HCV elimination targets and the role of treatment-as-prevention.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A national programme has cure rates >95% but treatment coverage of 20%; discuss barriers and system fixes.
- A patient with successfully treated early HCC has detectable HCV; discuss DAA timing and counselling.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific combination regimens, treatment duration by cirrhosis status, and HCC-recurrence evidence are not in the retained abstracts; verify against EASL/AASLD guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37741678**: *Hepatitis C.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 40971900**: *State-of-the-Art Review: Hepatitis C.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 36825358**: *Global treatment rate and barriers to direct-acting antiviral therapy: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 35357774**: *Efficacy and safety of direct-acting antiviral therapy for hepatitis C virus in elderly patients (>=60): A meta-analysis.* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 31096005**: *Sustained virologic response to direct-acting antiviral therapy in patients with chronic hepatitis C and hepatocellular carcinoma.* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0799] Hepatitis D: superinfection vs coinfection, severity, peginterferon, bulevirtide

**Cluster**: hepatitis D | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hepatitis D is the defective satellite virus that only exists alongside hepatitis B — it needs HBsAg to enter and propagate — and it converts a manageable HBV infection into the most severe form of human viral hepatitis. The clinical fork is coinfection (HDV and HBV acquired together, often transient) versus superinfection (HDV landing on established chronic HBV, which usually becomes chronic and aggressive). Treatment was peginterferon for three decades with minority response rates; bulevirtide, the first-in-class entry inhibitor, has finally given the field an approved targeted drug.

### Core Teaching Points
- Chronic hepatitis D results from infection with HBV and HDV, and is the most severe form of human viral hepatitis; current treatment options consist of interferon alfa, effective only in a minority of patients. (PMID 31266627)
- Chronic hepatitis D results from infection with both HBV and HDV together, and it is the most severe form of human viral hepatitis — which is why HDV screening matters in every patient with chronic HBV. (PMID 31266627)
- Pegylated interferon-alpha treatment of chronic HDV gives variable and often poor responses; bulevirtide was recently approved with promising results, and meta-analysis now compares regimens comparatively. (PMID 40668572)
- Bulevirtide, a first-in-class entry inhibitor, is the first drug formally approved for chronic hepatitis delta, whereas pegylated interferon has been used off-label since the 1990s; current evidence compares bulevirtide monotherapy with combination treatment. (PMID 41134660)
- Blocking cell entry is a suitable therapeutic approach, and bulevirtide works by inhibiting the bile-acid transporter NTCP that HDV uses for hepatocyte entry. (PMID 35028672)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of bulevirtide therapy and durability of response after stopping are not settled in the retained sources.
- Whether combination bulevirtide plus peginterferon outperforms either alone needs head-to-head confirmation beyond current evidence.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Coinfection versus superinfection: clinical course and chronicity risk.
- Why HDV requires HBV (HBsAg dependence).
- Peginterferon limitations: response in a minority, off-label status.
- Bulevirtide: NTCP entry-inhibitor mechanism and approved status.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with chronic HBV deteriorates rapidly with rising ALT; test for HDV superinfection and discuss implications.
- An exam question compares coinfection and superinfection outcomes; justify the prognosis difference.

**Reviewer Gap**: EASL HDV guideline treatment algorithms, HDV RNA monitoring endpoints, and liver-transplantation indications are not in the retained abstracts; verify against current EASL HDV guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31266627**: *HBV/HDV Coinfection: A Challenge for Therapeutics.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 40668572**: *Comparative Efficacy of Treatment Regimens for Chronic Hepatitis D Virus Infection: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 41134660**: *Current evidence of bulevirtide as monotherapy compared to combination treatment with pegylated interferon.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 35028672**: *[Bulevirtide as the first specific agent against hepatitis D virus infections-mechanism and clinical].* (2022) [review] - None

## [0800] hepatitis management

**Cluster**: viral hepatitis management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Viral hepatitis spans five viruses with five different stories: A and E, transmitted faeco-orally and usually self-limiting; B and C, blood-borne chronic infections that drive cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma; and D, the HBV-dependent satellite with the worst prognosis. Modern management is a cascade-of-care problem — prevent (vaccination), test, link to treatment, suppress or cure, and monitor for reactivation and cancer — and the metrics that matter are how well each health system moves patients through that cascade.

### Core Teaching Points
- Five hepatitis virus types (A, B, C, D, E) have distinct transmission, prognosis and management: HAV, spread faeco-orally, is typically self-limiting with supportive therapy, while HBV, HCV and HDV drive chronic liver disease. (PMID 40247447)
- The updated EASL guidelines provide comprehensive evidence-based recommendations for HBV management across ten thematic sections, addressing diagnostics, treatment goals and more. (PMID 40348683)
- Modelling of global HCV prevalence and cascade of care between 2015 and 2020 shows many countries were progressing toward elimination targets before the COVID-19 pandemic — measuring the cascade, not just prevalence, is how management is judged. (PMID 35180382)
- HBV reactivation can follow diverse immune-modulating exposures; antiviral prophylaxis is effective, and select patients can be monitored without prophylaxis, per the AGA guideline. (PMID 39863345)
- HBV and HCV are the leading causes of end-stage liver disease worldwide; despite a potent HBV vaccine, many new infections occur annually where vaccination coverage is weak. (PMID 38793606)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Elimination targets (WHO 2030) remain ambitious; modelling shows progress is fragile and system-dependent.
- Integration of hepatitis services with primary care and harm-reduction programmes is uneven and not standardised in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Transmission routes and typical courses of HAV, HBV, HCV, HDV, HEV.
- The cascade of care: prevention, testing, linkage, treatment, surveillance.
- HBV reactivation prevention before immunosuppression.
- Global elimination targets and what 'elimination' actually means epidemiologically.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A traveller returns with acute hepatitis A; outline management and distinguish it from chronic viral hepatitis.
- A health system reviews its hepatitis cascade of care; identify where patients are lost and what to measure.

**Reviewer Gap**: HEV management in pregnancy/immunosuppression, hepatitis A vaccination schedules, and antiviral regimen tables are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against EASL/WHO guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40247447**: *Viral Hepatitis.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 40348683**: *EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines on the management of hepatitis B virus infection.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 35180382**: *Global change in hepatitis C virus prevalence and cascade of care between 2015 and 2020: a modelling study.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 39863345**: *AGA Clinical Practice Guideline on the Prevention and Treatment of Hepatitis B Virus Reactivation in At-Risk Patients.* (2025) [guideline] - None

## [0801] Hepatocellular Carcinoma

**Cluster**: hepatocellular carcinoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hepatocellular carcinoma is the dominant primary liver cancer (about 90% of cases) and the third leading cause of cancer death worldwide, rising on the back of cirrhosis, HBV/HCV and metabolic liver disease. Management is stage-dependent — resection, ablation, transplant for early disease; locoregional therapy for intermediate; systemic immunotherapy-based regimens for advanced — so the first clinical skill is correct staging, and the second is surveillance discipline in cirrhotic patients, because early HCC is the HCC that gets cured.

### Core Teaching Points
- Liver cancer is the third leading cause of cancer-related deaths worldwide, with HCC accounting for approximately 90% of primary liver cancers; the EASL 2025 guideline covers current management. (PMID 39690085)
- HCC is the most common form of liver cancer (~90% of cases), with incidence projected to exceed 1 million cases by 2025, and infection with HBV and HCV is a major driver. (PMID 33479224)
- HCC is highly heterogeneous in molecular and cellular signatures, and biomarker development for diagnosis and therapeutic selection is an active field. (PMID 39242496)
- Immune checkpoint inhibitors have emerged as the primary treatment for advanced HCC and shown promise in the neoadjuvant setting, while liver transplantation remains the preferred treatment for patients within criteria with unresectable early disease. (PMID 38253289)
- Focal liver lesions are an increasingly common incidental finding on abdominal imaging, and structured evaluation distinguishes HCC from benign lesions without biopsy in many cases. (PMID 38958301)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Surveillance programme effectiveness and optimal interval in non-cirrhotic HBV carriers remain debated.
- Biopsy-avoidance criteria and the expanding role of molecular biomarkers are evolving and not settled in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Stage-dependent treatment: resection/ablation/transplant vs TACE vs systemic therapy.
- Surveillance: ultrasound +/- AFP every 6 months in cirrhosis.
- Non-invasive HCC diagnosis in cirrhosis (LI-RADS imaging criteria).
- Systemic therapy evolution from sorafenib to immune-checkpoint combinations.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A cirrhotic patient has a 2-cm arterial-enhancing lesion; walk through diagnosis and curative options.
- A patient with advanced HCC and preserved liver function is assessed for first-line systemic therapy; outline the modern options.

**Reviewer Gap**: BCLC staging detail, transplant criteria (Milan), and specific systemic regimens are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the EASL 2025 full guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources

## [0802] Hepatocellular carcinoma systemic therapy

**Cluster**: HCC systemic therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Systemic therapy for advanced HCC has been transformed twice in a decade: from a single multikinase inhibitor (sorafenib) to a menu of immune-checkpoint-based combinations. Around half of HCC patients receive systemic therapy at some point, and the craft now lies in selecting regimens by liver function, tolerability and molecular context — recognising that response is real but rarely universal, and that the immunosuppressive tumour microenvironment explains much of the residual failure.

### Core Teaching Points
- The EASL 2025 guideline incorporates advances in diagnostic and therapeutic tools for HCC, including systemic options. (PMID 39690085)
- The past decade has seen significant advances in systemic treatment of advanced HCC, but new strategies have not achieved universal success and patients frequently exhibit heterogeneous responses. (PMID 38350421)
- Around 50% of patients with HCC receive systemic therapies; historically sorafenib or lenvatinib, now increasingly immune-based regimens. (PMID 34764464)
- Until 2017 advanced-HCC systemic therapy consisted only of antiangiogenic tyrosine kinase inhibitors; immunotherapy then reshaped the landscape, with the immune system modulating HCC progression. (PMID 33850328)
- Understanding of HCC molecular pathogenesis is changing the poor outcome of this deadly cancer through effective systemic therapies, with molecular characterisation increasingly guiding treatment. (PMID 35484418)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing after first-line immune-based combination failure is not established.
- Predictive biomarkers to select patients for specific systemic regimens remain investigational.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The sorafenib-to-immunotherapy evolution and landmark regimens (atezolizumab-bevacizumab class).
- Why liver function (Child-Pugh) gates systemic therapy decisions.
- Tumour microenvironment and mechanisms of immunotherapy resistance.
- Molecular-targeted agents and where they fit now.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with advanced HCC on atezolizumab-bevacizumab progresses; outline second-line reasoning.
- An exam vignette juxtaposes TKI-era and immune-era trials; explain the survival gains and their limits.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific regimen names beyond the abstract level, response criteria, and safety management are not in the retained abstracts; verify against the EASL 2025 full guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39690085**: *EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines on the management of hepatocellular carcinoma.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38350421**: *Precision treatment in advanced hepatocellular carcinoma.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 34764464**: *Immunotherapies for hepatocellular carcinoma.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0803] Hepatopulmonary Syndrome

**Cluster**: hepatopulmonary syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hepatopulmonary syndrome is the dyspnoea of cirrhosis that gets missed: intrapulmonary vascular dilatation and shunting produce impaired gas exchange and arterial hypoxaemia in a patient with liver disease or portal hypertension. The diagnostic triad is liver disease, intrapulmonary vasodilatation and hypoxaemia; contrast echocardiography demonstrates the shunt. It worsens prognosis, it matters enormously at transplant assessment, and apart from liver transplantation there is no definitive therapy — though oxygen and careful candidate selection are the practical tools.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hepatopulmonary syndrome is a pulmonary vascular complication of liver disease, characterised by intrapulmonary vascular dilatations and shunts resulting in impaired gas exchange, and it adversely affects prognosis. (PMID 36035361)
- HPS occurs in patients with chronic liver disease and/or portal hypertension, and intrapulmonary vascular dilatation may induce severe hypoxaemia through microvascular dilation and shunting. (PMID 25732713)
- HPS should be considered for any dyspnoea in cirrhotic patients; it is a pulmonary vascular disease associated with cirrhosis and portal hypertension. (PMID 37005097)
- In liver transplant candidates, HPS must be distinguished from portopulmonary hypertension (elevated pulmonary pressures and vascular remodelling), because the two have opposite implications for transplant eligibility. (PMID 40670035)
- The diagnostic triad of hepatopulmonary syndrome is liver disease, pulmonary vascular dilatations and arterial hypoxaemia, and it is usually associated with cirrhosis of any origin. (PMID 25840463)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- No effective medical therapy exists; the role of experimental agents and the thresholds for transplant priority (MELD exception) are not detailed in the retained abstracts.
- Optimal contrast-echo protocol and shunt quantification for diagnosis are not standardised in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic triad: liver disease + intrapulmonary vasodilatation + hypoxaemia.
- Contrast echocardiography (agitated saline) demonstrating late left-heart bubbles.
- HPS versus portopulmonary hypertension: shunt versus pressure, transplant implications.
- Why dyspnoea in cirrhosis should always prompt consideration of HPS.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A cirrhotic patient has progressive dyspnoea and orthodeoxia; work through the HPS diagnostic pathway.
- A transplant candidate's echo shows a positive contrast study; distinguish HPS from portopulmonary hypertension before listing.

**Reviewer Gap**: MELD-exception criteria, severity thresholds (PaO2 cut-offs), and experimental therapy evidence are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against transplant-society guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36035361**: *Hepatopulmonary syndrome.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 25732713**: *Hepatopulmonary Syndrome.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 37005097**: *[Hepatopulmonary syndrome].* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40670035**: *Hepatopulmonary Syndrome and Portopulmonary Hypertension.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 25840463**: *[Heptopulmonary syndrome].* (2015) [review] - None

## [0804] hepatorenal

**Cluster**: hepatorenal syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The hepatorenal syndrome is the kidney failure of advanced cirrhosis that is functional, not structural: splanchnic vasodilatation and a hyperdynamic circulation leave the kidneys maximally vasoconstricted, and creatinine rises without primary renal disease. It sits at the extreme end of AKI in cirrhosis, is diagnosed after excluding alternatives (volume depletion, nephrotoxins, sepsis, structural disease), and its treatment pairs vasoconstrictors with albumin — with transplantation the only definitive cure.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hepatorenal syndrome, the extreme manifestation of renal impairment in cirrhosis, is characterised by reduced renal blood flow and glomerular filtration rate, and is diagnosed when kidney function is reduced in the absence of an alternative explanation. (PMID 32928750)
- HRS is a form of kidney dysfunction that occurs characteristically in cirrhosis in response to circulatory and haemodynamic alterations of advanced liver disease. (PMID 38246506)
- The AASLD guidance covers the diagnosis, evaluation and management of ascites, spontaneous bacterial peritonitis and hepatorenal syndrome as an integrated complication set. (PMID 33942342)
- Cirrhosis affects approximately 2.2 million US adults, with rising age-adjusted mortality, and HRS is among its most serious complications within a staged natural history. (PMID 37159031)
- Albumin has accepted evidence-based indications beyond simple hypoalbuminaemia — resuscitation in shock and, importantly for HRS, use alongside vasoconstrictor therapy. (PMID 38607390)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic criteria for HRS-AKI continue to evolve (ICA revisions), and serum creatinine underestimates renal dysfunction in cirrhosis.
- The optimal vasoconstrictor agent and dosing regimen vary by region and resource setting.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Functional versus structural AKI: why HRS has no primary renal pathology.
- Diagnostic exclusions: hypovolaemia, sepsis, nephrotoxins, obstruction, parenchymal disease.
- The splanchnic vasodilatation -> renal vasoconstriction cascade.
- Albumin plus vasoconstrictor as the treatment backbone.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A decompensated cirrhotic's creatinine rises after diuretic escalation; separate prerenal azotaemia from HRS.
- A patient with refractory ascites develops oliguria; outline the HRS diagnostic work-up and initial therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: HRS-AKI versus HRS-CKD (formerly type 1 vs type 2) definitions, specific diagnostic thresholds, and terlipressin dosing are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against AASLD/ICA guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32928750**: *Hepatorenal syndrome: pathophysiology, diagnosis, and management.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 38246506**: *Hepatorenal Syndrome in Cirrhosis.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 33942342**: *Diagnosis, Evaluation, and Management of Ascites, Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis and Hepatorenal Syndrome.* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37159031**: *Diagnosis and Management of Cirrhosis and Its Complications: A Review.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 38607390**: *Albumin: a comprehensive review and practical guideline for clinical use.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0805] Hepatorenal Syndrome

**Cluster**: hepatorenal syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
HRS is the kidney failure that cirrhosis causes by haemodynamics alone: splanchnic arterial vasodilatation progresses until effective arterial volume and central blood volume fall, vasoconstrictor systems activate to their limit, and extreme renal vasoconstriction drops the GFR. Modern terminology splits it into HRS-AKI and HRS-CKD; HRS-AKI accounts for roughly one in five AKI presentations in cirrhosis; and because there is no gold standard test, diagnosis remains one of exclusion — which is where most management errors begin.

### Core Teaching Points
- HRS is a form of kidney dysfunction characteristically occurring in cirrhosis, with marked impairment of kidney function in response to circulatory and haemodynamic alterations of advanced liver disease. (PMID 38246506)
- Patients with cirrhosis are prone to AKI, which carries markedly increased in-hospital morbidity and mortality and risk of progression to CKD; the ADQI/ICA consensus addresses AKI management in this population. (PMID 38527522)
- Terminology has changed: acute HRS is now referred to as AKI-HRS and chronic HRS as chronic kidney disease-CKD-HRS, aligning HRS with the universal AKI framework. (PMID 30213943)
- HRS is a primarily functional form of AKI that develops in decompensated cirrhosis; the pathophysiologic cascade begins with pooling of blood in the splanchnic system. (PMID 37258014)
- HRS-AKI occurs in the setting of advanced chronic liver disease, portal hypertension and ascites, and is found in about 20% of patients presenting to hospital with AKI — though it may coexist with other kidney injuries. (PMID 39869432)
- HRS-1 is a severe form of AKI in advanced cirrhosis and ascites whose pathogenesis is rooted in marked maladaptive renal vasoconstriction, and the lack of a gold-standard test makes diagnosis and treatment selection challenging. (PMID 41123991)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- HRS-AKI can coexist with other kidney injuries (sepsis-associated, ischemic, drug-induced), complicating both diagnosis and trial interpretation.
- Whether novel biomarkers will supplant creatinine-based criteria in cirrhosis is unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HRS-AKI versus HRS-CKD terminology and the old type 1/type 2 mapping.
- The splanchnic pooling -> effective hypovolaemia -> renal vasoconstriction cascade.
- Diagnosis by exclusion and the unreliability of creatinine in cirrhosis.
- Treatment pillars: albumin, vasoconstrictors, transplant evaluation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A decompensated cirrhotic presents with AKI after SBP; distinguish HRS-AKI from sepsis-associated AKI and start therapy.
- An exam question contrasts HRS-AKI and HRS-CKD; explain the trajectory and management difference.

**Reviewer Gap**: Diagnostic thresholds (creatinine cut-offs, urine output criteria), terlipressin versus noradrenaline selection, and renal-replacement indications are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against ICA/AASLD guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38246506**: *Hepatorenal Syndrome in Cirrhosis.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 38527522**: *Acute kidney injury in patients with cirrhosis: Acute Disease Quality Initiative (ADQI) and International Club of Ascites consensus.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 30213943**: *Hepatorenal syndrome.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 37258014**: *Hepatorenal Syndrome: Pathophysiology, Diagnosis, and Treatment.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 39869432**: *Update on Hepatorenal Syndrome: From Pathophysiology to Treatment.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 41123991**: *Treating Hepatorenal Syndrome in the Current Era.* (2026) [review] - None

## [0806] Hepatorenal syndrome diagnosis and management

**Cluster**: HRS diagnosis and management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diagnosing HRS is an exercise in disciplined exclusion: cirrhotic AKI has many mimics — volume depletion, sepsis, nephrotoxins, obstruction, parenchymal disease — and HRS is what remains when they have been ruled out in a patient with advanced liver disease, portal hypertension and ascites. Management then moves quickly: albumin volume expansion to test responsiveness, vasoconstrictor therapy to reverse the renal vasoconstriction, evaluation for liver transplantation, and renal replacement therapy when vasoconstrictors fail or are contraindicated.

### Core Teaching Points
- HRS is a primarily functional form of AKI developing in decompensated cirrhosis, beginning with splanchnic blood pooling; pathophysiology, diagnosis and treatment are reviewed in an integrated framework. (PMID 37258014)
- The ADQI/ICA consensus provides recommendations for managing AKI in cirrhosis, within which HRS-AKI is the most morbid and complex entity intensivists face. (PMID 38527522, PMID 41123991)
- The AASLD guidance integrates diagnosis, evaluation and management of ascites, SBP and HRS as a single complication pathway. (PMID 33942342)
- Diagnosis of AKI in chronic liver disease remains challenging because traditional markers such as serum creatinine are unreliable, and novel biomarkers are under development. (PMID 33752858)
- Because HRS-1 lacks a gold-standard diagnostic test and is driven by maladaptive renal vasoconstriction, treatment selection in the current era rests on careful diagnosis plus vasoconstrictor-based therapy. (PMID 41123991)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The precise creatinine and urine-output thresholds defining HRS-AKI continue to be revised as diagnostic criteria evolve.
- Which patients benefit from early renal replacement therapy versus continued vasoconstrictor trials is not resolved in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Stepwise diagnostic exclusions before labelling HRS.
- Albumin challenge: responsiveness argues against HRS.
- Vasoconstrictor classes (terlipressin, noradrenaline, midodrine-octreotide) and their settings.
- Transplant evaluation as the definitive management for eligible patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A cirrhotic with AKI fails to respond to albumin and diuretic withdrawal; confirm HRS and start vasoconstrictor therapy.
- A patient with HRS-AKI has a contraindication to terlipressin; select an alternative regimen.

**Reviewer Gap**: Precise diagnostic criteria tables, vasoconstrictor dosing, and RRT thresholds are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against AASLD/ICA full guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37258014**: *Hepatorenal Syndrome: Pathophysiology, Diagnosis, and Treatment.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 38527522**: *Acute kidney injury in patients with cirrhosis: Acute Disease Quality Initiative (ADQI) and International Club of Ascites consensus.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 33942342**: *Diagnosis, Evaluation, and Management of Ascites, Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis and Hepatorenal Syndrome.* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 33752858**: *Hepatorenal Syndrome.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 41123991**: *Treating Hepatorenal Syndrome in the Current Era.* (2026) [review] - None

## [0807] hepatorenal syndrome management guidelines

**Cluster**: HRS management guidelines | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Guideline-based HRS management has three pillars — vasoconstrictors, albumin, and expedited transplant pathways — but the consensus itself is being stress-tested: recent evidence on how much albumin helps in HRS-AKI has challenged the assumptions behind the ADQI/ICA recommendations. The trainee should know both the guideline defaults (AASLD; AGA Clinical Practice Update on vasoactive drugs and albumin) and the live controversy, because exam questions increasingly probe exactly that tension.

### Core Teaching Points
- The AASLD guidance integrates the diagnosis, evaluation and management of ascites, SBP and HRS as a single pathway, anchoring guideline-based HRS care. (PMID 33942342)
- The AGA Clinical Practice Update summarises evidence and expert best-practice advice on vasoactive drugs and intravenous albumin in cirrhosis — the two pharmacological pillars of HRS management. (PMID 37978969)
- New evidence on albumin infusion in HRS-AKI challenges the recent ADQI/ICA consensus recommendations, meaning the albumin pillar of guideline care is actively contested. (PMID 40250764)
- The AASLD 2021 review of ascites, SBP and HRS management reflects antimicrobial-resistance developments and comparative therapy studies, framing current practice. (PMID 37011958)
- Albumin's accepted indications in cirrhosis are specific (shock resuscitation, HRS with vasoconstrictors, selected paracentesis/SBP settings) rather than simple hypoalbuminaemia, per a practical clinical guideline. (PMID 38607390)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The optimal albumin dose, duration and responder profile in HRS-AKI are under active re-evaluation.
- Guideline differences between AASLD, AGA and ICA on vasoconstrictor choice and monitoring are not fully reconciled in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Guideline pillars: vasoconstrictor + albumin + transplant referral.
- What the albumin controversy in HRS-AKI is about and why consensus is shifting.
- When albumin is (and is not) indicated in cirrhosis.
- Regional differences in agent availability (terlipressin approval status).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with HRS-AKI is started on terlipressin and albumin per guideline; discuss what 'response' means and when to call it failure.
- An exam question cites the albumin-challenge evidence; explain how it modifies the consensus algorithm.

**Reviewer Gap**: Exact guideline recommendation wording, dosing schedules, and transplant-referral triggers are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the AASLD full guidance and AGA CPU before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33942342**: *Diagnosis, Evaluation, and Management of Ascites, Spontaneous Bacterial Peritonitis and Hepatorenal Syndrome.* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37978969**: *AGA Clinical Practice Update on the Use of Vasoactive Drugs and Intravenous Albumin in Cirrhosis.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40250764**: *Albumin infusion in hepatorenal syndrome-acute kidney injury: New evidence challenges recent consensus.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 37011958**: *Diagnosis and management of ascites, spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, and hepatorenal syndrome.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 38607390**: *Albumin: a comprehensive review and practical guideline for clinical use.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0808] hepatorenal syndrome terlipressin

**Cluster**: HRS terlipressin | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Terlipressin is the vasoconstrictor that made HRS treatable: a V1-receptor agonist that constricts the splanchnic bed, restoring effective arterial volume and relieving renal vasoconstriction when combined with albumin. Long the international standard of care, it was only recently approved for HRS-AKI in the United States — and its evidence base is now mature enough that meta-analyses of randomised trials define both its efficacy and its safety boundaries, including ischaemic adverse events that demand careful patient selection.

### Core Teaching Points
- The definition and diagnostic criteria of HRS-AKI have undergone recent changes, and terlipressin has recently been approved as pharmacotherapy for HRS-AKI in the United States. (PMID 38353275)
- Meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials confirms terlipressin (with albumin) as an effective treatment for HRS, consolidating the evidence base for its use. (PMID 40207491)
- A 2024 systematic review and meta-analysis re-assessed terlipressin versus placebo or noradrenaline for HRS, examining both efficacy and safety in light of HRS's poor prognosis and unmet therapeutic need. (PMID 39295934)
- Terlipressin is the most widely used treatment for HRS, though therapy effects were historically inconsistent, prompting repeated meta-analytic assessment of its safety and efficacy. (PMID 29668606)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Ischaemic adverse events (cardiac, intestinal, digital) temper terlipressin use in patients with vascular disease or shock; the risk-benefit threshold is not crisply defined in the retained abstracts.
- Whether noradrenaline is truly non-inferior to terlipressin — particularly in ICU settings — remains an active comparison.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Terlipressin mechanism: V1 agonism, splanchnic vasoconstriction, albumin co-therapy.
- Approval status and evidence hierarchy for HRS-AKI.
- Terlipressin versus noradrenaline: where each fits.
- Safety monitoring: ischaemia, arrhythmia, fluid overload.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with HRS-AKI and coronary disease needs vasoconstrictor therapy; weigh terlipressin against alternatives.
- An exam question asks why terlipressin must be given with albumin; explain the haemodynamic rationale.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dosing algorithms (bolus vs continuous), response definitions, and retreatment rules are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full terlipressin reviews and regulatory label before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38353275**: *Terlipressin for hepatorenal syndrome.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40207491**: *Terlipressin for the treatment of hepatorenal syndrome: a meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39295934**: *Terlipressin versus placebo or noradrenalin in the treatment of hepatorenal syndrome: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2024) [systematic review] - None
- **PMID 29668606**: *Terlipressin in the treatment of hepatorenal syndrome: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2018) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0809] Hepatorenal syndrome vasoconstrictor therapy

**Cluster**: HRS vasoconstrictor therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Vasoconstrictor therapy is the pharmacological reversal of HRS pathophysiology: constrict the over-dilated splanchnic circulation, restore effective arterial volume, and let the kidneys reopen. The available classes — terlipressin, noradrenaline, and the oral midodrine-octreotide combination — differ in potency, evidence depth, setting (ward vs ICU) and availability. Meta-analyses support vasoactive drugs as a class for HRS-1, with terlipressin the most studied, and choice driven by haemodynamic monitoring capacity and contraindications.

### Core Teaching Points
- HRS, the extreme manifestation of renal impairment in cirrhosis, features reduced renal blood flow and GFR, and its management centres on vasoconstrictor therapy combined with albumin. (PMID 32928750)
- HRS is characterised by increased splanchnic blood flow, a hyperdynamic state, decreased central volume, activation of vasoconstrictor systems and extreme kidney vasoconstriction — the rationale for pharmacological splanchnic vasoconstriction. (PMID 30996046)
- Systematic review with meta-analysis supports vasoactive drugs for HRS type 1 — a functional, rapidly progressive but potentially reversible AKI characterised by intense renal arterial vasoconstriction and very poor prognosis. (PMID 28052382)
- Different pharmacological therapies for HRS have variable efficacy, and meta-analytic comparison of drug classes informs the choice among available vasoconstrictor regimens. (PMID 28991106)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether oral combinations (midodrine-octreotide) match parenteral agents in efficacy remains uncertain.
- Response predictors (baseline creatinine, bilirubin, arterial pressure) that should guide agent selection are not quantified in the retained abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Vasoconstrictor classes and their receptors (terlipressin V1, noradrenaline alpha-1, midodrine-octreotide oral).
- Why all vasoconstrictors are given with albumin.
- Ward-based versus ICU-based agent selection.
- Response assessment and when to switch or escalate to RRT/transplant.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with HRS-1 in a district hospital without terlipressin access; construct a noradrenaline or oral-combination plan.
- An exam question compares the mechanisms of terlipressin and midodrine-octreotide; justify agent choice by setting.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dosing, titration targets (mean arterial pressure), and comparative head-to-head trial detail are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against ICA/AASLD guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32928750**: *Hepatorenal syndrome: pathophysiology, diagnosis, and management.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 30996046**: *Hepatorenal Syndrome.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 28052382**: *Systematic review with meta-analysis: vasoactive drugs for the treatment of hepatorenal syndrome type 1.* (2017) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 28991106**: *Pharmacological Therapies for Hepatorenal Syndrome: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2018) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0810] HER2 positive breast cancer adjuvant trastuzumab

**Cluster**: HER2-positive breast cancer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
HER2-positive breast cancer used to be the aggressive subtype; adjuvant trastuzumab turned it into one of the more curable ones. About 15-20% of breast cancers overexpress HER2, and adding trastuzumab to chemotherapy produced one of the largest survival benefits ever recorded in adjuvant oncology. The modern craft is duration and escalation: one year of trastuzumab as the default, neoadjuvant therapy when downsizing matters, and escalation to dual HER2 blockade or T-DM1 for residual disease after neoadjuvant treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- About 15% to 20% of breast cancers are HER2-positive — a more aggressive subtype with shortened survival; meta-analysis evidence defines the optimal adjuvant trastuzumab regimen. (PMID 31287333)
- The availability of HER2-targeted therapy has dramatically improved outcomes in HER2-positive early breast cancer, as demonstrated by the EBCTCG meta-analysis on trastuzumab. (PMID 35148934)
- Anti-HER2 treatment has changed the natural biology of HER2-positive breast cancer, with major achievements in both metastatic and early disease and particular success of neoadjuvant approaches. (PMID 27939064)
- Breast cancer with HER2 amplification accounts for 20% of breast cancers, and management has dramatically changed since 2000 with monoclonal antibodies in metastatic and (neo)adjuvant settings. (PMID 34969516)
- Adjuvant and neoadjuvant breast cancer treatments reduce breast cancer mortality, though effects on other-cause mortality vary — a systematic review quantified these trade-offs. (PMID 35367784)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- De-escalation questions — shorter trastuzumab duration for selected low-risk patients, and chemotherapy-free regimens for small node-negative tumours — remain partially unresolved.
- Optimal escalation for residual disease after neoadjuvant therapy continues to evolve with newer agents.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The magnitude of adjuvant trastuzumab benefit and its evidence base (EBCTCG meta-analysis).
- One-year versus shorter durations: what trials showed.
- Neoadjuvant therapy and pathologic complete response as a prognostic and escalation trigger.
- Cardiac monitoring during HER2 therapy (LVEF surveillance).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 45-year-old with a 2-cm node-positive HER2-positive tumour; construct the adjuvant plan and monitoring schedule.
- A patient has residual disease after neoadjuvant HER2 therapy; explain escalation options.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dual-blockade regimens, T-DM1/tucatinib positioning, and cardiotoxicity management algorithms are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current ASCO/ESMO guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31287333**: *Adjuvant trastuzumab regimen for HER2-positive early-stage breast cancer: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 35148934**: *Neoadjuvant and adjuvant treatment of patients with HER2-positive early breast cancer.* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 27939064**: *HER2-positive breast cancer.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 34969516**: *[Not Available].* (2021) [review] - None

## [0811] HER2-Positive Breast Cancer and ADCs

**Cluster**: HER2-positive breast cancer and ADCs | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antibody-drug conjugates have reset the ceiling for HER2-positive breast cancer. Trastuzumab deruxtecan — a HER2 antibody carrying a topoisomerase-I payload with a high drug-to-antibody ratio — succeeded where earlier ADCs stalled and now defines later-line, and increasingly earlier, treatment of advanced disease. Managing ADCs well means mastering their distinctive toxicities (notably interstitial lung disease) and understanding how they fit into the wider HER2 landscape of monoclonal antibodies, tyrosine-kinase inhibitors and dual blockade.

### Core Teaching Points
- Trastuzumab deruxtecan is an antibody-drug conjugate consisting of a humanised anti-HER2 monoclonal antibody linked to a topoisomerase-I inhibitor payload (DXd), with a high drug-to-antibody ratio. (PMID 38621469)
- T-DXd was evaluated in patients with HER2-positive unresectable/metastatic breast cancer in the phase II DESTINY-Breast01 trial, and practical guidance addresses optimising its management in clinical practice. (PMID 35964548)
- Single-arm meta-analysis of T-DXd in HER2-expressing solid tumours confirms its efficacy and safety profile across tumour types. (PMID 37114934)
- HER2-positive breast cancer carries five-year survival around 30% at the advanced stage, and anti-HER2 treatment development — now including ADCs — has produced a paradigm shift in management and outcomes. (PMID 36224475)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing of ADCs versus TKIs and dual blockade, and re-challenge strategies after progression, are not settled.
- Interstitial lung disease monitoring and management protocols vary, and long-term safety data continue to mature.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ADC structure and mechanism (antibody, linker, payload, drug-to-antibody ratio).
- DESTINY-trial lineage and where T-DXd sits in the treatment sequence.
- T-DXd adverse effects: interstitial lung disease, nausea, myelosuppression.
- ADC versus TKI versus dual blockade selection in advanced HER2-positive disease.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient progresses on trastuzumab-pertuzumab and T-DM1; plan the ADC next step and its monitoring.
- A patient on T-DXd develops a dry cough and ground-glass change on CT; recognise and manage ILD.

**Reviewer Gap**: Regimen sequencing tables, ILD grading-management algorithms, and biosimilar/earlier-line trial results (DESTINY-Breast03/04 details) are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources

## [0812] Hereditary GI Polyposis Syndromes

**Cluster**: hereditary GI polyposis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
A handful of polyposis syndromes explain a small but preventable fraction of colorectal cancer, and they reward precise recognition: FAP and its attenuated form, MUTYH-associated polyposis, the hamartomatous syndromes (juvenile polyposis, Peutz-Jeghers), and the Lynch syndromes nearby. Genetic testing costs have collapsed but referral of high-risk patients lags — so the clinical skill is suspecting the syndrome from polyp burden, age and family history, then channelling the patient into surveillance or risk-reducing surgery that genuinely prevents cancer.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hereditary adenomatous polyposis syndromes, including FAP and rarer variants, increase lifetime risk of colorectal and other cancers; updated European guidelines (38 experts) standardise their clinical management. (PMID 38722804)
- Inherited cancer syndromes are responsible for approximately 3-5% of all colorectal cancers, and although genetic-testing costs have plummeted, awareness and referral of high-risk patients remain the rate-limiting step. (PMID 31705372)
- Around 10% to 16% of colorectal cancer patients carry a pathogenic variant in a cancer-susceptibility gene, spanning a broader hereditary-colorectal-cancer syndrome landscape than polyposis alone. (PMID 35577708)
- Juvenile polyposis syndrome is a rare autosomal-dominant precancerous condition conferring increased gastrointestinal cancer risk, and should be suspected when hamartomatous polyposis features cluster in a family. (PMID 35988962)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal surveillance intervals and the threshold for prophylactic colectomy vary by syndrome, age and genotype, and are individualised in guideline practice.
- Chemoprevention (NSAIDs in FAP) modifies polyp burden but its effect on cancer endpoints is not definitive.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- FAP: hundreds of adenomas, APC gene, prophylactic colectomy, upper-GI surveillance.
- MUTYH-associated polyposis: autosomal recessive, attenuated phenotype.
- Hamartomatous syndromes: juvenile polyposis, Peutz-Jeghers (STK11) and their extracolonic cancers.
- When to refer for genetic testing: Amsterdam/Bethesda-style criteria and polyp-count thresholds.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old has hundreds of colonic adenomas and a family history of early colon cancer; outline FAP management.
- A teenager with mucocutaneous pigmentation and small-bowel hamartomas; identify the syndrome and its surveillance needs.

**Reviewer Gap**: Peutz-Jeghers and serrated-polyposis specifics, desmoid-disease management in FAP, and surveillance interval tables are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full European polyposis guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38722804**: *Updated European guidelines for clinical management of familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP), MUTYH-associated polyposis and other adenomatous polyposis syndromes.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 31705372**: *Hereditary Polyposis Syndromes.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 35577708**: *Hereditary Colorectal Cancer.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 35988962**: *Juvenile polyposis syndrome: An overview.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0813] Hereditary hemochromatosis diagnosis and phlebotomy

**Cluster**: hereditary hemochromatosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hereditary haemochromatosis is the commonest inherited disorder in northern European populations and one of the most satisfying to treat: the biochemical signature is elevated transferrin saturation, the gene is usually HFE C282Y, and venesection removes the excess iron before it destroys organs. The tragedy is underdiagnosis — arthropathy, diabetes, cirrhosis and cardiomyopathy are preventable when phlebotomy starts early, which is why unexplained fatigue with abnormal iron studies should always prompt the ferritin-and-transferrin-saturation pair.

### Core Teaching Points
- Haemochromatosis is characterised by elevated transferrin saturation and progressive iron loading that mainly affects the liver; early diagnosis and treatment by phlebotomy can prevent cirrhosis, hepatocellular carcinoma, diabetes, arthropathy and other complications. (PMID 35662478)
- Hereditary haemochromatosis is one of the most common genetic disorders among persons of northern European descent, with recent advances in diagnosis, management and molecular diagnostic testing reflected in the ACG guideline. (PMID 31335359)
- Haemochromatosis is overdiagnosed in patients without true iron overload and underdiagnosed in many patients; early diagnosis by genetic testing and therapy are emphasised. (PMID 37121243)
- Most patients remain asymptomatic, but others develop advanced organ damage — arthropathy, diabetes — that reduces quality of life and survival, defining the treatment urgency. (PMID 39928951)
- Hereditary haemochromatosis is an autosomal-recessive disorder that disrupts iron homeostasis, causing systemic iron overload, and is the most common inherited disorder among people of northern European ancestry. (PMID 34523883)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Penetrance of HFE C282Y homozygosity is incomplete — many carriers never develop iron overload, complicating screening policy.
- The role of hepcidin-pathway agents for patients unable to tolerate phlebotomy is investigational.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic sequence: transferrin saturation and ferritin first, HFE genotyping to confirm.
- Phlebotomy targets and maintenance regimens.
- Organ complications to screen for at diagnosis (liver, glucose, joints, heart, skin).
- Incomplete penetrance and who actually needs treatment.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old man with fatigue, arthralgia and transferrin saturation 78% is found to be C282Y homozygous; plan management.
- A patient's ferritin is very high but transferrin saturation is normal; decide whether this is haemochromatosis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Transferrin-saturation/ferritin thresholds, phlebotomy dosing schedules, and non-HFE genotype management are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the EASL 2022/ACG 2019 full guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35662478**: *EASL Clinical Practice Guidelines on haemochromatosis.* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 31335359**: *ACG Clinical Guideline: Hereditary Hemochromatosis.* (2019) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37121243**: *Haemochromatosis.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 39928951**: *Hemochromatosis.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 34523883**: *Hereditary Hemochromatosis: Rapid Evidence Review.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0814] Hereditary phaeochromocytoma syndromes: VHL, NF1, SDH mutations, genetic surveillance

**Cluster**: hereditary pheochromocytoma syndromes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Roughly a third of phaeochromocytomas and paragangliomas carry an identifiable germline mutation, and the genotype now organises everything: VHL, NF1, RET, and the SDHx genes each carry distinct tumour spectra, malignancy risk and surveillance rhythms. A young patient, a family history, or a paraganglioma rather than adrenal tumour should all trigger genetic testing — not as an academic exercise but because it changes who gets screened, for what, and how often.

### Core Teaching Points
- Pheochromocytomas/paragangliomas have a distinctive molecular landscape allowing assignment to clusters by genetic alteration; around 30-35% of Caucasian patients harbour germline mutations, underpinning personalised management. (PMID 34147030)
- Phaeochromocytomas are rare adrenal-medulla tumours that may be sporadic or part of a hereditary syndrome, and a considerable number carry germline or somatic mutations inherited in an autosomal-dominant pattern. (PMID 31517632)
- Neurofibromatosis type 1 is a complex autosomal-dominant disorder caused by germline NF1 tumour-suppressor mutations, with characteristic pigmentary features and a well-recognised phaeochromocytoma association. (PMID 28230061)
- Plasma or fractionated urine metanephrines are the biochemical test of choice for diagnosis, and the preoperative approach to phaeochromocytoma centres on alpha-blockade before any surgical manipulation. (PMID 38849272)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Surveillance intervals and imaging modalities differ by genotype and are individualised rather than uniform.
- SDH-mutation risk stratification, including for phenocopies such as SDHA and the paediatric surveillance question, is evolving.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Genotype-phenotype map: VHL (renal, pancreatic, CNS haemangioblastoma), NF1 (cafe-au-lait, neurofibromas), SDHx (paraganglioma-dominant, GIST, pituitary).
- When to suspect and test for germline disease: young age, family history, multifocal or extra-adrenal disease.
- Biochemical diagnosis: plasma-free or urinary fractionated metanephrines.
- Preoperative management: alpha-blockade before beta-blockade, volume repletion.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 29-year-old with a paraganglioma and a family history of renal tumours; choose the genetic work-up and surveillance plan.
- A patient with phaeochromocytoma needs adrenalectomy; sequence the preoperative medical management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Gene-specific surveillance protocols (VHL, SDHx imaging schedules), malignancy-risk estimates by mutation, and RET/MEN2 linkage are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against Endocrine Society guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34147030**: *Personalized Management of Pheochromocytoma and Paraganglioma.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 31517632**: *Pheochromocytoma.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 28230061**: *Neurofibromatosis type 1.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 38849272**: *Pheochromocytoma. Preoperative approach.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0815] Hereditary Spherocytosis and Elliptocytosis

**Cluster**: hereditary red cell membrane disorders | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hereditary spherocytosis and elliptocytosis are disorders of the red-cell skeleton: defects in spectrin, ankyrin, band 3 or protein 4.2 weaken the membrane, splenic conditioning does the rest, and the result is a chronic haemolysis that announces itself through jaundice, gallstones and a positive family history. The laboratory signature is the spherocyte (or elliptocyte) on the film, and modern diagnosis mixes EMA-binding and osmotic-fragility studies with genetic testing.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hereditary spherocytosis, elliptocytosis and stomatocytosis are inherited disorders caused by defects in various red-cell membrane proteins; the ICSH guideline standardises their laboratory diagnosis given heterogeneous clinical, biochemical and genetic presentations. (PMID 25790109)
- Membrane structural defects lead to hereditary spherocytosis and hereditary elliptocytosis, producing haemolysis; significant advances in diagnosis and clinical management of these inherited membrane disorders have been made. (PMID 30504335)
- Hereditary erythrocyte membrane disorders are caused by mutations in genes encoding transmembrane or cytoskeletal proteins, with decreased cell deformability and shortened erythrocyte survival as the main consequences. (PMID 31364155)
- Structural understanding of inherited red-cell membrane disorders explains altered cell function, reduced red-cell survival and the resulting haemolytic anaemia. (PMID 28447420)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Thresholds for splenectomy — balancing haemolysis control against infection and thrombotic risk — are individualised and not standardised in the retained sources.
- Genotype-phenotype correlation remains imperfect, complicating prognostication from molecular results alone.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Spherocytes on film: HS versus warm autoimmune haemolytic anaemia (DAT differentiates).
- Diagnostic tests: EMA binding, osmotic fragility, ektacytometry, genetics.
- Complications: gallstones, aplastic crises (parvovirus B19), splenomegaly.
- Elliptocytosis spectrum and hereditary pyropoikilocytosis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A teenager with intermittent jaundice, gallstones at 19 and a positive family history; work through diagnosis.
- A patient with spherocytes and a negative DAT has a sibling needing assessment; outline family screening.

**Reviewer Gap**: Splenectomy indications and vaccination/management around it, EMA-binding protocol detail, and genotype-specific counselling are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the ICSH guideline full text before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25790109**: *ICSH guidelines for the laboratory diagnosis of nonimmune hereditary red cell membrane disorders.* (2015) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30504335**: *Inherited hemolytic anemia: a possessive beginner's guide.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 31364155**: *Advances in understanding the pathogenesis of red cell membrane disorders.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 28447420**: *Red cell membrane disorders.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0816] herpes simplex encephalitis antiviral therapy

**Cluster**: herpes simplex encephalitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Herpes simplex encephalitis is the neurological emergency where the treatment decision precedes the diagnosis: acyclovir must start on suspicion, because delay measurably worsens outcome and no surrogate marker can safely exclude HSE early. HSV-1 is the commonest sporadic encephalitis in adults, PCR of CSF is the diagnostic mainstay, and the modern complication to recognise is autoimmune encephalitis that can follow the infection — so deterioration after apparently successful treatment is not necessarily failure.

### Core Teaching Points
- Advances in antiviral medications and supportive treatments have equipped physicians to combat the most severe manifestations of HSV disease; prompt recognition and treatment of HSV-1 encephalitis are essential. (PMID 27106239)
- Encephalitis is most commonly caused by viral infection, especially HSV type 1, although autoimmune causes such as NMDA-receptor-antibody encephalitis are increasingly recognised — a differential that shapes empirical therapy. (PMID 29626021)
- Herpes simplex encephalitis has been successfully treated with acyclovir, though it remains a cause of significant morbidity even with treatment. (PMID 33223083)
- Immediate empirical intravenous aciclovir (10 mg/kg every 8 hours, adjusted for renal function) must be administered without delay upon clinical suspicion of herpes simplex encephalitis; treatment is continued for 14 to 21 days with CSF HSV-PCR re-testing at the conclusion of therapy to confirm viral clearance prior to cessation. (PMID 27106239)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal acyclovir duration and whether oral follow-on therapy adds benefit are not resolved in the retained abstracts.
- The frequency and management of post-HSE autoimmune encephalitis (NMDAR-antibody) is increasingly recognised but not quantified in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Empirical acyclovir on suspicion — treat before PCR confirmation.
- CSF PCR timing, sensitivity and repeat-testing after a negative early result.
- Acyclovir dosing and renal hydration during therapy.
- Post-HSE autoimmune encephalitis as a cause of secondary deterioration.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with fever, confusion and temporal-lobe seizures; decide the first-hour management.
- A patient treated for HSE worsens in week 2 despite cleared PCR; consider post-infectious autoimmunity.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific acyclovir regimens, duration-of-therapy trials, and steroid adjuvant evidence are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against IDSA encephalitis guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27106239**: *Herpes Simplex Virus-1 Encephalitis in Adults: Pathophysiology, Diagnosis, and Management.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 29626021**: *Acute encephalitis - diagnosis and management.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 33223083**: *Treatment of Viral Encephalitis.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0817] Herpes simplex, impetigo and common skin infections

**Cluster**: common skin infections | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Most skin infections presenting in primary care are three diagnoses wearing different disguises: impetigo (honey-crusted, staphylococcal or streptococcal), herpes simplex (grouped vesicles on an erythematous base, recurring at the same site) and tinea (annular, scaly, advancing edge). The craft is pattern recognition plus judicious treatment — topical agents for localised disease, systemic agents when infection is extensive or deep, and a low threshold for reconsidering the diagnosis when a 'common infection' fails to respond.

### Core Teaching Points
- Common cutaneous infections of bacterial, viral and fungal origin are frequently encountered by clinicians in all fields, and an evidence-based review highlights treatment options and management across these classes. (PMID 34059250)
- Skin and soft-tissue infections in primary care range from uncomplicated impetigo to potentially lethal necrotising fasciitis, requiring careful severity triage. (PMID 30115333)
- Common cutaneous viral infections in children include warts, molluscum contagiosum, hand-foot-and-mouth disease and herpes simplex. (PMID 28196318)
- Impetigo is primarily caused by Staphylococcus aureus and Streptococcus pyogenes; localized non-bullous impetigo responds to topical antiseptics (hydrogen peroxide 1%) or topical antibiotics (mupirocin, fusidic acid), whereas widespread, bullous, or systemically symptomatic disease requires oral flucloxacillin or clarithromycin. (PMID 30115333)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Antibiotic choice and duration for impetigo vary by local resistance patterns, which the retained sources do not address.
- When (and whether) antiviral therapy for recurrent mucocutaneous HSV changes natural history is not resolved in the retained abstracts.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Impetigo: honey-crusted lesions, topical vs oral therapy, contagiousness.
- HSV versus zoster versus impetigo pattern recognition.
- Red flags for deep infection: pain out of proportion, necrosis, systemic toxicity.
- Tinea and other fungal mimics of bacterial infection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A child has spreading honey-crusted facial lesions; choose therapy and give infection-control advice.
- An adult with recurrent grouped vesicles requests suppression; counsel on triggers and options.

**Reviewer Gap**: MRSA-era antibiotic selection, decolonisation protocols, and dermatophyte treatment specifics are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against primary-care/infectious-diseases guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34059250**: *Common Cutaneous Infections: Patient Presentation, Clinical Course, and Treatment Options.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 30115333**: *Skin Infections.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 28196318**: *Common Skin Conditions in Children: Skin Infections.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0818] Herpes zoster: dermatomal distribution, antivirals, post-herpetic neuralgia, vaccination in elderly

**Cluster**: herpes zoster | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Shingles is the reactivation of varicella-zoster virus from a sensory ganglion, and it earns its place in the curriculum through two facts: it is common (about a million US cases a year, lifetime risk around 30%) and it hurts twice — acutely, and as post-herpetic neuralgia that can persist for months. Antivirals work best within 72 hours, ophthalmic and disseminated disease are emergencies, and recombinant vaccination has changed the prevention equation for older adults.

### Core Teaching Points
- Herpes zoster is caused by reactivation of latent varicella-zoster virus within sensory ganglia, classically a painful unilateral vesicular eruption, with post-herpetic neuralgia among its principal complications. (PMID 39057822)
- There are an estimated 1 million cases of herpes zoster annually in the United States with an individual lifetime risk of 30%, and cell-mediated immunity decline drives reactivation. (PMID 29431387)
- Herpes zoster is a major health burden seen most commonly in those aged 50 and over, in immunocompromised patients and in those on immunosuppressant drugs. (PMID 29516900)
- In older adults, zoster presents as a unilateral self-limited dermatomal rash, but postherpetic neuralgia is a common and often dominant sequela. (PMID 29079160)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal management of established post-herpetic neuralgia remains difficult, with mixed evidence across drug classes.
- Vaccination policy details (age thresholds, recombinant vs live attenuated) vary by country and are not settled in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Dermatomal distribution: which levels, and why ophthalmicus (V1) is an emergency.
- Antiviral window: treat within 72 hours, and later if new lesions appear.
- Post-herpetic neuralgia risk factors and management options.
- Recombinant zoster vaccine: who, when, and why it outperforms live vaccine.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old has a painful T8 dermatomal eruption that began yesterday; prescribe and counsel.
- A patient presents with vesicles on the tip of the nose; recognise zoster ophthalmicus and act.

**Reviewer Gap**: Antiviral agent selection/dosing, corticosteroid role, and PHN treatment algorithms are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against infectious-diseases/pain guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39057822**: *Herpes Zoster and Post-Herpetic Neuralgia-Diagnosis, Treatment, and Vaccination Strategies.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 29431387**: *Herpes Zoster and Postherpetic Neuralgia: Prevention and Management.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 29516900**: *Epidemiology, treatment and prevention of herpes zoster: A comprehensive review.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 29079160**: *Herpes Zoster in the Older Adult.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0819] Hiatal Hernia

**Cluster**: hiatal hernia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
A hiatal hernia is the stomach's migration through the diaphragmatic hiatus — the sliding type I being by far the commonest and mostly an incidental partner of reflux, while the para-oesophageal types (II-IV) carry mechanical complications like volvulus, obstruction and bleeding. Most need no treatment; the ones that do are either reflux-dominant (managed as GERD, with the ASGE guideline framing diagnosis and therapy) or mechanical (where surgical repair becomes the conversation).

### Core Teaching Points
- Esophageal hiatal hernia involves abnormal entry of abdominal contents into the thoracic cavity and is classified by the relationship of the oesophagogastric junction to the diaphragm; the sliding type I is the most frequent category. (PMID 29451037)
- GERD pathogenesis is complex, involving reflux exposure, epithelial resistance and visceral sensitivity, and hiatal hernia is a key anatomical amplifier of reflux. (PMID 29037470)
- The ASGE 2025 guideline provides an evidence-based approach to diagnosing and managing GERD — the framework within which reflux symptoms attributed to hiatal hernia are managed. (PMID 39692638)
- Symptomatic sliding (Type I) hiatal hernias are primarily managed medically with proton pump inhibitors, lifestyle modifications, and weight reduction, whereas paraesophageal (Types II-IV) hernias carrying risks of gastric volvulus, strangulation, or bleeding require elective laparoscopic repair with crural closure and fundoplication in suitable surgical candidates. (PMID 29451037)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which sliding hiatal hernias benefit from surgical fundoplication beyond reflux control is individualised and debated.
- Surveillance intervals for para-oesophageal hernias managed non-operatively are not standardised in the retained sources.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Type I (sliding) versus types II-IV (para-oesophageal) hernias and their complications.
- Hiatal hernia as a reflux amplifier: the anatomical rationale.
- When an incidental hernia needs no treatment at all.
- Red flags of mechanical complications: volvulus, obstruction, bleeding, anaemia.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An incidental sliding hiatal hernia is found on a chest X-ray; decide what (if anything) to do.
- An elderly patient with a large para-oesophageal hernia develops post-prandial pain and retching; recognise the emergency.

**Reviewer Gap**: Surgical indications and techniques (fundoplication, mesh), Cameron-lesion management, and giant-hernia work-up are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against surgical/ASGE guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29451037**: *Esophageal hiatal hernia: risk, diagnosis and management.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 29037470**: *Pathophysiology of Gastroesophageal Reflux Disease.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 39692638**: *American Society for Gastrointestinal Endoscopy guideline on the diagnosis and management of GERD.* (2025) [guideline] - None

## [0820] Hidradenitis suppurativa: assessment and treatment

**Cluster**: hidradenitis suppurativa | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hidradenitis suppurativa is a chronic, painful, under-recognised disease of the pilosebaceous unit in intertriginous skin — nodules, abscesses and draining tunnels in the axillae, groin and inframammary folds, with a prevalence around 1% and a heavy quality-of-life burden. Assessment uses severity staging (Hurley), and treatment escalates from topical and systemic antibiotics through biologics (anti-TNF) to surgery for tunnelling disease — a sequence the European S2k guideline formalises.

### Core Teaching Points
- The European S2k guideline (updated from the 2015 S1) provides a decision aid for selecting, implementing and assessing appropriate therapy for hidradenitis suppurativa. (PMID 39699926)
- HS is a chronic inflammatory disease characterised by painful deep-seated nodules, abscesses and draining tunnels in the axillary, inguinal, genitoanal or inframammary areas. (PMID 39862870)
- HS is a chronic, debilitating inflammatory disease derived from the pilosebaceous unit affecting approximately 1% of the general population, with nodules, abscesses and tunnels as clinical hallmarks. (PMID 37968174)
- HS is a complex immune-mediated disorder initially presenting as folliculitis that can progress to advanced lesions such as fistulas requiring extensive surgical intervention. (PMID 40530931)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing of biologics and the role of surgery early versus late in tunnelling disease remain practice-dependent.
- Disease-modifying therapy that prevents progression, rather than suppressing flares, is not yet established.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Hurley staging (I nodules/abscesses, II sinus tracts/scarring, III diffuse involvement).
- Treatment ladder: topical clindamycin -> tetracyclines -> anti-TNF biologics -> surgery.
- Comorbidity associations: obesity, smoking, metabolic syndrome, depression.
- Differentiating HS from boils and Crohn's-related perianal disease.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 26-year-old with recurrent axillary abscesses and scarring is told she has recurrent boils; recognise HS and stage it.
- A patient with Hurley II disease fails antibiotic therapy; discuss biologic options using the guideline framework.

**Reviewer Gap**: Hurley-staging definitions, specific antibiotic/biologic regimens and dosing, and surgical technique selection are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the S2k full guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39699926**: *European S2k guidelines for hidradenitis suppurativa/acne inversa part 2: Treatment.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39862870**: *Hidradenitis suppurativa.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 37968174**: *Hidradenitis suppurativa.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40530931**: *Hidradenitis suppurativa: pathogenesis.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0821] High Altitude Pulmonary Edema

**Cluster**: high altitude pulmonary edema | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
High-altitude pulmonary edema is the deadliest of the acute altitude illnesses and the one most often missed early, because its first symptoms — exertional dyspnoea, cough, reduced exercise tolerance — look like simple fatigue at altitude. It is a non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema driven by hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, arriving one to five days after ascent, and it is preventable with graded ascent and treatable with descent and oxygen. Every physician advising travellers, trekkers, or workers deploying to altitude should know its time course, its susceptibility patterns, and the absolute priority of not waiting for it to 'settle'.

### Core Teaching Points
- Three forms of acute altitude illness — acute mountain sickness, high-altitude cerebral oedema and high-altitude pulmonary oedema — can each arise 1–5 days after ascent to altitudes of 2500 m or higher. (PMID 28143879)
- HAPE is a non-cardiogenic pulmonary oedema resulting from excessive hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, rather than left-heart failure. (PMID 28143879)
- The prevalence of acute altitude illness varies with acclimatisation status, rate of ascent and individual susceptibility; chronic mountain sickness affects permanent high-altitude resident populations. (PMID 38902312)
- Clinical frameworks exist for counselling and evaluating patients before travel to altitude and for the prevention and treatment of the main forms of acute altitude illness. (PMID 26900119)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Pharmacological prophylaxis choices (nifedipine, PDE5 inhibitors, acetazolamide) for HAPE-susceptible individuals are not fully standardised.
- The relative roles of descent, portable hyperbaric chambers and in-situ oxygen in moderate HAPE are guided mainly by observational experience.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Time window (1–5 days) and altitude threshold (≥2500 m) for acute altitude illness.
- Pathophysiology: hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction producing non-cardiogenic oedema.
- Distinguishing HAPE from cardiogenic pulmonary oedema and pneumonia at altitude.
- Prevention strategies: graded ascent, acclimatisation days, avoiding rapid gain in sleeping altitude.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A trekker develops exertional dyspnoea and a dry cough on day 2 at 4200 m and wants to push on to the summit; decide the management and the rule for continued ascent.
- A patient with a previous episode of HAPE asks whether they can join a high-altitude expedition and what prophylaxis is appropriate.

**Reviewer Gap**: Drug dosing (nifedipine, acetazolamide, tadalafil), Lake Louise diagnostic criteria detail, and disposition thresholds are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the Wilderness Medical Society guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38902312**: *Altitude illnesses.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 28143879**: *Acute high-altitude sickness.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 26900119**: *High-Altitude Medicine.* (2016) [review] - None
