## [0822] High-flow nasal oxygen and non-invasive ventilation in acute hypoxaemic failure

**Cluster**: non-invasive respiratory support | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Choosing between high-flow nasal oxygen, CPAP and bilevel NIV in acute hypoxaemic failure is now one of the most consequential triage decisions in the ICU, because the wrong choice does not merely fail — it delays intubation past the point where it helps. The first official clinical practice guideline on non-invasive respiratory support has been published specifically because modality selection was so variable. Knowing what each device does physiologically, when each is indicated, and how to recognise failure early is core competency for any physician managing acute respiratory failure.

### Core Teaching Points
- Non-invasive respiratory support strategies — HFNC, NIV and CPAP — may prevent intubation and improve outcomes in acute hypoxaemic and hypercapnic respiratory failure, but uncertainty about optimal patient and modality selection led to the first official clinical practice guideline. (PMID 42371750)
- High-flow nasal therapy provides low-level positive airway pressure, anatomical dead-space washout, good tolerance, and can be used during breaks from CPAP or BiPAP; CPAP and BiPAP deliver distinct physiological effects and require interface- and settings-specific expertise. (PMID 41254751)
- Extubation failure — reintubation within 2–7 days of planned extubation — is associated with prolonged ventilation, increased ventilator-associated pneumonia, longer ICU and hospital stays, and increased mortality. (PMID 30629933)
- High-quality evidence guiding HFNC discontinuation is lacking; weaning of flow, FiO2, or both is largely left to clinician discretion, and failure to de-escalate has measurable consequences. (PMID 39929280)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which hypoxaemic patients benefit from NIV versus HFNC first-line, and how to identify those in whom NIV merely delays intubation, remain contested.
- Optimal HFNC weaning and liberation protocols (what to reduce first, how fast) are not evidence-defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Physiological comparison: HFNC (dead-space washout, low PEEP) versus CPAP/BiPAP.
- Post-extubation failure definition (reintubation within 2–7 days) and its consequences.
- Guideline-based modality selection for hypoxaemic versus hypercapnic failure.
- Recognising NIRS failure and indications to escalate to intubation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with severe hypoxaemic pneumonia is started on HFNC; outline what you monitor to decide whether intubation is needed, and when NIV would have been the wrong choice.
- A COPD patient is extubated after an exacerbation; choose and justify the post-extubation respiratory support and the criteria for reintubation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific intubation-trigger scores (ROX index, HACOR), interface selection detail and ventilator settings tables are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full ATS/ERS/JRS guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42371750**: *Noninvasive respiratory support for adult patients with acute respiratory failure: an official clinical practice guideline.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41254751**: *A clinical guide to non-invasive respiratory support in acute respiratory failure: ventilation settings, techniques and monitoring.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 30629933**: *Ventilatory support after extubation in critically ill patients.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 39929280**: *High-Flow Nasal Cannula Liberation: Who, When, and How?* (2025) [review] - None

## [0823] High-flow nasal oxygen in acute hypoxemic respiratory failure

**Cluster**: high-flow nasal oxygen | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
High-flow nasal cannula oxygen has moved from novelty to first-line therapy in acute hypoxaemic respiratory failure within a decade, because it does several things simple oxygen cannot: it washes out anatomical dead space, generates a small positive-pressure effect, delivers a constant FiO2, and is comfortable enough that patients actually keep it on. The European Respiratory Society guideline formalises where the evidence supports it, the Cochrane review and meta-analysis quantify the benefit versus conventional oxygen, and understanding the device physiology is what lets you use it intelligently rather than reflexively.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ERS clinical practice guideline provides GRADE-based recommendations for HFNC alongside other non-invasive forms of respiratory support in adults with acute respiratory failure. (PMID 34649974)
- HFNC uses an air/oxygen blender, active humidifier and heated single tube to deliver flows up to 60 L/min, with physiological advantages including reduced anatomical dead space, a PEEP effect, constant FiO2 and good humidification. (PMID 27016353)
- A Cochrane systematic review evaluates HFNC versus standard oxygen therapy or non-invasive ventilation for adults with, or at risk of, acute respiratory failure in the ICU. (PMID 33661521)
- Meta-analysis of randomised trials compares HFNC with conventional oxygen therapy specifically in acute hypoxaemic respiratory failure, pooling safety and efficacy outcomes. (PMID 30888444)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The evidence base for HFNC in hypercapnic respiratory failure is separate and less mature than for hypoxaemic failure.
- Optimal flow and FiO2 titration, and objective thresholds for escalation, are not standardised across studies.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HFNC components and physiological effects (dead-space washout, PEEP, constant FiO2, humidification).
- ERS guideline recommendations for HFNC in acute respiratory failure.
- HFNC versus conventional oxygen: what the meta-analysis shows.
- Patient selection: who benefits most and when to escalate.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with acute hypoxaemic failure tolerates HFNC poorly at 60 L/min; troubleshoot fit, flow and interface before abandoning the modality.
- An NIV-intolerant hypoxaemic patient needs an alternative; argue for or against HFNC using the guideline evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: Flow/FiO2 titration protocols, ROX-index escalation thresholds, and device-specific contraindications are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full ERS guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34649974**: *ERS clinical practice guidelines: high-flow nasal cannula in acute respiratory failure.* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 27016353**: *High-Flow Nasal Cannula Oxygen Therapy in Adults: Physiological Benefits, Indication, Clinical Benefits, and Adverse Effects.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 33661521**: *High-flow nasal cannulae for respiratory support in adult intensive care patients.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 30888444**: *High flow nasal cannula compared with conventional oxygen therapy for acute hypoxemic respiratory failure: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0824] Hip fracture management and perioperative optimisation

**Cluster**: hip fracture perioperative care | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
A hip fracture is never just an orthopaedic problem — it is a whole-patient event that lands on an older adult with multiple comorbidities, and the quality of the medical care around the operation determines a large share of the outcome. The internist's role spans preoperative risk assessment and risk reduction, timing of surgery, fluid optimisation, anaesthesia-mode selection and secondary-fracture prevention. The evidence for each component is thinner than patients assume, which makes disciplined perioperative optimisation, not any single technique, the real intervention.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hip fracture is a frequent cause of hospitalisation in the elderly with significant morbidity and mortality, and the internist/hospitalist plays a critical role across preoperative risk assessment, risk reduction, and timing of surgery. (PMID 39341619)
- An organised, systematic, coordinated multidisciplinary approach to perioperative management of geriatric surgical patients reduces complications, improves outcomes and lowers cost, and includes work-up and treatment of osteoporosis and fragility fractures. (PMID 30390985)
- In proximal femoral fracture, the role of advanced methods of perioperative fluid optimisation (invasive haemodynamic monitoring, protocolised strategies) in reducing risk remains unclear, per a Cochrane review. (PMID 26976366)
- Evidence comparing modes of anaesthesia for hip fracture surgery has been limited by heterogeneity in how perioperative outcomes are reported and defined. (PMID 29397135)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal timing of surgery (early versus delayed for medical optimisation) and which preoperative corrections change outcomes.
- Whether regional or general anaesthesia meaningfully affects mortality and major complications remains unresolved because of outcome heterogeneity.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of orthogeriatric perioperative optimisation and secondary-fracture prevention.
- Timing of surgery: trade-offs between delay for work-up and early fixation.
- Evidence limits around anaesthesia mode selection.
- Fluid management strategies and the unclear role of invasive haemodynamic monitoring.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 84-year-old on a DOAC presents with an intracapsular hip fracture; plan timing of surgery and perioperative anticoagulation management.
- A frail patient with CKD and anaemia is listed for hip repair; build the preoperative optimisation checklist and decide what must be corrected before theatre.

**Reviewer Gap**: Anticoagulation reversal protocols, tranexamic acid use, delirium-prevention bundles and specific nerve-block techniques are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against NICE/AAHKS guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39341619**: *The Patient with Hip Fracture.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 30390985**: *Orthopedic Surgery and the Geriatric Patient.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 26976366**: *Perioperative fluid volume optimization following proximal femoral fracture.* (2017) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 29397135**: *Perioperative outcomes in the context of mode of anaesthesia for patients undergoing hip fracture surgery.* (2018) [systematic-review] - None

## [0825] HIV antiretroviral therapy initiation

**Cluster**: HIV antiretroviral therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Starting antiretroviral therapy is no longer a decision about whether but about with what and how fast. Contemporary guidelines recommend rapid initiation for everyone diagnosed, and the modern first-line landscape is dominated by integrase-inhibitor single-tablet regimens — above all bictegravir/emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide — chosen for efficacy, high resistance barrier, safety and simplicity. Regimen selection for a treatment-naive adult now turns on comorbidity fit, drug interactions, and how quickly the patient can start, with two-drug simplification reserved for specific maintenance situations.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2024 IAS-USA panel updated consensus recommendations for HIV treatment and prevention from 249 relevant citations identified from literature searches. (PMID 39616604)
- A systematic literature review and network meta-analysis compares safety outcomes of bictegravir/emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide with other ART regimens in treatment-naive people with HIV-1, addressing a key driver of regimen selection. (PMID 40442921)
- Bictegravir/emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide is a once-daily single-tablet regimen whose phase 3 trials showed noninferiority to dolutegravir-based therapy; bictegravir is an integrase strand transfer inhibitor with a high genetic barrier to resistance. (PMID 30460547)
- Strong evidence supports selected two-drug regimens as a strategy to address long-term toxicity, cost and convenience concerns of lifelong therapy, though not all two-drug combinations are equivalent. (PMID 33557651)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term metabolic effects of integrase inhibitors, including weight gain, are still being characterised.
- Eligibility boundaries and durability of two-drug maintenance regimens versus standard three-drug therapy are not fully settled.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line regimen selection in a treatment-naive adult (INSTI-based single-tablet regimens).
- Integrase inhibitor mechanism and resistance barrier.
- Advantages and limits of two-drug simplification.
- What the IAS-USA 2024 update changed in treatment and prevention guidance.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A newly diagnosed patient with eGFR 45 presents; choose an initial regimen and justify what you would avoid.
- A virologically suppressed patient on a three-drug regimen asks about reducing pills; assess eligibility for two-drug maintenance.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific dosing tables, HLA-B*5701/abacavir screening, renal and bone monitoring schedules, and TB co-treatment interactions are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full IAS-USA 2024 guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39616604**: *Antiretroviral Drugs for Treatment and Prevention of HIV in Adults: 2024 Recommendations of the International Antiviral Society-USA Panel.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40442921**: *Comparative safety of B/F/TAF versus other antiretroviral therapy regimens for treatment-naive people with HIV-1.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 30460547**: *Bictegravir/Emtricitabine/Tenofovir Alafenamide: A Review in HIV-1 Infection.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 33557651**: *Risks and benefits of reducing the number of drugs to treat HIV-1 infection.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0826] HIV-Associated Rheumatic Manifestations

**Cluster**: HIV-associated rheumatic disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Rheumatic complaints in a person with HIV sit at a genuinely three-way diagnostic fork: they may be direct viral or immune-dysregulation effects, musculoskeletal opportunistic infection, or immune reconstitution after starting ART. The spectrum runs from trivial self-limiting arthralgia through seronegative spondyloarthropathy and HIV-associated arthritis to vasculitis and myopathy. ART transformed the whole picture — prevalence patterns shifted and immune-mediated disease re-emerged with immune restoration — so the era in which a patient was treated matters as much as the joint that hurts.

### Core Teaching Points
- Rheumatic manifestations of HIV encompass arthralgia, HIV-associated arthritis, spondyloarthropathies, inflammatory myopathies, vasculitides and opportunistic musculoskeletal infections, arising from direct viral effects, immune dysregulation, opportunistic infection or ART-induced complications. (PMID 40374411)
- With highly active antiretroviral therapy and restored immunity, multiple immune-mediated rheumatic diseases have resurfaced in the HIV population. (PMID 37723618)
- Described articular syndromes include HIV-associated arthropathy, seronegative spondyloarthropathies (reactive arthritis, psoriatic arthritis, undifferentiated SpA), rheumatoid arthritis and painful articular syndrome. (PMID 26932524)
- Combination ART has normalised life expectancy for adherent patients, so HIV management must now also address long-term complications of HIV and its treatment, including the evolving spectrum of rheumatic syndromes. (PMID 26362742)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Safety and timing of immunosuppression and biologics in patients with untreated or uncontrolled HIV are not well defined.
- Immune reconstitution inflammatory syndrome can mimic flares of rheumatic disease, complicating attribution.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Classifying a joint complaint: direct viral effect, opportunistic infection, or immune reconstitution.
- ART-era shift in the spectrum of HIV-associated rheumatic disease.
- Painful articular syndrome versus true inflammatory arthritis in HIV.
- Seronegative spondyloarthropathy patterns in HIV-positive patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient three months after starting ART develops new inflammatory oligoarthritis and enthesitis; decide whether this is reconstitution or a primary SpA.
- An HIV patient with CD4 60 presents with a hot swollen knee; work through septic arthritis and opportunistic infection versus inflammatory causes.

**Reviewer Gap**: Guidance on biologic DMARD use, TB/HBV screening before immunosuppression, and specific treatment algorithms are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against rheumatology guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40374411**: *Rheumatic manifestations of HIV/AIDS.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 37723618**: *HIV-Associated Rheumatic Diseases: A Narrative Review.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 26932524**: *Inflammatory arthritis in HIV positive patients: A practical guide.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 26362742**: *Evolving spectrum of HIV-associated rheumatic syndromes.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0827] HIV: initial diagnosis, CD4 count, viral load, when to start ART

**Cluster**: HIV initial management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The question 'when should this patient start ART?' has a definitive answer now: immediately, regardless of CD4 count. The trials behind treat-all showed clinical and transmission benefits that ended the CD4-threshold era, and implementation studies such as Eswatini's MaxART trial proved a treat-all strategy is deliverable in real health systems. CD4 counts have not disappeared — they still stratify opportunistic-infection risk and flag advanced disease — but they no longer gate therapy. What remains is execution: same-day starts, linkage to care, and durable suppression, with roughly half of people with HIV worldwide still not on suppressive ART.

### Core Teaching Points
- Starting ART early, followed by continuous lifetime treatment, most effectively achieves durable virologic suppression and immune restoration, improving clinical outcomes and preventing sexual transmission. (PMID 33788514)
- Following randomised trials showing clinical and public-health benefits of immediate initiation, the CD4 count's role has shifted away from deciding when to start ART. (PMID 28059957)
- The MaxART Early Access to ART for All trial in Eswatini demonstrated that a Treat-All strategy can be implemented within a government-managed public health system to advance the UNAIDS 90-90-90 targets. (PMID 32474844)
- Despite effective therapies, approximately one-half of people with HIV are not receiving suppressive ART. (PMID 33788514)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Same-day ART initiation in patients with advanced disease or opportunistic infections needs individualised timing.
- Optimal frequency of CD4 monitoring once a patient is virologically suppressed with a stable count is unresolved across guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Treat-all: start ART regardless of CD4 count, including the trial evidence that drove the change.
- What CD4 count still tells you once it no longer gates therapy (OI risk, advanced disease).
- UNAIDS 90-90-90 targets and treat-all implementation evidence.
- Barriers to the first 90: testing, linkage, and the suppression cascade.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A newly diagnosed patient with CD4 850 asks whether treatment can wait until they 'feel ready'; counsel using the treat-all evidence.
- A late presenter with CD4 80 and weight loss is diagnosed in clinic; plan sequencing of evaluation, prophylaxis and ART start.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific first-line regimen selection, baseline resistance testing indications, and opportunistic-infection prophylaxis thresholds are cross-referenced to topics 825 and 829 and not duplicated here.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33788514**: *HIV Infection in Adults: Initial Management.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 28059957**: *The evolving role of CD4 cell counts in HIV care.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 32474844**: *Getting to 90-90-90: Experiences from the MaxART Early Access to ART for All (EAAA) Trial in Eswatini.* (2019) [trial-report] - None

## [0828] HIV initial work-up

**Cluster**: HIV initial work-up | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The first visit after a new HIV diagnosis sets the trajectory for everything that follows: confirmatory testing, baseline staging, comorbidity and coinfection screening, and a linkage plan that treats ART start as urgent rather than eventual. Evidence syntheses confirm that ART improves outcomes and reduces transmission, and that interventions compressing the time from diagnosis to treatment initiation measurably raise the proportion who start. The work-up must also see the whole patient — health literacy, stigma and social context predict adherence as strongly as any laboratory value.

### Core Teaching Points
- Management of newly diagnosed HIV must address health literacy and disease-related stigma, which are barriers to the lifelong treatment engagement required for HIV care. (PMID 30179141)
- Updated USPSTF evidence confirms ART is associated with improved clinical outcomes and decreased HIV transmission, underpinning screening-to-treatment linkage. (PMID 31184705)
- As policy moved to lifelong ART regardless of CD4 count, pre-ART interventions that improve the rate and timing of treatment initiation became increasingly important, per a systematic review. (PMID 27507249)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The optimal sequencing of baseline testing and same-day ART start versus full work-up first is not standardised.
- Which baseline investigations beyond CD4, viral load and resistance testing change management is guideline-dependent.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components and sequence of the baseline evaluation after a new HIV diagnosis.
- Confirmatory testing algorithm after a positive rapid/screening test.
- Linkage-to-care interventions that compress time to ART initiation.
- Addressing stigma and literacy at the first visit.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A clinic patient has a positive fourth-generation screening test; outline confirmatory testing and what happens at the first comprehensive visit.
- A newly arrived refugee screens positive during intake; plan the work-up with attention to literacy, stigma and linkage support.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE: the specific confirmatory algorithm, baseline laboratory panel (genotype, HBV/HCV, TB, pregnancy, renal/liver), and vaccination review are NOT covered by the retained abstracts. Verify every work-up element against current WHO/EACS guidance before clinician approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30179141**: *The Management of Newly Diagnosed HIV in a Sudanese Refugee in Canada: Commentary and Review of Literature.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 31184705**: *Screening for HIV Infection in Asymptomatic, Nonpregnant Adolescents and Adults: Updated Evidence Report and Systematic Review for the US Preventive Services Task Force.* (2019) [evidence-report] - None
- **PMID 27507249**: *Interventions to improve the rate or timing of initiation of antiretroviral therapy for HIV in sub-Saharan Africa.* (2017) [systematic-review] - None

## [0829] HIV opportunistic infections: PCP prophylaxis thresholds, MAC, CMV retinitis, toxoplasmosis

**Cluster**: HIV opportunistic infections | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Opportunistic infections are the price HIV extracts for untreated immunosuppression, and most of that price is avoidable: every major OI is tied to a CD4 threshold that should trigger either prophylaxis or heightened surveillance. Pneumocystis pneumonia remains the canonical example — CD4 below 200 cells/mm3 demands co-trimoxazole prophylaxis, and the same drug treats established disease — while toxoplasmosis, MAC and CMV retinitis each occupy their own strata deeper in the CD4 nadir. With effective ART, the modern skill is not just starting prophylaxis but knowing when stopping is safe.

### Core Teaching Points
- In HIV-positive patients the risk of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia is strongly correlated with decreased CD4 cell count, and trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole chemoprophylaxis is highly effective. (PMID 28035717)
- Evidence supports discontinuing primary and secondary PJP prophylaxis once CD4 counts have recovered on ART, per a systematic review of discontinuation studies. (PMID 26358129)
- TMP-SMX is the first-line agent for treating PJP; dihydropteroate synthase mutations raise potential sulfamide resistance, and PJP still occurs with late presentation or virologic/immunologic failure despite cART. (PMID 28782390)
- EACS guideline version 13.0 (2025) was comprehensively revised and reorganised into HIV management/prevention and comorbidity parts, covering first-line regimens and related-infection management. (PMID 41088922)
- Co-trimoxazole prophylaxis reduces morbidity and mortality in people with HIV; systematic-review evidence informs WHO guidance on initiation, discontinuation and dose. (PMID 26424674)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Exact CD4 thresholds and required duration of CD4 recovery differ between guidelines for stopping primary versus secondary PJP prophylaxis.
- The clinical impact of TMP-SMX resistance mutations on treatment outcomes is incompletely defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PJP prophylaxis threshold (CD4 <200 cells/mm3) and first-line agent (TMP-SMX).
- When prophylaxis can be safely discontinued after ART-mediated CD4 recovery.
- Adjunctive corticosteroids in moderate-to-severe PJP (guideline-based).
- CD4-stratified OI risk: toxoplasmosis and MAC territory (<100) versus CMV retinitis (<50), per EACS.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with CD4 150 is not on any prophylaxis; assess risk and start appropriate chemoprophylaxis.
- A patient with PJP and an oxygen saturation of 88% on room air is admitted; outline treatment and the indication for adjunctive steroids.

**Reviewer Gap**: CMV retinitis and MAC specifics rest on the EACS guideline source alone; toxoplasmosis prophylaxis thresholds, steroid dosing regimens and secondary-prophylaxis discontinuation criteria are not detailed in the retained abstracts. Verify against the full EACS v13.0 guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26358129**: *Discontinuing Pneumocystis jirovecii Pneumonia Prophylaxis in HIV-Infected Patients With a CD4 Cell Count <200 Cells/mm3.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 28035717**: *The role of CD4 cell count as discriminatory measure to guide chemoprophylaxis against Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 28782390**: *Treatment of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia in HIV-infected patients: a review.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 41088922**: *Major revision version 13.0 of the European AIDS Clinical Society guidelines 2025.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 26424674**: *Co-trimoxazole prophylaxis in adults, including pregnant women, with HIV: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2015) [systematic-review] - None

## [0830] HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis

**Cluster**: HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Pre-exposure prophylaxis is the most powerful prevention tool HIV medicine has ever had, and its failure so far is one of uptake, not efficacy: a daily tenofovir/emtricitabine pill (or two-monthly long-acting cabotegravir, which outperformed the oral standard in a landmark trial) reduces acquisition dramatically in randomised evidence, yet fewer than a fifth of those who could benefit in the US use it. The 2025 Canadian guideline update formalises indications and regimens, and dedicated guidance now addresses populations whose data lagged — notably transgender and nonbinary people, for whom hormone interactions and representation in trials remain live concerns.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 Canadian guideline updates clinical indications and drug regimens for both PrEP and PEP, drawing on systematic reviews and community-based organisation input. (PMID 41326046)
- PrEP effectiveness is well demonstrated in cisgender populations, while empirical data specific to transgender and nonbinary persons remain limited, warranting dedicated clinical guidance. (PMID 36370382)
- Systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised trials confirms the clinical effectiveness and safety of oral tenofovir-containing PrEP against HIV acquisition. (PMID 35545381)
- Long-acting cabotegravir dosed every 8 weeks has low adherence demands and demonstrated efficacy versus oral TDF/FTC for PrEP; it is the only long-acting agent FDA-approved for HIV PrEP. (PMID 37198721)
- A single daily TDF/emtricitabine pill prevents HIV transmission, yet is used by fewer than 20% of people who could benefit in the United States. (PMID 29584848)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Barriers to PrEP uptake and how best to implement long-acting injectable PrEP at scale are unresolved.
- Renal and bone monitoring frequency, and drug interactions with gender-affirming hormones, need individualised application.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PrEP regimen options: daily oral TDF/FTC, long-acting cabotegravir, and their comparative evidence.
- Eligibility assessment and key populations, including transgender and nonbinary patients.
- Trial evidence for cabotegravir versus oral PrEP.
- Monitoring on PrEP: HIV testing, renal function, STI screening.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An MSM patient requests PrEP; choose a regimen and outline baseline assessment and follow-up monitoring.
- A patient struggling with daily oral adherence asks about alternatives; discuss long-acting cabotegravir and its initiation schedule.

**Reviewer Gap**: On-demand (2-1-1) dosing, STI screening intervals, renal monitoring protocols and pregnancy-related guidance are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full Canadian 2025 guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41326046**: *Canadian guideline on HIV pre- and postexposure prophylaxis: 2025 update.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 36370382**: *HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) for transgender and nonbinary persons: literature review and guidelines for clinical practice.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 35545381**: *Oral pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) to prevent HIV: a systematic review and meta-analysis of clinical effectiveness and safety.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 37198721**: *Efficacy and safety of long-acting cabotegravir versus oral tenofovir disoproxil fumarate-emtricitabine as HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis.* (2023) [trial-report] - None
- **PMID 29584848**: *HIV Preexposure Prophylaxis: A Review.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0831] HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis

**Cluster**: HIV PrEP implementation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
PrEP's problem is no longer whether it works but whether people can get it, stay on it, and be supported through the cycles of starting, stopping and restarting that real life imposes. Implementation science now dominates the literature: systematic reviews of best practices, global syntheses of provider barriers, and frameworks describing how programs must move from development and diffusion to genuine integration. Two populations remain systematically under-served — gay and bisexual men facing access disparities, and pregnant and postpartum women, for whom prevention is also child health.

### Core Teaching Points
- A qualitative systematic review identifies characteristics of best practices for increasing PrEP use and persistence, and maps research gaps in current PrEP interventions. (PMID 38743381)
- A global systematic review and meta-analysis quantifies health-care providers' awareness, willingness and key barriers to implementing PrEP. (PMID 41761621)
- PrEP implementation has moved through waves of development (2012–2015) and diffusion (2016–2018); reducing persistent disparities in access requires a next wave focused on integration of services. (PMID 31094576)
- Pregnancy is a critical window to elicit HIV vulnerability, offer testing, and provide PrEP for the pregnancy, postpartum period and future conception planning. (PMID 28361503)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which delivery models best sustain PrEP persistence through discontinuation-and-restart cycles is not established.
- Provider-level barriers identified in global syntheses vary widely by health system, limiting generalisable solutions.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PrEP continuum of care: indication, initiation, persistence, discontinuation, restart.
- Provider barriers to PrEP implementation and how they are measured.
- PrEP in pregnancy and breastfeeding: indications and counselling.
- Next-wave implementation: from diffusion to integration for marginalised populations.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient stops and restarts PrEP twice in a year; design a follow-up approach that keeps them protected through the gaps.
- A pregnant patient at ongoing HIV risk presents for antenatal care; decide whether and how to offer PrEP.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific eligibility criteria, baseline and follow-up laboratory monitoring, and regimen dosing are covered under topics 830/832 and not duplicated here.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38743381**: *Identifying Best Practices for Increasing HIV Pre-exposure Prophylaxis (PrEP) Use and Persistence in the United States.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 41761621**: *Global Perspectives on Pre-Exposure Prophylaxis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Health Care Providers' Awareness, Willingness, and Barriers.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 31094576**: *Next-Wave HIV Pre-Exposure Prophylaxis Implementation for Gay and Bisexual Men.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 28361503**: *Offering pre-exposure prophylaxis for HIV prevention to pregnant and postpartum women: a clinical approach.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0832] HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis PrEP: tenofovir-emtricitabine, eligibility, monitoring, efficacy

**Cluster**: HIV PrEP drug therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Tenofovir disoproxil fumarate with emtricitabine is the drug pair on which oral PrEP was built, and its clinical profile is unusually favourable: trial-level efficacy of 74–92% among participants with detectable drug levels, a safety record gathered across large prevention trials, and adverse events that are real but mostly manageable — nausea at initiation, small declines in renal function, and bone-density effects that matter mainly in younger patients. Knowing this safety architecture is what lets clinicians prescribe confidently and monitor selectively rather than reflexively.

### Core Teaching Points
- Prophylactic TDF-based regimens reduce the risk of HIV infection by 74 to 92% among participants with detectable drug levels, and available evidence supports PrEP efficacy when combined with other preventive methods. (PMID 27391203, PMID 28130774)
- Safety concerns are a recognised challenge in PrEP implementation, and dedicated reviews discuss the adverse-event profile and its management. (PMID 28130774)
- Adverse events observed in clinical trials of TDF/FTC for PrEP are documented in a dedicated drug-safety evaluation, informing monitoring practice. (PMID 27391203)
- Daily oral tenofovir disoproxil fumarate/emtricitabine (TDF/FTC) or tenofovir alafenamide/emtricitabine (TAF/FTC) provides >99% protection against sexual HIV acquisition when taken with high adherence; pre-initiation testing mandates confirmed negative 4th-generation HIV antigen/antibody testing, hepatitis B screening, and baseline eGFR assessment with quarterly follow-up. (PMID 28130774)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term (beyond trial follow-up) renal and bone effects of TDF-based PrEP in younger populations need ongoing surveillance.
- How to individualise monitoring frequency for patients with borderline renal function is practice-dependent.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Efficacy magnitude of TDF/FTC PrEP and the central role of adherence (detectable drug levels).
- Adverse-effect profile: GI intolerance, renal tubular effects, bone density.
- Monitoring on PrEP: HIV testing, creatinine/eGFR, STI screening cadence.
- Eligibility: who qualifies and what must be excluded before starting.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old with eGFR 68 requests PrEP; decide whether TDF/FTC is appropriate and what monitoring to arrange.
- A patient on daily PrEP reports nausea in week 2; counsel on expected course and when to switch approach.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources, both 2016): current eligibility criteria, on-demand dosing, long-acting alternatives and updated renal monitoring protocols are not covered. Cross-check topics 830/831 and verify against the current guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28130774**: *Pre-Exposure Prophylaxis for HIV Prevention: Safety Concerns.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 27391203**: *Drug safety evaluation of oral tenofovir disoproxil fumarate-emtricitabine for pre-exposure prophylaxis.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0833] HIV prognosis before and after modern ART

**Cluster**: HIV prognosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The prognosis question in HIV has flipped completely: it is no longer 'how long' but 'how close to normal'. With modern ART, published life-expectancy estimates for people with HIV on treatment approach those of the general population, though a gap persists that depends on when treatment starts, baseline CD4, and health system context. The residual burden has also shifted — from AIDS-defining illness to non-AIDS comorbidity — which is why markers such as the CD4/CD8 ratio are being studied to flag patients whose immune dysfunction persists despite viral suppression. Late diagnosis remains the single most powerful determinant of a worse prognosis even after ART begins.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review of published estimates finds life expectancy of HIV-positive adults on ART increasingly approaches that of the general population, though gaps remain by timing of initiation and setting. (PMID 27254748)
- Individuals first diagnosed with advanced HIV have higher mortality than those diagnosed earlier, even after starting ART, making early diagnosis the key prognostic lever. (PMID 31285181)
- The CD4/CD8 ratio and CD8+ T-cell count are being evaluated as prognostic markers for non-AIDS mortality in treated people living with HIV, beyond what CD4 count alone explains. (PMID 38361925)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- How much of the residual life-expectancy gap is attributable to persistent immune activation versus lifestyle and traditional risk factors.
- Whether CD4/CD8-ratio-guided interventions actually improve non-AIDS outcomes is unproven.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Life expectancy of treated HIV versus the general population, and what modifies the gap.
- Advanced HIV at diagnosis as a prognostic determinant despite ART.
- Non-AIDS mortality and emerging markers (CD4/CD8 ratio, CD8 count).
- Prognosis counselling: what to tell a newly diagnosed patient.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A newly diagnosed 30-year-old asks 'will I live a normal life?'; construct an evidence-based answer.
- Two patients start ART — one with CD4 600, one with CD4 40; compare expected trajectories and what changes counselling.

**Reviewer Gap**: Quantitative life-expectancy figures by region and subgroup, and smoking/ lifestyle adjustment data, are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full systematic review before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27254748**: *Trends in life expectancy of HIV-positive adults on antiretroviral therapy across the globe.* (2017) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 31285181**: *Advanced HIV: diagnosis, treatment, and prevention.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 38361925**: *CD4/CD8 ratio and CD8+ T-cell count as prognostic markers for non-AIDS mortality in people living with HIV.* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0834] HIV testing

**Cluster**: HIV testing | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
HIV testing technology has quietly revolutionised the epidemic's control: the evolution from antibody-only assays through fourth-generation antigen/antibody tests to nucleic-acid detection has compressed the window period dramatically, and each advance matters most in acute infection, when viral load and transmissibility peak. Universal screening is now recommended for all adolescents and adults at least once, with repeat testing for those at risk — yet testing is also where the cascade leaks, since a reactive test that is not confirmed and linked achieves nothing.

### Core Teaching Points
- The US Preventive Services Task Force recommends universal HIV screening at least once for adolescents and adults aged 15 to 65, with additional screening for higher-risk patients, reflecting recent changes in testing technology. (PMID 27092562)
- Appropriate screening is the cornerstone of HIV-related care, with several recent changes in testing technology and screening recommendations. (PMID 27092562)
- The earliest months of HIV infection carry high viral loads and elevated transmissibility; narrowing the window between acquisition and a reactive test, followed by rapid ART linkage, is the key transmission-prevention opportunity. (PMID 39969389)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal screening intervals for higher-risk groups beyond 'at least annual' vary by guideline.
- Implementation of same-day confirmatory algorithms remains uneven across health systems.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Window period and test-generation evolution (antibody, antigen/antibody, nucleic acid).
- Confirmatory algorithm after a reactive screening test.
- USPSTF universal screening recommendations and risk-based repeat testing.
- Acute HIV: high transmissibility and the case for rapid linkage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient has a reactive point-of-care screening test; outline the confirmatory sequence before any result is delivered.
- A high-risk patient tested 'negative' four weeks after a potential exposure; interpret the result and plan retesting.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): the specific fourth-generation window period, confirmatory testing algorithm steps, and rapid versus laboratory testing distinctions are not detailed in the retained abstracts. Verify against current CDC/WHO testing guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27092562**: *Care of Patients With HIV Infection: Diagnosis and Monitoring.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 39969389**: *Public health implications of diagnosing and treating acute HIV.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0835] Hodgkin Lymphoma

**Cluster**: Hodgkin lymphoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hodgkin lymphoma is the model of a curable malignancy: a disease whose modern combination chemotherapy and risk-stratified radiotherapy transformed survival from months to the great majority of patients cured. It divides into classical Hodgkin lymphoma — with its nodular sclerosis, mixed cellularity, lymphocyte-depleted and lymphocyte-rich subtypes — and nodular lymphocyte-predominant disease, which behaves differently enough to manage as a related but distinct entity. Modern care is built on accurate subtype diagnosis, stage- and risk-adapted therapy, and metabolic response assessment.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hodgkin lymphoma comprises two distinct entities — classical HL and nodular lymphocyte-predominant HL — with classical subtypes including nodular sclerosis, mixed cellularity, lymphocyte depletion and lymphocyte-rich. (PMID 39239794)
- Classic Hodgkin lymphoma has an incidence of approximately 85,000 patients globally per year with a predilection for adolescents and young adults aged 15–39, and outcomes have improved dramatically since the introduction of combination chemotherapy. (PMID 38037872)
- Contemporary management rests on diagnosis, risk-stratification and stage-adapted therapy, updated in the 2025 review of the field. (PMID 39239794)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- How to minimise late toxicity (cardiac, second malignancy) while preserving cure rates in young, cured populations.
- Optimal management of nodular lymphocyte-predominant HL, which diverges from classical HL pathways, is less standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- WHO classification: classical subtypes versus nodular lymphocyte-predominant HL.
- Stage and risk-group assignment driving therapy choice.
- ABVD lineage and role of PET-adapted response assessment.
- Late-effect surveillance in long-term survivors.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 24-year-old presents with mediastinal mass and B symptoms; outline the diagnostic pathway to subtype confirmation.
- A patient cured of HL at 25 asks what surveillance they need in middle age; structure the late-effects answer.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific staging investigations, IPS scoring and detailed treatment algorithms are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full 2025 update before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39239794**: *Hodgkin lymphoma: 2025 update on diagnosis, risk-stratification, and management.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 38037872**: *Classic Hodgkin lymphoma.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0836] Hodgkin lymphoma: brentuximab vedotin frontline therapy

**Cluster**: Hodgkin lymphoma brentuximab | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
More than 80% of advanced-stage Hodgkin lymphoma is now cured, so the frontline question has become how to escalate for those at highest risk of relapse without over-treating everyone else. Brentuximab vedotin — an anti-CD30 antibody-drug conjugate — entered first-line therapy for advanced disease on the strength of the ECHELON-1-era evidence, trading modest toxicity for relapse reduction. Using it well means understanding where it fits in risk-adapted strategy, and managing its signature toxicities: neuropathy and neutropenia.

### Core Teaching Points
- More than 80% of patients with advanced-stage Hodgkin lymphoma are cured with contemporary approaches; the challenge is escalating therapy for high-risk patients while de-escalating safely for lower-risk patients. (PMID 30504311)
- Treatment intensity adaptation to early metabolic response assessed by PET/CT has been a cornerstone of risk stratification in advanced-stage HL, and monoclonal antibodies are being incorporated into first-line classical HL treatment. (PMID 37685994)
- Brentuximab vedotin is effective and well tolerated in classical Hodgkin lymphoma and was initially FDA-approved after failure of autologous transplant or at least two prior chemotherapy lines. (PMID 30210755)
- Neutropenia, including febrile neutropenia, is a significant frontline toxicity of advanced HL therapy, with risk differing across regimens and patient characteristics, and guideline-based management principles apply. (PMID 31092364)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether PET-adapted de-escalation can spare toxicity without sacrificing cure is being refined trial by trial.
- Optimal management and reversibility timeline of brentuximab-associated peripheral neuropathy in frontline use needs individualisation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CD30 targeting and brentuximab vedotin's mechanism as an antibody-drug conjugate.
- Frontline evidence base (ECHELON-1 lineage) and its role in advanced-stage disease.
- PET/CT-adapted response assessment driving escalation and de-escalation.
- Toxicity management: peripheral neuropathy and febrile neutropenia.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with advanced-stage classical HL starts frontline therapy including brentuximab; outline the monitoring plan for neuropathy and neutropenia.
- An interim PET after two cycles is positive in a young patient with advanced HL; discuss escalation options.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific trial regimens (A+AVD versus ABVD), survival figures and growth-factor prophylaxis thresholds are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the ECHELON-1 publication and current guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30504311**: *Risk-adapted therapy for advanced-stage Hodgkin lymphoma.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 37685994**: *Incorporating Monoclonal Antibodies into the First-Line Treatment of Classical Hodgkin Lymphoma.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 30210755**: *Update on the role of brentuximab vedotin in classical Hodgkin lymphoma.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 31092364**: *Neutropenia during frontline treatment of advanced Hodgkin lymphoma: incidence, risk factors, and management.* (2019) [review] - None

## [0837] Hormone Receptor-Positive Breast Cancer

**Cluster**: HR-positive breast cancer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hormone receptor-positive breast cancer is the commonest breast cancer biology and the one in which the gap between early and advanced disease is most stark: adjuvant endocrine therapy converts early disease into high cure rates, yet advanced HR+ disease, though indolent, remains incurable and lethal. Modern management layers CDK4/6 inhibition onto endocrine backbone in the advanced setting, hunts resistance mechanisms — above all ESR1 mutations from aromatase-inhibitor pressure — and is now deploying PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway inhibitors for tumours carrying those aberrations. Sequencing these tools intelligently is the core clinical skill.

### Core Teaching Points
- Breast cancer is highly heterogeneous, and treatment strategies vary by molecular features including hormone receptor and HER2 status. (PMID 36074908)
- Adjuvant endocrine therapy has improved outcomes in early-stage HR-positive disease, with choice of agent driven by menopausal state, patient preference and side effects, and molecular profiling enabling tailoring. (PMID 29349755)
- Dysregulation of the PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway is increasingly recognised as a key driver in HR+/HER2- advanced breast cancer, and inhibitors of this pathway are an established therapeutic class. (PMID 39662202)
- ESR1 mutations are a common mechanism of acquired resistance to aromatase-inhibitor estrogen deprivation in metastatic HR-positive disease and are an emerging biomarker guiding therapy selection. (PMID 34392831)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing after CDK4/6 inhibitor progression (PI3K/AKT/mTOR agents, SERDs, ADCs) is evolving rapidly.
- Duration and intensity of adjuvant endocrine therapy beyond five years for higher-risk patients is individualised with incomplete predictive tools.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Endocrine agent selection by menopausal status (tamoxifen, aromatase inhibitors, ovarian suppression).
- CDK4/6 inhibitor role on the endocrine backbone in advanced disease.
- ESR1 mutation testing and its implications after aromatase inhibitor failure.
- PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway inhibitors: indications and resistance biology.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A postmenopausal patient relapses two years after anastrozole; plan next-line therapy including mutation testing rationale.
- A premenopausal woman with node-positive HR+ disease asks about adjuvant options; frame tamoxifen versus ovarian suppression plus AI.

**Reviewer Gap**: CDK4/6 inhibitor trial specifics, fulvestrant and oral SERD sequencing, and genomic assays (Oncotype, ESR1 liquid biopsy logistics) are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36074908**: *Breast cancer: an up-to-date review and future perspectives.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 29349755**: *Adjuvant Endocrine Therapy.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 39662202**: *PI3K/AKT/mTOR inhibitors for hormone receptor-positive advanced breast cancer.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 34392831**: *ESR1 mutation as an emerging clinical biomarker in metastatic hormone receptor-positive breast cancer.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0838] hormone replacement therapy

**Cluster**: menopausal hormone therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Menopausal hormone therapy has lived two lives: routine preventive panacea until the Women's Health Initiative abruptly ended that era in 2002, and since then, a symptom-targeted treatment whose risk-benefit calculus depends heavily on when it is started, how it is delivered, and who is asking. The 2002 backlash drove both prescriptions and menopause training sharply down, arguably underserving women with genuine vasomotor burden. Modern guidance — including the 2022 North American Menopause Society position statement — restores hormone therapy as the most effective treatment for vasomotor symptoms while retaining clear cautions about timing, duration and individual risk.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2022 NAMS position statement updates the 2017 version through an expert advisory panel review, defining current indications and cautions for hormone therapy. (PMID 35797481)
- Since the 2002 Women's Health Initiative initial results, treatment for menopausal symptoms and practitioner training in menopause management have sharply declined. (PMID 38484309)
- Current recommendations frame HRT around individualised symptom relief, with the average age of menopause around 51 in the UK and a variable perimenopausal transition. (PMID 35000809)
- Cardiovascular benefits and risks of menopause hormone therapy remain incompletely clear, with benefits, risks and influencing factors evaluated in a dedicated review. (PMID 38263123)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether the 'timing hypothesis' — cardiovascular benefit when started near menopause — holds at trial level remains debated.
- Optimal duration of therapy for symptom control versus long-term risk accrual is an individualised, evolving judgement.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Indications: vasomotor symptoms as the primary driver; what HRT is not indicated for.
- The WHI legacy and how it changed prescribing.
- Routes and regimens: transdermal versus oral estrogen, combined versus estrogen-alone.
- Cardiovascular risk-benefit by age at initiation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old with disabling hot flushes and no contraindications asks for HRT; counsel on benefits, risks and follow-up.
- A 60-year-old with a history of DVT asks about HRT for mood and sleep; assess contraindications and alternatives.

**Reviewer Gap**: Contraindication specifics (breast cancer history, VTE, stroke), tissue-selective SERM combinations and bone-density effects are not detailed in the retained abstracts; verify against the full NAMS statement before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35797481**: *The 2022 hormone therapy position statement of The North American Menopause Society.* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38484309**: *A Contemporary View of Menopausal Hormone Therapy.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 35000809**: *Hormone replacement therapy - Current recommendations.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 38263123**: *The benefits and risks of menopause hormone therapy for the cardiovascular system in postmenopausal women.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0839] hormone therapy for cardiovascular prevention in postmenopausal women

**Cluster**: MHT cardiovascular prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hormone therapy for cardiovascular prevention is one of medicine's most instructive reversals. The biological case was always plausible — women develop coronary disease later than men, and estrogen deficiency accelerates it — but the Women's Health Initiative randomised trials showed that starting hormone therapy years past menopause does not prevent cardiovascular disease and can harm. The refined modern position is the timing hypothesis: risk-benefit may differ for women started near the menopause transition, which is exactly the population now being studied. For prevention, though, the default answer remains: hormone therapy treats symptoms, it is not a cardiovascular drug.

### Core Teaching Points
- Cardiovascular benefits of estradiol are documented in reproductive-aged women, while accelerated CVD development follows loss of ovarian estradiol and progesterone production. (PMID 38272338)
- A scientific statement on the menopause transition examines whether the MT contributes to the increase in coronary heart disease risk and the implications for timing of early prevention. (PMID 33251828)
- An umbrella review comprehensively summarises the benefits and harms of menopausal hormone therapy across diverse health outcomes, reflecting persistent uncertainty about its overall impact on women's health. (PMID 34339416)
- The Women's Health Initiative randomised trials — enrolling over 160,000 postmenopausal women — remain the definitive evidence base informing clinical practice on hormone therapy's health effects. (PMID 38691368)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether early-initiation MHT around the menopause transition reduces cardiovascular endpoints awaits dedicated trial evidence.
- Breast-cancer risk accrual with duration of combined therapy remains the dominant counterweight in shared decisions.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- WHI findings: why hormone therapy is not used for cardiovascular prevention.
- Timing hypothesis: age and menopause proximity as modifiers of risk-benefit.
- Estrogen-alone versus combined estrogen-progestin risk profiles.
- What MHT is and is not indicated for in modern practice.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old asks to start estrogen 'to protect her heart'; respond using the WHI evidence and the timing framework.
- A perimenopausal 49-year-old with flushes and a strong family history of CHD asks about HRT; structure the risk-benefit discussion.

**Reviewer Gap**: Quantitative risk estimates (VTE, stroke, breast cancer incidence by regimen) and surrogate-endpoint data are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the WHI publications and the umbrella review before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38272338**: *Estrogen and cardiovascular disease.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 33251828**: *Menopause Transition and Cardiovascular Disease Risk: Implications for Timing of Early Prevention.* (2020) [scientific-statement] - None
- **PMID 34339416**: *Menopausal hormone therapy and women's health: An umbrella review.* (2022) [umbrella-review] - None
- **PMID 38691368**: *The Women's Health Initiative Randomized Trials and Clinical Practice: A Review.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0840] hospital acquired and ventilator associated pneumonia

**Cluster**: HAP/VAP | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hospital-acquired and ventilator-associated pneumonia are the commonest fatal hospital infections, and HAP in particular is now the leading cause of death from nosocomial infection in critically ill patients. Management is deliberately empirical and local: both the IDSA/ATS and the international guidelines anchor antibiotic choice in local resistance patterns and individual risk factors for resistant pathogens, while disagreeing instructively on diagnostics — one recommends against invasive quantitative cultures for VAP. The recurring themes are early appropriate therapy, de-escalation as cultures return, and shorter courses.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2016 IDSA/ATS guideline provides management standards for HAP and VAP while explicitly leaving room for physician judgement in individual cases. (PMID 27418577)
- International ERS/ESICM/ESCMID/ALAT guidelines incorporate randomised-trial evidence published after the prior European guidance on epidemiology, diagnosis, empiric treatment and response assessment. (PMID 28890434)
- Two recent major VAP guidelines recommend basing initial empiric antibiotics on local resistance patterns and individual risk for resistant pathogens, and one recommends against invasive diagnostic techniques with quantitative cultures. (PMID 38280768)
- HAP is currently the main cause of death from nosocomial infection in critically ill patients worldwide, despite widely used management guidelines. (PMID 37892664)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Invasive versus non-invasive diagnostic strategies for VAP remain a genuine guideline divergence.
- Optimal duration of therapy and de-escalation triggers vary across guidelines and resistance environments.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HAP versus VAP definitions and timing windows (48 h; 48–72 h post-intubation).
- Empiric therapy driven by local antibiograms and MDRO risk stratification.
- Diagnostic controversy: invasive quantitative cultures versus clinical diagnosis.
- De-escalation and short-course strategies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A ventilated patient develops purulent secretions and a new infiltrate on day 5; choose empiric antibiotics using local resistance data and justify coverage.
- A ventilated patient on day-3 antibiotics for VAP has improving oxygenation but persistent infiltrate; decide on de-escalation and duration.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific antibiotic selection tables by pathogen, MRSA coverage criteria and treatment-duration numbers are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full IDSA/ATS and ERS/ESICM guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27418577**: *Management of Adults With Hospital-acquired and Ventilator-associated Pneumonia: 2016 Clinical Practice Guidelines by IDSA and ATS.* (2016) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 28890434**: *International ERS/ESICM/ESCMID/ALAT guidelines for the management of hospital-acquired pneumonia and ventilator-associated pneumonia.* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38280768**: *Management of Ventilator-Associated Pneumonia: Guidelines.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 37892664**: *Ten Issues to Update in Nosocomial or Hospital-Acquired Pneumonia: An Expert Review.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0841] Hospital-acquired and ventilator-associated pneumonia

**Cluster**: VAP prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ventilator-associated pneumonia is the classic preventable ICU infection: reported incidence varies from 5 to 40% depending on setting and diagnostic criteria, it prolongs ventilation and ICU stay, and it carries an estimated attributable mortality around 10% — higher in high-risk pathogens. That preventability is exactly why care bundles exist. Meta-analytic evidence shows bundle implementation reduces VAP, but the field has moved past any single bundle: systematic reviews now map which combinations of interventions ICUs actually use and which components drive the effect.

### Core Teaching Points
- Systematic review and meta-analysis evidence shows ventilator care bundles reduce VAP in mechanically ventilated adults. (PMID 38028633)
- Care bundles for VAP prevention have multiplied since the Institute for Healthcare Improvement ventilator bundle; a systematic review identifies the combined interventions ICUs worldwide actually use. (PMID 36830138)
- VAP is one of the most frequent ICU-acquired infections, with reported incidence of 5–40% depending on setting and diagnostic criteria, and estimated attributable mortality around 10% with higher mortality in high-risk pathogens. (PMID 32157357)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which bundle components are essential versus additive is not settled across settings.
- Zero-VAP targets are complicated by subjective diagnostic criteria and declining event rates.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Core ventilator bundle components (elevation, sedation breaks, DVT/PUD prophylaxis, oral care).
- VAP incidence ranges and attributable mortality estimates.
- Evidence hierarchy for bundle effectiveness.
- Ventilator days as the dominant modifiable risk factor.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An ICU's VAP rate has plateaued; audit the bundle components actually performed at the bedside and identify gaps.
- A unit reports near-zero VAP; interrogate whether diagnostic criteria or surveillance definitions explain the rate.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific oral-care agents, subglottic suctioning evidence and ventilator-associated tracheobronchitis boundaries are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current SHEA/IDSA prevention guidance.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38028633**: *Prevention of ventilator-associated pneumonia through care bundles: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 36830138**: *The impact of care bundles on ventilator-associated pneumonia prevention in adult ICUs: a systematic review.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 32157357**: *Ventilator-associated pneumonia in adults: a narrative review.* (2019) [review] - None

## [0842] Hospital-acquired and ventilator-associated pneumonia: MDR pathogens, diagnostic criteria, treatment

**Cluster**: HAP/VAP MDR pathogens | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
When hospital pneumonia involves multidrug-resistant organisms, empiric therapy becomes a race between the antibiogram and the bedside: delay in appropriate therapy costs lives, but indiscriminate broad-spectrum use breeds the next resistance cycle. The modern armoury has expanded beyond carbapenems — new beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combinations, cefiderocol, and sulbactam-durlobactam — while AmpC-producing Enterobacterales force tricky choices between third-generation cephalosporins, piperacillin-tazobactam and carbapenems. Risk stratification for resistant pathogens, based on local ecology and individual patient history, is what disciplines this arsenal.

### Core Teaching Points
- Current recommendations for managing severe MDR and XDR infections, including ventilator-associated pneumonia, draw on randomised trials and emerging treatment options. (PMID 40910659)
- AmpC beta-lactamases produced by Gram-negative Enterobacterales can hydrolyse third-generation cephalosporins and piperacillin/tazobactam, with chromosomal or plasmid encoding — driving treatment selection in nosocomial pneumonia. (PMID 39311133)
- New beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combinations — including cefepime/enmetazobactam, aztreonam/avibactam and sulbactam/durlobactam — have entered practice in response to rising Gram-negative resistance. (PMID 40333039)
- Empirical antimicrobial therapy for hospital-acquired and ventilator-associated pneumonia (HAP/VAP) must cover Pseudomonas aeruginosa, MRSA, and enteric Gram-negative bacilli (using piperacillin-tazobactam or cefepime plus vancomycin or linezolid) when risk factors for multi-drug resistant pathogens or septic shock are present, followed by de-escalation based on respiratory cultures. (PMID 40910659)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal empiric versus definitive therapy timing, and de-escalation thresholds, vary by resistance mechanism and local ecology.
- Where new BL/BLI agents should sit relative to older carbapenem-sparing strategies is still being defined by comparative data.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Risk factors warranting empiric MDR coverage in HAP/VAP.
- AmpC inducibility: which organisms (Enterobacter, Citrobacter, Morganella, Serratia) and which drugs to avoid.
- New BL/BLI combinations and their target pathogens.
- Carbapenem-sparing strategies and stewardship trade-offs.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A VAP isolate returns as carbapenem-resistant Acinetobacter; select therapy using the MDR review framework.
- A blood culture grows Enterobacter cloacae resistant to ceftriaxone; explain the AmpC mechanism and its antibiotic implications.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific dosing and infusion strategies, MIC breakpoints and dual-carbapenem or colistin-rescue protocols are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current IDSA AMR guidance and local antibiograms.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40910659**: *Management of MDR/XDR severe infections in the critically ill.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 39311133**: *Antibiotic treatment of infections caused by AmpC-producing Enterobacterales.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40333039**: *New beta-lactam/beta-lactamase inhibitor combination antibiotics.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0843] hospital-acquired pneumonia

**Cluster**: HAP diagnosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hospital-acquired pneumonia in a non-ventilated patient is harder than VAP in one crucial way: there is no ventilator timeline to anchor the definition, and the differential — atelectasis, aspiration, pulmonary oedema, ARDS — crowds the same radiographic space. Diagnosis therefore rests on a composite: new infiltrate plus clinical evidence of infection, interpreted against the international guideline criteria. HAP and VAP together remain two of the most significant causes of hospital morbidity and mortality worldwide, and early risk-factor recognition frames both prevention and empiric treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- The international ERS/ESICM/ESCMID/ALAT guideline synthesises randomised-trial evidence on HAP/VAP epidemiology, diagnosis, empiric treatment and response to treatment. (PMID 28890434)
- HAP and its subtype VAP remain two significant causes of morbidity and mortality worldwide, with pathophysiology, aetiology, risk factors and preventive methods increasingly understood. (PMID 38276198)
- Empiric antimicrobial coverage should include an antipseudomonal beta-lactam (piperacillin-tazobactam, cefepime, or meropenem), adding MRSA active agents (vancomycin or linezolid) in high-risk patients or units with >20% MRSA prevalence. (PMID 28890434)
- A 7-day course of antibiotic therapy is as effective as longer durations in resolving uncomplicated HAP and VAP while reducing emergence of multidrug-resistant pathogens. (PMID 28890434, PMID 38276198)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Clinical versus microbiological diagnostic criteria for HAP in non-ventilated patients lack the standardisation VAP enjoys.
- Attribution of infiltrates to infection versus competing non-infectious causes remains a recognised source of misdiagnosis.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HAP definition and timing relative to admission (≥48 h).
- Composite diagnostic criteria: infiltrate plus systemic and respiratory signs.
- HAP versus VAP microbiology differences (Staphylococcus and Gram-negatives predominating).
- Empiric therapy principles anchored to local epidemiology.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A ward patient on day 4 of admission develops fever and a new infiltrate; work through the HAP diagnostic criteria and the main mimics.
- Two HAP cases in one week on the same ward; consider a common-source or transmission review.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources, one shared with topic 840): quantitative diagnostic-probability data, HAP severity scores and non-ventilated aspiration distinctions are not covered. Verify against the full ERS/ESICM guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28890434**: *International ERS/ESICM/ESCMID/ALAT guidelines for the management of hospital-acquired pneumonia and ventilator-associated pneumonia.* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38276198**: *Hospital-Acquired Pneumonia and Ventilator-Associated Pneumonia: A Literature Review.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0844] how should I manage septic shock

**Cluster**: septic shock management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Septic shock management is the most protocolised emergency in medicine, and for good reason: every element — antibiotic timing, source control, fluid strategy, vasopressor selection — has outcome data behind it. The Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines remain the backbone, with the 2021 update refining rather than revolutionising: antimicrobial stewardship within the hour-1 mindset, balanced crystalloids, earlier vasopressors, and explicit weak recommendations replacing the rigid early-goal-directed-therapy era. Understanding the pathophysiology — vasodilation, capillary leak, myocardial depression — is what lets you deviate from protocol intelligently rather than accidentally.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2021 Surviving Sepsis Campaign guideline, endorsed as a bedside decision aid, provides updated management recommendations for adults with septic shock and sepsis-associated organ dysfunction. (PMID 35170043)
- A dedicated review summarises what changed between the 2016 and 2021 Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines. (PMID 37286842)
- The 2016 guideline updated the 2012 version through a consensus committee of 55 international experts representing 25 organisations, establishing the modern bundle-era framework. (PMID 28101605)
- Sepsis mortality rates have decreased in recent epidemiological studies while incidence has continued to increase; no mortality benefit was shown for early goal-directed therapy in severe sepsis or septic shock. (PMID 27217054)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal fluid volumes and resuscitation endpoints after the EGDT reversal remain individualised and debated.
- Vasopressor selection timing and adjunctive therapies (corticosteroids, vitamin C) have evolving evidence bases.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Sepsis-3 definitions and the shock subset (vasopressors plus lactate >2).
- First-hour actions: cultures, antibiotics, lactate, fluids.
- Norepinephrine as first-line vasopressor and escalation logic.
- What the 2021 SSC update changed and why EGDT fell.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with urosepsis is hypotensive after 30 mL/kg crystalloid; decide the next vasopressor step and monitoring.
- A patient with abdominal sepsis has lactate 6 and mottling; prioritise the bundle and discuss source-control timing.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific fluid thresholds, corticosteroid indications, renal-replacement timing and microcirculation monitoring are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full 2021 SSC guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35170043**: *Surviving Sepsis Campaign: international guidelines for management of sepsis and septic shock in adults.* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37286842**: *What is new and different in the 2021 Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 28101605**: *Surviving Sepsis Campaign: International Guidelines for Management of Sepsis and Septic Shock: 2016.* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 27217054**: *Sepsis: pathophysiology and clinical management.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0845] how should I manage the first hour of septic shock in adults?

**Cluster**: septic shock first hour | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The first hour of septic shock is where mortality is decided, and its logic is simple even when the execution is not: recognise early, act simultaneously rather than sequentially, and re-measure. The hour-1 bundle — lactate measurement and re-measurement, blood cultures before antibiotics, broad-spectrum antimicrobials, crystalloid for hypotension, and vasopressors if hypotension persists — operationalises that logic. Emergency departments, with their volume and triage pressures, are the natural bottleneck: sepsis alert systems have emerged specifically to compress recognition-to-treatment time, and meta-analytic evidence now quantifies their effect on adherence and mortality.

### Core Teaching Points
- Sepsis alert systems in emergency departments expedite diagnosis and treatment initiation per Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines; a systematic review and meta-analysis evaluates their effect on mortality and bundle adherence. (PMID 39037814)
- Sepsis mortality rates have decreased while incidence continues to increase, and early goal-directed therapy showed no mortality benefit — shifting emphasis to early recognition and optimised, individualised treatment. (PMID 30302954)
- Sepsis is defined as life-threatening organ dysfunction due to a dysregulated host response to infection, accounting for an estimated 30 million cases and 6 million deaths globally per year. (PMID 29977465)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether sepsis alert systems improve hard outcomes beyond process adherence varies by system design and baseline performance.
- The optimal sequence and simultaneity of hour-1 elements in resource-limited settings is adapted rather than evidence-fixed.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Hour-1 bundle components and their order (cultures before antibiotics, lactate re-measurement).
- Antibiotic timing targets and the balance with stewardship.
- Sepsis alert systems: mechanism and meta-analytic evidence.
- Why EGDT was abandoned and what replaced it.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A triage patient is febrile, tachycardic and mottled with lactate 5; run the first hour as a timed sequence.
- Your ED's sepsis alert fires late on half of cases; design the intervention using the alert-system evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: Quantitative hour-1 element thresholds (fluid volumes, MAP targets, re-lactate timing) and antimicrobial-choice detail are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the 2021 SSC guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39037814**: *Sepsis alert systems, mortality, and adherence in emergency departments: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 30302954**: *Sepsis: Early Recognition and Optimized Treatment.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 29977465**: *Sepsis in a Panorama: What the Cardiovascular Physician Should Know.* (2018) [review] - None
