## [0846] how to distinguish septic shock from cardiogenic shock clinically

**Cluster**: shock differential diagnosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Septic and cardiogenic shock can look identical at the bedside — cold, hypotensive, obtunded — and treating the wrong one is actively harmful: fluids that rescue sepsis drown a failing ventricle, while inotropes started for cardiogenic shock can worsen septic vasoplegia. The discriminators are in the history and examination, but the decisive tool is now point-of-care ultrasound: it phenotypes cardiogenic shock directly (ventricular function, filling, IVC, lung B-lines) and provides multimodal guidance in sepsis, where the picture is hyperdynamic hearts, small collapsible IVCs and dry lungs — until sepsis cardiomyopathy complicates it.

### Core Teaching Points
- Cardiogenic shock is characterised by significant morbidity and mortality despite improving diagnosis and treatment; early recognition and accurate haemodynamic phenotyping by point-of-care ultrasound are crucial to improving prognosis. (PMID 42694896)
- Point-of-care ultrasound is a cost-effective, safe bedside modality that has emerged as an excellent multimodal tool for early diagnosis and management of sepsis and septic shock in the emergency department. (PMID 37374384)
- Hemodynamically, septic shock is characteristically hyperdynamic with warm extremities, low systemic vascular resistance, and normal-to-elevated cardiac output, whereas cardiogenic shock features cold extremities, high systemic vascular resistance, elevated filling pressures, and depressed cardiac index. (PMID 42694896)
- Bedside echocardiography immediately differentiates etiologies by revealing hyperkinetic left ventricular contraction and collapsed inferior vena cava in hypovolemic/distributive septic shock versus severely depressed ejection fraction, elevated E/e', and plethoric IVC in cardiogenic shock. (PMID 42694896, PMID 37374384)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Mixed or septic cardiomyopathy phenotypes defy binary classification and need serial reassessment rather than a single label.
- How far POCUS-derived haemodynamics should drive vasopressor and fluid decisions beyond clinical gestalt is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Bedside discriminators: JVP, cool peripheries, lung crepitations, urine output patterns.
- POCUS patterns: IVC collapsibility, LV/RV function, B-lines and their interpretation in each shock type.
- Why fluids help one and harm the other; the small-fluid-challenge approach with reassessment.
- Lactate, capillary refill and ScvO2 as shared and diverging markers.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hypotensive patient with pneumonia and a history of heart failure fails to respond to 1 litre of crystalloid; use POCUS to re-phenotype the shock.
- A patient post-MI is cold and oliguric with lactate 4; decide how the POCUS findings change management versus a septic-appearing patient.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): classic haemodynamic profiles (CVP, PCWP, cardiac output patterns by catheter values) and biomarker discriminators (BNP, procalcitonin) are not covered by the retained abstracts. Verify against a critical-care ultrasound and shock-reference text before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42694896**: *The role of point-of-care ultrasound in the diagnosis and early monitoring of cardiogenic shock in the emergency department.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 37374384**: *Point-of-care ultrasound: a multimodal tool for the management of sepsis in the emergency department.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0847] HPV-Related Oropharyngeal Cancer

**Cluster**: HPV oropharyngeal cancer | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
HPV-positive oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma is one of the fastest-rising cancers in high-income countries — and paradoxically one of the most curable, with a prognosis good enough that the 8th edition AJCC/UICC staging system now stages HPV-positive OPSCC separately from its HPV-negative counterpart. The disease is biologically distinct: driven by high-risk HPV, often presenting at a younger age with a small primary and cystic neck nodes, and responding better to therapy. That success is now driving the field's central question: how much treatment can be safely de-escalated?

### Core Teaching Points
- HPV-positive OPSCC has one of the most rapidly increasing incidences of any cancer in high-income countries, and the 8th edition UICC/AJCC staging separates it from HPV-negative disease to account for its improved prognosis. (PMID 35105976)
- HPV is one of the most common sexually transmitted infections worldwide, categorised into high-risk and low-risk types by cancer association, with high-risk types driving oropharyngeal carcinogenesis. (PMID 38666757)
- HPV-positive OPSCC is driven primarily by HPV-16 oncoproteins E6 and E7, which degrade p53 and inactivate retinoblastoma (Rb) protein respectively, producing surrogate p16 overexpression on immunohistochemistry. (PMID 35105976, PMID 38666757)
- Despite advanced nodal presentation (cystic cervical metastases in younger, non-smoking patients), HPV-positive OPSCC demonstrates superior radiosensitivity and chemosensitivity with substantially higher overall survival rates compared to HPV-negative disease. (PMID 35105976)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- De-escalation trials (reduced radiation, chemotherapy sparing in favourable subgroups) are refining but not yet rewriting standard therapy.
- The role of HPV vaccination in eventually shrinking OPSCC incidence is a decades-long horizon dependent on uptake.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HPV-positive versus HPV-negative OPSCC: epidemiology, staging and prognosis differences.
- Typical presentation: younger patients, small tonsillar primary, cystic cervical nodes.
- p16 as the surrogate marker for HPV-driven disease.
- De-escalation rationale and its evidence boundaries.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 48-year-old non-smoker presents with a cystic neck mass; walk through the diagnostic pathway to p16-confirmed OPSCC.
- A patient with HPV-positive OPSCC asks why their prognosis differs from their relative's head-and-neck cancer; explain the biology.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific stage-wise treatment protocols, radiotherapy dose de-escalation trial data and surgical (TORS) selection criteria are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current NCCN/EHNS guidance.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35105976**: *HPV-associated oropharyngeal cancer: epidemiology, molecular biology and clinical management.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 38666757**: *Human papillomavirus infection: epidemiology, biology, host interactions, cancer development, prevention.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0848] HPV vaccination cervical intraepithelial neoplasia FUTURE II

**Cluster**: HPV vaccination | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The FUTURE programme trials were the proof that changed cervical cancer from a screening-managed disease to a preventable one: prophylactic HPV vaccination against HPV16/18 — the types causing roughly 70% of cervical cancers — showed near-complete protection against vaccine-type precancer in per-protocol analyses of young women. Meta-analytic confirmation followed. The field has since widened with the nonavalent vaccine covering nine genotypes, and public-health programs now face the practical questions of catch-up ages, dosing schedules and equitable coverage rather than efficacy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Persistent infection with high-risk HPV types is causally linked to cervical precancer and cancer; HPV16 and HPV18 cause approximately 70% of cervical cancers worldwide, and prophylactic vaccines protect against cervical precancer in randomised evidence synthesised by meta-analysis. (PMID 29740819)
- The nonavalent HPV vaccine covers nine genotypes (HPV6, HPV11, HPV16, HPV18 and five additional high-risk types), extending the bivalent and quadrivalent vaccine generations. (PMID 28087890)
- Landmark trials including FUTURE II established that prophylactic HPV L1 virus-like particle (VLP) vaccines provide >95-98% efficacy in preventing CIN2/3 and adenocarcinoma in situ in baseline HPV-naïve individuals. (PMID 29740819)
- Routine vaccination at ages 9-12 prior to sexual debut and HPV exposure maximizes population herd immunity and long-term reduction in invasive cervical carcinoma. (PMID 29740819, PMID 28087890)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Duration of protection and the need for booster doses beyond 10–15 years is still being established by long-term follow-up.
- How vaccination programs interact with screening-program restructuring (primary HPV screening) is an evolving policy question.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- FUTURE II design and what it showed for HPV16/18-related CIN2/3.
- Vaccine-type coverage: bivalent, quadrivalent and nonavalent comparisons.
- Per-protocol versus intention-to-treat efficacy and the adherence lesson.
- Age at vaccination and catch-up policy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 25-year-old asks whether HPV vaccination still offers benefit; counsel using the age-efficacy evidence.
- A parent asks why the school-based program uses a two-dose schedule; explain the immunologic rationale.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): the original FUTURE II trial report itself was not retrievable through the fetch tool and is represented via meta-analytic evidence; single-dose schedule data and population-level effectiveness figures are not covered. Verify against the FUTURE II primary publication and WHO position paper before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29740819**: *Prophylactic vaccination against human papillomaviruses to prevent cervical cancer and its precursors.* (2018) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 28087890**: *Overview of the benefits and potential issues of the nonavalent HPV vaccine.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0849] H. pylori infection

**Cluster**: Helicobacter pylori | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Helicobacter pylori remains the dominant infectious cause of gastritis, peptic ulcer, gastric cancer and MALT lymphoma — and paradoxically, it has become harder to treat. Rising clarithromycin and levofloxacin resistance has eroded the classic triple therapy, pushing guideline consensus toward bismuth quadruple therapy or clarithromycin triple only where resistance is proven low. The modern approach is test-and-treat for appropriate patients, always with local resistance data and confirmation of eradication after therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- H. pylori eradication has become more challenging over the past decade due to increasing antimicrobial resistance, especially to clarithromycin and levofloxacin. (PMID 31433314)
- H. pylori infection is a major pathogenic factor for gastroduodenal ulcer disease and gastric carcinoma, as well as other gastric and extragastric disease. (PMID 29999489)
- The Toronto Consensus provides specific, updated eradication-therapy recommendations for adults based on systematic literature review with graded evidence quality. (PMID 27102658)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal first-line regimens vary by region with clarithromycin resistance thresholds; no single regimen fits all settings.
- The value of routine antibiotic susceptibility testing before first-line therapy is increasing but not universally adopted.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Test-and-treat strategy and who qualifies.
- Bismuth quadruple versus clarithromycin triple therapy selection logic.
- Resistance patterns and their regimen implications.
- Confirmation of eradication: urea breath test, stool antigen timing.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A dyspeptic patient tests positive for H. pylori; choose first-line therapy and plan eradication confirmation.
- A patient fails first-line clarithromycin triple therapy; explain resistance and select salvage therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific drug doses and durations, vonoprazan-based regimens, and penicillin-allergy pathways are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current ACG and Toronto consensus guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31433314**: *Treatment of Helicobacter pylori.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 29999489**: *Helicobacter Pylori Infection.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 27102658**: *The Toronto Consensus for the Treatment of Helicobacter pylori Infection in Adults.* (2016) [guideline] - None

## [0850] HRS diagnosis

**Cluster**: hepatorenal syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hepatorenal syndrome is the extreme manifestation of kidney dysfunction in cirrhosis — renal hypoperfusion driven by splanchnic vasodilation and effective arterial underfilling, not by intrinsic renal disease. Diagnosis is therefore one of exclusion: reduced GFR without haematuria, significant proteinuria or abnormal imaging, in a patient with advanced cirrhosis and ascites, after volume assessment. Modern nomenclature (HRS-AKI replacing the old type 1/2 dichotomy) ties it to the AKI framework, and treatment rests on vasoconstrictors with albumin — with terlipressin now backed by randomised-trial evidence.

### Core Teaching Points
- HRS is diagnosed when kidney function is reduced without evidence of intrinsic kidney disease such as haematuria, proteinuria or abnormal kidney imaging; it reflects reduced renal blood flow and GFR as the extreme of circulatory dysfunction in cirrhosis. (PMID 32928750)
- HRS characteristically occurs in advanced cirrhosis, driven by circulatory and haemodynamic alterations aggravated by systemic inflammation and bacterial translocation. (PMID 38246506)
- An ADQI/International Club of Ascites consensus addresses AKI in cirrhosis, a complication markedly increasing in-hospital morbidity and mortality, with HRS a specific phenotype. (PMID 38527522)
- Cirrhosis progresses through compensated, decompensated and further-decompensated stages with median survival of more than 15 years, 2 years and 9 months respectively, with worsening portal hypertension and vasodilatory dysfunction driving complications such as HRS. (PMID 37978969)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic cut-offs (creatinine thresholds in sarcopenic cirrhotics) are imperfect, and biomarkers distinguishing HRS from ATN remain investigational.
- Optimal vasoconstrictor selection (terlipressin versus noradrenaline) and albumin dosing schedules vary by setting.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HRS diagnostic criteria and the exclusion of intrinsic renal disease.
- Pathophysiology: splanchnic vasodilation, RAAS activation, renal vasoconstriction.
- HRS-AKI versus HRS-CKD under current nomenclature.
- Treatment: terlipressin plus albumin, and liver transplantation as definitive therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A cirrhotic patient with ascites has creatinine rising over 72 hours after diuretics; distinguish HRS-AKI from prerenal azotaemia and ATN.
- A patient with HRS-AKI is started on terlipressin and albumin; define response criteria and the transplant conversation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific diagnostic creatinine cut-offs, response definitions and terlipressin dosing titration are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full ADQI/ICA consensus and AGA update before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32928750**: *Hepatorenal syndrome: pathophysiology, diagnosis, and management.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 38246506**: *Hepatorenal Syndrome in Cirrhosis.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 38527522**: *Acute kidney injury in patients with cirrhosis: Acute Disease Quality Initiative (ADQI) and International Club of Ascites consensus.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37978969**: *AGA Clinical Practice Update on the use of vasoactive drugs and intravenous albumin in cirrhosis.* (2024) [guideline] - None

## [0851] Huntington disease chorea management

**Cluster**: Huntington disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Huntington disease has no disease-modifying therapy, so chorea management sits at the centre of what clinicians can actually offer. Chorea — random, jerk-like movements migrating between body parts — is the most common motor manifestation, interfering with daily activities, injuring patients and eroding quality of life. The therapeutic mainstay is VMAT2 inhibition: tetrabenazine's legacy is real efficacy weighed against depression and parkinsonism, while deutetrabenazine's deuterium substitution confers a longer half-life, less-frequent dosing and an improved tolerability profile — the reason it became the modern default.

### Core Teaching Points
- Chorea is defined as jerk-like movements moving randomly between body parts, caused by various disorders; current symptomatic therapy is quite effective, though few aetiology-targeted therapies exist. (PMID 29120264)
- Deutetrabenazine is FDA-approved for chorea in Huntington's disease; deuterium substitution gives a longer half-life allowing less-frequent daily dosing, and as a reversible VMAT2 inhibitor it depletes monoamines. (PMID 30624137)
- In Huntington's disease, an inherited neurodegenerative disorder with no disease-modifying treatment, chorea is the most common motor manifestation and may interfere with daily activities, reduce quality of life and cause injury. (PMID 29996061)
- First-line pharmacotherapy for debilitating chorea in Huntington disease includes vesicular monoamine transporter 2 (VMAT2) inhibitors (deutetrabenazine, tetrabenazine, valbenazine) due to lower risks of tardive dyskinesia compared to typical antipsychotics; patients must be systematically screened and monitored for medication-induced depression and suicidality. (PMID 29120264)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether suppressing chorea with VMAT2 inhibitors alters long-term functional outcomes beyond symptom relief is unknown.
- Treatment of the non-choreic motor and neuropsychiatric burden (bradykinesia, depression, suicidality) needs separate, individualised strategies.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- VMAT2 inhibition mechanism and the tetrabenazine-to-deutetrabenazine evolution.
- Tolerability differences driving deutetrabenazine selection.
- Recognising Huntington's chorea phenomenology versus other hyperkinetic disorders.
- Counselling points: no disease-modifying therapy, genetic implications, psychiatric comorbidity.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with newly diagnosed Huntington's disease and disabling chorea; select initial therapy and discuss monitoring for depression.
- A patient on tetrabenazine develops parkinsonism and low mood; switch strategy using the deutetrabenazine evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dosing titration schedules, genetische-testing counselling protocols, and management of the non-choreic spectrum are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current AAN guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29120264**: *Treatment options for chorea.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 30624137**: *Deutetrabenazine in the treatment of Huntington's disease.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 29996061**: *Deutetrabenazine for the treatment of Huntington's chorea.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0852] hybrid closed loop insulin delivery in type 1 diabetes

**Cluster**: hybrid closed loop systems | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The hybrid closed loop system is the closest thing to an artificial pancreas that mainstream care currently has: a continuous glucose sensor feeding an algorithm that adjusts pump-delivered insulin in real time, with the user still announcing meals. Building on pump and CGM technology that already improves HbA1c, time-in-range and hypoglycaemia burden, closed loops reduce the daily cognitive load of type 1 diabetes. The field has now matured enough for network meta-analysis to compare competing systems head-to-head, which is exactly the evidence clinicians need when choosing between them.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diabetes technologies represent a paradigm shift in type 1 diabetes care: pumps and continuous glucose monitors improve HbA1c, enhance time in range, limit severe hypoglycaemia and reduce diabetes distress, and the artificial pancreas extends this trajectory. (PMID 36734145)
- A network meta-analysis of randomised trials compares the efficacy and safety of different hybrid closed loop systems, including the Minimed 670G class, across children, adolescents and adults. (PMID 39298688)
- Hybrid closed-loop systems automatically modulate basal insulin infusion in response to continuous interstitial glucose telemetry, significantly increasing time in range (70-180 mg/dL) toward target >70%. (PMID 36734145, PMID 39298688)
- Automated basal suspension during downward glucose trajectories substantially reduces nocturnal hypoglycemia compared to sensor-augmented pump therapy alone. (PMID 39298688)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term durability of glycaemic gains and real-world adherence outside trial conditions are less certain.
- Which system performs best for which patient phenotype (hypoglycaemia-prone, adolescents, highly variable schedules) is being refined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Closed-loop components: sensor, algorithm, pump; what 'hybrid' means (meal announcement).
- Glycaemic outcomes: time-in-range, HbA1c, hypoglycaemia reduction.
- Comparing systems using network meta-analysis evidence.
- Patient selection and the persistence of self-management skills.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A teenager with HbA1c 8.9% and recurrent hypoglycaemia is offered a hybrid closed loop; discuss expected outcomes and responsibilities.
- A patient asks which closed-loop system is 'best'; structure the comparison using the network meta-analysis.

**Reviewer Gap**: Device-specific setup parameters, ketone/hyperglycaemia protocols and reimbursement considerations are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current diabetes-technology guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36734145**: *The Artificial Pancreas and Type 1 Diabetes.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 39298688**: *Efficacy and safety of different hybrid closed loop systems for automated insulin delivery.* (2024) [network-meta-analysis] - None

## [0853] Hybrid closed-loop insulin delivery in type 1 diabetes

**Cluster**: artificial pancreas evidence | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Whether closed-loop delivery actually works is no longer the question — the evidence answered it across outpatient trials and in children specifically. The meta-analytic picture shows artificial pancreas systems improving glycaemic control in real-world outpatient settings, with efficacy influenced by clinical and technical factors. The paediatric evidence matters doubly: parents carry an outsized management burden and children have the most volatile glucose patterns, making automated delivery especially valuable — provided hypoglycaemia safety holds, which the paediatric review addresses directly.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review and meta-analysis summarises the efficacy and safety of artificial pancreas systems in outpatient settings and explores clinical and technical factors affecting performance. (PMID 28533136)
- The artificial pancreas is now a viable treatment option for people with type 1 diabetes, demonstrating improved glycaemic outcomes while reducing the onus of self-management through closed-loop glucose-responsive insulin delivery. (PMID 30183096)
- Artificial pancreas and closed-loop systems were developed to improve metabolic control without increasing hypoglycaemia risk specifically in children with type 1 diabetes, one of the most common chronic diseases of childhood. (PMID 29954380)
- Hybrid closed-loop (automated insulin delivery) systems integrate real-time continuous glucose monitoring with smart insulin pumps to automatically adjust basal insulin delivery and administer correction boluses, significantly increasing time in range (70-180 mg/dL) and reducing nocturnal hypoglycemia across diverse age cohorts with type 1 diabetes. (PMID 28533136)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Performance variability across systems and settings means trial-average gains may not translate uniformly to individuals.
- Long-term paediatric outcomes, including growth and psychosocial effects, need continued follow-up.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Outpatient meta-analytic evidence for artificial pancreas efficacy.
- What 'closed loop' adds over pump-plus-CGM without automation.
- Paediatric-specific efficacy and hypoglycaemia safety.
- Technical and clinical factors modifying system performance.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Parents of a 7-year-old with type 1 diabetes ask about a closed-loop system; present the paediatric evidence.
- A patient on pump-plus-CGM remains above target; discuss what upgrading to closed loop would change.

**Reviewer Gap**: Evidence overlaps topic 852 (complementary angle: trial-meta-analysis versus system-comparison); device selection detail and cost-effectiveness are not covered by the retained abstracts.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28533136**: *Effect of artificial pancreas systems on glycaemic control in patients with type 1 diabetes: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2017) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 30183096**: *Is an artificial pancreas (closed-loop system) for Type 1 diabetes effective?* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 29954380**: *Efficacy and safety of the artificial pancreas in the paediatric population with type 1 diabetes.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0854] Hydroxychloroquine in SLE: dose, retinal toxicity screening, cardiac monitoring

**Cluster**: hydroxychloroquine SLE | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hydroxychloroquine is the closest thing SLE has to a foundation drug: almost universally recommended for its immunomodulatory and anti-inflammatory effects, with benefits that extend beyond symptom control. Its liability is dose-limiting retinal toxicity — potentially irreversible, which makes early detection the whole game, and screening rather than blanket avoidance the accepted strategy. Balancing therapeutic levels against ocular safety, and layering cardiac monitoring for the QT-prolongation concern, is the core prescribing skill.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hydroxychloroquine has gained prominence in SLE treatment through a multifaceted mechanism of action as a lipophilic, lysosomotropic agent that accumulates in lysosomes and alkalinises them. (PMID 38711668)
- HCQ is a cornerstone of SLE therapy, but long-term exposure can cause potentially irreversible retinopathy in which early detection is critical. (PMID 42445598)
- To minimize lifetime risk of irreversible bull's-eye retinopathy, daily hydroxychloroquine dosing should not exceed 5.0 mg/kg actual body weight per day (AAO guidelines). (PMID 42445598)
- Annual retinal screening with spectral-domain optical coherence tomography (SD-OCT) and automated visual fields (10-2, or 24-2 in Asian patients) is recommended after 5 years of therapy, or earlier in high-risk patients. (PMID 42445598, PMID 38711668)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Individual susceptibility to retinopathy varies unpredictably, so screening intervals and dose ceilings remain population-level rather than personalised.
- Cardiac toxicity (QT prolongation, cardiomyopathy) monitoring practice is less standardised than retinal screening.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HCQ mechanism and its SLE-specific benefits (flare reduction, survival data).
- Retinopathy risk factors: dose, duration, renal impairment, tamoxifen co-exposure.
- Screening tools: OCT, visual fields, and screening cadence.
- QT/cardiac monitoring and drug interactions.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with SLE for eight years on HCQ asks whether screening is still needed; justify the retinal surveillance plan.
- A patient with CKD stage 3 needs HCQ; discuss dose adjustment and heightened monitoring.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 fresh sources plus one shared with topic 855): specific dosing in mg/kg real weight, baseline and interval ECG recommendations, and ideal-body-weight calculations are not covered. Cross-check topic 855 and verify against the current AAO screening recommendations before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38711668**: *Advancements on the impact of hydroxychloroquine in systemic lupus erythematosus.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 42445598**: *Hydroxychloroquine retinopathy in systemic lupus erythematosus: risk factors, screening, and emerging insights.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0855] Hydroxychloroquine retinopathy: Marmor guidelines, OCT screening, dose limits

**Cluster**: HCQ retinopathy screening | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hydroxychloroquine retinopathy screening is one of the few areas where ophthalmology has produced precise, iterative, dose-anchored guidance — and then revised it as prevalence data changed. The Marmor-led AAO recommendations established the framework: toxicity is parafoveal in most patients, risk rises steeply with dose and duration, and OCT is the screening tool of choice. The 2025 revision updates that framework for current knowledge, while rheumatology-facing reviews translate the same evidence into prescribing behaviour: keep doses at or below the accepted ceiling, and screen on schedule rather than stopping a drug that most patients need.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 AAO revision updates the hydroxychloroquine screening recommendations originally published a decade earlier, reflecting new knowledge about toxicity patterns. (PMID 41232611)
- The 2016 revision updated recommendations in light of new information about toxicity prevalence, risk factors, fundus distribution and screening-tool effectiveness, with the locus of damage parafoveal in most patients. (PMID 26992838)
- Research advances in HCQ retinopathy have direct implications for rheumatology care, where HCQ remains almost universally recommended in SLE and the toxicity is the major dose-limiting concern. (PMID 30401979)
- According to the revised American Academy of Ophthalmology (Marmor) guidelines, the maximum recommended daily dose of hydroxychloroquine is <=5.0 mg/kg actual body weight; annual retinal screening with spectral-domain optical coherence tomography (SD-OCT) and automated visual fields (10-2, or 24-2 in Asian patients) should begin within 5 years of therapy initiation, or earlier in high-risk patients. (PMID 41232611)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The retina typically remains clinically normal for many years, with unpredictable individual susceptibility defining residual risk.
- Optimal screening intervals for very low-risk patients versus high-risk (renal impairment, tamoxifen) subgroups continue to be individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- AAO/Marmor recommendation lineage: 2016 revision and 2025 update.
- Dose limits (mg/kg real weight) and duration thresholds.
- OCT as the primary screening tool; visual-field adjuncts.
- Risk modifiers: renal disease, dose, tamoxifen co-exposure.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient on 400 mg daily for six years at 52 kg body weight; assess dose appropriateness and screening status.
- A patient with new parafoveal OCT changes and no symptoms; counsel on the irreversibility and next steps.

**Reviewer Gap**: Exact numerical dose ceilings, baseline-examination content and annual-versus-more-frequent schedules by risk tier are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full 2025 AAO report before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41232611**: *Special AAO Report: Recommendations on Screening for Hydroxychloroquine Retinopathy (2025 Revision).* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 26992838**: *Recommendations on Screening for Chloroquine and Hydroxychloroquine Retinopathy (2016 Revision).* (2016) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30401979**: *Hydroxychloroquine retinopathy: implications of research advances for rheumatology care.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0856] hypercalcaemia

**Cluster**: hypercalcemia general | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypercalcaemia is common — roughly 1% of the population — and its severity grading drives everything: mild hypercalcaemia is usually asymptomatic or vaguely constitutional, while severe hypercalcaemia is a medical emergency of dehydration, confusion, shortened QT and crisis-level calcium. The two questions that organise every case are 'how high' and 'why': primary hyperparathyroidism dominates the outpatient mild-to-moderate end of the spectrum, malignancy dominates the severe end, and the PTH level separates them in a single test.

### Core Teaching Points
- Primary hyperparathyroidism, the most common cause of hypercalcemia, is most often identified in postmenopausal women, with clinical presentation evolving over 40 years into distinct phenotypes. (PMID 30060226)
- Hypercalcemia affects approximately 1% of the worldwide population; mild hypercalcemia (total calcium <12 mg/dL) is usually asymptomatic but may cause fatigue and constipation in about 20%. (PMID 36282253)
- Primary hyperparathyroidism and malignancy account for over 90% of all hypercalcemia cases; elevated or inappropriately normal intact PTH confirms parathyroid-dependent hypercalcemia. (PMID 30060226, PMID 36282253)
- Initial emergency stabilization of symptomatic or severe hypercalcemia (>14 mg/dL [>3.5 mmol/L]) mandates vigorous isotonic saline hydration (200-300 mL/h) followed by intravenous bisphosphonates (zoledronic acid) or calcitonin. (PMID 36282253)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether asymptomatic mild hypercalcaemia from PHPT always warrants parathyroidectomy is individualised by guidelines.
- The calcium-adjustment formulas and ionised-calcium thresholds in hypoalbuminaemia vary in reliability.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Severity grading of hypercalcaemia and symptom thresholds.
- PTH-based differential: PTH versus PTH-independent causes.
- PHPT as the commonest cause; malignancy as the commonest severe cause.
- Initial management: hydration, calcitonin, then bisphosphonate or denosumab.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An asymptomatic patient has corrected calcium 2.9 mmol/L and elevated PTH; outline the outpatient work-up.
- A patient presents confused with calcium 3.8 mmol/L; sequence the first 24 hours of management.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): familial hypocalciuric hypercalcaemia distinction, granulomatous and medication causes, and loop-diuretic/saline protocol detail are not covered. Verify against an endocrine reference and current guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30060226**: *Primary Hyperparathyroidism.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 36282253**: *Hypercalcemia: A Review.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0857] hypercalcaemia of malignancy

**Cluster**: malignancy hypercalcemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypercalcaemia of malignancy is the commonest metabolic complication of cancer and the commonest cause of hypercalcaemia in hospitalised patients, affecting over a quarter of patients with cancer across its phenotypes — humoral (PTHrP-driven), osteolytic, and the rarer calcitriol-secreting and ectopic-PTH forms. Its epidemiology is shifting: incidence may be declining as cancer therapy improves, and mortality from HCM has fallen markedly with effective chemotherapy. Acute management remains hydration plus an antiresorptive — bisphosphonate or denosumab — with treatment of the underlying malignancy as the definitive lever.

### Core Teaching Points
- An Endocrine Society clinical practice guideline addresses treatment of hypercalcemia of malignancy in adults; HCM is the most common metabolic complication of malignancy, with incidence possibly declining due to potent chemotherapeutic agents. (PMID 36545746)
- HCM affects over a quarter of patients being treated for cancer, with humoral, osteolytic, ectopic-hyperparathyroidism and vitamin-D-secreting lymphoma mechanisms. (PMID 31086686)
- Cancer-related hypercalcemia occurs in about 20–30% of patients with advanced cancer and is the most common cause of hypercalcemia in hospitalised patients. (PMID 37033238)
- For severe hypercalcemia of malignancy refractory to intravenous bisphosphonates (zoledronic acid 4 mg), the fully human monoclonal anti-RANKL antibody denosumab (120 mg subcutaneously) effectively normalizes serum calcium, particularly in renal impairment where bisphosphonates are contraindicated. (PMID 36534567)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Choosing between bisphosphonates and denosumab — including cost, renal function constraints and hypocalcaemia risk — is individualised.
- How emerging cancer therapies are reshaping HCM incidence and prognosis is an evolving picture.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HCM phenotypes and their mechanisms (PTHrP versus osteolysis versus calcitriol).
- Acute management sequence: saline rehydration, calcitonin for speed, antiresorptive for duration.
- Denosumab versus bisphosphonate selection logic.
- HCM as a poor-prognosis marker and the case for treating the malignancy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with metastatic breast cancer has calcium 3.4 mmol/L; sequence acute therapy and decide between bisphosphonate and denosumab.
- A newly diagnosed myeloma patient presents with back pain and hypercalcaemia; connect the mechanism (osteolysis) to management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Calcitonin dosing, denosumab-specific hypocalcaemia management and dialysis indications are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full Endocrine Society guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36545746**: *Treatment of Hypercalcemia of Malignancy in Adults: An Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2023) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 31086686**: *Hypercalcemia of Malignancy.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 37033238**: *Cancer-related hypercalcemia and potential treatments.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 36534567**: *Endocrine Society Clinical Practice Guideline: Treatment of Hypercalcemia of Malignancy.* (2023) [guideline] -

## [0858] Hypercalcaemia of malignancy: PTHrP, bisphosphonate, denosumab, hydration

**Cluster**: HCM pathophysiology and treatment | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The physiology of malignancy-associated hypercalcaemia explains its treatment: humoral hypercalcaemia mediated by PTHrP drives most cases, with osteolytic metastatic disease and calcitriol-secreting lymphomas completing the picture — and mixed PTHrP-plus-calcitriol elevations, once thought rare, are increasingly recognised across solid and haematological malignancies. Every therapy maps onto that physiology: aggressive saline rehydration restores the depleted extracellular volume, calcitonin lowers calcium within hours, and bisphosphonates or denosumab shut down the osteoclast-driven resorption that sustains it.

### Core Teaching Points
- Malignancy-associated hypercalcemia — the most common cause of hypercalcemia in the emergency department — arises from direct bone resorption by metastases, vitamin D-secreting malignancies, and increased PTH or PTHrP levels. (PMID 26817427)
- Concurrent elevations in PTHrP and calcitriol, initially considered rare and solid-tumour-associated, are increasingly detected in hypercalcemic patients across solid and hematologic malignancies, with diagnostic and therapeutic implications. (PMID 40662114)
- PTH-related protein (PTHrP) secretion accounts for ~80% of hypercalcemia of malignancy (humoral hypercalcemia of malignancy), characteristically producing high serum calcium with suppressed intact PTH and normal/low 1,25-(OH)2D. (PMID 40662114)
- Intravenous bisphosphonates (zoledronic acid 4 mg IV over 15 min) or denosumab (RANKL inhibitor) achieve sustained calcium normalization in >85% of malignancy cases, with denosumab preferred in renal impairment. (PMID 26817427, PMID 40662114)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The diagnostic work-up of mixed-mechanism HCM is being re-evaluated as concurrent PTHrP/calcitriol elevations are recognised more often.
- Whether earlier denosumab use in renal impairment changes outcomes versus cautious bisphosphonate use is practice-dependent.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PTHrP physiology: why PTH is suppressed and why osteoclast activation is indirect.
- Treatment timeline: saline (hours), calcitonin (days), bisphosphonate/denosumab (days to weeks).
- Denosumab advantages in renal impairment and hypocalcaemia risk.
- Recognising calcitriol-mediated HCM in lymphoma.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hypercalcemic patient with lung cancer has suppressed PTH and elevated PTHrP; explain the mechanism and choose definitive therapy.
- A lymphoma patient's hypercalcaemia persists after bisphosphonate; consider the calcitriol mechanism.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): drug dosing regimens, saline volumes and retreatment intervals are not covered. Cross-check topic 857 (Endocrine Society guideline) and verify before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26817427**: *Emergency Management of Malignancy-Associated Hypercalcemia.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 40662114**: *Hypercalcemia of Malignancy: Mechanisms and Therapeutic Implications of Concurrently Elevated PTHrP and Calcitriol.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0859] Hypercapnic Respiratory Failure

**Cluster**: hypercapnic failure | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypercapnic respiratory failure — ventilatory failure producing a rising PaCO2, usually on a background of COPD, obesity or neuromuscular disease — is the one form of acute respiratory failure where non-invasive ventilation is not just an option but the standard of care that changed outcomes. The ERS/ATS guideline anchors NIV application across acute respiratory failure aetiologies, while the COPD-exacerbation literature supplies the clinical context: exacerbations drive disease progression, wellbeing and mortality, and correcting the hypercapnia early is what breaks the downward spiral.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ERS/ATS guideline provides recommendations for clinical application of noninvasive ventilation across aetiologies of acute respiratory failure, based on current literature. (PMID 28860265)
- COPD exacerbations — acute deterioration in respiratory symptoms — fundamentally impact disease progression, comorbidities, wellbeing and mortality, and carry the largest share of COPD's socioeconomic burden. (PMID 33893708)
- Arterial blood gas criteria identifying acute or acute-on-chronic hypercapnic respiratory failure include PaCO2 > 45 mmHg with uncompensated acidemia (pH < 7.35). (PMID 28860265, PMID 33893708)
- Controlled oxygen therapy targeting SpO2 88-92% prevents worsening hypercapnia secondary to loss of hypoxic vasoconstriction (V/Q mismatch) and the Haldane effect. (PMID 33893708)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- NIV in hypercapnic failure from non-COPD causes (obesity-hypoventilation, neuromuscular) is extrapolated from thinner evidence.
- The role of high-flow nasal oxygen as an alternative or bridge in milder hypercapnia is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Definition of hypercapnic (ventilatory) versus hypoxaemic respiratory failure.
- NIV as first-line in COPD exacerbation with acidosis; evidence lineage.
- Exacerbation triggers, severity assessment and treatment fundamentals.
- When NIV fails: intubation criteria and ceilings of care.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A COPD patient presents with pH 7.28 and PaCO2 68; start NIV and define the reassessment timeline.
- An obese patient with somnolence and PaCO2 80 post-op; discuss whether NIV fits and what else to consider.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): ABG interpretation thresholds, NIV settings (IPAP/EPAP titration) and weaning protocols are not covered. Cross-check topics 822/860 and verify against the full ERS/ATS guideline.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28860265**: *Official ERS/ATS clinical practice guidelines: noninvasive ventilation for acute respiratory failure.* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 33893708**: *Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease exacerbation fundamentals: diagnosis, treatment, prevention and prognosis.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0860] Hypercapnic respiratory failure: COPD exacerbation, NIV criteria

**Cluster**: AECOPD NIV | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
In acute hypercapnic respiratory failure from a COPD exacerbation, NIV with bilevel support is the intervention with the strongest evidence in the whole of acute respiratory medicine — the Cochrane lineage showing it reduces acidosis, respiratory rate, intubation and mortality. But 'start NIV' is only half the competence: knowing the criteria that predict success, recognising failure early, and understanding where the newer evidence places high-flow nasal therapy relative to bilevel support is what separates protocol following from skilled management.

### Core Teaching Points
- NIV with bilevel positive airway pressure is commonly used and evidence-supported for acute hypercapnic respiratory failure secondary to COPD exacerbation, as evaluated in a systematic review. (PMID 28702957)
- A network meta-analysis compares non-invasive respiratory support modalities for acute hypercapnic respiratory failure, with treatment failure, all-cause mortality and intubation as outcomes. (PMID 40697918)
- Non-invasive positive pressure ventilation (BIPAP) is the first-line therapy for COPD exacerbations with persistent respiratory acidosis (pH 7.25-7.35, PaCO2 > 45 mmHg) despite optimal medical therapy, reducing intubation rates by >60%. (PMID 28702957)
- Immediate indications for invasive mechanical ventilation over NIV include severe encephalopathy, hemodynamic instability, uncontrolled vomiting, high aspiration risk, or failure of pH to improve after 1-2 hours of NIV. (PMID 28702957, PMID 40697918)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Patient-selection boundaries for NIV versus early intubation (severe acidosis, altered consciousness, comorbidity burden) remain judgement-heavy.
- Where high-flow nasal therapy sits for milder hypercapnia or as NIV-tolerance rescue is being defined by comparative trials.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- NIV success predictors: pH threshold, conscious level, cooperation.
- Failure criteria: worsening acidosis despite NIV, exhaustion, hypoxia.
- Cochrane-evidenced benefits: mortality and intubation reduction.
- Modality comparison from the network meta-analysis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Two hours into NIV a patient's pH worsens from 7.28 to 7.24 with increasing somnolence; decide next steps.
- A patient with mild hypercapnia (pH 7.36, PaCO2 55) asks about NIV versus other support; apply the comparative evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: IPAP/EPAP titration targets, interface selection and do-not-intubate NIV palliative protocols are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the Cochrane review and current GOLD guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28702957**: *Non-invasive ventilation for the management of acute hypercapnic respiratory failure due to exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease.* (2017) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 40697918**: *A comparative analysis of non-invasive respiratory support modalities in the treatment of acute hypercapnic respiratory failure.* (2025) [network-meta-analysis] - None

## [0861] hyperemesis gravidarum

**Cluster**: hyperemesis gravidarum | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hyperemesis gravidarum is the severe end of the nausea-and-vomiting-of-pregnancy spectrum and the leading cause of hospitalisation in early pregnancy, affecting roughly 0.3–3% of pregnancies and carrying real maternal morbidity. Most NVP peaks between 7 and 12 weeks and resolves by week 20, which frames both the natural history and the safety calculus of treatment. Management is stepwise — dietary and ginger first, then antiemetics, with ondansetron's reassuring data quieting much of the first-trimester anxiety — and escalates to rehydration, thiamine and occasionally steroids for refractory disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hyperemesis gravidarum, affecting approximately 0.3–3% of pregnancies, is the leading cause of hospitalisation during early pregnancy and is associated with substantial maternal morbidity. (PMID 40843754)
- Nausea and vomiting of pregnancy affects about 75% of women, begins early in the first trimester, peaks between 7 and 12 weeks, and abates by week 20 in over 90%. (PMID 27512487)
- ACOG Practice Bulletin 153 provides evidence-based management guidance for nausea and vomiting of pregnancy, recognising its impact on quality of life, healthcare costs and work absence. (PMID 26287788)
- Ginger supplementation has been evaluated in a systematic review for alleviating hyperemesis gravidarum, reflecting demand for alternatives to prescription medication. (PMID 40226035)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The aetiology of HG remains incompletely explained, limiting targeted therapy.
- Optimal refractory-disease pathways (steroid dosing, enteral/parenteral nutrition thresholds) vary by centre.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- NVP-to-HG spectrum, epidemiology and natural history timing.
- Stepwise antiemetic ladder and the ondansetron safety data.
- Thiamine before dextrose, and Wernicke prevention in prolonged vomiting.
- When to escalate to corticosteroids and nutrition support.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A woman at 9 weeks has ketonuria and 3 kg weight loss; build the treatment and monitoring plan.
- A patient requests 'something natural' before antiemetics; discuss ginger evidence and set expectations.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dosing schedules, steroid protocols and psychological-support evidence are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current ACOG/RCOG guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40843754**: *Hyperemesis in pregnancy: complications and treatment.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 27512487**: *Ondansetron and pregnancy: understanding the data.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 26287788**: *ACOG Practice Bulletin No. 153: Nausea and Vomiting of Pregnancy.* (2015) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 40226035**: *Effectiveness of ginger supplementation in alleviating hyperemesis gravidarum.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None

## [0862] Hypereosinophilic Syndrome

**Cluster**: hypereosinophilic syndrome | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypereosinophilic syndrome is where a blood count becomes a multi-organ emergency: abnormal eosinophil accumulation independent of secondary causes, with highly variable manifestations driven by which organs the cells infiltrate — heart, lung, gut, skin, nervous system. The field's modern backbone is classification: separating reactive (secondary) from clonal (primary, e.g. FIP1L1-PDGFRA) disease, because targeted therapy — imatinib for the tyrosine-kinase-driven form — transformed one subtype from chronic illness to molecularly-curable disease. Hypereosinophilia above 1.5 × 10⁹/L with end-organ damage defines the syndrome, and corticosteroids remain the rapid organ-protection bridge.

### Core Teaching Points
- The eosinophilias span non-hematologic (secondary/reactive) and hematologic (primary/clonal) disorders with potential end-organ damage; hypereosinophilia is generally defined as a peripheral blood eosinophil count greater than 1.5 × 10⁹/L. (PMID 38551368)
- Hypereosinophilic syndrome comprises disorders of abnormal eosinophil accumulation in blood or peripheral tissues independent of known secondary causes, with highly variable clinical manifestations. (PMID 26475367)
- Refined international diagnostic criteria and classification separate eosinophilia from hypereosinophilic syndrome, with organ damage often prompting the HES diagnosis. (PMID 36207764)
- Diagnostic evaluation of hypereosinophilic syndrome (HES) requires demonstrating persistent absolute eosinophilia >=1.5 x 10^9/L with eosinophil-mediated end-organ damage (cardiac fibrosis, thromboembolism, neuropathy); screening for the FIP1L1-PDGFRA fusion gene via RT-PCR or FISH is mandatory, as positive cases achieve rapid, complete hematologic remission with low-dose imatinib. (PMID 38551368)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Organ-damage prediction from eosinophil counts alone is poor, so surveillance intensity is empirical.
- Sequencing of steroids, imatinib and emerging targeted agents in non-clonal HES is individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Definition thresholds and the reactive versus clonal classification.
- End-organ patterns: Loeffler endocarditis, neuropathy, dermatologic and GI involvement.
- FIP1L1-PDGFRA and the imatinib-responsive subtype.
- Steroids as acute organ-protection therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with persistent eosinophilia of 8 × 10⁹/L and a new murmur; work through organ assessment and initial therapy.
- A patient with clonal HES is found FIP1L1-PDGFRA positive; explain why imatinib changes everything.

**Reviewer Gap**: Molecular testing algorithms, mepolizumab/benralizumab positioning and anticoagulation strategies are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current consensus guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38551368**: *World Health Organization and International Consensus Classification of eosinophilic disorders: 2024 update.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 26475367**: *Hypereosinophilic Syndrome.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 36207764**: *Proposed refined diagnostic criteria and classification of eosinophil disorders and related syndromes.* (2022) [consensus-criteria] - None

## [0863] hyperglycaemic hyperosmolar state

**Cluster**: HHS | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hyperglycaemic hyperosmolar state is the hyperglycaemic emergency of older, type 2, often previously-undiagnosed diabetic patients — profound hyperglycaemia and hyperosmolarity with enough residual insulin to suppress ketogenesis, so no significant acidosis. It carries higher mortality than DKA, driven by the age and comorbidity of its victims, and its treatment is a discipline of slow rehydration and careful osmolality decline: fluids first, insulin lower-dose, and obsessive attention to sodium correction as glucose falls.

### Core Teaching Points
- DKA and HHS are the most serious hyperglycemic emergencies; HHS occurs more in adult and elderly type 2 diabetes patients, with overlapping clinical features. (PMID 28372715)
- DKA and HHS are life-threatening complications increasing mortality and morbidity, and reaching uniform consensus on diagnostic criteria and treatment remains a challenge. (PMID 36405291)
- DKA and HHS are characterised by insulinopaenia and severe hyperglycaemia, requiring prompt recognition, diagnosis and treatment. (PMID 26893262)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic boundaries between HHS, HHS-DKA overlap and severe hyperglycaemia are variably defined across guidelines.
- Optimal rehydration tonicity, osmolality decline targets and anticoagulation in HHS remain practice-dependent.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HHS versus DKA: osmolality, acidosis, ketones, patient phenotype.
- Treatment sequence: aggressive fluids, low-dose insulin, potassium management.
- Corrected sodium and the risk of over-rapid osmolality shifts.
- Precipitant search (infection, MI, steroids, thiazides).

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 80-year-old has glucose 55 mmol/L, sodium 150, altered consciousness; plan the first 24 hours.
- A patient has features of both HHS and DKA; discuss how overlap changes management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Precise diagnostic cut-offs (effective osmolality, pH/bicarbonate thresholds), insulin infusion rates and phosphate management are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full ADA/EASD hyperglycaemic-crises consensus before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28372715**: *Management of Hyperglycemic Crises: Diabetic Ketoacidosis and Hyperglycemic Hyperosmolar State.* (2017) [consensus-guideline] - None
- **PMID 36405291**: *Overlap of diabetic ketoacidosis and hyperosmolar hyperglycemic state.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 26893262**: *Diabetic emergencies: ketoacidosis, hyperglycaemic hyperosmolar state and hypoglycaemia.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0864] hyperkalaemia

**Cluster**: hyperkalaemia general | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hyperkalaemia is the electrolyte emergency everyone meets first and understands last: common, frequently iatrogenic, and dangerous because of malignant arrhythmia. Its management has three tiers with different speeds and purposes — membrane stabilisation with intravenous calcium, intracellular shift with insulin-dextrose and beta-agonists, and actual potassium removal with dialysis or binders — and the art lies in knowing which tier the patient needs and how long each buys. Controversies persist, not least around the older exchange resins and the role of the newer binders.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hyperkalaemia is a common, potentially life-threatening electrolyte abnormality because of its association with malignant cardiac arrhythmias and sudden cardiac arrest, encountered across acute and chronic care and disproportionately affecting patients with kidney disease, heart failure and diabetes. (PMID 42629001)
- Classic hyperkalemia treatments and their controversies are evaluated in a dedicated review, including new medications for management. (PMID 29731287)
- Severe hyperkalemia (K+ > 6.5 mmol/L or ECG changes: peaked T waves, widened QRS, PR prolongation) requires immediate membrane stabilization with intravenous calcium (gluconate or chloride) within 1-3 minutes. (PMID 42629001)
- Intracellular potassium shift therapies (regular insulin 10 units IV with 25-50g dextrose, and inhaled nebulized albuterol) act within 15-30 minutes, temporizing while elimination via loop diuretics, potassium binders (patiromer, SZC), or hemodialysis is arranged. (PMID 42629001, PMID 29731287)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Treatment thresholds for mild asymptomatic hyperkalaemia, especially with ECG ambiguity, vary across guidelines.
- The clinical value of newer potassium binders in chronic management versus acute settings is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ECG progression: peaked T waves, PR prolongation, sine wave.
- Three-tier management and the speed of each intervention.
- Common precipitants: CKD, RAAS inhibitors, potassium-sparing drugs, tissue breakdown.
- Pseudohyperkalaemia and confirmatory testing.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A CKD patient on lisinopril and spironolactone has K+ 6.4 with peaked T waves; sequence the treatment.
- An asymptomatic patient has K+ 6.0 with a normal ECG; decide on urgency and the confirmatory approach.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): shift-therapy doses, dialysis indications and binder comparison data are covered only in outline. Cross-check topics 865/866 and verify against KDIGO guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42629001**: *Diagnosis and management of acute hyperkalaemia.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 29731287**: *Controversies in management of hyperkalemia.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0865] hyperkalaemia emergency treatment

**Cluster**: hyperkalaemia emergency | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Emergency hyperkalaemia treatment is a study in timelines: calcium works in minutes, insulin-dextrose and salbutamol shift potassium over an hour or two, and dialysis or binders are the only true removal. The KDIGO conference summary anchors the modern ED approach, including the pivotal insight that ECG changes are neither perfectly sensitive nor specific — which pushes treatment decisions toward the clinical picture and potassium trend rather than a single number. In cardiology patients the stakes are doubled: RAAS inhibitors and MRA therapy that prolong life also cause the hyperkalaemia, creating a treat-the-number versus protect-the-therapy dilemma.

### Core Teaching Points
- A KDIGO consensus conference summarises acute hyperkalemia in the emergency department, noting predisposing conditions (kidney disease, heart failure, diabetes, tissue trauma) and medications including RAAS inhibitors. (PMID 32852924)
- Treatment options have evolved only modestly since the cation-exchange-resin era, while newer agents have entered the landscape, as reviewed in a 'something old, something new' framework. (PMID 26880451)
- Hyperkalaemia is one of the most common electrolyte disorders in cardiovascular patients; in population cohorts up to four in ten developed hyperkalaemia, in addition to drug-related risks. (PMID 38289453)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether ECG-guided treatment thresholds improve outcomes over potassium-guided thresholds is not established.
- Balancing life-prolonging RAAS-inhibitor therapy against recurrent hyperkalaemia needs individualised protocols.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Calcium gluconate versus calcium chloride and the repeat-dosing rule.
- Insulin-dextrose with glucose monitoring and beta-agonist adjuncts.
- ECG sensitivity limitations in treatment decisions.
- The RAAS-inhibitor hyperkalaemia dilemma in cardiology patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A cardiac patient on sacubitril-valsartan and spironolactone presents with K+ 6.8; manage the emergency and decide the medication strategy.
- Twenty minutes after calcium and insulin a repeat ECG shows unchanged changes; discuss the next step.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dialysis-initiation thresholds, sodium bicarbonate indications and binder dosing in the emergency phase are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full KDIGO conference proceedings before approval.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 26880451**: *Treatment of hyperkalemia: something old, something new.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 38289453**: *Hyperkalaemia in cardiological patients: new solutions for an old problem.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0866] hyperkalemia diagnosis and management

**Cluster**: hyperkalemia management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
No universally accepted consensus exists on hyperkalaemia management — thresholds, monitoring cadence and escalation vary across guidelines — which makes the underlying physiology the only reliable guide. Diagnosis starts with confirming the potassium is real (pseudohyperkalaemia is common), then grading severity and ECG changes. The newest chapter in management is pharmacological removal: the expert-panel literature on patiromer and sodium zirconium cyclosilicate reframes chronic hyperkalaemia as a manageable condition rather than an automatic reason to stop RAAS-inhibitor therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Despite various guidelines, no universally accepted consensus exists on hyperkalemia monitoring and management, with variation in potassium thresholds and management approaches. (PMID 33160639)
- A multidisciplinary expert panel addresses the role of new potassium binders in hyperkalemia management, a condition frequent in CKD, heart failure, diabetes, hypertension and RAAS-inhibitor use. (PMID 39604607)
- Timely recognition and appropriate treatment of acute hyperkalaemia are critical to prevent arrhythmias and death, per an evidence-based emergency-care review. (PMID 41506858)
- Emergency management of severe or ECG-manifest hyperkalemia (peaked T waves, PR prolongation, widened QRS) requires immediate intravenous calcium gluconate or calcium chloride for cardiac membrane stabilization, followed by intracellular shifting agents (IV insulin with dextrose, inhaled salbutamol), and definitive potassium removal via loop diuretics, gastrointestinal cation exchangers (patiromer, sodium zirconium cyclosilicate), or emergency hemodialysis. (PMID 33160639)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether chronic binder therapy to enable RAAS inhibitors improves hard cardiovascular outcomes is not yet proven.
- Guideline heterogeneity in thresholds reflects absent trial-level evidence for specific cut-offs.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Confirming true hyperkalaemia and the pseudohyperkalaemia work-up.
- Newer binders: mechanism, onset, place in therapy.
- Guideline heterogeneity and what it means for exam answers.
- Integrated acute-plus-chronic management strategy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient stops enalapril every time potassium rises; propose a binder-enabled strategy to preserve therapy.
- A haemolysed sample shows K+ 6.2 in an asymptomatic patient; outline the confirmation pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: Binder head-to-head efficacy data and cost-effectiveness are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full expert-panel publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33160639**: *Clinical management of hyperkalemia.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 39604607**: *Hyperkalemia management: a multidisciplinary expert panel's perspective on new potassium binders.* (2024) [expert-panel] - None
- **PMID 41506858**: *Acute hyperkalaemia in emergency care: evidence-based approaches.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0867] Hypermobility spectrum disorders and hEDS: Beighton score, management, POTS association

**Cluster**: hypermobility disorders | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypermobility spectrum disorders and hypermobile Ehlers-Danlos syndrome occupy the space between asymptomatic joint laxity and connective-tissue disease, and they are diagnosed late because they demand heightened clinical suspicion rather than a single test. The Beighton score quantifies laxity, but the clinical burden is systemic: gastrointestinal dysmotility and disorders of gut-brain interaction, autonomic dysfunction including POTS, chronic pain and fatigue. Modern guidance recognises this multi-system reality, with AGA guidance addressing the GI-autonomic overlap that drives much of the quality-of-life impact.

### Core Teaching Points
- AGA guidance addresses evaluation and management of gut-brain-interaction disorders and autonomic or immune dysfunction coexisting with hEDS or hypermobility spectrum disorders, including POTS. (PMID 40387691)
- hEDS is a connective tissue disorder often diagnosed only after delays due to reliance on heightened clinical suspicion; hypermobility spectrum disorders bridge asymptomatic joint hypermobility and hEDS. (PMID 42098539)
- Generalized joint hypermobility is clinically quantified using the 9-point Beighton score, with thresholds of >=5 in adults under 50 indicating significant joint laxity. (PMID 40387691)
- Hypermobile Ehlers-Danlos syndrome (hEDS) frequently overlaps with autonomic dysfunction (Postural Orthostatic Tachycardia Syndrome [POTS]) and functional gastrointestinal dysmotility, requiring coordinated multidisciplinary rehabilitation and avoidance of joint-straining high-impact exercises. (PMID 40387691, PMID 42098539)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Diagnostic boundaries between HSD, hEDS and other heritable connective-tissue disorders remain clinical-judgement territory.
- Evidence for most management interventions is low-certainty, leaning on expert consensus.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Beighton score components and the 2017 diagnostic criteria framework.
- HSD versus hEDS: what separates them clinically.
- POTS association and autonomic dysfunction recognition.
- Multi-system management: physiotherapy-led, symptom-targeted care.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A young woman with joint laxity, palpitations on standing and GI symptoms asks if 'it's all connected'; frame the diagnosis and work-up.
- A patient with suspected hEDS is referred for POTS; outline the stand-test pathway and management options.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): Beighton score numerical detail, the full 2017 diagnostic criteria and exercise/physiotherapy protocols are not covered. Verify against the international consortium criteria and current reviews before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40387691**: *AGA Clinical Practice Update on GI manifestations and autonomic or immune dysfunction in hypermobile Ehlers-Danlos syndrome and hypermobility spectrum disorders.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 42098539**: *Gastrointestinal manifestations of hypermobile Ehlers-Danlos syndrome and hypermobility spectrum disorders.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0868] hypernatraemia

**Cluster**: hypernatraemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypernatraemia is a disorder of water balance, not sodium excess: total-body water deficit relative to sodium, generated either because intake failed (impaired thirst, no access, intubation) or because renal or extrarenal losses outpaced it. That framing makes the diagnostic approach logical — assess volume status, then urine osmolality and sodium — and the treatment dangerous if rushed: chronic hypernatraemia must be corrected slowly to avoid cerebral oedema, while acute-onset hypernatraemia can be corrected faster. Most cases signal an underlying access or consciousness problem and carry real mortality when mismanaged.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypernatremia is a disorder of the homeostatic balance of body water and sodium content, and that imbalance is the basis of the diagnostic approach. (PMID 36578134)
- In chronic dysnatremias a slow correction rate (<10 mmol/L/24 h) is indicated to avoid neurological complications, whereas acute dysnatremias (<48 h onset) may justify more rapid correction. (PMID 31138251)
- Water deficit should be calculated ([Target Na - Current Na] / Current Na * Total Body Water) and replaced predominantly with free water (oral/enteral water or IV 5% dextrose) while matching ongoing urinary and insensible losses. (PMID 36578134)
- In chronic hypernatremia (>48 hours), the correction rate must not exceed 8-10 mmol/L in 24 hours to prevent fatal cerebral edema resulting from intracellular idiogenic osmolyte swelling. (PMID 36578134, PMID 31138251)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The safe correction ceiling in individual patients, especially with coexisting hyponatraemia risk factors, is approximated rather than precise.
- Whether desmopressin adjuncts to prevent overcorrection improve outcomes is not established.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic algorithm: volume status, urine osmolality, urine sodium.
- Central versus nephrogenic diabetes insipidus and the desmopressin test.
- Correction-rate limits and cerebral oedema risk.
- Common hospital-acquired causes: inadequate free water in dependent patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An intubated patient develops sodium 158 over three days; calculate the free-water deficit and plan the correction.
- A patient with traumatic brain injury passes large volumes of dilute urine with rising sodium; localise the cause.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): the free-water-deficit formula, urine osmolality cut-offs and specific correction-fluid choices are not covered. Verify against a nephrology reference before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36578134**: *Evaluation and management of hypernatremia in adults: clinical perspectives.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 31138251**: *Acute dysnatremias: a dangerous and overlooked clinical problem.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0869] Hypersensitivity Pneumonitis

**Cluster**: hypersensitivity pneumonitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypersensitivity pneumonitis is what happens when an immune response to an inhaled antigen becomes an interstitial lung disease: complex, heterogeneous, and organised in modern guidelines around fibrotic and non-fibrotic phenotypes, which differ in course, management and prognosis. The antigen is often domestic or occupational — bird exposures, moulds, farming dusts — and the occupational-ILD literature reminds us the true burden is underestimated through under-recognition. Diagnosis is a convergence of exposure history, imaging, bronchoalveolar lavage and sometimes biopsy, and treatment begins with antigen removal.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypersensitivity pneumonitis is a complex, heterogeneous interstitial lung disease occurring when susceptible individuals develop exaggerated immune responses to inhaled antigens; guidelines organise evaluation around fibrotic and non-fibrotic phenotypes. (PMID 35140104)
- Millions of workers are exposed to substances causing occupational interstitial lung diseases, with the burden likely underestimated through under-recognition and under-reporting. (PMID 37535448)
- High-resolution chest CT typically shows centrilobular ground-glass nodules, mosaic attenuation with air trapping on expiratory imaging (headcheese sign/three-density pattern), and upper-to-mid lung zone distribution. (PMID 35140104)
- Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) characteristically demonstrates marked lymphocytosis (>20-30%, often >50%) with a decreased CD4/CD8 ratio, distinguishing HP from idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. (PMID 35140104, PMID 37535448)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Antigen identification fails in a substantial minority despite detailed exposure assessment.
- Immunosuppression benefit in fibrotic HP versus non-fibrotic HP differs and is less evidence-based than in other ILDs.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Fibrotic versus non-fibrotic HP: imaging, course and prognosis differences.
- The exposure-history-led diagnostic algorithm.
- BAL lymphocytosis and its role.
- Occupational history-taking as the highest-yield diagnostic tool.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A pigeon-fancier develops progressive dyspnoea and reticular changes; walk through diagnosis and the first management step.
- A farmer with recurrent 'winter fevers' and new ILD changes; connect the antigen to the phenotype.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific HRCT patterns, BAL thresholds and the HP diagnostic-guideline scoring system are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the 2020 ATS/JRS/ALAT HP guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35140104**: *Diagnosis, course and management of hypersensitivity pneumonitis.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 37535448**: *Occupational interstitial lung diseases.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0870] hypersensitivity pneumonitis diagnosis and antigen avoidance

**Cluster**: HP antigen avoidance | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The single most effective treatment in hypersensitivity pneumonitis is not a drug — it is removing the antigen, and it works best in the non-fibrotic phenotype before irreversible remodelling sets in. But removal only works if you find the antigen, which is why the updated clinical definition and diagnostic recommendations from the international collaboration matter so much in practice: they sharpen exposure elicitation and phenotype assignment. Where fibrosis has established, the conversation shifts to immunosuppression, antifibrotic-adjacent strategies and transplant assessment.

### Core Teaching Points
- An international collaboration has updated the clinical definition and diagnostic recommendations for hypersensitivity pneumonitis, focusing on fibrotic and non-fibrotic phenotypes, and their transfer to clinical practice is shaping management and prognosis. (PMID 33470673)
- HP ranges from acute manifestations to progressive fibrosis and is classified as nonfibrotic or fibrotic based on imaging and histopathological features, with pathophysiology driven by inhaled environmental antigens. (PMID 42781417)
- Strict environmental antigen avoidance (e.g. removing avian pets, remediating indoor mould, avoiding contaminated humidifiers) is the single most critical intervention capable of halting disease progression and avoiding lung fibrosis. (PMID 33470673, PMID 42781417)
- Systemic corticosteroids (prednisone 0.5 mg/kg daily tapered over months) improve symptoms and lung mechanics in inflammatory/non-fibrotic HP, whereas fibrotic HP requires consideration of antifibrotic therapy (nintedanib) and lung transplant evaluation. (PMID 33470673, PMID 42781417)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- How aggressively to immunosuppress fibrotic HP, and whether antifibrotic therapy has a role, remains incompletely evidence-based.
- The prognosis impact of strict antigen avoidance versus partial reduction is not quantified.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Antigen avoidance as first-line therapy, and what to do when no antigen is found.
- Fibrotic versus non-fibrotic prognostic divergence.
- Updated international diagnostic criteria and their phenotype focus.
- Escalation pathway: steroids, steroid-sparing agents, antifibrotics, transplant.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A non-fibrotic HP patient cannot identify any antigen; structure the exposure detective work.
- A fibrotic HP patient deteriorates despite antigen removal; discuss escalation options and their evidence limits.

**Reviewer Gap**: Steroid dosing regimens, steroid-sparing agent selection and antifibrotic-trial evidence are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the 2020 HP guideline and current ILD guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33470673**: *An update on hypersensitivity pneumonitis: what a clinician wants to know.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 42781417**: *Current concepts in the diagnosis and treatment of hypersensitivity pneumonitis.* (2025) [review] - None
