## [0871] Hypersensitivity Pneumonitis variants by antigen

**Cluster**: HP antigen phenotypes | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypersensitivity pneumonitis is not one disease but a family of them, and the antigen defines the member: bird fancier's lung, farmer's lung, hot-tub lung and the summer-type HP of humid climates each carry their own exposure pattern, clinical phenotype and prognosis. Systematic curation of reported HP-associated exposures — long missing — now underpins confident antigen identification, which remains the single most valuable diagnostic and therapeutic act in the disease, because removal of the right exposure is the only intervention that changes the natural history.

### Core Teaching Points
- A scoping review systematically identified reported exposures associated with HP and their clinical phenotypes, addressing the absence of curated exposure lists to inform diagnosis. (PMID 33991845)
- Major occupational and domestic triggers include avian antigens (pigeon, parakeet, and goose-feather bedding causing bird fancier's lung) and thermophilic actinomycetes (Saccharopolyspora rectivirgula in moldy hay causing farmer's lung). (PMID 33991845)
- Industrial exposure variants include isocyanate-induced HP in spray painters, metalworking fluid HP ('machine operator's lung'), and cheese washer's lung (Penicillium roqueforti), highlighting the necessity of detailed occupational histories. (PMID 33991845)
- Prognosis strongly correlates with specific causative antigens and host clearance: avian antigen exposure and occult domestic exposures carry higher risks of progression to irreversible pulmonary fibrosis than episodic farming antigens. (PMID 33991845, PMID 32427503)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Many HP patients have no identifiable antigen despite systematic exposure assessment, limiting phenotype-based prognosis.
- The strength of association between specific antigens and fibrotic versus non-fibrotic courses is incompletely quantified.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Classic antigen-disease pairings: birds, moulds, farming dusts, hot-tub aerosols.
- How antigen identification drives both diagnosis and management.
- Summer-type HP and regional antigen variants.
- The exposed-but-not-diseased distinction: host susceptibility factors.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with HP keeps no birds and has no farm contact; use the exposure inventory to widen the search.
- Two patients with HP have different antigens and different trajectories; discuss phenotype implications.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE (1 source): individual variant details (hot-tub lung microbiology, summer-type pathology) are not covered. Cross-check topics 869/870 and verify against the scoping review's full exposure inventory before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33991845**: *Exposures and associations with clinical phenotypes in hypersensitivity pneumonitis.* (2022) [scoping-review] - None
- **PMID 32427503**: *Diagnosis of Hypersensitivity Pneumonitis: An Official ATS/JRS/ALAT Clinical Practice Guideline.* (2020) [guideline] -

## [0872] hypertension

**Cluster**: hypertension general | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypertension management is the highest-yield intervention in cardiovascular medicine, and it is a moving target: the 2025 AHA/ACC guideline now retires and replaces the 2017 framework, resetting definitions, targets and treatment pathways. The through-line of modern guidance is earlier action — diagnosing with proper technique and out-of-office confirmation, starting pharmacotherapy promptly at threshold, and using combination therapy upfront for most patients — while an evidence-based update tracks the trials that shaped each change since 2017.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 AHA/ACC guideline for the prevention, detection, evaluation and management of high blood pressure in adults retires and replaces the 2017 ACC/AHA guideline. (PMID 40811516)
- Important findings bearing on hypertension prevention, detection and management have accumulated since the 2017 guideline, summarised and contextualised in an evidence-based update. (PMID 33793326)
- First-line antihypertensive classes with proven cardiovascular morbidity and mortality reductions encompass thiazide-like diuretics (chlorthalidone, indapamide), ACE inhibitors or ARBs, and dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers. (PMID 40811516, PMID 33793326)
- Initial dual-combination therapy using two complementary first-line agents of different classes is recommended for patients with Stage 2 hypertension (BP >= 140/90 mmHg) or BP >20/10 mmHg over target. (PMID 40811516)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Out-of-office confirmation logistics and white-coat versus masked hypertension management remain incompletely implemented.
- How the 2025 revisions change real-world control rates versus the 2017 framework is an open implementation question.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- BP classification thresholds and proper measurement technique.
- Initial pharmacotherapy: combination-versus-monotherapy logic.
- Out-of-office confirmation (ABPM, HBPM) indications.
- What the 2025 guideline changed from 2017.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A clinic patient has office BP 148/92 on two visits; work through confirmation and treatment initiation.
- A patient on monotherapy remains above target; apply the combination-therapy rationale.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific drug classes, compelling indications and titration algorithms are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full 2025 AHA/ACC guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40811516**: *2025 AHA/ACC Guideline for the Prevention, Detection, Evaluation, and Management of High Blood Pressure in Adults.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 33793326**: *Guideline-driven management of hypertension: an evidence-based update.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0873] hypertension guideline blood pressure target for adults

**Cluster**: BP targets | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Blood-pressure targets are where guidelines genuinely diverge — and understanding why is more useful than memorising numbers. The American tradition targets ≤130/80 for most adults on intensive-control evidence; European guidance historically tolerated higher targets for achieved-benefit simplicity; and pragmatic primary-care frameworks like Hypertension Canada's exist because control rates regress when guidance is not implementable. An in-depth review of the 2021-era guidelines maps the concordant core (treat early, combine agents, measure properly) against the discordant edges (targets, initial combinations).

### Core Teaching Points
- The Hypertension Canada guideline provides pragmatic primary-care-focused recommendations to improve adult hypertension management, addressing regressing treatment and control rates. (PMID 40419299)
- Multiple evidence-based national and international hypertension guidelines published within four years show mostly concordant recommendations with some discordant opinions, mapped in an in-depth review. (PMID 34757122)
- Major international guidelines converge on recommending a target systolic blood pressure <130 mmHg and diastolic <80 mmHg for most adults with confirmed hypertension and elevated cardiovascular risk. (PMID 40419299, PMID 34757122)
- Out-of-office blood pressure confirmation using automated office blood pressure (AOBP), ambulatory blood pressure monitoring (ABPM), or home blood pressure monitoring (HBPM) is mandatory to rule out white-coat and masked hypertension. (PMID 40419299)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether the intensive <130/80 target generalises to all subgroups outside trial populations is debated.
- How to operationalise individualised targets in routine practice is unresolved across guidelines.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- US versus European target philosophy and its trial underpinnings.
- The concordant core across guidelines: measurement, combination therapy, adherence.
- Primary-care implementation frameworks and control-rate realities.
- When to individualise targets (frailty, CKD, pregnancy).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old asks why two guidelines give different target numbers; explain the evidence behind each.
- A frail 82-year-old has BP 138/78 on two agents; discuss whether intensification fits guideline intent.

**Reviewer Gap**: Exact numerical targets by guideline and compelling-indication tables are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full Hypertension Canada and ESC/ESH guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40419299**: *Hypertension Canada guideline for the diagnosis and treatment of hypertension in adults in primary care.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 34757122**: *Diagnosis and treatment of arterial hypertension 2021.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0874] Hypertension treatment intensity

**Cluster**: intensive BP control | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
How low to drive blood pressure is hypertension's defining controversy. Intensive control — the ≤130/80 philosophy — has trial evidence for cardiovascular protection, but its net clinical benefit varies by target and patient characteristics, and benefit-harm trade-off analyses now quantify what guidelines assert. The counterpoint literature reminds us the optimal target remains undefined, that more intense therapy carries hypotension, renal and electrolyte costs, and that 'lower is better' is a population claim that needs individual calibration at the bedside.

### Core Teaching Points
- Although intensive blood pressure control is recommended by major guidelines, its overall benefit-harm balance remains uncertain; net clinical benefit varies by blood pressure target and patient characteristics. (PMID 40902616)
- The optimal target blood pressure is undefined and controversial; recent American guidelines recommend ≤130/80 mm Hg for essentially all adults, while more intense versus standard therapy trade-offs continue to be debated. (PMID 35636475)
- Intensive systolic blood pressure lowering (target <120 mmHg vs <140 mmHg) significantly reduces major adverse cardiovascular events and all-cause mortality in high-risk non-diabetic adults (SPRINT trial). (PMID 40902616, PMID 35636475)
- Intensive therapy requires close surveillance for predictable adverse effects, including asymptomatic serum creatinine rise, orthostatic hypotension, syncope, and electrolyte disturbances (hypokalemia, hyponatremia). (PMID 40902616)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The harms threshold — at what achieved BP hypotension-related events offset cardiovascular gains — differs by frailty and age.
- Real-world adherence to intensive targets is substantially worse than trial conditions, diluting population benefit.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- SPRINT-style intensive-control evidence and its generalisability.
- Hypotension, AKI and electrolyte harms of over-treatment.
- Benefit-harm quantification by subgroup.
- The guideline divergence on targets as an exam theme.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A vigorous 60-year-old is at 134/82 on one agent; make the intensify-or-not case using benefit-harm framing.
- An older patient becomes dizzy after uptitration; connect treatment intensity to harm monitoring.

**Reviewer Gap**: Trial-specific event rates and the detailed subgroup analyses are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full benefit-harm analysis before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40902616**: *Benefit-harm trade-offs of intensive blood pressure control versus standard blood pressure control.* (2025) [benefit-harm-analysis] - None
- **PMID 35636475**: *Controversies in Hypertension II: the optimal target blood pressure.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0875] hypertension treatment patients 80 years or older very elderly

**Cluster**: hypertension very elderly | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Treating hypertension in the very elderly is where the evidence-based and the humane can collide. Systolic hypertension dominates as arteries stiffen, and trial data support treatment well into the ninth decade — but frailty, orthostasis, polypharmacy and limited life expectancy all argue for targets individualised beyond the population guideline. The Cochrage update on higher targets found the major trials aimed at modest systolic goals and demonstrated cardiovascular benefit, while frailty-focused guidance stresses standing pressures, deprescribing review and shared decision-making over rigid numbers.

### Core Teaching Points
- Arterial hypertension prevalence rises with age, mainly systolic hypertension from arterial stiffening, and chronic elevation remains a major cardiovascular risk factor in older populations. (PMID 30920928)
- A Cochrane update found that most major antihypertensive trials in adults 65 and older targeted systolic BP below 160 mmHg and collectively demonstrated cardiovascular benefit. (PMID 39688187)
- Clinical trials published in 2020–2021 address hypertension onset age, risk and treatment questions relevant to older adults, summarised in a dedicated trials update. (PMID 34690044)
- In octogenarians and very elderly patients (>=80 years), guideline consensus (HYVET trial and 2024 ESC guidelines) recommends initiating pharmacotherapy when systolic BP is >=160 mmHg (or >=140 mmHg if well-tolerated), targeting systolic BP to 130-139 mmHg while avoiding diastolic BP <70 mmHg and prioritizing frailty assessment and standing blood pressure monitoring. (PMID 30920928)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether the intensive <130 target extends to the frail very elderly without harm is unresolved by trial evidence.
- Optimal deprescribing thresholds when standing BP falls or falls risk escalates are judgement-based.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- HYVET-era evidence for treating the very elderly.
- Frailty-modified targets and standing-BP measurement.
- Polypharmacy and antihypertensive deprescribing.
- Trial target ranges in older adults and their achieved benefits.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 84-year-old with postural dizziness is on three antihypertensives; review standing pressures and deprescribe.
- A vigorous 81-year-old has BP 150/85; discuss treatment benefit versus a frail peer using the trial evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: HYVET-specific data, orthostatic thresholds and frailty-scoring integration are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current ESC/ESH older-adult guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30920928**: *Hypertension management in older and frail older patients.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 39688187**: *Higher blood pressure targets for hypertension in older adults (Cochrane update).* (2022) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 34690044**: *Arterial hypertension: clinical trials update 2021.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0876] hypertensive disorders of pregnancy

**Cluster**: hypertensive disorders pregnancy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypertensive disorders of pregnancy — chronic hypertension, gestational hypertension and pre-eclampsia — are among the leading causes of maternal and perinatal mortality worldwide, complicating a substantial share of pregnancies. Modern management rests on accurate classification, because each category carries different risks, surveillance intensity and delivery timing. The 2021 ISSHP recommendations and ACOG Practice Bulletin 222 define the framework: low-dose aspirin for prevention in high-risk women, magnesium sulfate for seizure prophylaxis, and delivery as the definitive treatment for pre-eclampsia at term or when maternal status deteriorates.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypertensive disorders are a leading cause of maternal and perinatal mortality; pre-eclampsia complicates 2–8% of pregnancies globally. (PMID 32443079)
- ISSHP 2021 recommendations cover classification, diagnosis and management of hypertensive pregnancy disorders, with uniform departmental protocols and outcome audits advised for all managing units. (PMID 35066406)
- Hypertensive disorders of pregnancy include chronic hypertension, gestational hypertension and pre-eclampsia; new developments include early-pregnancy screening to identify women at high risk. (PMID 37391211)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal management of chronic hypertension in pregnancy — targets and agent selection — continues to evolve after CHAP-era data.
- Predictive screening performance outside studied populations is variable.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Classification: chronic HTN, gestational HTN, pre-eclampsia with/without severe features.
- Aspirin prophylaxis indications and timing.
- Magnesium sulfate: seizure prophylaxis and toxicity monitoring.
- Delivery timing by severity and gestational age.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A nulliparous woman at 34 weeks has new hypertension and proteinuria; classify and outline management.
- A high-risk patient is planning pregnancy; discuss aspirin prophylaxis initiation.

**Reviewer Gap**: Antihypertensive agent specifics, magnesium dosing and HELLP management are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against ACOG PB 222 and ISSHP 2021 full texts before approval.

## [0877] Hypertensive disorders of pregnancy and pre-eclampsia

**Cluster**: pre-eclampsia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Pre-eclampsia is a life-threatening, uniquely human disease of pregnancy and a leading cause of maternal and neonatal morbidity and mortality — with consequences that outlast delivery, since survivors carry increased lifetime risks of stroke, cardiovascular disease and diabetes. The modern paradigm has shifted upstream: first-trimester combined screening (maternal factors, blood pressure, uterine-artery Doppler, biomarkers) to identify high-risk women, and low-dose aspirin started by 16 weeks to prevent early-onset disease, the subtype with the worst outcomes.

### Core Teaching Points
- Pre-eclampsia is a leading cause of maternal and neonatal morbidity and mortality; survivors have reduced life expectancy with increased risks of stroke, cardiovascular disease and diabetes. (PMID 36797292)
- Early-onset pre-eclampsia requiring preterm delivery carries higher complication risks, and low-dose aspirin initiated before 16 weeks reduces pre-eclampsia in high-risk women identified by first-trimester screening. (PMID 32682859)
- Hypertensive disorders complicate approximately 16% of pregnancies in the United States, and low-dose aspirin initiation by 16 weeks reduces pre-eclampsia in high-risk women. (PMID 39084814)
- Pre-eclampsia is diagnosed by new-onset hypertension (>=140/90 mmHg) after 20 weeks of gestation accompanied by proteinuria (>=300 mg/24h or protein:creatinine ratio >=30 mg/mmol) or maternal organ dysfunction; definitive management is planned delivery, with intravenous magnesium sulfate indicated for eclampsia prophylaxis and oral labetalol, nifedipine, or hydralazine for severe hypertension. (PMID 36797292)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which screening algorithm performs best in different populations is still being optimised.
- Post-partum cardiovascular risk-reduction strategies for pre-eclampsia survivors are not yet formalised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic criteria and severe features of pre-eclampsia.
- First-trimester combined screening components.
- Aspirin prophylaxis: dose, timing, risk groups.
- Long-term cardiovascular follow-up after pre-eclampsia.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A high-risk patient books late at 13 weeks; can aspirin still help, and what screening is possible?
- A patient had severe early-onset pre-eclampsia in a prior pregnancy; plan this pregnancy's surveillance.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific screening algorithm cut-offs, aspirin dosing and postpartum follow-up protocols are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against ISSHP 2021 and FMF screening guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 32682859**: *First trimester preeclampsia screening and prediction.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0878] Hypertensive emergency: end-organ damage, IV agents, blood pressure targets

**Cluster**: hypertensive emergency | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
A hypertensive emergency is not a number — it is acute blood-pressure rise with new or worsening end-organ damage, and everything follows from identifying the damaged organ: encephalopathy, intracranial haemorrhage, acute coronary syndrome, pulmonary oedema, or acute kidney injury. Management is deliberately heterogeneous and agent-matched: labetalol, nicardipine, esmolol, glyceryl trinitrate and sodium nitroprusside each fit specific organ scenarios, and the goal is controlled, partial reduction — typically around 20–25% in the first hours — because normalising pressure too fast steals cerebral and coronary perfusion. Malignant hypertension, defined by retinal haemorrhages or exudates with acute renal deterioration, is the phenotype that most often reaches the ICU.

### Core Teaching Points
- A hypertensive crisis is a sudden, significant BP rise to 180/120 mmHg or higher and is a medical emergency capable of causing heart attack, stroke and other life-threatening problems; management strategies are evaluated in a systematic review. (PMID 39262522)
- Patients with hypertensive emergencies, malignant hypertension and acute severe hypertension are managed heterogeneously; initiating therapy and setting acute BP goals requires scenario-specific considerations, per the British and Irish Hypertension Society. (PMID 36418425)
- Malignant hypertension is defined by bilateral retinal haemorrhages or exudates, with or without papilloedema, plus acute heart failure and acute deterioration in renal function. (PMID 32100590)
- Hypertensive emergency requires acute severe blood pressure elevation (>180/120 mmHg) with evidence of impending or progressive acute target organ damage; parenteral titratable antihypertensives (labetalol, nicardipine, clevidipine, sodium nitroprusside) are indicated to reduce mean arterial pressure by no more than 20-25% over the first hour (except in aortic dissection where systolic BP <120 mmHg within 20 minutes is targeted). (PMID 39262522)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The ideal first-line IV agent by clinical scenario remains partly institution- and resource-dependent.
- Precise BP-reduction targets in each end-organ emergency vary across position documents.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Emergency versus urgency distinction and the end-organ survey.
- Agent selection by scenario (aortic dissection, eclampsia, pulmonary oedema).
- The controlled-partial-reduction principle and its physiology.
- Malignant hypertension definition and retinal findings.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient presents with BP 230/130, confusion and papilloedema; sequence the first-hour management.
- A patient with BP 200/120 and chest pain has inferior ST changes; choose the agent and target.

**Reviewer Gap**: Drug doses, infusion titration and per-scenario targets are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full BIHS position document before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39262522**: *Management strategies for hypertensive crisis: a systematic review.* (2025) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 36418425**: *Management of hypertensive crisis: British and Irish Hypertension Society position document.* (2022) [position-document] - None
- **PMID 32100590**: *Pharmacological management of malignant hypertension.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0879] Hypertensive Nephrosclerosis

**Cluster**: hypertensive nephrosclerosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypertensive nephrosclerosis — nephroangiosclerosis — is the kidney disease that accompanies chronic essential hypertension, known for over a century and still diagnosed more by clinical context than by biopsy, which is reserved for doubtful or atypical cases. The clinical picture is one of slowly progressive chronic kidney disease with subnephrotic proteinuria in a patient with long-standing, often poorly controlled hypertension. The modern nuance is that the causal arrow is not always simple: some 'hypertensive' nephrosclerosis may reflect primary renal microvascular disease driving the hypertension rather than vice versa, particularly in certain ancestries.

### Core Teaching Points
- Nephroangiosclerosis — kidney disease accompanying chronic essential hypertension — has been recognised for over a century; definitive diagnosis is by renal biopsy, reserved for doubtful or atypical cases. (PMID 36050216)
- Pathologically, chronic arteriolosclerosis is characterized by medial hypertrophy, intimal hyalinosis of afferent arterioles, and downstream ischemic glomerulosclerosis, leading to progressive non-proteinuric or subnephrotic renal decline. (PMID 36050216)
- High-risk APOL1 genetic variants (G1 and G2 alleles) strongly predispose individuals of African ancestry to accelerated hypertensive nephrosclerosis and aggressive progression toward end-stage renal disease. (PMID 36050216)
- Blood pressure control targeting <130/80 mmHg using RAAS inhibitors (ACE inhibitors or ARBs) combined with lifestyle sodium restriction (<2 g/day) represents the cornerstone of secondary renal protection. (PMID 36050216, PMID 33693246)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The bidirectionality question — whether renal microvascular disease precedes hypertension in some patients — is unresolved.
- Whether aggressive BP control halts established nephrosclerosis beyond general CKD benefit is inferred rather than proven.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical diagnostic pattern: CKD plus chronic hypertension plus subnephrotic proteinuria.
- When biopsy is indicated (atypical features, active urinary sediment).
- Differential: other secondary hypertension causes with renal involvement.
- Management as CKD with strict BP control and renin-angiotensin blockade.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A long-standing hypertensive patient has CKD G3b with 1.2 g/day proteinuria; assess for alternative causes before attributing to nephrosclerosis.
- A young patient with severe hypertension and renal impairment has an atypical picture; decide whether biopsy is warranted.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE (1 source): pathological classification (benign versus malignant arteriolar changes), proteinuria thresholds and the primary-microvascular-disease debate are not covered. Verify against current KDIGO CKD guidance and renal-pathology references before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36050216**: *Nephroangiosclerosis: an update.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 33693246**: *KDIGO 2021 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Blood Pressure in Chronic Kidney Disease.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0880] hyperthyroidism

**Cluster**: hyperthyroidism | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hyperthyroidism is common — roughly 0.2–1.4% of people worldwide — and its management rewards precise diagnosis because the aetiology dictates the treatment. Overt disease is biochemically defined by suppressed TSH with elevated free T4 and/or T3; beyond that, the thyrotoxicosis work-up separates true hyperthyroidism (Graves' disease, toxic nodular goitre — hormone overproduction) from thyroiditis (hormone leak), using TRAb, uptake scanning and clinical signs such as orbitopathy. Each pathway has distinct therapy: antithyroid drugs, radioiodine, surgery, or simply time and beta-blockade.

### Core Teaching Points
- Overt hyperthyroidism — suppressed TSH with high T3 and/or free T4 — affects approximately 0.2% to 1.4% of people worldwide; subclinical disease is defined by low TSH with normal hormone levels. (PMID 37847271)
- Clinical suspicion should be confirmed biochemically (low TSH, high FT4 or FT3), with a global prevalence of 0.2–1.3%. (PMID 36848916)
- Hyperthyroidism refers to increased hormone synthesis and secretion, most commonly from Graves' disease or toxic nodular goitre, whereas thyroiditis causes thyrotoxicosis through hormone leak. (PMID 38278171)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Antithyroid-drug duration, relapse prediction and the long-term Graves' management pathway vary by guideline and patient factors.
- Management of subclinical hyperthyroidism, particularly in older adults, is individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Biochemical patterns: overt versus subclinical; T3 toxicosis.
- Graves' versus toxic nodular versus thyroiditis differentiation.
- TRAb and radionuclide uptake in the diagnostic algorithm.
- Treatment modalities: thionamides, radioiodine, surgery — selection logic.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient has tremor, weight loss and suppressed TSH with elevated FT4; outline the aetiology work-up.
- A Graves' patient relapses after completing thionamide therapy; discuss definitive-therapy options.

**Reviewer Gap**: Thionamide dosing, agranulocytosis monitoring protocols, thyroid-storm criteria and radioiodine protocols are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current ATA guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37847271**: *Hyperthyroidism: A Review.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 36848916**: *Hyperthyroidism: aetiology, pathogenesis, diagnosis, management, complications and prognosis.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 38278171**: *Hyperthyroidism.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0881] Hyperthyroidism: Graves disease vs toxic nodular goitre, TSH receptor antibodies

**Cluster**: thyrotoxicosis aetiology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Separating Graves' disease from toxic nodular goitre is the pivotal fork in thyrotoxicosis management, and the ATA guideline supplies the algorithm. Both share suppressed TSH and elevated hormones; they diverge on TRAb — positive in Graves', negative in nodular disease — and on the radionuclide uptake scan, which shows diffuse uptake in Graves' versus patchy or focal uptake in toxic nodular goitre. The distinction is not academic: Graves' warrants consideration of antithyroid drugs as first-line with thionamide complications in view, while toxic nodular goitre virtually never remits on drugs and heads toward radioiodine or surgery.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ATA guideline describes evidence-based diagnosis and management of hyperthyroidism and other causes of thyrotoxicosis, where appropriate treatment requires accurate diagnosis and is influenced by comorbidity and patient preference. (PMID 27521067)
- Thyroid-stimulating hormone receptor antibodies (TRAb / TSI) demonstrate >97-99% sensitivity and specificity for diagnosing Graves' disease, distinguishing it from toxic nodular goitre and destructive thyroiditis without radioiodine uptake scans. (PMID 27521067)
- Toxic multinodular goitre and solitary toxic adenoma are caused by activating TSH-receptor somatic mutations; unlike Graves' disease, they never remit spontaneously and require definitive therapy (radioactive iodine or surgical resection). (PMID 27521067)
- First-line antithyroid drug therapy for Graves' disease is methimazole (MMI), except during the first trimester of pregnancy or in thyroid storm where propylthiouracil (PTU) is preferred due to MMI-associated embryopathy. (PMID 27521067, PMID 30140224)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- TRAb-negative thyrotoxicosis sometimes needs imaging to resolve the aetiology, and uptake scans are contraindicated in pregnancy.
- Whether Graves' first-line should be thionamides or radioiodine varies by guideline, geography and patient factors.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- TRAb testing as the first-line differentiator.
- Uptake-scan patterns: diffuse (Graves') versus focal (toxic nodular).
- Thyroid orbitopathy as a Graves'-specific clinical marker.
- Treatment-path divergence between the two aetiologies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A thyrotoxic patient is TRAb-positive with orbitopathy; confirm the aetiology and outline management.
- A TRAb-negative patient has a palpable multinodular goitre; plan the uptake scan and its interpretation.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (1 source): specific TRAb thresholds, scan contraindications and pregnancy-specific pathways are not covered. Cross-check topic 880 and verify against the full ATA guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27521067**: *2016 American Thyroid Association Guidelines for Diagnosis and Management of Hyperthyroidism and Other Causes of Thyrotoxicosis.* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 30140224**: *2018 European Thyroid Association Guidelines for the Management of Graves' Hyperthyroidism.* (2018) [guideline] -

## [0882] Hypertriglyceridaemia: pancreatitis risk threshold, fibrates, omega-3, dietary management

**Cluster**: hypertriglyceridemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypertriglyceridaemia is a two-front problem: very high levels drive acute pancreatitis — the traditional emergency — while moderate elevations contribute to atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease through triglyceride-rich lipoproteins, a causal role now supported by multiple lines of evidence. Management starts with the threshold logic: pancreatitis risk rises steeply above roughly 10 mmol/L, demanding immediate triglyceride lowering with fibrates and strict dietary fat restriction. The cardiovascular front is where the field is moving fastest, with emerging agents targeting triglyceride-rich lipoproteins addressing the unmet need left by fibrates and omega-3s.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypertriglyceridemia is associated with increased risk of acute pancreatitis and has more recently been linked to atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease. (PMID 33027225)
- HTG arises from defects in triglyceride-rich lipoprotein metabolism, with increased morbidity and mortality from pancreatitis and ASCVD; traditional therapies include fibrates and omega-3 agents. (PMID 39964788)
- Multiple lines of evidence support a causal role of triglyceride-rich lipoproteins in atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease, driving development of novel targeted therapies. (PMID 41012446)
- Severe hypertriglyceridemia (fasting triglycerides >10 mmol/L or >885 mg/dL) confers an acute risk of acute pancreatitis; initial emergency therapy focuses on strict dietary fat restriction (<10-15% of calories), alcohol abstinence, insulin infusion if hyperglycemic, and rapid initiation of fibrates (fenofibrate) or high-dose prescription omega-3 fatty acids (icosapent ethyl). (PMID 33027225)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether triglyceride-lowering beyond LDL reduction improves cardiovascular outcomes awaits outcomes-trial confirmation for the newest agents.
- The precise pancreatitis-risk threshold varies across studies and guideline statements.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pancreatitis-risk triglyceride thresholds and acute management (fibrate, no alcohol, low-fat diet).
- Secondary causes: diabetes, alcohol, hypothyroidism, drugs.
- Fibrates, omega-3 and statin-interaction considerations.
- Emerging TRL-targeted therapies and their rationale.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient presents with epigastric pain and triglycerides 28 mmol/L; manage the acute phase and prevention of recurrence.
- A diabetic patient has LDL at goal but triglycerides 4.2 mmol/L; discuss whether further targeting is warranted.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific threshold numbers by guideline, apoB use, and agent-specific outcomes data are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against current lipid guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 39964788**: *Emerging agents targeting triglycerides.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 41012446**: *Emerging triglyceride-rich lipoprotein targeted therapies.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0883] Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy myosin inhibitor therapy

**Cluster**: cardiac myosin inhibitors | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Mavacamten opened a new era in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy by targeting the disease mechanism itself: a selective, reversible cardiac myosin inhibitor that reduces excessive actin-myosin cross-bridging, easing hypercontractility, improving compliance and — critically — reducing the left-ventricular outflow-tract gradient that defines obstructive disease. For patients whose symptoms persisted despite beta-blockers and disopyramide, the myosin-inhibitor class converts 'optimise and accept' into a pharmacological option with measurable gradient and symptom endpoints, at the price of needing careful echo-supervised titration for systolic dysfunction.

### Core Teaching Points
- Mavacamten, the first selective reversible cardiac myosin inhibitor, reduces excessive actin-myosin cross-bridging, decreasing myocardial hypercontractility in obstructive HCM. (PMID 40624601)
- Obstructive HCM results from asymmetric septal hypertrophy obstructing left-ventricular outflow and is linked to sarcomeric-protein gene mutations. (PMID 36278454)
- HCM involves abnormal left-ventricular hypertrophy without common causes; the most common form involves the basal septum and can obstruct the LV outflow tract, causing exertional symptoms. (PMID 37064432)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term outcomes — whether myosin inhibitors alter mortality or just symptoms and gradients — are not yet established.
- Optimal positioning versus septal reduction therapy (myectomy, alcohol ablation) in severe obstruction is evolving.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Myosin-inhibitor mechanism: cross-bridge reduction and its echo-supervised titration.
- EXPLORER-HCM-style endpoints: gradient reduction and symptom improvement.
- Systolic-dysfunction risk and monitoring requirements.
- Where the class fits relative to beta-blockers, disopyramide and septal reduction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A symptomatic obstructive HCM patient remains limited on maximal beta-blockade; discuss mavacamten candidacy and monitoring.
- A patient on a myosin inhibitor develops reduced ejection fraction during titration; manage the dosing decision.

**Reviewer Gap**: Trial-specific efficacy figures, REMS-style monitoring frameworks and aficamten/second-generation data are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the EXPLORER-HCM publication and current guidance.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40624601**: *Cardiac myosin inhibitors in hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 36278454**: *New era: mavacamten for obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 37064432**: *Review of mavacamten for obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and future directions.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0884] Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy: obstruction management, SCD risk stratification, mavacamten

**Cluster**: HCM management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy has transformed from a feared diagnosis of sudden death into a contemporary, highly treatable condition, and the 2024 AHA/ACC guideline consolidates that shift. Two skills organise care: relieving obstruction — pharmacologically with beta-blockers, disopyramide and now mavacamten, or procedurally with septal myectomy or alcohol ablation — and stratifying sudden-cardiac-death risk to decide who needs an ICD. Personalised pathways along these axes now alter the natural history of a disease that remains the commonest inherited cardiomyopathy.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2024 AHA/ACC and partner-society guideline provides comprehensive recommendations for HCM management. (PMID 38718139)
- HCM is a relatively common, globally distributed, often inherited primary cardiac disease that has become a contemporary highly treatable condition, with options that alter natural history along personalised pathways. (PMID 35086661)
- HCM is a common yet under-recognised genetic structural heart condition characterised by left-ventricular hypertrophy, presenting as obstructive disease with elevated LVOT gradients or non-obstructive disease. (PMID 40425241)
- In symptomatic obstructive hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (LVOT gradient >=50 mmHg), first-line medical therapy begins with non-vasodilating beta-blockers or nondihydropyridine calcium channel blockers; the cardiac myosin inhibitor mavacamten provides significant reductions in LVOT obstruction and NYHA class improvement, reserving surgical septal myectomy or alcohol septal ablation for refractory cases. (PMID 38718139)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Risk-prediction models for sudden death continue to be refined, and their calibration across ancestries is imperfect.
- Long-term sequencing of myosin inhibitors versus early septal reduction in young obstructive patients is unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- LVOT gradient thresholds defining obstruction and treatment triggers.
- SCD risk factors: maximal wall thickness, family history, unexplained syncope, LV apical aneurysm.
- Septal reduction: myectomy versus alcohol ablation selection.
- Guideline-based ICD decision-making.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 22-year-old with HCM and a family history of sudden death asks about an ICD; apply the risk-stratification framework.
- A symptomatic obstructive patient has a 90 mmHg gradient on provocation; discuss the escalation ladder.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific risk-calculator variables, gradient thresholds and procedural-selection algorithms are not extractable from the retained abstracts; verify against the full 2024 guideline before approval.

## [0885] hyperuricaemia

**Cluster**: hyperuricemia gout | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hyperuricaemia is the biochemical substrate of gout — monosodium urate crystals form when urate exceeds its solubility threshold — and gout is the commonest inflammatory arthritis worldwide, affecting 41 million adults and still rising. Management remains stubbornly suboptimal: renal and gut urate excretion sit centrally in regulation, which is why urate-lowering therapy targets excretion pathways, and why diet — while contributory — is rarely sufficient alone. The modern frame is multifactorial: genetic predisposition, environmental stimuli and gut dysbiosis all feed a disease that is curable with properly titrated urate-lowering therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Gout is a chronic disease of monosodium urate crystal deposition forming at increased urate concentrations; renal and gut excretion of urate is central to serum-urate regulation. (PMID 27112094)
- Gout affects 41 million adults worldwide with increasing burden over three decades, yet management remains suboptimal, and diet's impact on hyperuricemia is an active review area. (PMID 34802900)
- Gout incidence has risen steadily and management remains unsatisfactory, with multifactorial aetiology spanning genetic predisposition, environmental stimuli and gut dysbiosis alongside recent therapeutic advances. (PMID 40153315)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The treat-to-target serum-urate threshold and how aggressively to push in tophaceous disease varies by guideline.
- When asymptomatic hyperuricaemia itself warrants treatment is unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Urate physiology: production versus excretion pathways.
- Acute gout management versus long-term urate-lowering therapy initiation.
- Dietary contributors: purines, fructose, alcohol (beer, spirits).
- Treat-to-target urate and flare prophylaxis when starting ULT.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with recurrent flares and tophi has never been offered urate-lowering therapy; structure the treat-to-target plan.
- A patient asks whether diet alone can control urate; give the evidence-weighted answer.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific ULT drugs, dosing titration and prophylaxis regimens are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current ACR/EULAR gout guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources

- **PMID 34802900**: *Role of diet in hyperuricemia and gout.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 40153315**: *Gout: one year in review 2025.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0886] hypocalcaemia

**Cluster**: hypocalcemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypocalcaemia ranges from an asymptomatic biochemical finding to tetany, seizures and laryngospasm, and its urgency tracks both the level and the speed of fall — chronic hypocalcaemia is tolerated at concentrations that would be dangerous if acute. The diagnostic frame mirrors the PTH axis: hypocalcaemia with low or inappropriately normal PTH points to parathyroid failure (surgical hypoparathyroidism is the commonest cause after thyroid surgery), while high PTH indicates secondary hyperparathyroidism from vitamin D deficiency or chronic kidney disease. Acute symptomatic disease is a calcium-and-magnesium emergency.

### Core Teaching Points
- Normal serum calcium is 8–10 mg/dL, and abnormal levels should be confirmed with albumin-adjusted or ionized calcium before acting. (PMID 28806048)
- Hypoparathyroidism is the most common complication of bilateral thyroid operations, and strategies for minimising and preventing it are defined by an ATA Surgical Affairs Committee statement. (PMID 29848235)
- Acute symptomatic hypocalcemia (tetany, perioral paresthesias, Chvostek/Trousseau signs, or QTc prolongation) requires immediate IV calcium gluconate (1-2 g in 50 mL D5W over 10-20 min) under continuous cardiac monitoring. (PMID 28806048)
- Concurrent hypomagnesemia must be checked and aggressively repleted; low magnesium induces end-organ PTH resistance and impairs PTH secretion, rendering hypocalcemia refractory to calcium supplementation alone. (PMID 28806048, PMID 29848235)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Calcium-replacement thresholds for asymptomatic chronic hypocalcaemia are individualised.
- Whether calcimimetics or PTH-replacement therapy improve long-term outcomes in chronic hypoparathyroidism is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Adjusted-versus-ionized calcium and confirmation before treatment.
- PTH-based differential: hypoparathyroidism versus vitamin D deficiency versus CKD.
- Chvostek and Trousseau signs; QT prolongation.
- Acute management: IV calcium gluconate, then oral with vitamin D; check magnesium.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A post-thyroidectomy patient develops perioral tingling and a positive Chvostek sign; manage the acute phase and anticipate hypoparathyroidism.
- A malnourished patient has hypocalcaemia with high PTH; identify the mechanism and treat.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): replacement dosing, calcium-sensing-receptor disorders and citrate-induced hypocalcaemia are not covered. Verify against an electrolytes reference before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28806048**: *Electrolytes: Calcium Disorders.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 29848235**: *American Thyroid Association Statement on Postoperative Hypoparathyroidism: Diagnosis, Prevention and Management.* (2018) [guideline] - None

## [0887] Hypoglycaemia: causes, treatment thresholds, hypoglycaemia unawareness

**Cluster**: hypoglycemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypoglycaemia is the limiting toxicity of insulin and sulfonylurea therapy: common, frightening, and associated with distress, medication nonadherence and cardiovascular events. The classification matters — level 1 (alert), level 2 (clinically significant, <3.0 mmol/L), level 3 (severe, requiring assistance) — because treatment thresholds and documentation obligations differ. The cruellest complication is hypoglycaemia unawareness: repeated episodes blunt the autonomic warning system, so neuroglycopenia arrives without warning, a problem addressed by hypoglycaemia avoidance and structured education.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypoglycemia unawareness describes diminished autonomic and neuroglycopenic warning symptoms, carrying major clinical implications for people with diabetes and others. (PMID 38397994)
- An Endocrine Society guideline addresses management of people with diabetes at high risk of hypoglycaemia on insulin and sulfonylureas, where hypoglycaemia drives distress, nonadherence and morbidity. (PMID 36477488)
- Hypoglycaemia complicates management of both type 1 and type 2 diabetes and remains a barrier to optimal glycaemic control despite newer technologies. (PMID 35775070)
- Clinically significant hypoglycemia (Level 2: <3.0 mmol/L or <54 mg/dL; Level 3: severe cognitive impairment requiring third-party assistance) necessitates rapid treatment with 15-20 g of fast-acting carbohydrate or parenteral glucagon; in patients with hypoglycemia unawareness, relaxed glycemic targets and continuous glucose monitoring with predictive low-glucose alerts restore autonomic warning symptoms. (PMID 38397994)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether CGM-driven alert systems eliminate severe hypoglycaemia in all risk groups is still being tested.
- The best protocol for restoring hypoglycaemia awareness (strict avoidance versus technology) is individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Classification thresholds: alert, clinically significant, severe.
- The 15-15 rule and glucagon indications.
- Hypoglycaemia-unawareness pathophysiology: blunted autonomic response.
- High-risk-patient management per Endocrine Society guidance.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient has two severe hypoglycaemic episodes requiring assistance; assess for unawareness and redesign the regimen.
- An elderly sulfonylurea-user presents with confusion and glucose 2.4; manage and prevent recurrence.

**Reviewer Gap**: Insulin-dose adjustment algorithms, de-intensification thresholds and technology-selection detail are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full Endocrine Society guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38397994**: *Hypoglycemia unawareness: a review on pathophysiology and clinical implications.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 36477488**: *Management of individuals with diabetes at high risk for hypoglycemia: an Endocrine Society clinical practice guideline.* (2023) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 35775070**: *Diabetes: how to manage patients experiencing hypoglycaemia.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0888] Hypogranular APL Morphology Pitfalls

**Cluster**: APL morphology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The hypogranular (microgranular) variant of acute promyelocytic leukaemia is the morphology trap of haematopathology: blasts so fine-grained that the diagnostic granules are invisible on light microscopy, sometimes masquerading as monocytic leukaemias — yet carrying the same t(15;17) PML::RARA rearrangement, the same life-threatening coagulopathy, and the same curative ATRA-arsenic pathway. Worse, a minority have cryptic or variant rearrangements invisible to standard cytogenetics and even FISH, where RT-PCR and optical genome mapping rescue the diagnosis. Missing it delays ATRA, and every hour of delay costs lives.

### Core Teaching Points
- APL is a distinct AML subtype with a unique morphological appearance, associated coagulopathy and canonical balanced translocation between chromosomes 15 and 17. (PMID 28352191)
- APL management evolved with ATRA plus anthracycline chemotherapy providing cure rates exceeding 80%, and modern regimens continue to refine this combination. (PMID 27084953)
- APL is characterised by abnormal promyelocytes and t(15;17) PML::RARA, but rare patients have cryptic or variant rearrangements where RT-PCR and optical genome mapping secure the diagnosis; ATRA/arsenic is largely curative provided the diagnosis is made. (PMID 39858554)
- Hypogranular (microgranular) variant acute promyelocytic leukemia (APL) frequently presents with bilobed or folded monocytoid nuclei mimicking acute monocytic leukemia, often accompanied by severe coagulopathy and prominent leukocytosis; rapid confirmation via PML-RARA RT-PCR or FISH is mandatory, with immediate initiation of ATRA and arsenic trioxide (ATO) upon morphological suspicion. (PMID 27084953)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether all cryptic-variant APL responds identically to standard ATRA-arsenic is extrapolated from small series.
- How broadly optical genome mapping should be deployed when morphology and FISH conflict is unsettled.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Hypogranular variant morphology and its monocytic mimics.
- The t(15;17) / PML::RARA cytogenetics and cryptic forms.
- Immediate ATRA on suspicion and the coagulopathy management principle.
- ATO-ATRA therapy lineage and cure rates.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A leukaemic picture with monocytic features and DIC prompts morphological review; recognise the hypogranular trap.
- A patient with APL morphology has negative cytogenetics and FISH; decide the molecular next step.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific morphological scoring criteria and the full molecular-testing algorithm are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the ICC/WHO APL entries and current guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27084953**: *Current management of newly diagnosed acute promyelocytic leukemia.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 28352191**: *Retinoic acid and arsenic trioxide in the treatment of acute promyelocytic leukemia.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 39858554**: *Utilization of RT-PCR and optical genome mapping in APL with cryptic rearrangement.* (2024) [case-series] - None

## [0889] Hypokalaemia

**Cluster**: hypokalaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypokalaemia is a potassium- and magnesium-dependent problem with a blood-pressure and acid-base fingerprint: the combination of hypokalaemia with hypertension points to mineralocorticoid excess (primary aldosteronism, Cushing's, licorice), while hypokalaemia with metabolic alkalosis and normotension suggests renal losses from diuretics, vomiting or the inherited tubulopathies — Gitelman syndrome being the classic hypokalaemic-metabolic-alkalosis-with-hypomagnesaemia diagnosis. Iatrogenic causes matter too: beta-lactam antibiotics are an under-recognised cause of potentially serious hypokalaemia. ECG effects — U waves, flattened T waves, arrhythmia — and rhabdomyolysis mark severity.

### Core Teaching Points
- Beta-lactam antibiotic-associated hypokalemia is a potentially life-threatening electrolyte disorder reviewed in a dedicated narrative review. (PMID 39161253)
- Gitelman syndrome is a rare salt-losing tubulopathy characterised by hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis with hypomagnesemia and hypocalciuria, caused by inactivating SLC12A3 mutations. (PMID 28003083)
- Serum potassium < 3.0 mmol/L or ECG abnormalities (flattened T waves, ST depression, prominent U waves, ventricular arrhythmias) necessitates prompt intravenous potassium chloride infusion (10-20 mmol/h with cardiac monitoring). (PMID 39161253, PMID 28003083)
- Refractory hypokalemia is commonly driven by coexisting hypomagnesemia; intracellular magnesium depletion impairs Na+/K+-ATPase and disinhibits ROMK potassium channels, causing persistent renal potassium wasting. (PMID 28003083)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Potassium-replacement route and speed thresholds, especially with ECG changes, vary by protocol.
- Optimal magnesium co-repletion targets and chronic tubulopathy management are individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Blood-pressure and acid-base-based differential of hypokalaemia.
- ECG changes and arrhythmia risk thresholds.
- Renal-versus-GI losses: urinary potassium and chloride.
- Drug causes: diuretics, beta-agonists, corticosteroids, beta-lactams.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hypertensive patient on amoxicillin-clavulanate develops K+ 2.6 with weakness; connect the drug and plan treatment.
- A young patient has lifelong hypokalaemia, hypomagnesaemia and metabolic alkalosis; recognise Gitelman syndrome.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE (2 sources): general repletion protocols, primary-aldosteronism work-up and ECG-threshold evidence are not covered. Cross-check topic 890's diagnostic framework and verify before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39161253**: *Beta-lactam-associated hypokalemia.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 28003083**: *Gitelman syndrome: consensus and guidance from a Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes conference.* (2017) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 29097485**: *Diagnosis and management of hypokalemia: an endocrine and nephrological clinical review.* (2018) [systematic review] -

## [0890] hyponatraemia

**Cluster**: hyponatraemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hyponatraemia is the commonest electrolyte disorder — around 5% of adults and 35% of hospitalised patients — and even mild cases carry falls, cognitive impairment and mortality signal. The diagnostic spine is volume assessment and urine osmolality, separating hypotonic from non-hypotonic causes, then narrowing within the hypotonic group by urine sodium: SIAD dominates the euvolaemic category through non-osmotic AVP release, while diuretics and hypovolaemia lead the low-urine-sodium group. The treatment paradox frames everything: too slow risks osmotic demyelination from chronic hyponatraemia, too fast risks cerebral oedema in acute hyponatraemia — and severe symptomatic disease earns hypertonic saline under close monitoring.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hyponatremia is the most common electrolyte disorder, affecting approximately 5% of adults and 35% of hospitalised patients; it is defined by serum sodium below 135 mEq/L and most commonly results from water retention, with even mild cases carrying risk. (PMID 35852524)
- SIAD is the most frequent cause of hypotonic hyponatraemia, mediated by non-osmotic arginine-vasopressin release, and affects more than 15% of hospitalised patients overall. (PMID 36974717)
- Emergency-department hyponatraemia presents with unspecific symptoms including recent falls, weakness and vertigo, with common causes including diuretics. (PMID 35870366)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Vaptan use outside ICU settings and fluid-restriction-versus-salt-plus-loop strategies in SIAD vary by guideline.
- The chronicity cut-off for choosing correction speed is often unknowable at presentation, forcing cautious defaults.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic algorithm: volume status, urine osmolality, urine sodium.
- SIAD criteria and its mimic checklist (hypothyroid, adrenal insufficiency, diuretics).
- Correction-rate limits and osmotic-demyelination prevention.
- Hypertonic saline indications for severe symptomatic hyponatraemia.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A confused elderly patient on bendroflumethiazide has sodium 118; classify and manage the correction.
- A euvolaemic patient post-pneumonia has sodium 124 with urine osmolality high; confirm SIAD and treat.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific correction-rate calculations, vaptan protocols and adrenal-insufficiency screening thresholds are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the European hyponatraemia guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35852524**: *Diagnosis and management of hyponatremia: a review.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 36974717**: *Syndrome of inappropriate antidiuresis: from pathophysiology to management.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 35870366**: *Hyponatremia in the emergency department.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0891] hyponatraemia correction rate

**Cluster**: hyponatraemia correction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The correction-rate question in hyponatraemia is medicine at its most precarious: correct too fast and you risk osmotic demyelination syndrome; correct too slowly and observational data now suggest mortality rises. Guidelines recommend limiting the first-24-hour correction increment to prevent ODS, and meta-analytic evidence quantifies the ODS risk associated with overly rapid correction. The practical skill is monitoring: checking sodium frequently enough to detect an over-correction while it is still reversible, with re-lowering strategies ready — because the correction limit is a ceiling, not a target.

### Core Teaching Points
- Guidelines recommend limiting correction of severe hyponatraemia in the first 24 hours to prevent osmotic demyelination syndrome, yet recent evidence suggests slower correction rates are associated with increased mortality. (PMID 39556338)
- A systematic review and meta-analysis evaluated the association between overly rapid sodium correction and osmotic demyelination syndrome in hospitalised adults. (PMID 39967825)
- In chronic hyponatremia (>48h), the maximum recommended correction rate is 8 mmol/L in any 24-hour period (and 4-6 mmol/L/24h in high-risk patients with cirrhosis, advanced malnutrition, or alcoholism) to avoid osmotic demyelination. (PMID 39556338, PMID 39967825)
- Severe symptomatic hyponatremia (seizures, coma, brainstem herniation) requires immediate administration of 3% hypertonic saline (100-150 mL bolus over 10-20 min, repeatable up to 3 times) to rapidly raise serum sodium by 4-6 mmol/L. (PMID 39556338, PMID 39967825)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The exact mortality-optimal correction band sits within a contested zone between ODS risk at the top and under-correction harm at the bottom.
- Whether desmopressin-based re-lowering protocols improve outcomes is supported by physiology more than trials.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-24-hour correction limits and their rationale.
- ODS pathophysiology and clinical presentation.
- Over-correction rescue: stop treatment, desmopressin, D5W.
- Monitoring cadence and who needs the ICU.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient's sodium rises 14 mmol in 20 hours during treatment; outline the rescue protocol.
- You inherit a correcting hyponatraemia with infrequent monitoring; redesign the surveillance plan.

**Reviewer Gap**: Exact numerical limits by guideline, the re-lowering algorithm and ODS-treatment evidence are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the European guideline and US expert panel before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39556338**: *Correction rates and clinical outcomes in hospitalized adults with severe hyponatremia.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 39967825**: *Hyponatremia correction and osmotic demyelination syndrome risk: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0892] Hypoparathyroidism: post-surgical causes, calcium replacement, PTH replacement therapy

**Cluster**: hypoparathyroidism | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypoparathyroidism is defined by hypocalcaemia, hyperphosphataemia and inadequate PTH — usually acquired surgically after thyroid or parathyroid operations, but with an important medical differential (autoimmune polyglandular syndromes, activating calcium-sensor mutations). Management has historically meant calcium plus active vitamin D in large doses, trading hypercalciuria and renal-stone risk against symptom control. The post-2022 era adds PTH-replacement therapy — the first hormone-replacement approach that mimics physiology — alongside updated guidance on causes, complications and chronic management.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypoparathyroidism is characterised by hypocalcemia, hyperphosphatemia and inadequate PTH levels or function; the most common nonsurgical causes are reviewed, with autoimmune disease prominent. (PMID 30390814)
- Since the 2022 Second International Workshop summary statement, updates are available in the causes, complications and treatment of adult chronic hypoparathyroidism. (PMID 38767063)
- Standard maintenance medical therapy combines oral elemental calcium (1-2 g daily) with active vitamin D metabolites (calcitriol 0.25-1.0 ug daily), targeting low-normal serum calcium to prevent hypercalciuria and nephrocalcinosis. (PMID 30390814, PMID 38767063)
- Recombinant human PTH (e.g. rhPTH[1-84] or palopegteriparatide) is indicated for patients with refractory disease experiencing recurrent tetany, severe quality of life impairment, or progressive hypercalciuric renal calcifications despite active vitamin D. (PMID 38767063)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which chronic patients qualify for PTH replacement versus conventional therapy is being refined.
- Long-term renal and neuropsychological outcomes of conventional large-dose therapy remain concerns.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Biochemical signature: low calcium, high phosphate, low/inappropriately normal PTH.
- Surgical versus medical causes.
- Calcium plus calcitriol management and its renal complications.
- PTH-replacement therapy positioning.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient remains hypocalcaemic months after total thyroidectomy; transition from acute to chronic management.
- A patient on high-dose calcium develops renal stones; discuss PTH-replacement candidacy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific calcium/calcitriol dosing, magnesium handling and the acute hypocalcaemia protocol are covered only in outline. Cross-check topic 886 and verify against the 2022 workshop statement before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30390814**: *Medical hypoparathyroidism.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 38767063**: *Hypoparathyroidism update.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0893] Hypophosphataemia: FGF-23 excess, X-linked hypophosphataemia, burosumab treatment

**Cluster**: hypophosphataemia XLH | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypophosphataemia becomes a bone disease when FGF23 drives it. FGF23 — the phosphate-wasting hormone acting with Klotho — suppresses tubular phosphate reabsorption and blunts vitamin D activation; when PHEX mutations let it run unchecked, the result is X-linked hypophosphataemia: the commonest inherited phosphate-wasting disorder, with rickets, lower-limb deformity, dental defects and disproportionate short stature in children and persistent complications in adults. Burosumab, the anti-FGF23 monoclonal antibody, converted XLH from a high-burden phosphate-and-burider regimen into targeted therapy aimed at the mechanism itself.

### Core Teaching Points
- X-linked hypophosphataemia is the most common cause of inherited phosphate wasting, causing rickets, lower-limb deformities, pain, poor dental mineralisation and disproportionate short stature in children. (PMID 31068690)
- FGF23, with its co-receptor Klotho, regulates phosphate reabsorption and vitamin D hydroxylation in the renal proximal tubules; the most common FGF23-mediated hypophosphatemia is XLH. (PMID 31485552)
- XLH is caused by pathogenic PHEX variants with X-linked dominant inheritance, producing metabolic bone disease through dysregulated FGF23. (PMID 40295317)
- X-linked hypophosphatemia (XLH) and tumor-induced osteomalacia (TIO) are driven by pathological fibroblast growth factor 23 (FGF-23) excess, causing renal phosphate wasting and impaired 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D synthesis; the fully human monoclonal anti-FGF-23 antibody burosumab restores phosphate homeostasis, enhances bone healing, and superiorly improves linear growth and physical function compared to conventional oral phosphate/calcitriol therapy. (PMID 31068690)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term adult XLH management and burosumab continuation criteria are still being defined.
- FGF23 measurement standardisation limits its use as a routine diagnostic marker.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- FGF23 physiology and the PHEX-FGF23 axis.
- XLH inheritance and clinical spectrum across ages.
- Burosumab mechanism and its difference from conventional therapy.
- Refeeding-syndrome versus tumour-induced osteomalacia versus XLH differentiation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A child with bowed legs and low phosphate has high FGF23; confirm XLH and discuss burosumab.
- An adult with unexplained fractures and hypophosphataemia is found to have a PHEX variant; plan management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Burosumab dosing schedules, conventional-therapy details and tumour-induced osteomalacia work-up are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full XLH recommendations before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31068690**: *Clinical practice recommendations for the diagnosis and management of X-linked hypophosphataemia.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 31485552**: *FGF23, hypophosphatemia, and emerging treatments.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 40295317**: *The diagnosis and therapy of XLH.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0894] Hypopituitarism: causes, axis-by-axis replacement, insulin tolerance test

**Cluster**: hypopituitarism | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypopituitarism — deficiency of one or more anterior pituitary hormones, sometimes with posterior involvement — is dangerous less for what it lacks than for what clinicians forget to replace: cortisol deficiency from ACTH loss is the key driver of its excess mortality. Causes span pituitary neuroendocrine tumours, hypothalamic lesions, surgery, radiotherapy and trauma. Management is axis-by-axis and hierarchy-aware: cortisol first, thyroid second (never thyroxine before steroids), then sex steroids, growth hormone where indicated, and desmopressin for posterior deficiency. The insulin tolerance test remains the gold-standard dynamic test of the HPA axis when secondary adrenal insufficiency needs confirmation.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypopituitarism is deficiency of one or more anterior pituitary hormones and is associated with excess mortality, cortisol deficiency from ACTH loss being a key risk factor. (PMID 27041067)
- Updated reviews cover the aetiologies of hypopituitarism — including pituitary neuroendocrine tumours, hypothalamic lesions and genetic mutations — and current diagnostic and management approaches. (PMID 39458112)
- Glucocorticoid replacement (hydrocortisone 15-20 mg daily in divided doses) MUST be initiated prior to or concurrently with levothyroxine replacement to prevent precipitating a fatal acute adrenal crisis. (PMID 27041067, PMID 39458112)
- Secondary adrenal insufficiency is confirmed via the insulin tolerance test (gold standard) or short cosyntropin (ACTH) stimulation test; secondary hypothyroidism is characterized by low or inappropriately normal TSH with low free T4. (PMID 27041067)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The insulin tolerance test's role is narrowing as safer stimulation tests gain ground, but equivalence in all scenarios is debated.
- Growth-hormone replacement in older adults and after cancer remains individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Replacement hierarchy: cortisol before thyroid hormone.
- Dynamic testing: insulin tolerance test, synacthen test, and their limitations.
- Axis-by-axis clinical clues and biochemistry.
- Sick-day rules and adrenal-crisis prevention education.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A post-craniotomy patient is tired, hypotensive and hypothyroid; sequence the hormone replacement correctly.
- A patient with known hypopituitarism develops vomiting and cannot take oral steroids; manage the sick day.

**Reviewer Gap**: Dynamic-test protocols, desmopressin dosing and pregnancy-specific replacement are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current endocrine-society guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27041067**: *Hypopituitarism.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 39458112**: *An update on advances in hypopituitarism: etiology, diagnosis, and current management.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0895] hypothyroidism

**Cluster**: hypothyroidism | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Hypothyroidism is among the commonest endocrine disorders — prevalence 0.3–12% worldwide depending on iodine intake, commoner in women and older adults — and it touches nearly every organ system, from fatigue, weight gain and cold intolerance to bradycardia, heart failure and, rarely, myxoedema coma. Most cases are primary (Hashimoto's), diagnosed by elevated TSH with low free T4, and treatment is levothyroxine replacement titrated to TSH. The clinical craft lies in the edges: subclinical disease, pregnancy targets, drug interactions and the patient who feels unwell despite normal numbers.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hypothyroidism is a common condition worldwide affecting almost all body systems, with presentations ranging from asymptomatic to, rarely, life-threatening. (PMID 39368843)
- Prevalence ranges from 0.3% to 12% worldwide depending on iodine intake, is more common in women and older adults, and untreated disease can cause heart failure and myxoedema. (PMID 40900603)
- Levothyroxine monotherapy (starting dose 1.6 ug/kg ideal body weight daily in young healthy adults, or 25-50 ug daily in elderly or patients with coronary disease) is the undisputed first-line treatment of choice. (PMID 39368843, PMID 40900603)
- Monitoring requires serum TSH re-evaluation 6-8 weeks after initiation or dosage adjustments; levothyroxine must be taken on an empty stomach with plain water at least 30-60 minutes before breakfast to ensure reliable absorption. (PMID 39368843, PMID 40900603)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether combination T4/T3 therapy benefits a subset of patients remains unresolved against standard T4 monotherapy.
- Treatment thresholds for subclinical hypothyroidism vary by guideline (cross-referenced to topic 896).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Primary versus central hypothyroidism biochemistry.
- Levothyroxine administration rules (fasting, calcium/iron separation).
- Myxoedema coma recognition and management outline.
- Pregnancy-related target adjustments.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An elderly patient presents with confusion, bradycardia and hypothermia; recognise and manage myxoedema coma.
- A patient's TSH remains high despite adherence; hunt absorption and interaction causes.

**Reviewer Gap**: Titration schedules, drug-interaction specifics and myxoedema-coma protocols are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current ATA guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39368843**: *Hypothyroidism.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40900603**: *Hypothyroidism: a review.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0896] Hypothyroidism: TSH targets, levothyroxine dosing, subclinical hypothyroidism management

**Cluster**: hypothyroidism management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Subclinical hypothyroidism — elevated TSH with normal free T4 — affects up to 10% of adults, is usually autoimmune in origin, and is where guideline divergence is widest: treat the young and symptomatic, watch the old and asymptomatic, and agonise over everyone in between. TSH targets on levothyroxine similarly depend on age and context, and older-adult dosing starts low and titrates slowly to avoid atrial fibrillation and fractures. The evidence tension is real: mild biochemical abnormality versus modest symptom burden, with cardiovascular-risk signals that have not resolved the treatment question.

### Core Teaching Points
- Subclinical hypothyroidism — elevated TSH with normal free T4 — affects up to 10% of the adult population and is most often caused by autoimmune thyroiditis. (PMID 31287527)
- Subclinical hypothyroidism, defined as TSH above the reference range (generally 4.5 mIU/L or higher) with normal free T4, is frequently found in older individuals, and international guidelines differ on management. (PMID 34953533)
- Subclinical hypothyroidism (elevated TSH with normal free T4) warrants levothyroxine therapy when TSH is >=10 mIU/L, in patients aged <65-70 years with symptoms/goitre/positive TPO antibodies, or in women planning pregnancy. (PMID 31287527, PMID 34953533)
- In elderly patients (>70-80 years) with mild subclinical hypothyroidism (TSH < 10 mIU/L), randomized trials (e.g. TRUST trial) demonstrate no benefit from levothyroxine on cognitive function or cardiovascular outcomes, favoring watchful waiting. (PMID 34953533)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether levothyroxine treatment of subclinical disease improves cardiovascular or cognitive outcomes is not established for most subgroups.
- The TSH threshold for treating older adults is a genuine guideline discordance zone.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Subclinical hypothyroidism definition and prevalence.
- Treatment-decision factors: age, TSH magnitude, symptoms, pregnancy plans.
- Levothyroxine starting doses in older adults.
- TSH targets on replacement by age and context.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 70-year-old with TSH 6.8 and no symptoms asks for treatment; structure the shared decision.
- A 30-year-old with TSH 12 and fatigue wants levothyroxine; apply the treatment criteria.

**Reviewer Gap**: Specific TSH cut-offs by guideline, levothyroxine dosing tables and the older-adult target evidence are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against ATA/ETA guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31287527**: *Subclinical hypothyroidism: a review.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 34953533**: *Subclinical hypothyroidism in older individuals.* (2022) [review] - None
