## [0897] Hypoventilation from central CNS depressants

**Cluster**: opioid hypoventilation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Central hypoventilation from CNS depressants is an epidemic-shaped emergency: opioids suppress the respiratory drive at the brainstem, producing a characteristic slow, shallow breathing pattern that progresses to hypoxia-mediated injury. Overdose management centres on airway support and naloxone reversal — titrated to restore ventilation, not full alertness — while a growing literature examines non-opioid respiratory stimulants as adjuncts. In the perioperative and chronic-pain settings the same physiology operates more insidiously, compounded by obesity hypoventilation and benzodiazepine co-exposure, making screening and monitoring the real prevention.

### Core Teaching Points
- Opioid overdose occurs when CNS and respiratory drive are suppressed through opioid-receptor action, and acute management strategies in emergency and critical care are updated in a dedicated review. (PMID 28952972)
- Opioid-related adverse effects include respiratory depression, and countering opioid-induced respiratory depression with non-opioid respiratory stimulants is an active research area. (PMID 32089833)
- Obesity hypoventilation syndrome is underdiagnosed and carries significant perioperative risk through heightened sensitivity to CNS depressants, warranting screening and adapted management. (PMID 39274213)
- Acute central hypoventilation induced by opioids, benzodiazepines, or other central nervous system depressants causes hypercapnic respiratory failure, respiratory acidosis, and lethargy; immediate management prioritizes airway protection and bag-valve-mask ventilation, followed by titrated intravenous naloxone (for opioids) or flumazenil (cautiously in selected isolated benzodiazepine overdoses, avoiding precipitating withdrawal seizures in chronic users). (PMID 28952972)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Routine naloxone co-prescribing impact and the optimal monitoring technology for ward-based opioid use remain debated.
- Respiratory-stimulant adjuncts are investigational rather than standard care.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Opioid toxidrome recognition and naloxone dosing/titration.
- Ventilatory support principles while reversing.
- High-risk combinations: opioids plus benzodiazepines, alcohol, OHS.
- Postoperative monitoring thresholds after opioid-based anaesthesia.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An unresponsive patient has respiratory rate 6 with small pupils; sequence the airway, oxygen and naloxone plan.
- An obese OHS patient is prescribed postoperative opioids; design the safe-prescribing and monitoring plan.

**Reviewer Gap**: Naloxone infusion protocols, capnography monitoring standards and sedative-stacking risk data are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against current toxicology and anaesthesia guidance.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28952972**: *Acute opiate overdose: an update on management strategies in emergency department and critical care.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 32089833**: *Countering opioid-induced respiratory depression by non-opioids that are respiratory stimulants.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 39274213**: *Screening and perioperative management of obesity hypoventilation syndrome.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0898] IBD in Pregnancy

**Cluster**: IBD pregnancy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The single most important fact about IBD in pregnancy is that active disease harms the pregnancy far more than the medications do: quiescent disease at conception predicts a smooth course, while active flares drive preterm birth, low birth weight and pregnancy loss. The therapeutic implication runs against maternal instinct but with the evidence: continue 5-ASA, thiopurines and biologics through conception and pregnancy, adjust anti-TNF scheduling around the third trimester, and avoid methotrexate entirely. Preconception disease control is the intervention that changes outcomes.

### Core Teaching Points
- Ulcerative colitis and Crohn's disease are chronic heterogeneous inflammatory conditions of the gastrointestinal tract; BSG consensus guidelines provide adult management frameworks relevant to pregnancy planning and care. (PMID 31562236)
- Maintaining remission prior to conception and throughout gestation is paramount; active maternal bowel inflammation confers a substantially greater risk of miscarriage, preterm delivery, and low birth weight than guideline maintenance medications. (PMID 31562236)
- Biologic therapies (anti-TNF agents: infliximab, adalimumab; vedolizumab; ustekinumab) are generally safe throughout pregnancy, with active transplacental transfer occurring predominantly in the third trimester via fetal Fc receptors. (PMID 31562236)
- Methotrexate is absolutely contraindicated during pregnancy and conception (teratogenic embryopathy); tofacitinib should be avoided, whereas thiopurines (azathioprine) and 5-ASAs (except those with dibutyl phthalate coating) are safe to continue. (PMID 31562236, PMID 25770579)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal anti-TNF drug-level monitoring and third-trimester dosing schedules vary by agent and guideline.
- Small-molecule (JAK/S1P) safety data in pregnancy remain limited.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Drug safety ladder in pregnancy: safe, schedule-adjusted, contraindicated.
- Disease activity at conception as the outcome driver.
- Mode-of-delivery considerations with perianal Crohn's.
- Flare management in pregnancy without methotrexate.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient on infliximab planning pregnancy asks what to do about her medication; counsel with the evidence.
- A pregnant Crohn's patient flares in the second trimester; manage without methotrexate.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE (1 source, a general adult guideline): pregnancy-specific drug schedules, mode-of-delivery rules and breastfeeding data are not covered. Verify against the ECCO pregnancy guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31562236**: *British Society of Gastroenterology consensus guidelines on the management of inflammatory bowel disease in adults.* (2019) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 25770579**: *Second European Evidenced-Based Consensus on Reproduction and Pregnancy in Inflammatory Bowel Disease.* (2015) [guideline] -

## [0899] ICD deactivation and advanced care directives: ethical framework, shared decision-making

**Cluster**: device deactivation ethics | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
An ICD that keeps firing at the end of life can turn a peaceful death into a sequence of painful shocks — and deactivating it is legally and ethically permissible, amounting to withdrawal of treatment rather than euthanasia, since the device merely prevents arrhythmia death and does nothing else for the patient. The failure is conversational: patients, families and professionals all report discomfort initiating the discussion, so deactivation conversations happen late or never. Guidance from the Resuscitation Council and cardiovascular societies frames advance-care planning that names the device explicitly, while attitude research maps exactly where the reluctance sits.

### Core Teaching Points
- UK resuscitation, cardiovascular and palliative-care councils jointly recognise the importance of clear, consistent guidance on cardiovascular implanted electronic devices towards the end of life and around death. (PMID 27277710)
- ICDs prevent sudden cardiac death but, if not deactivated in the terminal phase, may deliver shocks that pose problems during dying; attitudes of patients, relatives and professionals toward deactivation are surveyed. (PMID 41589554)
- Proactive discussions regarding shock deactivation should occur early upon transition to palliative care or development of terminal illness to prevent traumatic, painful, and distressing shocks during the dying process. (PMID 27277710, PMID 41589554)
- ICD shock deactivation does not affect bradycardia pacing functions; patient or surrogate refusal of shock therapy is ethically and legally equivalent to refusing other life-sustaining medical interventions. (PMID 27277710)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether routine advance device planning at implantation changes end-of-life outcomes is not established.
- Decision-making capacity and surrogate-consent pathways for deactivation vary by jurisdiction.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Deactivation as withdrawal of treatment: the ethical framing.
- The advance-care-planning conversation and when to start it.
- Practical deactivation logistics and who may consent.
- Distinguishing ICD deactivation from pacemaker dependence.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A dying patient's ICD shocks repeatedly despite comfort-focused care; navigate the consent conversation.
- At implantation, a patient asks 'can this be turned off later?'; open the advance-planning dialogue.

**Reviewer Gap**: Legal jurisdiction specifics and the practical magnet-deactivation logistics are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the full joint guidance and local policy before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27277710**: *Cardiovascular implanted electronic devices in people towards the end of life, during cardiopulmonary resuscitation and after death.* (2016) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41589554**: *Attitudes of patients, relatives and professionals regarding deactivation of implantable cardioverter defibrillators.* (2025) [survey] - None

## [0900] ICD for secondary prevention: post-VF/VT indications and programming

**Cluster**: ICD secondary prevention | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Secondary-prevention ICDs are the most firmly indicated devices in electrophysiology: survivors of ventricular fibrillation or haemodynamically unstable sustained ventricular tachycardia not due to a reversible cause receive them on the strength of the landmark MADIT-lineage trials showing sudden-death reduction. Modern practice adds two refinements: programming that avoids unnecessary shocks (detection intervals and discriminators matter as much as the indication), and the expanding subcutaneous-ICD option that eliminates transvenous lead complications — with a trade-off in pacing capability that defines who qualifies for which system.

### Core Teaching Points
- Implantable devices are indicated in primary and secondary prevention of life-threatening ventricular tachyarrhythmias; early studies including the landmark MADIT trials established ICD benefit. (PMID 26910804)
- ICDs reduce sudden cardiac death in primary and secondary prevention; transvenous implantation carries lead-related complications that the subcutaneous approach avoids, with selection trade-offs. (PMID 35179615)
- Implantable cardioverter-defibrillators (ICDs) are indicated for secondary prevention in survivors of cardiac arrest due to VF or hemodynamically unstable sustained VT in the absence of a completely reversible acute cause (AVID, CASH, and CIDS trials). (PMID 26910804)
- Contemporary programming strategies (high-rate cutoffs >200 bpm and delayed detection intervals >60 seconds) substantially reduce inappropriate and unnecessary shocks and improve survival without increasing syncope. (PMID 26910804, PMID 35179615)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal programming parameters (detection zones, SVT discriminators) continue to be refined against shock rates.
- Which secondary-prevention patients are better served by subcutaneous versus transvenous systems is individualised by pacing needs and vascular access.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Secondary versus primary prevention indications and trial lineage.
- Reversible causes that negate the indication (ischaemia, electrolytes, drugs).
- Programming basics: detection intervals and SVT discrimination.
- S-ICD versus transvenous ICD: advantages and limitations.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A survivor of out-of-hospital VF has a normal coronary angiogram; argue the ICD indication and discuss programming.
- A young patient needing an ICD has no venous access options; evaluate subcutaneous candidacy.

**Reviewer Gap**: Programming parameter specifics and current guideline indication tables are not covered by the retained abstracts; verify against the 2022 ESC/2017 AHA ventricular-arrhythmia guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26910804**: *Roles and indications for use of implantable defibrillator and resynchronization therapy.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 35179615**: *Subcutaneous ICD for more and transvenous ICD for fewer?* (2022) [review] - None

## [0901] Icosapent ethyl residual cardiovascular risk

**Cluster**: icosapent ethyl | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Icosapent ethyl sits at the intersection of residual-risk medicine and trial controversy. In REDUCE-IT, high-purity EPA reduced cardiovascular events among statin-treated patients with elevated triglycerides, but the magnitude of benefit, the mineral-oil comparator question, and the failure of the DHA-containing STRENGTH trial have kept the indication debated. Clinically, the drug is not for everyone with high triglycerides: the benefit signal concentrates in statin-treated patients with established ASCVD (or high risk) and triglycerides in the 135–499 mg/dL band, making patient selection the core skill.

### Core Teaching Points
- REDUCE-IT established that high-purity eicosapentaenoic acid (icosapent ethyl) reduces cardiovascular events on top of statins in high-risk patients with persistent hypertriglyceridaemia. (PMID 31715195)
- Eligibility nuance matters: benefit concentrates in statin-treated patients with triglycerides roughly 135–499 mg/dL and LDL-C controlled, not in unselected hypertriglyceridaemia. (PMID 32770402)
- The neutral DHA-containing STRENGTH trial and the mineral-oil comparator debate mean EPA-specific benefit is contested and generalisation to mixed omega-3 preparations is not supported. (PMID 35499805, PMID 33651748)
- Adverse-effect monitoring includes atrial fibrillation and bleeding signals that weighed into regulatory and formulary decisions. (PMID 35499805)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether the REDUCE-IT benefit is fully attributable to EPA versus comparator effects remains debated; biomarker mediation analyses continue.
- Cost-effectiveness in lower-risk primary-prevention populations is unresolved in most health systems.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- REDUCE-IT population, triglyceride band, and endpoint hierarchy.
- IPE versus mixed EPA/DHA preparations after STRENGTH.
- The mineral-oil comparator controversy and how to counsel patients.
- AF and bleeding adverse effects and drug interactions (anticoagulants).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A statin-controlled post-MI patient has triglycerides 210 mg/dL; decide whether icosapent ethyl is indicated and defend the answer against a STRENGTH objection.
- A patient on apixaban with intermittent AF is offered icosapent ethyl for residual risk; weigh benefit against bleeding.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are reviews rather than the REDUCE-IT primary report; verify exact eligibility thresholds, endpoint rates, and NNT against the NEJM 2019 primary publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32770402**: *Icosapent Ethyl: Niche Drug or for the Masses?* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 31715195**: *Implications for REDUCE IT in clinical practice.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 35499805**: *Controversies in the Use of Omega-3 Fatty Acids to Prevent Atherosclerosis.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 33651748**: *The clinical black, white, and gray lessons of recent cardiovascular outcome trials.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0902] ICU-acquired delirium: prevention, assessment, and treatment

**Cluster**: ICU delirium | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
ICU delirium is the syndrome the PADIS guidelines were built to prevent — and the 2022 SCCM update sharpened the message: routine deep sedation should be minimised, spontaneous awakening and breathing trials paired, early mobility promoted, and sleep protected. Pharmacological prevention and treatment with antipsychotics lacks outcome evidence, so management is environmental and process-based. The arc of care extends past discharge: post-intensive care syndrome bundles rehabilitation for the cognitive, physical, and psychological sequelae that outlast the ICU stay.

### Core Teaching Points
- The PADIS (2018) and 2022 SCCM guidelines frame delirium management around ABCDEF bundle elements: analgesia-first sedation, spontaneous awakening/breathing trials, choice of lighter sedation, delirium monitoring, early mobility, and family engagement. (PMID 30113379, PMID 35119438)
- Non-pharmacological prevention — reorientation, sleep hygiene, early mobilisation, sensory aids — is the evidence-backed core; antipsychotics are not recommended for routine prevention or treatment. (PMID 30113379, PMID 35119438)
- Delirium in older persons is common, serious, and under-recognised, with lasting effects on function and cognition that justify systematic screening in at-risk populations. (PMID 28973626)
- Post-intensive care syndrome (physical, cognitive, psychological impairments after critical illness) warrants structured multimodal rehabilitation and follow-up after ICU discharge. (PMID 37525219)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether any pharmacological agent (dexmedetomidine contexts, melatonin-agonists) improves delirium-free days is unresolved; trial signals have not translated into routine recommendations.
- Optimal intensity and setting of post-ICU rehabilitation for PICS is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ABCDEF bundle components and the evidence for each.
- Why haloperidol is not recommended for routine delirium prophylaxis.
- Dexmedetomidine versus benzodiazepine sedation in delirium risk.
- Recognising and triaging PICS after discharge.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A ventilated septic patient is deeply sedated on day 4 with a RASS of -4; outline the bundle-based de-escalation and delirium-prevention plan.
- A 78-year-old post-ICU patient has new confusion, weakness, and low mood at home; structure the PICS follow-up.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the primary randomised trials of individual bundle elements (e.g. SAT/SBT pairing trials); verify specific effect sizes against source trials before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30113379**: *Clinical Practice Guidelines for the Prevention and Management of Pain, Agitation/Sedation, Delirium, Immobility, and Sleep Disruption in Adult Patients in the ICU (PADIS).* (2018) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 35119438**: *2022 SCCM Clinical Practice Guidelines on Prevention and Management of Pain, Agitation/Sedation, Delirium, Immobility, and Sleep Disruption in Adult Patients in the ICU.* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 28973626**: *Delirium in Older Persons: Advances in Diagnosis and Treatment.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 37525219**: *Guideline on multimodal rehabilitation for patients with post-intensive care syndrome.* (2023) [guideline] - None

## [0903] ICU admission criteria and triage

**Cluster**: ICU triage | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
ICU triage is allocation under scarcity: who benefits most from a bed, who can be managed elsewhere, and who — despite need — will not benefit. The ConICTri consensus statement operationalises these judgments with explicit prioritisation criteria, while a qualitative systematic review shows how variable real-world selection criteria are across systems. For surgical patients, 'intensive care without borders' reframes the question as matching monitored-care intensity to risk rather than geography — using prediction scores and early-warning escalation to decide who needs the unit at all.

### Core Teaching Points
- Structured triage frameworks (e.g. ConICTri) use explicit prioritisation criteria — diagnosis, severity, reversibility, and life expectancy — to make bed allocation auditable and consistent under scarcity. (PMID 31456540)
- Real-world triage and inter-facility transfer criteria vary widely across systems, with clinical judgment, bed pressure, and prognosis all influencing decisions; standardised criteria are inconsistently applied. (PMID 32858530)
- For surgical patients, risk prediction scores and timely recognition of postoperative deterioration can match patients to the right level of care, including high-dependency and ward-based monitoring alternatives. (PMID 30299312)
- ICU admission decisions rely on physiological risk stratification (assessing severe respiratory, hemodynamic, or metabolic instability requiring invasive monitoring or mechanical organ support), objective reversibility of the acute illness, and the patient's baseline functional status and advance care directives, prioritizing patients who will derive clear physiological survival benefit over those with irreversible terminal illness. (PMID 31456540)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether formal scoring systems outperform structured clinical judgment for ICU admission decisions lacks definitive trial evidence.
- Ethical frameworks for rationing during full-capacity crises (e.g. pandemic triage) remain contested across jurisdictions.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Prioritisation criteria: benefit, reversibility, prognosis under scarcity.
- Triage decisions when the unit is full — referral, transfer, or ward care.
- Prediction scores for postoperative escalation.
- Documentation and audit of triage decisions.

### Case Generation Hooks
- Two patients compete for the last bed: a ventilated pneumonia with good reversibility and an end-stage malignancy with sepsis; apply structured triage reasoning.
- A postoperative patient scores high-risk but the ICU is full; design the ward-based monitoring escalation plan.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include SOFA-based or frailty-based ICU-admission prediction validation studies; verify score performance data before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31456540**: *The Critical Care Society of Southern Africa Consensus Statement on ICU Triage and Rationing (ConICTri).* (2019) [consensus-statement] - None
- **PMID 32858530**: *Practices in Triage and Transfer of Critically Ill Patients: A Qualitative Systematic Review of Selection Criteria.* (2020) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 30299312**: *How to optimize critical care resources in surgical patients: intensive care without physical borders.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0904] ICU delirium: CAM-ICU assessment, prevention, and management

**Cluster**: ICU delirium | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Where topic 902 covers the prevention and management architecture of ICU delirium, this topic is about the instrument itself: the Confusion Assessment Method for the ICU. CAM-ICU remains the most widely validated bedside tool for non-psychiatrists — an acute change in mental status with inattention, plus either altered consciousness or disorganised thinking. Psychometric synthesis confirms strong accuracy in general critically ill adults, but the tool only works when it is actually applied: most delirium in the ICU goes unrecognised without systematic screening, which is why guidelines embed CAM-ICU into hourly-to-daily assessment pathways.

### Core Teaching Points
- CAM-ICU is a valid, reliable instrument for delirium diagnosis in critically ill adults, including ventilated patients unable to speak, when administered by trained non-psychiatrists. (PMID 37905383, PMID 29437077)
- The tool's logic is an acute change or fluctuation in mental status plus inattention, disorganised thinking, or altered level of consciousness — features that distinguish delirium from dementia and depression. (PMID 29437077)
- Without systematic screening, the majority of ICU delirium goes undetected; routine instrument-based assessment is the prerequisite for any prevention or treatment bundle. (PMID 37905383)
- Delirium in intensive care is associated with increased mortality, prolonged mechanical ventilation, and long-term cognitive impairment; routine screening using the Confusion Assessment Method for the ICU (CAM-ICU) or Intensive Care Delirium Screening Checklist (ICDSC) alongside multicomponent non-pharmacological prevention (early mobility, sleep preservation, delirium bundle ABCDEF) represents the evidence-based standard of care. (PMID 37905383)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal screening frequency (every shift versus daily) and who should perform screening (nurses versus physicians) is institution-dependent.
- Performance of CAM-ICU in deeply sedated or severely encephalopathic patients is less certain; combined instruments (e.g. with EEG correlates) are under study.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CAM-ICU feature algorithm: acute change, inattention, disorganised thinking, altered consciousness.
- CAM-ICU versus ICDSC: strengths and feasibility at the bedside.
- Sensitivity/specificity of instruments in ventilated patients.
- Screening frequency and integration into sedation interruptions.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A ventilated patient fails an attention screening with RASS +1; walk through the CAM-ICU algorithm and state the diagnosis and next steps.
- A unit reports delirium rates near zero without routine screening; critique the data and design the screening rollout.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE: only two sources, both assessment-focused. The retained abstracts do not cover CAM-ICU performance in specific subgroups (neuro-ICU, ECMO); verify before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37905383**: *Validity and predictability of the confusion assessment method for the intensive care unit for delirium among critically ill patients.* (2023) [validation-study] - None
- **PMID 29437077**: *Delirium Assessment Tools for Use in Critically Ill Adults: A Psychometric Analysis and Systematic Review.* (2019) [systematic-review] - None

## [0905] ICU renal replacement therapy initiation timing

**Cluster**: RRT initiation | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
When to start renal replacement therapy in ICU acute kidney injury has been one of nephrology's most contested questions, and the contemporary answer is restraint: large randomised trials and the Cochrane synthesis found no survival advantage to early, protocol-driven initiation over a watchful strategy that waits for absolute indications — refractory hyperkalaemia, severe acidosis, pulmonary oedema, or uraemic complications. Starting RRT is therefore a decision about concrete complications, not creatinine alone. Once started, dosing, anticoagulation, and membrane choice follow the prescription-pathway and core-curriculum frameworks that standardise delivery across ICUs.

### Core Teaching Points
- Randomised evidence and systematic review show no mortality benefit from early versus delayed RRT initiation in AKI; a strategy awaiting absolute indications is supported. (PMID 30560582)
- Traditional absolute indications remain the trigger points: refractory hyperkalaemia, severe metabolic acidosis, diuretic-resistant volume overload, and uraemic complications (encephalopathy, pericarditis). (PMID 30560582, PMID 32144519)
- Once CKRT is started, prescription quality — effluent dose, anticoagulation strategy, circuit monitoring, and drug dosing adjustment — determines delivered dose and outcomes. (PMID 32144519, PMID 40072400)
- Multiple landmark randomized controlled trials (AKIKI, IDEAL-ICU, STARRT-AKI) demonstrate that an accelerated or early strategy for initiating renal replacement therapy in critically ill patients with acute kidney injury does not reduce mortality and increases adverse catheter/electrolyte events compared to a watchful waiting strategy that initiates RRT only for conventional urgent indications (refractory hyperkalemia, severe metabolic acidosis, or pulmonary edema). (PMID 30560582)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether specific phenotypes (septic AKI with fluid overload, severe hypercatabolism) benefit from earlier initiation is still being tested.
- Biomarker-guided (e.g. furosemide stress test, NGAL) initiation timing has not yet changed standard practice.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- AKIKI/IDEAL-ICU-style trial results: early versus delayed RRT and mortality.
- Absolute versus relative indications for RRT in AKI.
- CRRT prescription essentials: dose, anticoagulation, filter life.
- Drug dosing on CRRT and common errors.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An oliguric septic patient has K+ 6.2 mmol/L, pH 7.18, and rising creatinine on day 2; decide on RRT timing and modality with justification.
- A CKRT circuit keeps clotting within 12 hours; review the prescription pathway (dose, anticoagulation, blood flow).

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the primary AKIKI/IDEAL-ICU trial reports; verify exact eligibility criteria and secondary-endpoint results before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30560582**: *Timing of renal replacement therapy initiation for acute kidney injury.* (2019) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 32144519**: *Prescription of CRRT: a pathway to optimize therapy.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 40072400**: *Continuous Kidney Replacement Therapies: Core Curriculum 2025.* (2025) [curriculum] - None

## [0906] Idiopathic facial nerve palsy (Bell's palsy): corticosteroid timing, eye care

**Cluster**: Bell's palsy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Bell's palsy is the commonest cranial neuropathy, and its management hinges on two time-sensitive acts: starting corticosteroids early — ideally within 72 hours of onset, when the facial nerve is still inflamed within the fallopian canal — and protecting the eye, whose blink reflex is paralysed. Meta-analytic evidence supports adding antivirals to steroids for the best recovery rates in severe palsy, and guidelines (Japanese 2023 update among them) converge on early steroid therapy with graded add-on antivirals. The prognosis is favourable in most, but complete palsy, older age, and absent recovery by three months warrant referral for specialist assessment of synkinesis and surgical options.

### Core Teaching Points
- Corticosteroids started within 72 hours of onset improve facial-nerve recovery and are the cornerstone of treatment for adults with Bell's palsy. (PMID 37054419, PMID 39079445)
- Meta-analytic evidence indicates combination antiviral-plus-steroid therapy outperforms steroid alone in recovery, with greatest benefit in severe palsy — consistent with a likely viral (HSV) aetiology. (PMID 41019239, PMID 25857657)
- Eye protection is mandatory from day one: lubricating drops, ointment, and taping or patching at night to prevent corneal exposure and ulceration. (PMID 37054419, PMID 25857657)
- Red-flag exclusions before treating: vesicles suggest Ramsay Hunt (needs antivirals urgently), and no forehead sparing — but any atypical features (bilateral, recurrent, slow progression) demand imaging for alternative causes. (PMID 25857657)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The incremental benefit of antivirals in mild-to-moderate palsy is modest and debated across guidelines.
- Role of electrophysiology (ENoG) and surgical decompression in complete early paralysis is unresolved and specialist-dependent.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The 72-hour steroid window and dosing.
- When to add antivirals — severity and evidence.
- Corneal protection regimens and red-eye warning signs.
- Distinguishing Bell's palsy from Ramsay Hunt syndrome and central facial weakness.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 45-year-old presents 36 hours into a complete right facial palsy; prescribe management including eye care and counselling on prognosis.
- A patient with 'Bell's palsy' has slow progression over 6 weeks; list the features that should have prompted earlier imaging.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the primary trials (e.g. Scandinavian steroid trial) behind the steroid recommendation; verify effect sizes against primary sources before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37054419**: *Bell Palsy: Rapid Evidence Review.* (2023) [evidence-review] - None
- **PMID 41019239**: *Efficacy of Combination and Single Therapy in Bell's Palsy: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39079445**: *Summary of Japanese clinical practice guidelines for Bell's palsy - 2023 update.* (2023) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 25857657**: *Bell's palsy: aetiology, clinical features and multidisciplinary care.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0907] Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension (IIH): lumbar puncture opening pressure, acetazolamide, stenting

**Cluster**: IIH management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
IIH is a syndrome of raised intracranial pressure in obese women of childbearing age, diagnosed by the modified Dandy criteria: symptoms (headache, pulsatile tinnitus, visual disturbance), elevated lumbar-puncture opening pressure with normal CSF composition, and normal neuroimaging — with venous imaging to exclude cerebral venous sinus thrombosis. Management is tiered: weight loss is disease-modifying, acetazolamide is first-line pharmacotherapy with modest but real benefit on vision, and escalating interventions — CSF shunting, venous sinus stenting, optic nerve sheath fenestration — are reserved for rapidly declining vision, with stenting increasingly used where venous sinus stenosis with a pressure gradient is demonstrated.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnosis requires elevated LP opening pressure (typically ≥25 cm CSF in adults, lateral decubitus) with normal CSF composition and neuroimaging excluding secondary causes, especially venous sinus thrombosis. (PMID 34929642, PMID 26700907)
- Weight reduction is the only disease-modifying intervention; even modest sustained loss lowers intracranial pressure and can induce remission. (PMID 40459312, PMID 26700907)
- Acetazolamide is first-line medical therapy; trial evidence shows modest benefit on visual field outcomes when added to diet, and it is titrated against symptom control and tolerability. (PMID 40459312, PMID 34929642)
- For medically refractory or rapidly declining vision, surgical options include CSF diversion (shunt), optic nerve sheath fenestration, and venous sinus stenting — the latter for patients with documented sinus stenosis and a trans-stenosis pressure gradient. (PMID 34929642, PMID 40459312)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal acetazolamide dosing and duration, and whether it alters long-term vision loss independently of weight change, remains uncertain.
- Patient selection criteria for venous sinus stenting (pressure-gradient thresholds, stenosis morphology) are not standardised across centres.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Modified Dandy criteria and correct LP technique for opening pressure.
- Weight loss as disease modification — magnitude and mechanisms.
- Acetazolamide: evidence, titration, side-effect management.
- Escalation ladder: when to shunt, fenestrate, or stent.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An obese 29-year-old woman with daily headache and pulsatile tinnitus has papilloedema; outline the diagnostic pathway through LP and imaging.
- A patient on maximal acetazolamide has worsening visual fields; argue the case for venous sinus stenting versus shunt.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the IIHTT primary trial report or stenting case-series outcomes; verify visual-field effect sizes and complication rates before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34929642**: *Idiopathic intracranial hypertension: Pathophysiology, diagnosis and management.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 26700907**: *Understanding idiopathic intracranial hypertension: mechanisms, management, and future directions.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 40459312**: *Treatment and Monitoring of Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0908] Idiopathic intracranial hypertension vision protection

**Cluster**: IIH management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
In IIH, vision is the organ at stake: papilloedema reflects axonal compromise at the optic nerve head, and the clinical mandate is early, quantitative monitoring of visual function so that decline triggers escalation before permanent field loss. Automated perimetry (Humphrey 24-2 or 30-2) and optic-disc photography/OCT form the monitoring backbone, interpreted alongside symptoms — transient visual obscurations are a warning sign. The nuances that distinguish expert practice include recognising atypical presentations (men, lean patients, fulminant IIH with rapid field loss), the management of IIH in pregnancy, and the discipline of urgent surgical referral when fields deteriorate despite therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Visual field testing by automated perimetry is the primary outcome measure in IIH; regular, comparable field testing detects deterioration earlier than symptoms or fundoscopy alone. (PMID 38575259, PMID 35103000)
- Papilloedema grading and OCT of the retinal nerve fibre layer complement perimetry; discordance between disc appearance and field loss is common and the field takes precedence. (PMID 38575259)
- Fulminant IIH — rapid, severe visual decline over days to weeks — is a neuro-ophthalmic emergency requiring urgent surgical intervention rather than medical titration. (PMID 35103000, PMID 38575259)
- Atypical phenotypes (male, non-obese, paediatric, or secondary pseudotumour cerebri) require scrupulous exclusion of secondary causes while applying the same vision-protection framework. (PMID 35103000, PMID 38575259)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal monitoring intervals (monthly versus three-monthly fields in stable patients) are practice-defined rather than trial-defined.
- Whether optical coherence tomography can replace or triage formal perimetry in resource-limited settings is under evaluation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Choosing and interpreting perimetry in IIH.
- Transient visual obscurations versus field loss — significance.
- Fulminant IIH: definition and emergency escalation.
- Monitoring IIH in pregnancy and atypical phenotypes.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A compliant IIH patient on acetazolamide reports new transient visual obscurations with stable weight; interpret and act.
- A patient presents with severe papilloedema and a collapsing visual field over one week; outline the emergency pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE: only two sources, both reviews. The retained abstracts do not include perimetry-reliability studies or surgical-vision-outcome series; verify escalation thresholds against neuro-ophthalmology guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38575259**: *The Pseudotumor Cerebri Syndrome.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 35103000**: *Idiopathic Intracranial Hypertension - Challenges and Pearls.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0909] idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis

**Cluster**: IPF | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
IPF is the prototypic progressive fibrosing interstitial lung disease: irreversible parenchymal remodelling, honeycomb change on HRCT or UIP-pattern histology, and a median survival measured in years without treatment. Two antifibrotics — pirfenidone and nintedanib — slow forced vital capacity decline, and the progressive-pulmonary-fibrosis consensus extends the antifibrotic principle beyond IPF to other fibrosing ILDs that worsen despite management. Diagnosis is multidisciplinary, prognosis is tracked by FVC trajectory, and transplant referral is timed by risk stratification — the modern skill is recognising progression early and treating the trajectory, not the label.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnosis rests on HRCT UIP-pattern (or multidisciplinary confirmation with histology), exclusion of known causes, and recognition of the usual interstitial pneumonia pattern's prognostic weight. (PMID 39264073)
- Pirfenidone and nintedanib slow FVC decline in IPF; indirect-comparison evidence supports efficacy of antifibrotics in progressive fibrosing ILD beyond IPF. (PMID 34895203, PMID 36517177)
- The progressive-pulmonary-fibrosis framework defines worsening across symptoms, physiology, and imaging within a year of appropriate management, triggering antifibrotic therapy regardless of specific ILD label. (PMID 36517177)
- Monitoring relies on serial FVC and DLCO, with exacerbations — acute worsening with new ground-glass opacity — carrying high mortality and requiring aggressive management. (PMID 39264073)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing and combination of antifibrotics, and treatment of acute exacerbations, lack randomised evidence.
- Antifibrotic efficacy in early or asymptomatic IPF and in non-IPF progressive fibrosing ILD beyond progressive phenotypes is extrapolated rather than proven.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- UIP-pattern HRCT criteria and when biopsy/multidisciplinary review is needed.
- Antifibrotic mechanisms, monitoring, and side-effect management (photosensitivity, diarrhoea, LFTs).
- Defining progressive pulmonary fibrosis and when to treat non-IPF ILDs.
- Acute exacerbation of IPF: recognition and management.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old man with exertional dyspnoea and velcro crackles has probable UIP on HRCT; walk through diagnosis, antifibrotic counselling, and monitoring.
- A patient with rheumatoid-arthritis ILD declines over 8 months despite immunosuppression; apply the PPF definition and argue for antifibrotic therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the primary INPULSIS/ASCEND trial reports or the current ATS/ERS guideline; verify eligibility criteria and effect sizes against primary sources before approval.

## [0910] IgA Nephropathy

**Cluster**: IgA nephropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
IgA nephropathy, the commonest primary glomerulonephritis, is now managed as a progressive-risk disease: the KDIGO 2021 guideline established risk-based surveillance, and the 2025 update adds an evidence-treatment armamentarium that has expanded rapidly — supportive care with RAS blockade and blood-pressure control remains the foundation, with SGLT2 inhibitors, targeted-release budesonide (Nefecon), sparsentan, and endothelin-receptor antagonists layered by proteinuria and eGFR trajectory. The validation cohorts underpinning these additions showed proteinuria reduction as a surrogate for kidney outcomes, shifting practice from watchful waiting to early, goal-directed combination therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- All patients receive optimised supportive care — blood-pressure control, ACEi/ARB titrated to maximally tolerated dose — which remains the backbone regardless of risk. (PMID 34556300, PMID 40975525)
- Risk stratification uses proteinuria (particularly the persistently ≥0.75–1 g/day threshold), eGFR trajectory, and the International IgAN Prediction Tool to time immunosuppression. (PMID 40975525, PMID 34556300)
- The KDIGO 2025 update incorporates newer agents: SGLT2 inhibitors for residual proteinuria, targeted-release budesonide for high-risk primary IgAN, sparsentan, and endothelin-A receptor antagonists, based on proteinuria-lowering as a validated surrogate endpoint. (PMID 40975525)
- Systemic corticosteroids are reserved for high-risk patients after optimised supportive care, weighing infection and toxicity risk; tonsillectomy is considered in selected recurrent-synpharyngitic-haematuria populations. (PMID 34556300, PMID 40975525)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing and combination of the newer agents (SGLT2i, TR-budesonide, sparsentan) and their additive proteinuria effects are not yet defined.
- Long-term (>5-year) hard-endpoint data for the newer agents beyond proteinuria surrogates are pending.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- KDIGO 2021 versus 2025: what changed in IgAN therapy.
- Proteinuria thresholds that trigger treatment escalation.
- Nefecon (targeted-release budesonide): indication and evidence.
- When steroids are justified in IgAN and their risks.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 34-year-old with biopsy-proven IgAN has proteinuria 1.4 g/day on maximally dosed ACEi; apply the 2025 KDIGO pathway and justify each addition.
- A patient with recurrent synpharyngitic haematuria asks about tonsillectomy; weigh the evidence and regional practice variation.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are guideline summaries, not primary trials; verify agent-specific eligibility and effect sizes (PROTECT, NefIgArd, TESTING trials) before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40975525**: *Executive summary of the KDIGO 2025 Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Immunoglobulin A Nephropathy (IgAN).* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 34556300**: *Executive summary of the KDIGO 2021 Guideline for the Management of Glomerular Diseases.* (2021) [guideline] - None

## [0911] IgA nephropathy treatment (sparsentan and targeted therapy)

**Cluster**: IgA nephropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The sparsentan story shows how fast IgA nephropathy therapy is moving: the PROTECT trial's interim proteinuria win made the dual endothelin-A/angiotensin-receptor antagonist the first non-immunosuppressive drug with regulatory approval specifically for IgAN, built on proteinuria as a validated surrogate. Used correctly it requires attention its predecessors did not: it cannot be combined with ACEi/ARB, demands liver-enzyme and pregnancy monitoring, and embeds a REMS-style hepatotoxicity framework in some jurisdictions. Network meta-analysis now lets clinicians rank the expanding armamentarium — RAS blockade, SGLT2 inhibitors, targeted-release budesonide, sparsentan — by proteinuria effect, though head-to-head trials and long-term kidney-outcome data are still maturing.

### Core Teaching Points
- Sparsentan is a dual endothelin-A and angiotensin-II receptor antagonist approved for IgAN on the strength of PROTECT's proteinuria reduction, an accepted surrogate for kidney protection. (PMID 40635379, PMID 42368239)
- Because it blocks the renin-angiotensin system, sparsentan must not be combined with ACE inhibitors or ARBs; transition of care from RAS blockade is a core practical step. (PMID 38149041)
- Safety monitoring includes liver enzymes, pregnancy prevention (foetotoxicity), hyperkalaemia, and oedema — with structured hepatotoxicity risk-management programmes in some regions. (PMID 38149041, PMID 40635379)
- Network meta-analysis of RCTs now permits comparative ranking of IgAN agents by proteinuria lowering, supporting individualised sequencing as newer agents accumulate. (PMID 40606442)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term (>5 year) eGFR and ESRD outcomes for sparsentan beyond the surrogate endpoint are pending final PROTECT results.
- Optimal combination or sequencing with SGLT2 inhibitors and targeted-release budesonide is undefined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- PROTECT design: dual receptor blockade and the proteinuria surrogate.
- Switching rules from ACEi/ARB to sparsentan and washout considerations.
- Hepatotoxicity monitoring and pregnancy prevention requirements.
- Comparative efficacy of modern IgAN agents from network meta-analysis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An IgAN patient on maximal losartan has proteinuria 1.2 g/day and normal LFTs; plan the switch to sparsentan including counselling and monitoring.
- A transplant-listed IgAN patient asks how sparsentan compares with budesonide and SGLT2 inhibitors; frame the evidence hierarchy.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are reviews/meta-analysis, not the PROTECT primary report; verify interim effect sizes, eligibility thresholds, and complete safety tables before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40635379**: *Sparsentan: a dual endothelin and angiotensin II receptor antagonist approved for IgA nephropathy.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 38149041**: *Practical Considerations for the Use of Sparsentan in the Treatment of Patients with IgAN in Clinical Practice.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40606442**: *Efficacy and safety of agents for IgA nephropathy: a network meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 42368239**: *Recent Advances in the Management of IgA Nephropathy: From Supportive Care to Targeted Therapies.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0912] IgA Vasculitis

**Cluster**: IgA vasculitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
IgA vasculitis — Henoch-Schonlein purpura renamed — is a small-vessel IgA-dominant immune-complex vasculitis that is common and self-limiting in children but rarer, more severe, and more often renal in adults. The clinical skill is two-fold: recognising the tetrad of palpable purura, arthritis, abdominal pain, and renal involvement, and stratifying by organ threat, because management escalates from supportive care for skin-joint disease to immunosuppression for nephritis or severe GI disease. Adult disease behaves differently enough from paediatric disease that adult-specific evidence, though thinner, should drive treatment decisions.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnosis is clinical with characteristic IgA-dominant deposits on biopsy when needed; in adults, biopsy and secondary-cause work-up (infection, malignancy, drugs) carry more weight than in children. (PMID 34196893, PMID 32803924)
- Skin-limited and joint disease in adults is usually managed supportively; the systemic review evidence for immunosuppression is strongest for nephritis and severe gastrointestinal involvement. (PMID 39518760, PMID 32803924)
- IgAV nephritis management draws on IgA-nephropathy principles — RAS blockade for proteinuric disease, and corticosteroids/immunosuppression for higher-risk histology — extrapolated from the KDIGO framework. (PMID 34396492, PMID 39518760)
- Adult-onset IgAV has more frequent and more severe renal involvement than childhood-onset disease, with a higher proportion progressing to chronic kidney disease; prognosis is organ-threat dependent. (PMID 34196893)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Randomised evidence specific to adult IgAV is scarce; most treatment recommendations are extrapolated from paediatric trials and IgA-nephropathy data.
- The role of rituximab and other steroid-sparing agents in adult IgAV nephritis is not established.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Adult versus paediatric IgAV: severity and renal-risk differences.
- When to biopsy and what to work up in adult-onset disease.
- Management ladder: supportive care to immunosuppression by organ threat.
- IgAV nephritis versus primary IgA nephropathy: shared and distinct principles.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 52-year-old presents with palpable purpura, knee arthralgia, and melena; outline the diagnostic work-up and initial management.
- An adult IgAV patient has nephritic-range proteinuria with hypertension; justify the nephrology-referral threshold and treatment plan.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts lack adult-specific randomised trials; verify treatment recommendations against the 2024 adult-onset IgAV evidence-based guidelines (topic 913) before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39518760**: *IgA Vasculitis (Henoch-Schonlein Purpura): An Update on Treatment.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 32803924**: *Henoch-Schonlein Purpura (IgA Vasculitis): Rapid Evidence Review.* (2021) [evidence-review] - None
- **PMID 34196893**: *IgA Vasculitis in Adults: a Rare yet Challenging Disease.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 34396492**: *Management of IgA Vasculitis with Nephritis.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0913] IgA vasculitis Henoch-Schonlein: skin, joints, renal, GI involvement in adults

**Cluster**: IgA vasculitis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Where topic 912 frames IgA vasculitis as a disease entity, this topic organises it by organ system — the practical way adults actually present. Skin purpura and arthralgia are the commonest manifestations; renal involvement determines long-term prognosis; and gastrointestinal disease, present in a substantial minority of adults, is the most dangerous acute threat — with intussusception, perforation, and haemorrhage described in adult cohorts. The 2025 evidence-based guidelines for adult-onset IgAV give the first dedicated, adult-specific framework covering each organ system's assessment and management.

### Core Teaching Points
- Adult-onset IgAV classically involves skin (palpable purura, lower-limb predominant), joints (arthralgia/arthritis), kidneys (haematuria, proteinuria of graded severity), and GI tract — with renal involvement the principal driver of long-term outcome. (PMID 37231802, PMID 42625065)
- GI involvement in adults ranges from colicky pain and occult bleeding to intussusception, perforation, and severe haemorrhage; systematic assessment includes stool testing, cross-sectional imaging, and endoscopy when severe. (PMID 41939557)
- The 2025 evidence-based adult-onset IgAV guidelines provide organ-system-specific diagnostic and treatment recommendations, replacing extrapolation from paediatric practice. (PMID 42625065)
- Systemic involvement risk is harder to assess in adults than children; comprehensive cohort data emphasise demographic and histopathological features associated with renal progression for risk stratification. (PMID 37231802)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal treatment of severe GI involvement (steroids, IVIG, surgery thresholds) rests on low-grade evidence.
- Long-term renal surveillance schedules for adults with resolved acute disease are not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Organ-system manifestations and their relative frequency in adults.
- GI red flags: when to image, scope, or operate.
- 2025 adult-onset IgAV guidelines: what changed from paediatric extrapolation.
- Risk-stratifying renal involvement at presentation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An adult with IgAV develops severe abdominal pain and dropping haemoglobin; outline the GI-complication work-up and escalation.
- Two adults present — one skin-only, one with nephritic sediment; contrast their management and follow-up intensity.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the guideline's full recommendation tables; verify organ-specific treatment grades before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 42625065**: *Evidence-based guidelines for the diagnosis and management of adult-onset IgA vasculitis.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41939557**: *Gastrointestinal Involvement in Adult IgA Vasculitis: A Comprehensive Review.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 37231802**: *Adult Henoch-Schonlein Purpura: Comprehensive Assessment of Demographic, Clinical, and Histopathological Features.* (2022) [cohort-review] - None

## [0914] IgG4-Related Disease

**Cluster**: IgG4-related disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
IgG4-related disease, recognised only since 2003, is a systemic fibro-inflammatory condition that can mimic malignancy, infection, and other immune disease in nearly any organ — classically autoimmune pancreatitis, sialadenitis, orbital disease, and retroperitoneal fibrosis. Pathophysiologically it is a B-cell-driven, plasmablast-rich process with characteristic histology (dense IgG4+ plasma-cell infiltrates, storiform fibrosis, obliterative phlebitis), and serum IgG4 is elevated in most but not all patients — tissue diagnosis remains the reference standard. The clinical stakes are twofold: don't miss it (steroids usually produce a dramatic response) and don't overdiagnose it (elevated IgG4 alone is non-specific, and fibrotic late-stage disease responds poorly).

### Core Teaching Points
- IgG4-RD is a multi-organ fibro-inflammatory disease with a characteristic histopathological signature — dense lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate rich in IgG4+ plasma cells, storiform fibrosis, and obliterative phlebitis. (PMID 32939060, PMID 32546500)
- Pathophysiology centres on B cells and plasmablasts with oligoclonal expansions; this B-cell dependence explains the efficacy of B-cell depletion and informs biomarker development. (PMID 32939060)
- A fibrotic phenotype exists in which prominent tissue fibrosis predicts reduced glucocorticoid responsiveness — recognising it early matters for treatment selection and expectations. (PMID 38574746)
- Serum IgG4 concentration supports but does not establish diagnosis; tissue confirmation with IgG4/IgG ratio assessment is the reference standard, and organ-specific diagnostic criteria have been developed. (PMID 32546500, PMID 32939060)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether IgG4 itself is pathogenic or an epiphenomenon of underlying B-cell dysregulation is unresolved.
- Optimal glucocorticoid-sparing maintenance and relapse-prevention strategies are not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Classic organ manifestations and malignancy mimics.
- Histopathological triad and serum IgG4 interpretation.
- Fibrotic versus proliferative phenotypes and treatment response.
- B-cell pathogenesis as the rationale for rituximab.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with painless jaundice and a pancreatic mass has elevated IgG4; argue the biopsy strategy and the treatment response that would confirm IgG4-RD.
- A patient with IgG4-RD relapses twice on tapering steroids; discuss phenotype assessment and steroid-sparing options.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the 2019 ACR/EULAR classification criteria paper or the international consensus guidance on management; verify classification versus diagnostic criteria before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32939060**: *IgG4-related disease: an update on pathophysiology and implications for clinical care.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 38574746**: *Fibrotic phenotype of IgG4-related disease.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 32546500**: *Advances in the diagnosis and management of IgG4 related disease.* (2019) [review] - None

## [0915] igg4 related disease diagnosis and b cell depletion

**Cluster**: IgG4-related disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diagnosis and treatment of IgG4-RD have matured together: the 2019 ACR/EULAR classification criteria gave research a common language, while two decades of experience refined the clinical approach — suspect the multi-organ pattern, confirm with tissue where feasible, and treat with glucocorticoids first, accepting that relapse on tapering is common. Because the disease is B-cell driven, B-cell depletion with rituximab has moved from rescue therapy to an established steroid-sparing option with rapid biomarker responses; systematic-review evidence across immune-mediated disorders supports its efficacy and acceptable safety in experienced hands, though IgG4-RD-specific randomised trials are still lacking.

### Core Teaching Points
- Diagnostic work-up combines compatible organ involvement, elevated serum IgG4 (supportive, not specific), characteristic imaging, and, where safe, tissue showing the classic histopathology; classification criteria formalise entry into research and trials. (PMID 40071397, PMID 32348524)
- Glucocorticoids remain first-line and usually produce prompt clinical and serological response; the dominant long-term problem is relapse during or after tapering, driving maintenance strategies. (PMID 32348524, PMID 40071397)
- Rituximab depletes CD20+ B cells and produces rapid falls in serum IgG4 and plasmablast populations; systematic-review evidence supports efficacy and acceptable safety across immune-mediated disorders including IgG4-RD. (PMID 31555262, PMID 40071397)
- B-cell biomarkers (plasmablasts, IgG4 levels, circulating T-follicular-helper cells) enable treatment response monitoring beyond symptoms and imaging. (PMID 40071397)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Rituximab dosing schedules (fixed versus relapse-guided) and re-treatment triggers are not standardised.
- Whether early rituximab in fibrotic-phenotype disease changes outcomes is unknown.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Stepwise diagnostic algorithm and the role of biopsy.
- First-line therapy, tapering, and relapse patterns.
- Rituximab: mechanism, monitoring, and biomarker response.
- Distinguishing classification criteria from diagnostic criteria.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with submandibular swelling, orbital pseudotumour, and IgG4 6x ULN relapses on steroid taper; design the rituximab plan and monitoring schedule.
- A patient with suspected IgG4-RD has normal serum IgG4; justify whether and where to biopsy.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained rituximab evidence is a cross-disease systematic review, not an IgG4-RD-specific trial; verify regimen and response rates against IgG4-RD cohort series before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40071397**: *IgG4-related disease: lessons from the first 20 years.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 32348524**: *IgG4-related disease: a clinical perspective.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 31555262**: *Systematic Review of Safety and Efficacy of Rituximab in Treating Immune-Mediated Disorders.* (2020) [systematic-review] - None

## [0916] IL-6 inhibition: tocilizumab, sarilumab in RA and cytokine storm, COVID-19 trials

**Cluster**: IL-6 inhibition | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
IL-6 blockade is one of the most instructive examples of a pathway drug with dual lives. In rheumatoid arthritis, tocilizumab and sarilumab — humanised IL-6-receptor antibodies — are effective biologic DMARDs positioned by the EULAR recommendations alongside TNF inhibitors, with a distinctive side-effect signature (neutropenia, transaminitis, dyslipidaemia, bowel perforation risk). In hyperinflammatory states — cytokine-release syndromes, chimeric-antigen-receptor T-cell toxicities, and severe COVID-19 — the same blockade tames systemic inflammation, and the randomised-trial meta-analyses of COVID-19 defined exactly where benefit lies: severe, hypoxaemic, CRP-high patients, not everyone with the virus.

### Core Teaching Points
- IL-6 receptor blockade with tocilizumab or sarilumab is an established RA treatment option within the EULAR biologic-DMARD algorithm, effective as monotherapy or combination therapy. (PMID 41826212, PMID 28778870)
- The IL-6 signature adverse effects — neutropenia, raised transaminases, dyslipidaemia, and serious infection — plus the bowel-perforation signal in diverticulosis define pre-treatment screening and monitoring. (PMID 28778870, PMID 41826212)
- In COVID-19, meta-analysis of randomised trials shows tocilizumab reduces mortality in severe, systemically inflamed (high CRP, hypoxaemic) patients, translating IL-6-pathway biology into a targeted hyperinflammation indication. (PMID 35130679, PMID 33579777)
- Across IL-6-pathway inhibitors in COVID-19 (tocilizumab, sarilumab, siltuximab, anakinra), the class signal favoured benefit in the critically ill with hyperinflammation, not in unselected infection. (PMID 33579777)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Head-to-head positioning of IL-6 blockade versus JAK inhibitors after TNF failure in RA continues to evolve.
- Whether IL-6 blockade has a preventive role in cytokine-release syndrome prophylaxis is unsettled.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- IL-6 pathway biology and receptor versus ligand blockade.
- RA biologic sequencing per EULAR and IL-6 drug-specific monitoring.
- Tocilizumab in COVID-19: patient selection from trial meta-analyses.
- Cytokine-release syndrome management post-CAR-T.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A RA patient failing two TNFs has high inflammatory markers; justify IL-6 blockade choice and the screening bloods.
- A hypoxaemic COVID-19 patient on dexamethasone has CRP 280 mg/L and rising; argue for or against tocilizumab using trial evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the RECOVERY or REMAP-CAP primary reports; verify mortality effect sizes and subgroup definitions before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41826212**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs.* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 28778870**: *Interleukin (IL-6) Immunotherapy.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 35130679**: *Tocilizumab for COVID-19: A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomized controlled trials.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 33579777**: *Systematic review and meta-analysis of anakinra, sarilumab, siltuximab and tocilizumab for COVID-19.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0917] imatinib versus interferon chronic myeloid leukemia IRIS trial

**Cluster**: CML | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The IRIS trial is the pivotal inflection point in modern oncology: randomising newly diagnosed chronic-phase CML between interferon-alpha plus cytarabine and imatinib, it was stopped early because imatinib produced vastly superior complete cytogenetic and major molecular responses with far better tolerability — converting CML from a fatal disease into a chronic, often near-normal-life-expectancy condition. The legacy is visible in the ELN 2020 recommendations and 2025 updates: first-line choice among imatinib and second/third-generation TKIs is now individualised by comorbidity, risk score, and treatment goal — including the modern ambition of treatment-free remission after sustained deep molecular response.

### Core Teaching Points
- IRIS established imatinib, the first BCR::ABL1 tyrosine kinase inhibitor, as superior to interferon-based therapy for newly diagnosed chronic-phase CML, transforming survival. (PMID 39093014, PMID 32127639)
- Modern first-line choice — imatinib versus second-generation TKIs (dasatinib, nilotinib, bosutinib) — is individualised by Sokal/ELTS risk score, comorbidities (vascular, pulmonary, hepatic), and the treatment-free-remission goal. (PMID 32127639, PMID 40616814)
- Response monitoring is quantitative: BCR::ABL1 transcript by international scale with milestone targets at 3, 6, and 12 months defining optimal, warning, and failure responses that trigger therapy change. (PMID 32127639, PMID 39093014)
- Sustained deep molecular response (typically MR4 or deeper for years) permits carefully monitored TKI discontinuation in selected patients, with the ELN recommendations defining eligibility and monitoring intensity. (PMID 32127639, PMID 40616814)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term vascular and second-malignancy signals with second-generation TKIs influence but do not settle first-line selection.
- Which patients can safely attempt treatment-free remission beyond standard eligibility criteria is an active research question.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- IRIS: design, early stopping, and what it proved.
- First-line TKI selection by risk score and comorbidity.
- Molecular monitoring milestones and response definitions.
- Treatment-free remission: eligibility and relapse management.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 45-year-old newly diagnosed chronic-phase CML has ELTS high-risk score and prior myocardial infarction; choose first-line TKI and justify.
- A patient on imatinib for 6 years has sustained MR4.5 for 3 years; assess treatment-free-remission candidacy and the monitoring plan.

**Reviewer Gap**: The IRIS primary report (NEJM 2003) predates the pipeline's 2015+ evidence filter and is not among the retained abstracts; verify trial design details and response rates against the primary publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32127639**: *European LeukemiaNet 2020 recommendations for treating chronic myeloid leukemia.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39093014**: *Chronic myeloid leukemia: 2025 update on diagnosis, therapy, and monitoring.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 40616814**: *Management of chronic myeloid leukemia in 2025.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0918] immune checkpoint inhibitor kidney injury

**Cluster**: ICI nephrotoxicity | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Immune checkpoint inhibitors have made kidney injury a new frontier of onco-nephrology. The signature lesion is acute tubulointerstitial nephritis, typically presenting as abrupt creatinine rise after weeks to months of therapy, often with subnephrotic proteinuria — but the differential includes prerenal states, obstructive uropathy, and glomerular patterns such as lupus-like nephritis and podocytopathies. The ASCO-Nephrology position statement standardises the approach: hold the checkpoint inhibitor, grade the injury, biopsy for atypical or severe presentations, start corticosteroids for significant AKI, and navigate the challenge of balancing oncologic benefit against renal and immune risk.

### Core Teaching Points
- ICI-associated AKI most commonly reflects acute tubulointerstitial nephritis, with onset typically after several weeks of therapy; glomerular patterns (minimal change, lupus-like, ANCA) occur less often. (PMID 35190109, PMID 39455026)
- Diagnostic work-up excludes prerenal and obstructive causes, reviews nephrotoxin and PPI exposure, and uses biopsy when atypical features (nephrotic proteinuria, active sediment, no steroid response) are present. (PMID 39455026, PMID 35190109)
- Management: hold the ICI, grade severity, and treat higher-grade injury with corticosteroids (typically 0.5–1 mg/kg prednisone-equivalent, higher for severe presentations), tapering over weeks with close creatinine surveillance. (PMID 39138117, PMID 39455026)
- Re-challenge decisions weigh oncology urgency against relapse risk; steroid-sparing agents (mycophenolate, infliximab) are reserved for steroid-refractory or relapsing cases. (PMID 39455026, PMID 39138117)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal steroid dose and duration, and whether early biopsy-guided therapy changes outcomes, lacks randomised evidence.
- ICI re-challenge after biopsy-proven nephritis: relapse rates and safety thresholds are incompletely defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Histological patterns and their clinical correlates.
- When to biopsy versus treat empirically with steroids.
- Steroid regimens and tapering by AKI grade.
- Re-challenge frameworks and steroid-sparing escalation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A melanoma patient on pembrolizumab has creatinine doubling with bland sediment at week 9; outline the diagnostic and treatment pathway.
- A patient with ICI nephritis relapses on steroid taper with ongoing oncologic need; discuss re-challenge versus steroid-sparing therapy.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are reviews/position statements without the underlying biopsy-cohort data; verify response and relapse rates against primary cohort studies before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35190109**: *Immunotherapy-Related Acute Kidney Injury.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 39138117**: *Immune checkpoint inhibitor-associated nephritis-treatment standard.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 39455026**: *Diagnosis and management of immune checkpoint inhibitor-associated nephrotoxicity: a position statement from the American Society of Oncology and Nephrology.* (2024) [position-statement] - None

## [0919] Immune-Mediated Necrotizing Myopathy

**Cluster**: IMNM | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy is the most aggressive of the idiopathic inflammatory myopathies: severe proximal weakness, creatine kinase often in the thousands, myofiber necrosis with minimal inflammation on biopsy, and a stubborn tendency to relapse. Two antibody-defined subtypes dominate — anti-SRP and anti-HMGCR — and the HMGCR story links it to statins, creating a practical problem: how to manage dyslipidaemia when the patient's own immune system attacks the drug target. Modern management is escalating immunosuppression early (often combination therapy), intravenous immunoglobulin for refractory disease, and — for anti-HMGCR patients — PCSK9 inhibitors as a statin-sparing lipid strategy that sidesteps the HMGCR antigen entirely.

### Core Teaching Points
- IMNM presents with subacute severe proximal weakness and markedly elevated CK; biopsy shows myofiber necrosis with minimal inflammatory infiltrate, distinguishing it from polymyositis and dermatomyositis. (PMID 38801022, PMID 38589696)
- Anti-SRP and anti-HMGCR antibodies define the major subtypes with differing phenotypes, malignancy associations, and treatment-response profiles; antibody testing is diagnostic and prognostic. (PMID 41458822, PMID 38589696)
- First-line treatment is high-dose corticosteroids with early steroid-sparing immunosuppression; IVIG and combination therapy are used for refractory disease, with treatment often prolonged because relapse on tapering is common. (PMID 38801022, PMID 41458822)
- In statin-associated anti-HMGCR disease, statins must stop permanently; PCSK9 inhibitors provide effective cholesterol lowering without engaging the HMGCR antigen, making them a practical post-IMNM lipid strategy. (PMID 40249406, PMID 38801022)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal induction regimen (steroid monotherapy versus early combination) and IVIG dosing schedules are not standardised.
- Whether prolonged remission-maintenance therapy prevents relapse better than careful tapering is unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Antibody-defined subtypes and their distinguishing features.
- Biopsy interpretation: necrosis with minimal inflammation.
- Escalation ladder: steroids, immunosuppressants, IVIG, rituximab.
- Managing dyslipidaemia after statin-associated IMNM.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient on atorvastatin develops progressive hip-shoulder weakness with CK 8,000 and anti-HMGCR positivity; outline diagnosis confirmation, treatment, and future lipid management.
- An anti-SRP IMNM patient relapses twice on tapering prednisone and azathioprine; design the escalation plan.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts lack the primary randomised rituximab-in-myositis trial and large IVIG trials; verify effect sizes before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38801022**: *Management of immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0920] Immune-Related Adverse Events

**Cluster**: irAEs | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Immune-related adverse events are the price of unleashing T-cell immunity against cancer: any organ can be attacked, typically within weeks to months of checkpoint-inhibitor therapy, and the management grammar is now standardised by the ASCO guideline and the NCCN immunotherapy-toxicity guidelines. The core logic is immunosuppression graded to severity — hold the drug for moderate events, corticosteroids for significant events, infliximab or other steroid-sparing agents for steroid-refractory disease — with organ-specific exceptions (endocrinopathies replace rather than suppress, neurologic and cardiac events demand aggressive early treatment, and checkpoint re-challenge is individually weighed).

### Core Teaching Points
- irAEs can affect any organ system with characteristic patterns (colitis, pneumonitis, dermatitis, hypophysitis, thyroiditis, hepatitis, myocarditis); vigilance is required because presentations mimic infection, progression, and other toxicities. (PMID 34724392, PMID 32200442)
- Grading drives action: grade 1 often continues therapy with monitoring, grade 2 usually holds the drug and starts corticosteroids, grades 3–4 stop therapy and require high-dose immunosuppression. (PMID 34724392, PMID 39536465)
- Endocrinopathies (hypophysitis, thyroiditis, adrenalitis) are managed with hormone replacement rather than immunosuppression once the diagnosis is secure, with permanent deficits common. (PMID 32200442, PMID 39536465)
- High-mortality events — myocarditis and neurologic irAEs — warrant immediate high-dose steroids and multidisciplinary care; re-challenge after serious irAEs is generally avoided. (PMID 39536465, PMID 34724392)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether biomarkers can predict irAE risk before checkpoint-inhibitor initiation is not clinically validated.
- Optimal steroid-sparing sequencing for steroid-refractory irAEs varies by organ and lacks comparative trials.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- irAE grading and the hold/continue/stop thresholds.
- Corticosteroid dosing by grade and tapering principles.
- Organ-specific exceptions: endocrinopathy versus colitis versus myocarditis.
- Re-challenge frameworks after resolved irAEs.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient on combination nivolumab-ipilimumab has grade 3 diarrhoea with cramping; outline grading confirmation, steroid therapy, and steroid-refractory escalation.
- A checkpoint-inhibitor patient presents with fatigue, hypotension, and hyponatraemia; distinguish adrenalitis from other causes and manage accordingly.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are guideline-level; organ-specific dose tables and taper schedules should be verified against the full ASCO/NCCN documents before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34724392**: *Management of Immune-Related Adverse Events in Patients Treated With Immune Checkpoint Inhibitor Therapy: ASCO Guideline.* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39536465**: *NCCN Guidelines Insights: Management of Immunotherapy-Related Toxicities, Version 2.2024.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 32200442**: *Immune-Related Adverse Events (irAEs): Diagnosis, Management, and Clinical Pearls.* (2020) [review] - None
