## [0921] Immune Thrombocytopenia

**Cluster**: ITP | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Immune thrombocytopenia is a diagnosis of exclusion made at any age, and modern management is built around two documents: the updated International Consensus Report and the ASH 2019 guidelines. Both shift thinking away from platelet-number reflexes toward bleeding-risk reasoning — adults with counts above 20–30 x10^9/L and no bleeding often need only observation, while treatment is warranted for bleeding, procedures, or lifestyle constraints. Corticosteroids with or without IVIG remain first line, and the guidelines explicitly resist over-treatment, favouring shorter steroid courses and shared decision-making for everything beyond.

### Core Teaching Points
- ITP diagnosis requires exclusion of secondary causes and pseudothrombocytopenia; the international consensus report standardises investigation including HIV, HCV, and Helicobacter pylori testing where appropriate. (PMID 31770441)
- ASH 2019 guidelines recommend observation alone for many asymptomatic adults with platelets above 20–30 x10^9/L, and short-course corticosteroids over prolonged courses or IVIG as first-line treatment when therapy is needed. (PMID 31794604)
- Second-line options — TPO receptor agonists, rituximab, splenectomy, fostamatinib — are selected by patient factors including durability goals, comorbidities, and surgical candidacy rather than a fixed sequence. (PMID 31794604, PMID 31770441)
- Emergency management of severe bleeding uses combined platelet transfusion, IVIG, and high-dose corticosteroids; thrombopoietin agonists are not emergency agents. (PMID 31770441, PMID 31794604)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal sequencing of second-line therapies (TPO-RAs versus rituximab versus splenectomy) lacks head-to-head trials.
- Whether early TPO-RA use alters long-term natural history is unknown.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Observation thresholds and first-line treatment selection by guideline.
- Work-up for secondary ITP.
- Emergency ITP management with active bleeding.
- Second-line option selection logic.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An asymptomatic 30-year-old has platelets 24 x10^9/L on routine testing; decide whether to treat and what to work up.
- An ITP patient has gastrointestinal bleeding with platelets 4 x10^9/L; outline the emergency regimen.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are guideline-level; specific dosing tables and the TPO-RA evidence base are covered under topic 922 — verify cross-references before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31770441**: *Updated international consensus report on the investigation and management of primary immune thrombocytopenia.* (2019) [consensus-report] - None
- **PMID 31794604**: *American Society of Hematology 2019 guidelines for immune thrombocytopenia.* (2019) [guideline] - None

## [0922] Immune thrombocytopenia: thrombopoietin receptor agonists

**Cluster**: ITP | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Thrombopoietin receptor agonists changed the chronic-ITP landscape by stimulating platelet production rather than suppressing destruction: eltrombopag (oral, daily, food and chelation interactions) and romiplostim (weekly subcutaneous peptide) produce durable responses in most patients who fail first-line therapy, and a decade of use has clarified their real profile. They are not benign: arterial and venous thrombosis, paradoxical with a 'bleeding disease', is the signal that systematic review and cohort data quantify — risk concentrates with cardiovascular comorbidity, high normalised platelet counts, and portal-hypertension contexts. Dose is titrated to a safe platelet range, not to normalisation.

### Core Teaching Points
- TPO-RAs (eltrombopag, romiplostim) are second-line agents for persistent/chronic ITP that raise platelet counts through thrombopoietin-receptor stimulation of megakaryopoiesis, with response in a majority of patients. (PMID 30880797, PMID 31073079)
- Practical differences matter: eltrombopag is oral but requires separation from food and polyvalent-cation-containing medicines; romiplostim is administered weekly by injection — choice is individualised by adherence and preference. (PMID 31073079, PMID 30880797)
- Thrombosis is the principal serious risk: meta-analysis and observational cohorts show increased arterial and venous thrombotic events, modulated by age, comorbidity, and achieved platelet count. (PMID 35007700, PMID 38357229)
- Dosing targets a safe hemostatic platelet range (commonly 50 x10^9/L or above) rather than normalisation, balancing bleeding protection against thrombotic risk. (PMID 31073079, PMID 35007700)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether thrombosis risk is drug-specific (eltrombopag versus romiplostim) or class-wide is unresolved.
- Optimal duration and whether some patients can taper off TPO-RAs after sustained response is not established.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Mechanism and the production-versus-destruction distinction.
- Eltrombopag administration rules and interactions.
- Thrombosis risk quantification and mitigation.
- Platelet targets and monitoring schedules.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A chronic-ITP patient on eltrombopag takes antacids and calcium; explain the interaction and redesign the regimen.
- A 70-year-old with prior DVT needs a TPO-RA; weigh thrombosis risk and set the platelet target.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts lack the primary EXTEND/Long-Term extension trial reports; verify durability and long-term safety rates before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31073079**: *Thrombopoietin receptor agonists: ten years later.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 30880797**: *Thrombopoietin Receptor Agonists: Eltrombopag and Romiplostim for the Treatment of Chronic Immune Thrombocytopenia.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 35007700**: *Risk of thrombosis with thrombopoietin receptor agonists for ITP patients: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 38357229**: *Thrombopoietin receptor agonists use and risk of thrombotic events in patients with immune thrombocytopenic purpura.* (2024) [cohort-study] - None

## [0923] Immunosuppression Post-Transplant

**Cluster**: transplant immunosuppression | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Maintenance immunosuppression after solid-organ transplantation is a balancing act calibrated in milligrams and nanograms: too little invites rejection, too much delivers nephrotoxicity, infection, and malignancy. Tacrolimus — a calcineurin inhibitor with narrow therapeutic index — anchors nearly all modern regimens, combined with mycophenolate and tapered steroids; systematic-review evidence supports tacrolimus-based maintenance as the standard. The craft lies in the detail: trough-level monitoring against a steep concentration-response curve, CYP3A5 pharmacogenetics that explain much inter-patient variability, and long-term stewardship that weighs dose minimisation against the rising cardiovascular and metabolic burden of decades of therapy.

### Core Teaching Points
- Modern maintenance regimens centre on tacrolimus with mycophenolate and tapered corticosteroids; tacrolimus-based maintenance is supported by systematic-review evidence as the standard of care in kidney transplantation. (PMID 34963678, PMID 33853841)
- Tacrolimus has a narrow therapeutic window with nephrotoxicity and neurotoxicity above range and rejection below; trough monitoring with dose titration is mandatory, especially early post-transplant. (PMID 29380698)
- Pharmacogenetics — CYP3A5 expresser status — substantially alters tacrolimus metabolism and dosing requirements, informing genotype-guided initial dosing in some centres. (PMID 29380698)
- Long-term management must reconcile competing harms: chronic calcineurin-inhibitor toxicity, cardiovascular and metabolic burden, infection, and malignancy risk against late rejection from over-minimisation. (PMID 33853841)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal long-term minimisation strategies (steroid withdrawal, CNI reduction with mTOR substitution) remain individualised with incomplete evidence.
- Whether pharmacogenetic dosing improves hard graft outcomes versus trough-guided titration is not established.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of standard triple maintenance therapy.
- Tacrolimus monitoring, interactions (azoles, macrolides), and toxicity.
- CYP3A5 pharmacogenetics and dose individualisation.
- Long-term minimisation versus late-rejection trade-offs.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A kidney-transplant patient on tacrolimus starts fluconazole; predict the interaction and adjust the plan.
- Five years post-transplant with stable graft and rising metabolic burden; discuss minimisation options and their risks.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are kidney-focused; heart/liver/lung-specific regimen differences are not covered — verify against organ-specific guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29380698**: *Pharmacokinetics, Pharmacodynamics and Pharmacogenetics of Tacrolimus in Kidney Transplantation.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 34963678**: *Efficacy and Safety of Tacrolimus-Based Maintenance Regimens in De Novo Kidney Transplant Recipients.* (2021) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 33853841**: *Long-Term Immunosuppression Management: Opportunities and Uncertainties.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0924] Inclisiran for LDL cholesterol lowering

**Cluster**: inclisiran | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Inclisiran is the first siRNA to move lipid management from adherence-dependent daily pills to twice-yearly hepatocyte-directed dosing: it silences PCSK9 mRNA, lowering LDL-C by roughly 50% on top of statins — a similar magnitude to monoclonal PCSK9 antibodies but delivered in a way that solves the adherence problem that undermines chronic lipid therapy. The ORION programme established efficacy and a reassuring serious-adverse-event profile, and cardiovascular-outcome trials are maturing. Its practical appeal is healthcare-system as much as patient: administration by a clinician every six months converts a daily behaviour into a scheduled event.

### Core Teaching Points
- Inclisiran is a synthetic siRNA that silences hepatocyte PCSK9 production, lowering LDL-C by approximately 50% as monotherapy or on top of statins, with effects persisting between twice-yearly doses. (PMID 39640378, PMID 34199468)
- The twice-yearly maintenance dosing schedule (after initial and 3-month doses) addresses non-adherence, the dominant cause of suboptimal lipid control in practice. (PMID 39439657, PMID 39640378)
- Meta-analysis of the ORION trials found no significant increase in serious adverse events versus control, with injection-site reactions the commonest specific complaint. (PMID 37930224)
- Current positioning is adjunctive for patients not achieving LDL targets on maximally tolerated statins (with or without ezetimibe); cardiovascular-outcome trial evidence is still accumulating. (PMID 34199468, PMID 39640378)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Hard cardiovascular-outcome benefit (versus the well-established LDL-C surrogate) is pending outcome-trial completion.
- Long-term (>5 year) safety and durability of response with real-world adherence patterns are not yet mature.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- siRNA mechanism versus monoclonal PCSK9 antibodies.
- ORION trial efficacy magnitudes and persistence.
- Dosing schedule and the adherence rationale.
- Safety profile from ORION meta-analysis.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with familial hypercholesterolaemia has LDL-C 3.8 mmol/L on high-intensity statin plus ezetimibe with poor pill adherence; argue for inclisiran.
- A clinic debates inclisiran versus evolocumab for equivalent LDL-C lowering; compare mechanisms, frequency, and evidence maturity.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the primary ORION-10/11 reports or the ORION-4 outcomes trial; verify exact LDL-C effect sizes and outcome-trial status before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39640378**: *Inclisiran for the treatment of hypercholesterolaemia.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 34199468**: *Inclisiran - Silencing the Cholesterol, Speaking up the Prognosis.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 37930224**: *Serious Adverse Events of Inclisiran in Patients With Hypercholesterolemia, a Meta-Analysis of ORION Trials.* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39439657**: *Inclisiran: A Systematic Review Exploring the Twice-Yearly Dosing Regimen.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None

## [0925] Inclusion Body Myositis (IBM): asymmetric weakness, rimmed vacuoles, treatment resistance

**Cluster**: IBM | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Inclusion body myositis is the myopathy that refuses to behave: slowly progressive, asymmetric weakness — finger flexors and quadriceps characteristically — in older men, with rimmed vacuoles and protein aggregates on biopsy, and a well-earned reputation for treatment resistance. It is frequently misdiagnosed as polymyositis and exposed only when immunosuppression fails. Modern understanding frames it as a degenerative process with inflammatory features, which explains why immunotherapies that work for other myosities disappoint here; management is therefore functional preservation, falls prevention, and honest prognostic counselling, with clinical-trial awareness the main therapeutic hope.

### Core Teaching Points
- IBM presents in older adults with slowly progressive, often asymmetric weakness with early finger-flexor and quadriceps involvement; falling and grip loss are characteristic functional clues. (PMID 36537974, PMID 36069417)
- Biopsy shows rimmed vacuoles, endomysial inflammation, and protein aggregates (including TDP-43 and p62); the degenerative-plus-inflammatory signature underlies treatment resistance. (PMID 36069417, PMID 39469805)
- Immunosuppression lacks robust efficacy in IBM — a key discriminator from polymyositis and dermatomyositis; misdiagnosis as treatment-refractory polymyositis should prompt re-evaluation for IBM. (PMID 39469805, PMID 36537974)
- Management centres on exercise and rehabilitation to preserve function, falls prevention, dysphagia surveillance, and enrolment into clinical trials; no disease-modifying therapy is established. (PMID 39469805, PMID 36069417)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether targeted anti-inflammatory or anti-degenerative strategies can modify progression is untested at scale.
- Optimal role of corticosteroids or IVIG in selected subsets (e.g. rapidly progressive or dysphagia-predominant) remains individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Clinical pattern: asymmetric finger-flexor and quadriceps weakness in older men.
- Histology: rimmed vacuoles and inclusions versus other myositides.
- Why immunosuppression fails — the degeneration-inflammation duality.
- Functional management: rehab, falls, dysphagia, trials.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old man has 3 years of progressive grip weakness, quadriceps wasting, and falls, 'unresponsive to steroids'; reassess the diagnosis.
- An IBM patient develops progressive dysphagia; outline the swallowing-assessment and management pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are reviews without trial-level data; verify current clinical-trial landscape and any new agent signals before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36069417**: *Inclusion body myositis: evolving concepts.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 39469805**: *Inclusion body myositis: an update.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 36537974**: *Inclusion Body Myositis.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0926] Induction therapy in AAV: rituximab vs cyclophosphamide, RITUXVAS, RAVE trials

**Cluster**: AAV induction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Remission induction in ANCA-associated vasculitis was reshaped by two trials: RAVE, which showed rituximab non-inferior — and for relapsing disease superior — to cyclophosphamide for early severe GPA/MPA, and RITUXVAS, which confirmed non-inferiority in a broader severe cohort. The result, codified in the EULAR 2022 recommendations, is that rituximab is now preferred first-line induction for most new and relapsing patients, with cyclophosphamide retained for organ-threatening emergencies and cost-constrained settings. The nuance examiners love: rituximab's advantage is strongest at relapse and in PR3-ANCA disease, and glucocorticoid-sparing regimens (avacopan era) now accompany both arms.

### Core Teaching Points
- RAVE established rituximab as non-inferior to cyclophosphamide for remission induction in early severe GPA/MPA, with superiority in relapsing disease. (PMID 28155022, PMID 25753806)
- RITUXVAS confirmed non-inferiority of rituximab-based induction in severe AAV with renal involvement, cementing B-cell depletion as a standard induction option. (PMID 27471722, PMID 28155022)
- EULAR 2022 recommendations position rituximab as preferred induction for new and relapsing GPA/MPA, reserving cyclophosphamide for selected organ-threatening presentations and specific contraindications. (PMID 36927642)
- Relapse prediction guides intensity: PR3-ANCA positivity and prior relapse favour rituximab, which also serves as maintenance with repeat dosing. (PMID 36927642, PMID 25753806)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal glucocorticoid taper speed and the integrating role of C5a-receptor inhibition (avacopan) with either induction arm continue to evolve.
- Rituximab dosing schedules for maintenance (fixed interval versus CD19-guided) are not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- RAVE and RITUXVAS designs, populations, and what each proved.
- When cyclophosphamide is still preferred over rituximab.
- PR3-ANCA versus MPO-ANCA as treatment-selection biomarkers.
- Maintenance strategies after rituximab induction.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 54-year-old with new GPA, PR3-positive, and creatinine 340 umol/L needs induction; choose and justify the regimen.
- A patient relapses 18 months after cyclophosphamide induction; argue why rituximab is preferred now.

**Reviewer Gap**: The RAVE/RITUXVAS primary reports predate the pipeline's 2015+ filter and are not among the retained abstracts; verify trial arms, remission definitions, and exact outcomes against the primary publications before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36927642**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of ANCA-associated vasculitis: 2022 update.* (2023) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 28155022**: *Rituximab in ANCA-Associated Vasculitis.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 25753806**: *B-cell therapy in antineutrophil cytoplasmic antibody-associated vasculitis.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 27471722**: *Place in therapy of rituximab in the treatment of granulomatosis with polyangiitis and microscopic polyangiitis.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0927] Infection in pregnancy: GBS screening, listeria, toxoplasmosis, antiviral safety

**Cluster**: infection in pregnancy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Infections in pregnancy demand dual-patient thinking: the mother, the fetus, and the drugs that must be safe for both. Group B streptococcus is the organised success story — universal late-gestation screening with intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis for colonised mothers has slashed early-onset neonatal disease, with ACOG's Committee Opinion defining screening timing, risk-based alternatives, and prophylaxis regimens. Beyond GBS, the clinician must hold the less algorithmic territory: listeriosis and toxoplasmosis as foodborne and cat-associated risks managed by prevention and targeted testing, antiviral safety questions in influenza and herpes infections, and the recognition that sepsis remains a leading cause of maternal mortality with modified (pregnancy-appropriate) early-warning criteria.

### Core Teaching Points
- Universal antenatal GBS screening at 36 0/7–37 6/7 weeks with intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis (penicillin first-line) for colonised or high-risk mothers is the standard for preventing early-onset neonatal disease. (PMID 31977795, PMID 41740033)
- Approximately half of women colonised with GBS transmit to the newborn; intrapartum prophylaxis reduces early-onset disease transmission, with risk-based prophylaxis for untested women with obstetric risk factors. (PMID 39584819, PMID 31977795)
- Maternal sepsis is a leading cause of maternal mortality; major guidelines emphasise early recognition with pregnancy-adjusted warning scores, source control, and foetal surveillance alongside maternal resuscitation. (PMID 37043300)
- Prevention education remains central for listeriosis (food hygiene) and toxoplasmosis (cat-litter and undercooked-meat avoidance); serological testing and treatment decisions follow exposure-risk assessment. (PMID 39584819)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal management of penicillin-allergic GBS-colonised mothers (clindamycin resistance concerns) varies by region and testing availability.
- Toxoplasmosis treatment regimens in pregnancy (spiramycin versus pyrimethamine-sulfadiazine timing) follow regional protocols with limited trial evidence.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- GBS screening timing, prophylaxis indications, and antibiotic choice.
- Risk-based prophylaxis when screening status is unknown.
- Pregnancy-adjusted sepsis recognition and management.
- Listeriosis/toxoplasmosis prevention and counselling.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A woman at 39 weeks arrives in labour with unknown GBS status and prolonged rupture of membranes; decide on intrapartum prophylaxis.
- A pregnant patient with fever, tachycardia, and uterine tenderness is 'not that unwell' by generic sepsis scores; apply pregnancy-appropriate criteria.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is GBS- and sepsis-weighted; listeria, toxoplasmosis, and antiviral safety are covered only peripherally — verify management specifics against dedicated reviews before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31977795**: *Prevention of Group B Streptococcal Early-Onset Disease in Newborns: ACOG Committee Opinion, Number 797.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 39584819**: *Group B streptococcal infections in pregnancy and early life.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 41740033**: *Group B Streptococcal Disease.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 37043300**: *Sepsis in Pregnancy and the Puerperium: A Comparative Review of Major Guidelines.* (2023) [guideline-comparison] - None

## [0928] Infection in solid organ transplant: timeline, CMV prophylaxis, PCP, opportunistic pathogens

**Cluster**: transplant infection | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Infection after solid-organ transplantation is predictable by clock and immunosuppression intensity: the first month brings nosocomial and surgical complications, months one to six bring the opportunists — CMV above all, plus Pneumocystis, Aspergillus, and BK virus — and beyond six months, community-acquired infections dominate with immune-deficiency patterns in over-immunosuppressed patients. CMV is the single most consequential pathogen: direct disease (colitis, pneumonitis) and indirect effects (rejection, graft injury) are addressed by universal or targeted prophylaxis with valganciclovir and preemptive strategies guided by viral-load monitoring, with maribavir now available for refractory disease. PCP prophylaxis with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole is standard for the early period — one prescription that prevents the most preventable transplant death.

### Core Teaching Points
- The post-transplant infection timeline structures the differential: healthcare-associated infections in month one, opportunistic infections (CMV, PCP, moulds, BK) in months 1–6, and community-acquired infections thereafter. (PMID 33879502, PMID 31668195)
- CMV is the most common significant post-transplant infection; D+/R- serostatus defines highest risk, and direct (tissue-invasive) plus indirect (rejection-linked) effects both demand prevention. (PMID 37899366, PMID 33879502)
- Prevention strategies are prophylaxis (valganciclovir for high-risk recipients) versus preemptive therapy guided by quantitative PCR monitoring — both effective, selection depends on serostatus, organ, and logistics. (PMID 39492698, PMID 37899366)
- Pneumocystis prophylaxis with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole is standard for at least 6–12 months post-transplant; alternatives (atovaquone, dapsone, pentamidine) serve sulfa-allergic patients. (PMID 31668195)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of CMV prophylaxis and the long-term value of extending beyond six months in high-risk recipients are debated.
- Letermovir's expanding role in SOT (established in HCT) and its positioning versus valganciclovir is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The timeline framework by transplant month.
- CMV serostatus risk stratification and prophylaxis-versus-preemptive selection.
- PCP prophylaxis duration and sulfa-allergy alternatives.
- Late infections and over-immunosuppression patterns.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A D+/R- kidney recipient at week 9 has fever, diarrhoea, and a rising CMV PCR; outline the diagnostic and treatment pathway including resistance considerations.
- A transplant patient at 3 months has dyspnoea and hypoxia with normal chest X-ray; build the differential and the PCP work-up.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts lack the primary CMV management guideline (AST ID Community of Practice); verify prophylaxis durations and treatment dosing against it before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33879502**: *Long-Term Infectious Complications of Kidney Transplantation.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 37899366**: *What's New: Updates on Cytomegalovirus in Solid Organ Transplantation.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 31668195**: *Opportunistic Infections in Transplant Patients.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 39492698**: *Current and Future Strategies for the Prevention and Treatment of Cytomegalovirus Infections in Transplantation.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0929] Infection prevention and control: standard precautions, contact isolation, hand hygiene evidence

**Cluster**: infection prevention and control | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Infection prevention and control is the highest-yield low-technology discipline in medicine: healthcare-worker hands are the dominant transmission vehicle for hospital-acquired infection, and hand hygiene at the WHO five moments remains the single most effective intervention — systematic review confirms compliance reduces HAI, while behaviour-change literature shows multimodal programmes (education, feedback, cues, leadership) outperform posters alone. Above standard precautions, contact precautions (gowns, gloves, single rooms) for multidrug-resistant organisms are standard practice despite contested effectiveness data — the systematic-review evidence is conflicted, and the modern debate centres on targeted versus universal application, balancing transmission prevention against isolation's harms to patients and care processes.

### Core Teaching Points
- Hand hygiene is the cornerstone of standard precautions; systematic-review evidence links healthcare-worker hand-hygiene compliance with reduced hospital-acquired infection rates. (PMID 34582962)
- Multimodal interventions — education, performance feedback, reminders, environmental cues, and administrative support — improve and sustain hand-hygiene compliance better than single-strategy approaches. (PMID 28862335)
- Contact precautions (gown, gloves, single room) for MDRO-colonised patients are guideline-standard, but systematic-review evidence of their incremental effectiveness over standard precautions shows conflicting results. (PMID 26051927, PMID 31304525)
- Isolation carries measurable downsides — fewer clinician visits, adverse events, patient experience harm — informing the shift toward targeted precautions and universal gloving in endemic settings. (PMID 26051927)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether contact precautions add transmission protection beyond good standard precautions and terminal cleaning is unresolved in endemic settings.
- Optimal isolation discontinuation criteria (clearance cultures versus time-based) are not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- WHO five moments and compliance measurement.
- Evidence behind multimodal hand-hygiene programmes.
- Contact-precaution components and the MDRO indications.
- The evidence controversy: targeted versus universal precautions.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A ward's HAI rate is rising despite 'hand hygiene posters everywhere'; design an evidence-based multimodal intervention.
- A patient with carbapenemase-producing Klebsiella needs admission; outline the isolation decision and its trade-offs.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include WHO guideline documents themselves; verify precaution indications and durations against national/WHO IPC guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34582962**: *Hand hygiene compliance in the prevention of hospital-acquired infections: a systematic review.* (2022) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 28862335**: *Interventions to improve hand hygiene compliance in patient care.* (2017) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 26051927**: *Effectiveness of contact precautions against multidrug-resistant organism transmission in acute care: a systematic review.* (2015) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 31304525**: *Contact precaution procedures in healthcare facilities.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0930] Infection risk with biologics: TB, HBV reactivation, PJP prophylaxis thresholds

**Cluster**: biologics infection risk | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Biologic and targeted therapies have transformed immune-mediated disease, but each mechanism opens a specific infection door: TNF inhibitors reactivate latent tuberculosis and hepatitis B, B-cell depletion disarms long-term humoral immunity, JAK inhibitors carry herpes-zoster and opportunist signals, and IL-6 blockade masks fever and CRP — the inflammatory alarms that normally announce infection. The ESGICH consensus documents systematise the safety profile agent-by-agent, while the EULAR 2022 recommendations (built on systematic review) define the screening and prophylaxis grammar: latent-TB testing and HBV serology before starting, antiviral prophylaxis for HBV carriers, and PJP prophylaxis when compounded immunosuppression crosses defined thresholds.

### Core Teaching Points
- Screening before biologic initiation: latent-TB testing (IGRA/tuberculin) and HBV serology (HBsAg, anti-HBc) are standard across drug classes, with reactivation risk drug- and regimen-dependent. (PMID 36323488, PMID 29459143)
- HBV reactivation in resolved or chronic carriers on B-cell-depleting or TNF therapy requires antiviral prophylaxis (entecavir/tenofovir) or intensified monitoring, per EULAR-informed risk stratification. (PMID 36323488, PMID 29447988)
- PJP prophylaxis is recommended when high-dose steroids combine with biologic immunosuppression crossing defined thresholds (e.g. prolonged ≥20–30 mg prednisone-equivalent with second immunosuppressant). (PMID 36323488)
- Drug-specific infection signatures matter clinically: TNF inhibition raises TB, HBV, and deep fungal risk; IL-6 blockade blunts fever/CRP responses to infection; JAK inhibitors carry zoster and opportunist signals. (PMID 29459143, PMID 29447988)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal PJP prophylaxis thresholds for newer agents (JAK inhibitors, IL-23 blockade) are extrapolated rather than trial-defined.
- Whether HBV screening paradigms need updating for lower-risk agents is debated.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pre-biologic screening panel and timing.
- HBV reactivation risk tiers and prophylaxis decisions.
- PJP prophylaxis thresholds with combined immunosuppression.
- Drug-specific infection signatures (TB, zoster, blunted CRP response).

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient starting rituximab for vasculitis has anti-HBc positive, HBsAg negative serology; outline HBV management.
- A patient on tocilizumab plus 25 mg prednisolone presents afebrile with 'normal CRP' and subtle pneumonia; apply drug-specific vigilance.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained ESGICH documents span specific agent groups (parts I and II cover different biologic classes); verify the specific agent in question is covered before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29459143**: *ESCMID Study Group for Infections in Compromised Hosts (ESGICH) Consensus Document on the safety of targeted and biological therapies (part I).* (2018) [consensus-document] - None
- **PMID 29447988**: *ESCMID Study Group for Infections in Compromised Hosts (ESGICH) Consensus Document on the safety of targeted and biological therapies (part II).* (2018) [consensus-document] - None
- **PMID 36323488**: *Systematic literature review informing the 2022 EULAR recommendations for screening and prophylaxis of chronic and opportunistic infections in autoimmune inflammatory rheumatic diseases.* (2022) [systematic-review] - None

## [0931] Infectious diarrhea and travelers diarrhea management

**Cluster**: infectious diarrhoea | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute diarrhoea is one of the commonest presentations in any setting — and one where restraint is the evidence-based skill. Most adult infectious diarrhoea, traveller's included, is viral or self-limiting bacterial disease managed with rehydration alone; the art is identifying who needs stool testing, who needs antibiotics, and who needs admission. Inflammatory features (blood, mucus, fever, severe pain) predict bacterial aetiology and shape empiric choices, while the pretravel consultation adds prevention: food and water precautions, and standby antibiotics for high-risk itineraries. Norovirus remains the commonest overall cause — a diagnosis of clinical and infection-control consequence.

### Core Teaching Points
- Most acute diarrhoea in adults is self-limiting; oral rehydration is the foundation of therapy, with antibiotics reserved for suspected bacterial dysentery, severe disease, or specific risk factors. (PMID 35839362, PMID 30115338)
- Inflammatory features (bloody stool, fever, tenesmus, severe abdominal pain) distinguish dysentery from watery diarrhoea and drive stool-culture and empiric-antibiotic decisions. (PMID 35839362)
- Norovirus is the commonest cause of gastroenteritis overall; recognition matters for infection control and because antibiotics have no role. (PMID 30115338)
- Traveller's diarrhoea management combines rehydration, loperamide for non-bloody cases, and standby antibiotics (fluoroquinolone or azithromycin) for moderate-to-severe cases on high-risk itineraries. (PMID 32179056, PMID 35839362)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether routine probiotic use shortens acute diarrhoea in adults remains inconsistent across trials.
- Optimal empiric antibiotic choice is shifting with fluoroquinolone-resistance patterns in Campylobacter and Shigella.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Who gets antibiotics: dysentery features and risk stratification.
- Rehydration principles and loperamide cautions.
- Norovirus recognition and infection-control duties.
- Pretravel prophylaxis and standby-therapy selection.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A returned traveller has 5 days of watery diarrhoea without blood; decide on testing, loperamide, and standby-antibiotic use.
- A nursing-home outbreak of vomiting and diarrhoea spreads rapidly; identify the likely pathogen and the control measures.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include current antibiotic-susceptibility guidance for Shigella and Campylobacter; verify empiric choices against local/national guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35839362**: *Acute Diarrhea in Adults.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 32179056**: *The Pretravel Consultation: Recent Updates.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 30115338**: *Common Gastrointestinal Infections.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0932] Infectious diarrhoea: Campylobacter, Salmonella, STEC O157, norovirus, antibiotic indications

**Cluster**: infectious diarrhoea | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Where topic 931 covers the general management of diarrhoea, this topic is about the organism-level decision that catches clinicians out: whether to give antibiotics. Campylobacter and non-typhoidal Salmonella mostly need no antibiotics in the well adult; suspected Shiga-toxin-producing E. coli (STEC O157) is the dangerous exception in reverse — antibiotics increase haemolytic uraemic syndrome risk and must be avoided. Norovirus needs neither antibiotics nor testing in most settings, but strict infection control. The single most testable pearl: bloody diarrhoea plus exposure to undercooked beef equals no antibiotics, and HUS surveillance — the syndrome that defines thrombotic microangiopathy in this context.

### Core Teaching Points
- Antibiotics are indicated for severe suspected bacterial gastroenteritis, dysentery in specific groups, and confirmed treatable pathogens; most Campylobacter and non-typhoidal Salmonella infections in healthy adults resolve without them. (PMID 31271003)
- In suspected or confirmed STEC (E. coli O157) infection, antibiotics are contraindicated because they increase the risk of haemolytic uraemic syndrome; management is supportive with vigilant renal monitoring. (PMID 31271003, PMID 27723152)
- HUS is defined by the triad of non-immune haemolytic anaemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury from thrombotic microangiopathy; STEC is the dominant trigger and TTP the key differential within the TMA spectrum. (PMID 27723152, PMID 37949684)
- Norovirus, the commonest gastroenteritis cause, requires no antibiotics and no specific testing in sporadic adult cases; outbreak settings demand environmental disinfection and exclusion policies. (PMID 31271003)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether antibiotics benefit bloody Campylobacter dysentery in otherwise healthy adults is debated; practice varies.
- Optimal HUS management (hydration strategy, plasma exchange indications versus TTP) follows centre protocols rather than randomised evidence.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Antibiotic indications by pathogen: when and when-not.
- The STEC exception: why antibiotics are dangerous.
- HUS versus TTP: clinical and laboratory separation.
- Norovirus management and outbreak control.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A child-adjacent adult has bloody diarrhoea after a beef barbecue; justify withholding antibiotics and define the monitoring plan.
- A diarrhoea patient develops pallor, thrombocytopenia, and rising creatinine; distinguish HUS from TTP and outline initial management.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts lack organism-specific treatment reviews for Campylobacter and Salmonella; verify empiric regimens against the antibiotic guideline's full text before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31271003**: *Guideline for the Antibiotic Use in Acute Gastroenteritis.* (2020) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37949684**: *HUS and TTP: traversing the disease and the age spectrum.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 27723152**: *Haemolytic uraemic syndrome.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0933] infective endocarditis

**Cluster**: infective endocarditis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Infective endocarditis is a rare disease with outsized consequences, and its modern face has shifted: healthcare-associated infection, prosthetic valves, intracardiac devices, and staphylococci now dominate an epidemiology once ruled by viridans streptococci and rheumatic valves. The AHA scientific statement anchors diagnosis (modified Duke criteria), antimicrobial selection by organism and valve type, and complication management — heart failure, emboli, and the infected-device problems that need a team, not just a drug. Outcome still hinges on early recognition and the endocarditis-team model: infectious diseases, cardiology, cardiothoracic surgery, and microbiology deciding together.

### Core Teaching Points
- Modern IE epidemiology is driven by healthcare exposure, prosthetic valves, and intracardiac devices, with Staphylococcus aureus now the leading organism in many series. (PMID 31941729, PMID 40185554)
- The AHA scientific statement standardises diagnosis (modified Duke criteria), antimicrobial therapy by organism and valve type, and management of complications including heart failure, embolism, and device infection. (PMID 26373316)
- Outcomes improve with a multidisciplinary endocarditis team; surgery is indicated for heart failure, uncontrolled infection, and prevention of embolism in defined situations. (PMID 26373316, PMID 31941729)
- Blood cultures before antibiotics and echocardiography (TTE then TEE) are the non-negotiable first steps; the organism, not the syndrome, dictates the regimen. (PMID 26373316, PMID 40185554)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration and agent choice for device-related and culture-negative endocarditis remain guideline-extrapolated.
- The role of routine PET/CT beyond prosthetic-valve and device infection is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Modern epidemiology: who gets IE now.
- Blood-culture and imaging sequence before antibiotics.
- Complications mandating surgical referral.
- The endocarditis-team model and outcome benefit.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with a prosthetic valve and persistent fevers has two negative blood-culture sets; outline the extended work-up and empiric strategy.
- A febrile patient with a new murmur and a history of injection drug use is admitted; structure the first 24 hours of investigation.

**Reviewer Gap**: The cluster's antibiotic-regimen detail lives under topics 934/935/937; cross-reference those pages before approval to avoid content gaps.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26373316**: *Infective Endocarditis in Adults: Diagnosis, Antimicrobial Therapy, and Management of Complications: A Scientific Statement for Healthcare Professionals (AHA).* (2015) [scientific-statement] - None
- **PMID 31941729**: *Infective endocarditis: A contemporary update.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 40185554**: *Infective Endocarditis.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0934] Infective endocarditis antibiotic duration and surgery

**Cluster**: infective endocarditis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Two numbers govern infective endocarditis treatment: the weeks of antibiotics and the timing of surgery. Duration is set by organism and valve — viridans streptococci on a native valve can be cured in two weeks with combination therapy, while staphylococci on prosthetic material demand six — and reviews of the ESC framework examine where shorter, equally effective courses can replace tradition. Surgery is lifesaving but high-risk: perioperative optimisation, the classic indications (heart failure, uncontrolled infection, large vegetations with embolic risk), and the modern operative challenges of extensive root destruction and device extraction define the contemporary craft.

### Core Teaching Points
- Antibiotic duration in IE is pathogen- and valve-specific: 2 weeks is possible for selected viridans-group streptococcal native-valve disease with suitable agents, while staphylococcal and prosthetic-valve infection typically requires 4-6 weeks. (PMID 32728356)
- Reviews of the ESC treatment framework examine strategies to treat more effectively — shorter total courses and early oral switch in stable, selected patients — without loss of cure rates. (PMID 32728356)
- Surgical indications remain heart failure from valvular dysfunction, uncontrolled infection (abscess, persistent sepsis, prosthetic dehiscence), and embolism prevention for large/mobile vegetations, particularly with staphylococci. (PMID 37762834, PMID 38827544)
- Perioperative mortality in IE surgery is among the highest in cardiac surgery; preoperative optimisation of heart failure, sepsis, and organ dysfunction materially affects outcomes. (PMID 38827544)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether further duration shortening (e.g. two weeks for staphylococcal native-valve IE with surgery) is safe is untested outside selected series.
- Optimal timing of surgery after embolic stroke remains individualised by stroke severity and neuroimaging.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Duration by organism and valve type.
- Surgical indications: heart failure, uncontrolled infection, embolism.
- Perioperative optimisation priorities.
- Extensive-root-destruction and device-extraction operative challenges.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A viridans streptococcal native-valve IE patient is stable on day 12 of ceftriaxone; assess eligibility for short-course completion.
- An IE patient develops new heart failure with a large mobile aortic vegetation; argue the surgical timing.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts lack the primary DURATION-style trial data; verify short-course eligibility criteria against the full ESC/AHA documents before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32728356**: *Infective endocarditis - can we treat it more effectively?* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 38827544**: *Perioperative care in infective endocarditis.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 37762834**: *Surgical Challenges in Infective Endocarditis: State of the Art.* (2024) [review] - None

## [0935] Infective endocarditis antimicrobial and oral step-down strategy

**Cluster**: infective endocarditis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The POET trial broke the oldest rule in endocarditis treatment: that cure demands six weeks of intravenous antibiotics. In stable, non-septic patients on effective IV therapy with susceptible organisms, switching to oral combination therapy was non-inferior — transforming an inpatient doctrine into an outpatient pathway. The modern literature formalises who qualifies: clinical stability, documented susceptibility, suitable oral-agent bioavailability, no uncontrolled foci or high-resistance organisms, and a reliable patient with follow-up. Not every endocarditis qualifies — but the default of 'IV until week six' is now a choice, not a law.

### Core Teaching Points
- Partial oral treatment (POET) is non-inferior to continued IV therapy in stable left-sided endocarditis caused by susceptible streptococci, enterococci, and S. aureus, after an initial effective IV period. (PMID 41471204, PMID 42796692)
- Eligibility for oral step-down demands clinical stability, organism susceptibility, high-bioavailability oral agents, absence of uncontrolled infection foci or abscess, and reliable follow-up. (PMID 32240296, PMID 42796692)
- Oral regimens typically use combination therapy (two agents with different mechanisms) to protect against resistance emergence given sub-therapeutic tissue levels risk. (PMID 32240296, PMID 41471204)
- Exclusions for oral switch include prosthetic-valve infection in the early post-operative period, uncontrolled sepsis, heart failure awaiting surgery, and organisms with reduced oral-susceptibility options. (PMID 42796692, PMID 41471204)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether oral step-down applies to prosthetic-valve IE beyond selected low-risk cohorts is unresolved.
- Optimal duration of the initial IV period before switching (2 versus 4 weeks) by organism remains centre-defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- POET trial design, population, and non-inferiority result.
- Eligibility checklist for oral step-down.
- Combination-oral rationale and agent selection.
- Absolute and relative contraindications to switching.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A stable streptococcal mitral-valve IE patient completes 10 days of IV ceftriaxone, improving; assess oral-switch eligibility and design the regimen.
- A prosthetic-valve S. aureus IE patient asks about 'going home on tablets'; explain why the evidence does not support it here.

**Reviewer Gap**: The POET primary publication (NEJM 2019) is not among the retained abstracts; verify eligibility criteria and outcome rates against the primary report before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41471204**: *Oral Therapy for Infective Endocarditis: Where Do We Stand?* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 32240296**: *Oral antibiotics for infective endocarditis: a clinical review.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 42796692**: *Partial Oral Antibiotic Therapy for Infective Endocarditis: A Practical Review of Current Evidence and Guidelines.* (2025) [review] - None

## [0936] Infective endocarditis: Duke criteria, blood cultures, echocardiography, surgical indications

**Cluster**: infective endocarditis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The diagnostic spine of infective endocarditis is procedural discipline: at least three sets of blood cultures from separate sites before any antibiotics, followed by echocardiography — transthoracic first, transoesophageal when suspicion persists or prosthetic material is involved. The modified Duke criteria organise the result into definite, possible, and rejected, but their known weakness is sensitivity in the era of device infection and culture-negative disease, which is where advanced imaging earns its place. This topic is the instrument-focused view of the endocarditis cluster: get the cultures right, get the echo right, and the diagnosis usually declares itself.

### Core Teaching Points
- Blood-culture discipline: at least three sets from separate venepunctures over 12-24 hours before antibiotics, with cultures held for extended incubation when prior antibiotics were given. (PMID 31521667, PMID 27582414)
- Echocardiography follows a TTE-then-TEE logic: TEE is mandatory for prosthetic valves, persistent suspicion after negative TTE, and assessment of complications such as abscess and fistula. (PMID 28671807, PMID 31521667)
- The modified Duke criteria classify definite/possible/rejected endocarditis using major (positive echocardiogram, typical organism) and minor criteria; sensitivity limitations are increasingly recognised. (PMID 31521667, PMID 27582414)
- Surgical indications detected on imaging — heart failure from valve destruction, abscess/fistula formation, and persistent vegetations after emboli — convert the diagnostic pathway into an urgent surgical referral. (PMID 28671807, PMID 31521667)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- How to integrate PET/CT and cardiac CT into the Duke framework beyond prosthetic valves and devices is evolving.
- Optimal echocardiographic vegetation-size threshold for surgery is informed by trial subgroup data rather than absolute rules.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Blood-culture number, timing, and technique.
- TTE versus TEE indications.
- Duke major/minor criteria and their pitfalls.
- Imaging findings that mandate surgery.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A febrile patient received one dose of flucloxacillin in the emergency department before cultures; salvage the diagnostic pathway.
- A prosthetic-valve patient has negative TTE but persisting bacteraemia; justify TEE and the additional imaging you would consider.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the Duke criteria primary publication or multi-modality imaging consensus; verify imaging algorithms against ESC/AHA documents before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31521667**: *Clinical Practice Update on Infectious Endocarditis.* (2021) [clinical-practice-update] - None
- **PMID 27582414**: *Infective endocarditis.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 28671807**: *Valvular Heart Disease in Adults: Infective Endocarditis.* (2018) [review] - None

## [0937] Infective endocarditis: Duke criteria, empiric antibiotics, surgical indications

**Cluster**: infective endocarditis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Empiric antibiotics in infective endocarditis are a narrowing problem: the 2015 AHA and ESC guidelines converge on organism-driven choices — antistaphylococcal cover for acute community or healthcare presentation, penicillin/ceftriaxone for subacute native-valve disease — while explicitly disagreeing in places that examiners probe. Two modern refinements: aminoglycoside synergism, long dogma for enterococcal and streptococcal IE, now carries documented nephrotoxicity without proven outcome benefit, and guidelines increasingly say goodbye; and enterococcal disease — increasingly ampicillin-resistant — forces regimen individualisation where bacteraemia and endocardia merge clinically.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2015 AHA and ESC guidelines agree on organism-directed empiric therapy: cover S. aureus for acute presentations and healthcare-associated disease, and streptococcal cover with ceftriaxone/penicillin for subacute native-valve presentations pending cultures. (PMID 27297743)
- Empiric choices must anticipate the modern microbiology: staphylococci dominate healthcare-associated and device-related IE, with vancomycin reserved for MRSA risk or beta-lactam allergy. (PMID 27297743)
- Aminoglycoside synergy for enterococcal and streptococcal endocarditis is challenged by nephrotoxicity without demonstrated outcome benefit; contemporary analyses question their continued routine use. (PMID 31669426)
- Enterococcal bacteraemia and endocarditis management requires source assessment, susceptibility-guided regimens (ampicillin plus ceftriaxone for susceptible strains, daptomycin or linezolid for resistant), and infectious-disease input. (PMID 33152537)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether combination beta-lactam therapy for MSSA bacteraemia/endocardia improves outcomes is unresolved.
- The place of novel lipoglycopeptides (dalbavancin) and long-acting agents in outpatient IE pathways is under study.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Empiric regimens by acuity and acquisition setting.
- AHA versus ESC guideline differences examiners test.
- The aminoglycoside controversy and evidence against routine synergy.
- Enterococcal IE regimen construction by susceptibility.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An acutely unwell patient with native valves needs empiric therapy before cultures result; construct the regimen and the reasoning.
- An enterococcal (ampicillin-susceptible) mitral-valve IE patient has rising creatinine on gentamicin; redesign therapy using the aminoglycoside evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence reflects the 2015 guideline generation; verify empiric recommendations against the current ESC/AHA documents before approval, noting the pipeline's evidence filter limits retrieval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27297743**: *Analysis of the 2015 American and European guidelines for the management of infective endocarditis.* (2016) [guideline-analysis] - None
- **PMID 31669426**: *Aminoglycosides for infective endocarditis: time to say goodbye?* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 33152537**: *How do I manage a patient with enterococcal bacteraemia?* (2020) [review] - None

## [0938] inflammatory bowel disease

**Cluster**: IBD | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Inflammatory bowel disease — Crohn's disease and ulcerative colitis — is managed at a genuinely modern pace: the 2025 British Society of Gastroenterology guidelines codify treat-to-target medicine with early biologic escalation, biomarker-guided monitoring, and an expanding small-molecule armamentarium. The core skill is no longer memorising drugs but running the disease: objective assessment (endoscopy, faecal calprotectin, CRP) rather than symptom guesswork, escalation before damage accumulates, and complication surveillance that changes with disease duration and extent. IBD is now a specialty where induction speed and monitoring discipline determine long-term outcomes.

### Core Teaching Points
- IBD management is treat-to-target: the BSG 2025 guidelines emphasise objective remission targets (mucosal healing, biomarker normalisation) with regular re-assessment rather than symptom-based adjustment. (PMID 40550582, PMID 38934234)
- Faecal calprotectin and CRP are the workhorse biomarkers for activity monitoring and relapse prediction, reducing but not replacing endoscopic assessment. (PMID 35603998, PMID 40550582)
- Therapy spans 5-ASA and steroids for UC, immunomodulators, biologics (anti-TNF, vedolizumab, ustekinumab), and small molecules (JAK inhibitors, S1P modulators), selected by disease phenotype, severity, and prior response. (PMID 38934234, PMID 40550582)
- Complication surveillance — colorectal cancer screening by disease duration/extent in colitis, osteoporosis from repeated steroids, and vaccination before immunosuppression — is part of every IBD treatment plan. (PMID 40550582)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal first-line biologic selection (anti-TNF versus integrin versus IL-12/23) for individual phenotypes is partially answered by head-to-head data.
- Whether biomarker-normalisation alone can substitute for scheduled endoscopy in sustained remission is being tested.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Treat-to-target principles and objective monitoring.
- Calprotectin interpretation and escalation triggers.
- Therapy classes and positioning by phenotype.
- Cancer and infection surveillance schedules.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A Crohn's patient 'feels fine' but calprotectin is 900 ug/g; apply the treat-to-target framework and decide on endoscopy.
- A colitis patient of 12 years' extent asks about cancer surveillance; outline the programme.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the ECCO/STRIDE-II treat-to-target consensus; verify target definitions against it before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40550582**: *British Society of Gastroenterology guidelines on inflammatory bowel disease in adults.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38934234**: *Diagnosis and management of inflammatory bowel disease.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 35603998**: *Inflammatory bowel disease biomarkers.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0939] Inflammatory bowel disease biologic therapy

**Cluster**: IBD | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Biologic therapy in IBD is a story of expanding options and sharpening precision. Five biologics are approved for ulcerative colitis with differing efficacy and positioning; after anti-TNF failure in Crohn's, indirect-comparison meta-analysis informs the vedolizumab-versus-ustekinumab choice that lacks a head-to-head trial; dual biologic therapy pushes the frontier for refractory disease at accepted infection-cost. And therapeutic drug monitoring has converted biologic prescribing from 'intensify and hope' to pharmacokinetic reasoning — low drug levels with antibodies point to switching class, low levels without antibodies to dose escalation. Modern IBD biologic care is applied pharmacology.

### Core Teaching Points
- Five biologics are approved for UC (infliximab, adalimumab, golimumab, vedolizumab, ustekinumab) with differing efficacy and positioning; selection weighs disease severity, extraintestinal manifestations, and patient factors. (PMID 33121691)
- After anti-TNF failure in Crohn's disease, meta-analytic indirect comparison supports ustekinumab and vedolizumab as effective next-line options, with selection informed by phenotype and safety profile. (PMID 34854100)
- Dual biologic therapy (anti-TNF plus vedolizumab or ustekinumab) is an emerging strategy for refractory IBD with encouraging short-term outcomes in systematic review, at the cost of increased infection risk. (PMID 30945576)
- Therapeutic drug monitoring distinguishes mechanistic loss of response: low drug levels with anti-drug antibodies indicate switching class; low levels without antibodies indicate intensification — applying this prevents inappropriate escalation. (PMID 38399538)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether dual biologics offer durable remission beyond small series, and their long-term safety, is unknown.
- Optimal TDM thresholds and proactive-versus-reactive monitoring strategies are not fully standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Biologic positioning by disease and prior response.
- Vedolizumab versus ustekinumab after anti-TNF failure.
- Loss-of-response work-up: TDM interpretation.
- Dual biologic therapy: evidence and risks.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A Crohn's patient loses response to infliximab with low trough and anti-drug antibodies; decide escalate or switch and justify.
- A refractory IBD patient asks about dual biologics; weigh the systematic-review evidence against infection risk.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts predate the VARSITY head-to-head trial publication; verify vedolizumab-versus-adalimumab positioning against primary sources before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33121691**: *Biologic Therapy for Ulcerative Colitis.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 34854100**: *Systematic review with meta-analysis: the effectiveness of either ustekinumab or vedolizumab in patients with Crohn's disease after anti-TNF failure.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 30945576**: *Dual biological therapy with anti-TNF, vedolizumab or ustekinumab in inflammatory bowel disease: a systematic review.* (2021) [systematic-review] - None

## [0940] Inflammatory myopathies: dermatomyositis, polymyositis, IBM, anti-synthetase syndrome

**Cluster**: inflammatory myopathies | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The idiopathic inflammatory myopathies are a heterogenous family unified by proximal weakness and elevated creatine kinase but divided by skin, lung, antibody, and histology — and the modern classification (dermatomyositis, polymyositis, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy, anti-synthetase syndrome, inclusion body myositis) directly drives treatment. Dermatomyositis brings its cancer-surveillance duty and characteristic rashes; anti-synthetase syndrome brings the mechanic's-hands, fever, and interstitial lung disease that can dominate the prognosis; IBM (topic 925) resists immunotherapy by its degenerative core. First-line treatment remains high-dose corticosteroids with early steroid-sparing agents, with myositis-specific antibodies now guiding both prognosis and escalation choices.

### Core Teaching Points
- Modern classification divides IIM into dermatomyositis, polymyositis, immune-mediated necrotizing myopathy, anti-synthetase syndrome, and inclusion body myositis — categories with distinct treatment responses and prognoses. (PMID 30129477, PMID 34857798)
- Dermatomyositis carries a malignancy association warranting age-appropriate cancer screening at diagnosis; characteristic cutaneous signs (heliotrope, Gottron papules) complement proximal weakness. (PMID 30129477, PMID 35976321)
- Anti-synthetase syndrome (anti-Jo-1 and others) presents with myositis, mechanic's hands, fever, Raynaud, and interstitial lung disease; ILD severity often dictates the prognosis and escalation intensity. (PMID 34857798, PMID 35976321)
- First-line therapy is high-dose corticosteroids with early addition of steroid-sparing agents (methotrexate, azathioprine, mycophenolate, IVIG); myositis-specific antibodies increasingly inform prognosis and treatment selection. (PMID 30129477, PMID 34857798)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal steroid-sparing selection by myositis subtype and antibody profile is not standardised.
- The role of rituximab and new targeted agents beyond refractory disease is still being defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Classification categories and their distinguishing features.
- Dermatomyositis: rash recognition and cancer screening.
- Anti-synthetase syndrome and ILD management.
- First-line treatment and escalation principles.

- A patient has proximal weakness with heliotrope rash and Gottron papules; outline the cancer-screening work-up and initial treatment.
- A myositis patient has anti-Jo-1 positivity with progressive dyspnoea; prioritise the ILD assessment and escalate treatment.

## [0941] Inflammatory vs mechanical back pain: red flags, MRI indications, AS vs degenerative

**Cluster**: back pain | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Most low back pain is mechanical and self-limiting; the clinician's job is to find the small fraction that is not. Red flags — cancer history, fever, weight loss, night pain, neurological deficit, bowel/bladder dysfunction — are the screening grammar, and guideline reviews show which flags are actually endorsed and with what strength. The sobering evidence: individual red flags have weak diagnostic accuracy, so clustering and judgment matter more than any single question. Inflammatory back pain suggests axial spondyloarthritis — age under 40-45, insidious onset, morning stiffness, improvement with activity, alternating buttock pain — where early MRI and rheumatology referral change outcomes, while MRI for uncomplicated mechanical pain correlates poorly with symptoms and invites overtreatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- Most low back pain is mechanical/nonspecific; red flags are the screening tool for serious pathology (malignancy, infection, fracture, cauda equina), and their presence — not pain severity — drives imaging and referral. (PMID 30252425, PMID 27376890)
- Guideline reviews show variable endorsement of red flags; individual flags have limited diagnostic accuracy in emergency-department cohorts, so clusters of findings and clinical context must guide decisions. (PMID 27376890, PMID 31278933)
- Inflammatory back pain (insidious onset before 40-45, morning stiffness >30-60 minutes, improvement with exercise not rest, alternating buttock pain, night waking) suggests axial spondyloarthritis and warrants inflammatory work-up and appropriate imaging. (PMID 30252425)
- MRI is indicated for red flags, suspected cauda equina, infection, or inflammatory disease — not for uncomplicated mechanical pain, where degenerative findings are common in asymptomatic people and correlate poorly with symptoms. (PMID 31278933, PMID 30252425)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal combinations and weighting of red flags for screening rules is not settled across guidelines.
- When to refer possible axial spondyloarthritis with negative imaging (non-radiographic disease) follows expert frameworks rather than trial evidence.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Red-flag clusters and their limited individual accuracy.
- Cauda equina red flags and urgent-MRI thresholds.
- Inflammatory versus mechanical history features.
- Appropriate versus inappropriate MRI indications.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 38-year-old has 4 months of morning back stiffness improving with activity and alternating buttock pain; contrast with a mechanical-pain presentation and plan the work-up.
- A patient with back pain reports new urinary retention and saddle anaesthesia; outline the cauda equina pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the ASAS axial-spondyloarthritis classification criteria; verify imaging- referral thresholds against them before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 30252425**: *Mechanical Low Back Pain.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 27376890**: *Red flags presented in current low back pain guidelines: a review.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 31278933**: *Low Back Pain in the Emergency Department: Prevalence of Serious Spinal Pathologies and Diagnostic Accuracy of Red Flags.* (2018) [systematic-review] - None

## [0942] Influenza antiviral treatment

**Cluster**: influenza | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antiviral treatment of influenza is a timing and selection problem. Oseltamivir remains the workhorse neuraminidase inhibitor, ideally started within 48 hours of symptom onset, with benefit extending beyond that window in severe or progressive disease; baloxavir added a single-dose polymerase-acid-inhibitor option. The 2024 network meta-analyses for WHO guidance rank the options separately for severe and nonsevere influenza — an important distinction because evidence and effect sizes differ by severity. Hospitalised and high-risk patients should be treated regardless of illness duration; the '48-hour rule' is a community outpatient rule, not a hospital one.

### Core Teaching Points
- Neuraminidase inhibitors (oseltamivir oral, zanamivir inhaled, peramivir IV) and the cap-dependent endonuclease inhibitor baloxavir are the available classes; oseltamivir is the standard for most patients. (PMID 35270708)
- Treatment within 48 hours of symptom onset maximises benefit in outpatient influenza; hospitalised, severe, or high-risk patients should be treated even beyond 48 hours. (PMID 35270708, PMID 39804622)
- Network meta-analysis for severe influenza supports oseltamivir-based regimens as the evidence-backed choice, with other classes positioned by severity and resistance patterns. (PMID 39181595)
- For nonsevere influenza, comparative effectiveness across agents is modest; single-dose baloxavir offers adherence advantages in selected patients. (PMID 39804622)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether combination antiviral therapy adds benefit in severe influenza is untested adequately.
- Baloxavir resistance (PA/I38 substitutions) and its clinical significance are evolving concerns.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Antiviral classes, mechanisms, and resistance.
- The 48-hour rule and its hospital exceptions.
- Agent selection by severity from network meta-analysis.
- Treatment of high-risk and pregnant patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A hospitalised patient with confirmed influenza is on day 4 of symptoms; decide on antivirals and justify beyond the 48-hour window.
- An outpatient with mild influenza and renal impairment asks about baloxavir versus oseltamivir; compare the options.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the current WHO influenza clinical guideline document; verify recommendation strength against it before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35270708**: *Antiviral Drugs in Influenza.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 39181595**: *Antivirals for treatment of severe influenza: a systematic review and network meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials.* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39804622**: *Antiviral Medications for Treatment of Nonsevere Influenza: A Systematic Review and Network Meta-Analysis.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None

## [0943] Influenza: neuraminidase inhibitors, oseltamivir timing, high-risk groups, vaccination

**Cluster**: influenza | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
This topic pairs the two halves of influenza control: vaccination as prevention, and neuraminidase-inhibitor prophylaxis as the pharmacological bridge. Annual vaccination is recommended for high-risk groups — older adults, chronic disease, pregnancy, immunosuppression — and meta-analysis confirms effectiveness against laboratory-confirmed illness, though effectiveness varies by season, match, and group. Chemoprophylaxis with oseltamivir after exposure reduces symptomatic influenza in the unvaccinated and outbreak settings; it complements, never replaces, vaccination. The mature clinical skill is timing: vaccinate before the season, treat within 48 hours, and prophylax only where guidelines indicate.

### Core Teaching Points
- Annual influenza vaccination is recommended for high-risk groups (age 65+, chronic cardiopulmonary disease, diabetes, pregnancy, immunosuppression, healthcare workers); healthy-adult benefit is mainly work-loss reduction. (PMID 29388196, PMID 32109627)
- Meta-analysis confirms vaccine effectiveness in high-risk groups, though magnitude varies by season and match — protection is real but partial, which is why high-risk patients still need early antiviral treatment when ill. (PMID 28481673)
- Neuraminidase-inhibitor post-exposure prophylaxis (oseltamivir) reduces symptomatic influenza after exposure; network meta-analysis supports NIs over other classes for prophylaxis. (PMID 39181596)
- Antiviral treatment should not wait for vaccine protection decisions: vaccinated or not, high-risk patients with influenza symptoms warrant early oseltamivir within 48 hours. (PMID 32109627, PMID 28481673)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal vaccine composition strategies (egg versus cell versus recombinant; high-dose/adjuvanted for older adults) continue to evolve with effectiveness data.
- Duration and indications for extended prophylaxis in immunocompromised patients are not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Who should be vaccinated annually and why.
- Vaccine-effectiveness magnitudes and seasonal variation.
- Post-exposure prophylaxis indications and agent choice.
- The treat-don't-wait principle in high-risk patients.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An unvaccinated nursing-home resident is exposed to a confirmed influenza case; decide on prophylaxis and its duration.
- A vaccinated COPD patient develops influenza symptoms 36 hours ago; reconcile 'vaccinated' with treatment decisions.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained Cochrane healthy-adults review pre-dates current vaccine technologies; verify high-dose/adjuvanted recommendations against current guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29388196**: *Vaccines for preventing influenza in healthy adults.* (2016) [cochrane-review] - None
- **PMID 28481673**: *Influenza vaccine effectiveness among high-risk groups: A systematic literature review and meta-analysis.* (2016) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 32109627**: *Who and when to vaccinate against influenza.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 39181596**: *Antivirals for post-exposure prophylaxis of influenza: a systematic review and network meta-analysis.* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0944] inhaled corticosteroid long acting beta agonist asthma SMART strategy

**Cluster**: asthma SMART | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
SMART — single maintenance and reliever therapy — is the most important asthma strategy shift of the GINA era: one inhaler, low-dose inhaled corticosteroid-formoterol, serves as daily preventer and as-needed reliever, guaranteeing an anti-inflammatory dose with every reliever use. Evidence shows fewer severe exacerbations than traditional separate SABA reliever strategies, and real-world network meta-analysis confirms the benefit in populations trial criteria exclude. Implementation demands patient education — the inhaler counts as both preventer and reliever — but converts asthma care from two-inhaler complexity to a single consistent message: treat symptoms early, and every treatment is treatment.

### Core Teaching Points
- SMART uses a single ICS-formoterol inhaler for daily maintenance and as-needed relief, embedding anti-inflammatory treatment in every reliever dose. (PMID 34666208)
- ICS-formoterol as controller-and-reliever is associated with significantly fewer severe asthma exacerbations than SABA-reliever-based regimens across severity strata. (PMID 29554195, PMID 34536699)
- Network meta-analysis of real-world populations (often trial-ineligible) confirms SMART's exacerbation benefit beyond RCT settings. (PMID 34536699)
- The as-needed-ICS principle generalises: anti-inflammatory reliever therapy (SMART or ICS-containing reliever variants) treats the underlying inflammation, not just bronchospasm — a conceptual break from SABA-only rescue. (PMID 36702246)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which patients do best on SMART versus MART-naive step-up pathways, and long-term steroid exposure comparisons, need further data.
- SMART implementation in health systems with formulary constraints remains uneven.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- SMART definition, eligible patients, and dosing logic.
- Exacerbation-reduction evidence versus SABA strategies.
- Real-world versus RCT evidence.
- Patient education points for single-inhaler use.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient on separate preventer and SABA reliever has two exacerbations this year; explain and switch to SMART.
- A confused patient asks why their 'blue inhaler is gone'; counsel on the single-inhaler strategy.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the GINA strategy documents themselves; verify step placement against current GINA before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34666208**: *A Practical Guide to Implementing SMART in Asthma Management.* (2021) [practical-guide] - None
- **PMID 29554195**: *Association of Inhaled Corticosteroids and Long-Acting beta-Agonists as Controller and Quick Relief Therapy With Asthma Exacerbations.* (2019) [cohort-study] - None
- **PMID 34536699**: *SMART for the treatment of asthma: A network meta-analysis of real-world evidence.* (2021) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 36702246**: *'As-Needed' Inhaled Corticosteroids for Patients With Asthma.* (2023) [review] - None
