## [0945] Inherited arrhythmia syndromes: long QT, Brugada, catecholaminergic VT, ARVC

**Cluster**: inherited arrhythmias | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The inherited arrhythmia syndromes — long QT, Brugada, CPVT, ARVC — are rare conditions where a single missed diagnosis is a young sudden death, and a single over-diagnosis is a lifetime of restriction. Each has a signature trigger: exertion and emotion for long QT and CPVT, rest and fever for Brugada, competitive sport for ARVC. Diagnosis blends ECG patterns, exercise testing, drug challenge, and genetics — where modern panels increasingly identify pathogenic variants, though variants of uncertain significance complicate counselling. Management combines trigger avoidance, beta-blockade (long QT, CPVT), ICD decisions weighted by risk scores, and family cascade screening.

### Core Teaching Points
- Each syndrome has characteristic triggers — exertion/emotion in long QT and CPVT, rest/sleep/fever in Brugada, sustained exercise in ARVC — and trigger-specific history-taking drives diagnosis. (PMID 40505939, PMID 26897076)
- Diagnostic work-up combines ECG (including recovery-phase and high-lead recordings), exercise testing, ajmaline/flecainide challenge for Brugada, imaging for ARVC, and genetic testing with informed counselling. (PMID 40505939, PMID 27761163)
- Beta-blockade (preferably non-selective, e.g. nadolol/propranolol) is foundational for long QT and CPVT; ICDs are reserved for high-risk profiles given device-related harms in young patients. (PMID 40505939)
- Exercise recommendations are syndrome-specific and increasingly individualised; blanket sport bans are giving way to shared decisions weighing relative risk and psychological benefit. (PMID 26897076)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Risk-stratification for ICD in Brugada and ARVC remains imprecise; programme-shock trade-offs in the young are actively debated.
- Management of variants of uncertain significance and genotype-positive/phenotype-negative relatives follows expert consensus rather than trial evidence.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Trigger-specific history for each syndrome.
- Diagnostic tests: drug challenge, exercise ECG, genetics.
- Beta-blocker selection and ICD thresholds.
- Exercise restrictions and family screening.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 16-year-old with recurrent syncope during swimming has QTc 490 ms; outline diagnosis, treatment, and family screening.
- A genotype-positive, phenotype-negative relative of a Brugada proband asks about competitive sport; apply the exercise-restriction framework.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the 2022 ESC ventricular-arrhythmia guideline; verify risk-score ICD thresholds against it before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40505939**: *Updates on inherited arrhythmia syndromes (Brugada syndrome, long QT syndrome, CPVT, ARVC).* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 27761163**: *Recent advances in genetic testing and counseling for inherited arrhythmias.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 26897076**: *Exercise restrictions for patients with inherited cardiac conditions: Current guidelines, challenges and limitations.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0946] initial invasive or conservative strategy stable coronary disease

**Cluster**: stable CAD strategy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The COURAGE-era question — should stable coronary disease get stents first or medicine first? — was substantially answered by ISCHEMIA: in stable patients without left-main disease, an initial invasive strategy did not reduce hard events compared with conservative management with optimal medical therapy, though symptom relief favoured revascularisation in angina-heavy patients. The clinical translation is a triage skill: left-main, severe proximal multivessel with impaired function, and refractory symptoms despite medical therapy point to the cath lab; everything else starts with optimal medical therapy — antiplatelets, statins, beta-blockade, risk-factor control — and escalates by symptoms, not by stenosis percentages alone.

### Core Teaching Points
- In stable coronary disease without left-main or equivalent anatomy, an initial conservative strategy with optimal medical therapy is event-equivalent to routine invasive management (ISCHEMIA-era evidence). (PMID 37407216, PMID 29231917)
- Revascularisation retains clear roles: left-main disease, proximal multivessel disease with reduced function, and symptom relief when angina persists despite medical therapy. (PMID 37407216, PMID 33944871)
- Optimal medical therapy is itself prognostic: antiplatelet therapy, high-intensity statins, blood-pressure and glycaemic control, beta-blockers for angina, and smoking cessation modify outcomes independent of revascularisation. (PMID 29231917, PMID 33944871)
- Symptom burden, not stenosis percentage alone, should drive escalation decisions in stable disease; ischaemia-guided selection refines who benefits from the cath lab. (PMID 33944871, PMID 37407216)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term (>10 year) outcomes of initial conservative versus invasive strategies are still accumulating.
- Whether modern high-risk plaque imaging should redirect patients to early revascularisation is under study.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ISCHEMIA and COURAGE: designs, populations, conclusions.
- The anatomical exceptions mandating early revascularisation.
- Components of optimal medical therapy.
- Symptom-guided versus anatomy-guided escalation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A stable angina patient has 70% proximal LAD stenosis with preserved EF and well-controlled symptoms on medical therapy; argue the strategy.
- A patient with stable triple-vessel disease and EF 35% presents; decide on initial strategy and timing.

**Reviewer Gap**: The ISCHEMIA primary report (NEJM 2020) is not among the retained abstracts; verify endpoint details and subgroup findings against the primary publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37407216**: *Invasive versus Conservative Management in Coronary Artery Disease.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 29231917**: *Stable coronary artery disease - medical treatment.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 33944871**: *Diagnosis and Management of Stable Angina: A Review.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0947] inotrope use

**Cluster**: inotropes and shock | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Inotropes buy time in shock at a price: increased myocardial oxygen demand, arrhythmias, and — in the dopamine tradition — harm shown in trials. The modern grammar: norepinephrine is the vasopressor backbone of cardiogenic shock (renin-angiotensin support with the least tachyarrhythmia), dobutamine adds inotropy when output remains inadequate, and levosimendan or milrinone offer phosphodiesterase/calcium-sensitising alternatives without catecholamine load. Epinephrine and dopamine are de-emphasised after outcome signals. Inotropes are a bridge — to revascularisation, recovery, or temporary mechanical support — not a destination; the toughest modern skill is recognising when they merely prolong dying.

### Core Teaching Points
- Norepinephrine is the first-line vasopressor in cardiogenic shock, providing peripheral vascular support with less tachycardia and arrhythmia than dopamine or epinephrine. (PMID 38391010, PMID 37489740)
- Dobutamine is the standard inotrope add-on for low cardiac output; its beta-agonism increases contractility at the cost of oxygen demand and arrhythmia risk, warranting ECG and filling-pressure monitoring. (PMID 37489740, PMID 38391010)
- Non-catecholamine options — levosimendan (calcium sensitiser, active metabolites) and milrinone (PDE-3 inhibitor) — suit catecholamine-resistant or beta-blocked patients but cause vasodilatation and hypotension. (PMID 38391010)
- Inotropes and vasopressors bridge to definitive therapy — revascularisation, recovery, or escalation to temporary mechanical circulatory support in refractory shock. (PMID 32682486, PMID 38391010)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether levosimendan improves outcomes over dobutamine in cardiogenic shock remains unproven at trial level.
- Optimal timing and patient selection for mechanical support escalation is centre-dependent and incompletely evidence-based.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Vasopressor selection in cardiogenic shock and why dopamine lost favour.
- Dobutamine pharmacology, monitoring, and arrhythmia risk.
- Levosimendan and milrinone: mechanisms and hypotension caveat.
- The bridge concept: when inotropes yield to mechanical support.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A post-MI patient has cold peripheries, BP 80/50, and lactate 4 mmol/L; design the initial vasopressor/inotrope strategy.
- A shock patient on escalating dobutamine develops ventricular ectopy; outline the reassessment and alternatives.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts lack the primary DOREMI/dopamine trials and SCAI shock-staging consensus; verify shock-staging definitions before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38391010**: *Medical therapy of cardiogenic shock: Contemporary use of inotropes and vasopressors.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 37489740**: *State of Shock: Contemporary Vasopressor and Inotrope Use in Cardiogenic Shock.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 32682486**: *Temporary circulatory support for cardiogenic shock.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0948] Insulinoma: fasting hypoglycaemia, 72-hour fast, C-peptide, surgical resection

**Cluster**: insulinoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Fasting hypoglycaemia with endogenous hyperinsulinaemia is insulinoma until proven otherwise: the classic diagnostic triad (Whipple's) of symptoms with low glucose, relief with glucose, and — from the supervised 72-hour fast — inappropriately elevated insulin, C-peptide, and proinsulin during hypoglycaemia. The C-peptide is the discriminator that splits the differential: endogenous hyperinsulinaemia (insulinoma, sulfonylurea use) versus exogenous insulin (C-peptide suppressed) and IGF-2-mediated hypoglycaemia. Definitive treatment is surgical resection after localisation — the majority are benign solitary adenomas — with medical bridging (diazoxide, frequent feeds) for the unresectable minority.

### Core Teaching Points
- The supervised 72-hour fast is the reference diagnostic test: symptomatic hypoglycaemia with inappropriately elevated insulin, C-peptide, and proinsulin confirms endogenous hyperinsulinemic hypoglycaemia. (PMID 28381450, PMID 36459334)
- C-peptide and insulin antibodies discriminate the causes: elevated C-peptide indicates insulinoma or sulfonylurea ingestion; suppressed C-peptide indicates exogenous insulin; insulin antibodies suggest autoimmune hypoglycaemia. (PMID 36459334, PMID 28381450)
- Sulfonylurea screen (blood or urine) is essential before attributing hyperinsulinaemic hypoglycaemia to insulinoma; the biochemical profiles overlap. (PMID 36459334)
- Treatment of choice is surgical resection of the localised lesion (most insulinomas are benign and solitary); preoperative localisation and intraoperative glucose monitoring guide the operation. (PMID 28381450, PMID 36459334)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Localisation strategy for occult insulinomas (multiphasic CT, MRI, endoscopic ultrasound, selective arterial calcium stimulation) follows centre algorithms without comparative-trial evidence.
- Management of malignant or metastatic insulinoma (diazoxide, everolimus, peptide-receptor therapy) is individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Whipple's triad and the 72-hour fast protocol.
- C-peptide and antibody interpretation in the hypoglycaemia differential.
- Sulfonylurea screen necessity.
- Surgical indications and preoperative localisation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient has recurrent fasting confusion with glucose 2.1 mmol/L and insulin 25 mU/L; interpret the C-peptide and design the work-up.
- An insulinoma is suspected but CT shows no lesion; outline the escalating localisation pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE: only two sources, both reviews. The retained abstracts lack insulinoma surgical-outcome series; verify resection cure rates and localisation algorithms before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28381450**: *MANAGEMENT OF ENDOCRINE DISEASE: Pathogenesis and management of hypoglycemia.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 36459334**: *Endogenous hyperinsulinemic hypoglycemia: case series and literature review.* (2022) [case-series-review] - None

## [0949] Insulin pharmacokinetics: basal, prandial, premixed types - dosing and switching

**Cluster**: insulin pharmacokinetics | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Insulin prescribing is applied pharmacokinetics: the basal insulins (glargine, detemir, degludec) provide flat, peakless coverage tuned by duration — degludec's ultra-long profile smoothing day-to-day variability — while prandial analogues (lispro, aspart, glulisine) peak fast and clear quickly to match meals. Premixed and co-formulated products trade flexibility for simplicity: fixed ratios suit predictable routines and limited self-management capacity, at the cost of adjustment granularity. Switching between types — the commonest real-world manoeuvre — requires attention to unit-for-unit conversion caveats, overlap timing, and hypoglycaemia vigilance in the transition window.

### Core Teaching Points
- Basal analogues (glargine, detemir, degludec) provide flat 24-hour-plus coverage; degludec's duration beyond 42 hours confers the lowest day-to-day glucose variability and some dosing-time flexibility. (PMID 31876563, PMID 30639280)
- Prandial analogues (lispro, aspart, glulisine) mimic physiologic meal peaks with rapid onset and short duration; they are dosed with meals and allow flexible carbohydrate matching. (PMID 31876563, PMID 30639280)
- Co-formulations such as insulin degludec/insulin aspart (70/30) combine basal stability with prandial coverage in a single injection — the two components retain their individual pharmacokinetic profiles. (PMID 27696221)
- Premixed insulins suit patients with regular meals and limited self-management capacity, but the fixed ratio prevents independent titration of basal versus prandial components — a key switching consideration. (PMID 30639280, PMID 27696221)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether ultra-long basal analogues reduce severe hypoglycaemia enough to justify cost over glargine varies by population and setting.
- Optimal conversion ratios when switching between basal analogues or to co-formulations are partly empiric.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Onset/peak/duration tables for basal and prandial analogues.
- Degludec pharmacology and variability advantage.
- Premixed versus basal-bolus trade-offs.
- Switching conversions and hypoglycaemia vigilance.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An elderly patient with erratic meals is on basal-bolus and having hypos; assess a switch to co-formulation or premixed insulin.
- A patient switches from glargine to degludec at the same dose and mis-times doses; counsel on flexibility and monitoring.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are pharmacology reviews; clinical switching protocols should be verified against product monographs and guideline dose-conversion tables before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31876563**: *Biosynthetic Human Insulin and Insulin Analogs.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 30639280**: *Insulin analogs: Glimpse on contemporary facts and future prospective.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 27696221**: *A Review of Insulin Degludec/Insulin Aspart: Pharmacokinetic and Pharmacodynamic Properties and Their Implications for Clinical Use.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0950] Insulin pump therapy CSII: closed-loop systems, patient selection, sick day rules

**Cluster**: diabetes technology | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Insulin pump therapy — continuous subcutaneous insulin infusion — delivers basal-bolus physiology without injections, and its modern form is converging with continuous glucose monitoring into hybrid closed-loop systems that algorithmically adjust delivery. Candidate selection matters more than the hardware: motivated patients (or parents) able to count carbohydrates, troubleshoot occlusions, and manage the device unlock the benefit; system complexity without skills produces the same glycaemia with more failure modes. Sick-day rules complete the picture: illness, ketones, and pump interruption demand proactive sick-day insulin strategies and never omitting basal insulin — pump suspension plus illness is the classic route to DKA.

### Core Teaching Points
- CSII provides continuous basal infusion with bolus dosing for meals, enabling precise insulin delivery matched to lifestyle; systematic-review evidence supports glycaemic benefit over multiple daily injections in appropriate patients. (PMID 31833871)
- Hybrid closed-loop systems link CGM to algorithm-driven basal adjustment, reducing hypoglycaemia and improving time-in-range; systematic review and economic analysis support adoption in type 1 diabetes. (PMID 39673446, PMID 28585879)
- Patient selection is the rate-limiting step: carbohydrate-counting ability, device-competence, motivation, and support structure predict success better than any device feature. (PMID 31833871)
- Sick-day rules are safety-critical: never omit insulin during illness, monitor glucose and ketones frequently, have an injection backup plan for pump failure, and seek urgent care for vomiting with ketones. (PMID 31833871)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Fully closed-loop (unannounced-meal) systems and their real-world performance are maturing.
- Cost-effectiveness thresholds for hybrid closed-loop across health systems are still being established.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- CSII versus MDI: indications and evidence.
- Closed-loop components and glycaemic outcomes.
- Candidate selection criteria.
- Sick-day management and pump-failure contingencies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A motivated adolescent with type 1 diabetes and recurrent nocturnal hypos is assessed for pump/closed-loop therapy; evaluate candidacy.
- A pump patient with gastroenteritis has vomiting, rising ketones, and a suspected line occlusion; outline the sick-day response.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts predate the newest-generation closed-loop systems; verify current device comparisons before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31833871**: *Insulin Pump Therapy.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 39673446**: *Hybrid closed-loop systems for managing blood glucose levels in type 1 diabetes: a systematic review and economic model.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 28585879**: *Continuous Glucose Monitoring: A Review of Recent Studies Demonstrating Improved Glycemic Outcomes.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0951] intensive blood glucose control sulphonylureas insulin type 2 diabetes UKPDS

**Cluster**: glucose control legacy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
UKPDS taught endocrinology its defining lesson: in type 2 diabetes, early intensive glycaemic control with sulphonylureas or insulin does more than lower glucose — it prevents microvascular complications, and its benefits persist for years after the trial ended (the 'legacy effect'). That insight reframed glucose control as disease modification, not symptom control. The modern nuance: glycaemic control remains central but now shares the stage with cardiovascular-risk-directed therapy (statins, BP control, and newer agents with proven CV benefit) — the UKPDS lesson is necessary, not sufficient.

### Core Teaching Points
- UKPDS established that intensive glycaemic control with sulphonylureas or insulin reduces microvascular complications (retinopathy, nephropathy) in type 2 diabetes, with no excess cardiovascular harm. (PMID 35638331, PMID 39155282)
- The legacy effect describes durable long-term benefit from early intensive therapy observed years after trial completion — early glycaemic control 'banks' protection (UKPDS, and analogous findings in hypertension and lipids). (PMID 37915902)
- Sulphonylurea- and insulin-based intensification remain evidence-based for microvascular protection, though modern guidelines prioritise agents with cardiovascular benefit (GLP-1 receptor agonists, SGLT2 inhibitors) for patients with established disease. (PMID 39155282, PMID 35638331)
- Glucose control sits within a hierarchy: glycaemia management is necessary but must be integrated with blood pressure, lipid, and weight management for maximal cardiovascular prevention. (PMID 39155282, PMID 35638331)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether sulphonylureas retain a role versus newer agents in early intensive therapy is contested by cardiovascular-outcome data.
- The durability of legacy effects with modern agents is untested at UKPDS timescales.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- UKPDS: what intensive control achieved and with which drugs.
- The legacy effect and its clinical implication for early treatment.
- Microvascular versus macrovascular prevention hierarchy.
- Sulphonylureas/insulin in modern treatment algorithms.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A newly diagnosed type 2 diabetic patient asks whether early tight control 'matters later'; answer using UKPDS and the legacy effect.
- A patient on metformin needs intensification with established cardiovascular disease; reconcile UKPDS-era evidence with modern agent selection.

**Reviewer Gap**: The UKPDS primary reports (Lancet 1998) predate the pipeline's 2015+ evidence filter; verify trial arm details and effect sizes against the primary publications before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37915902**: *The long-term benefits of early intensive therapy in chronic diseases - the legacy effect.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 35638331**: *Treatment of diabetes mellitus has borne much fruit in the prevention of cardiovascular disease.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 39155282**: *Glycaemic control is still central in the hierarchy of priorities in type 2 diabetes management.* (2023) [consensus-review] - None

## [0952] intensive blood pressure control cardiovascular outcomes systolic

**Cluster**: intensive BP control | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
SPRINT re-set blood-pressure medicine: intensive control targeting systolic <120 mmHg reduced major cardiovascular events and mortality versus the <140 mmHg standard in non-diabetic high-risk patients — at the price of more hypotension, electrolyte disturbance, and acute kidney injury. The kidney follow-up taught the second lesson: the AKI signal was largely haemodynamic and recoverable, and intensive control did not accelerate CKD progression on extended observation. The modern skill is patient selection: intensive targets for fit high-risk patients, individualised relaxation for frailty, standing hypotension, and advanced CKD.

### Core Teaching Points
- SPRINT showed intensive systolic control (<120 mmHg) reduced major cardiovascular events and all-cause mortality in non-diabetic high-risk patients, driving guideline targets toward lower goals. (PMID 31646769, PMID 40902616)
- The benefit-harm balance of intensive control varies by patient characteristics: absolute benefit concentrates in higher-risk patients, while adverse effects (hypotension, syncope, AKI, electrolytes) increase with frailty. (PMID 40902616)
- SPRINT's AKI signal was predominantly haemodynamic and largely reversible; on extended follow-up, intensive control did not accelerate chronic kidney disease progression despite early creatinine rises. (PMID 30218213)
- Ideal targets are individualised: intensive (<120-130 systolic) for fit high-risk patients, with relaxed targets for frail elderly, standing hypotension, and advanced CKD. (PMID 31646769, PMID 40902616)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether intensive targets benefit patients with diabetes (ACCORD's negative primary result) or very elderly frail patients remains patient-specific.
- Optimal implementation (home versus office measurement, titration speed) varies by health system.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- SPRINT design, population, and primary results.
- The AKI signal: haemodynamic versus structural interpretation.
- Benefit-harm trade-offs by patient subgroup.
- Individualising targets: frailty, orthostasis, CKD.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A fit 68-year-old with prior stroke has office BP 138/78; argue the target using SPRINT evidence.
- A frail 82-year-old has a fall on intensified antihypertensives; apply the benefit-harm framework and adjust the target.

**Reviewer Gap**: The SPRINT primary report (NEJM 2015) is not among the retained abstracts; verify endpoint definitions and adverse-event rates against the primary publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40902616**: *Benefit-harm trade-offs of intensive blood pressure control versus standard blood pressure control on cardiovascular outcomes.* (2025) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 30218213**: *SPRINT and the Kidney: What Have We Learned?* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 31646769**: *Ideal Target Blood Pressure in Hypertension.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0953] Intensive care high yield clinical guidelines

**Cluster**: ICU guidelines | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
High-yield ICU guidelines share a signature: they convert physiology into checklists that change outcomes. Ventilator care bundles — head-of-bed elevation, sedation breaks, spontaneous breathing trials, oral care, prophylaxis — reduce ventilator-associated pneumonia in meta-analysis, and implementation science shows the bundle matters less than the strategy that embeds it. The ESICM fluid therapy guideline restricts resuscitation fluids to balanced crystalloids as default and questions starches; the ERS/ATS NIV guideline defines which acute respiratory failure phenotypes (COPD exacerbation with acidosis, cardiogenic pulmonary oedema) benefit from noninvasive ventilation and which need intubation. The through-line: guidelines work when they are specific, physiological, and monitored.

### Core Teaching Points
- Ventilator-associated pneumonia care bundles reduce VAP incidence in meta-analysis; bundle elements (positioning, sedation interruption, oral care, prophylaxis) are the highest-yield preventive practice in the ventilated patient. (PMID 38028633)
- Implementation strategy determines bundle success: systematic review identifies education, reminders, audit-feedback, and leadership engagement as the effective components, not the bundle document itself. (PMID 26276569)
- The ESICM fluid-therapy guideline positions balanced crystalloids as the default resuscitation fluid and recommends against starch-based fluids, with fluid choice and fluid amount addressed separately. (PMID 38771364)
- ERS/ATS NIV guidance: noninvasive ventilation benefits acute hypercapnic COPD exacerbation and cardiogenic pulmonary oedema, but not de novo hypoxaemic failure patterns where it may delay intubation. (PMID 28860265)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which bundle-implementation strategies produce durable adherence beyond the study period is incompletely answered.
- Optimal fluid-amount strategies (restrictive versus liberal) continue to be refined across specific ICU populations.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- VAP bundle elements and evidence.
- Why implementation strategy beats the checklist alone.
- Resuscitation fluid choice per ESICM.
- NIV indications and failure-recognition.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A unit's VAP rate has plateaued despite an existing bundle; apply the implementation-science evidence to restart improvement.
- A hypoxaemic pneumonia patient with COPD has pH 7.28 and PaCO2 68; argue for or against NIV per guideline phenotypes.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained set covers selected high-yield domains only; sepsis (Surviving Sepsis) and transfusion guidelines are covered elsewhere in this topic set — verify cross-links before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38028633**: *Prevention of ventilator-associated pneumonia through care bundles: A systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2024) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 26276569**: *What are effective strategies for the implementation of care bundles on ICUs: a systematic review.* (2016) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 38771364**: *European Society of Intensive Care Medicine clinical practice guideline on fluid therapy in adult critically ill patients.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 28860265**: *Official ERS/ATS clinical practice guidelines: noninvasive ventilation for acute respiratory failure.* (2017) [guideline] - None

## [0954] intensive glucose lowering type 2 diabetes

**Cluster**: glucose control harms | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Where topic 951 covers what intensive control can achieve, this topic covers what it can cost. The modern synthesis of intensive-versus-standard trials finds a consistent pattern: intensive lowering reduces microvascular events but its effect on macrovascular outcomes and mortality depends on context — with ACCORD's excess mortality in aggressively targeted patients as the cautionary counterweight. Regional meta-analysis adds the generalisability caveat: benefits and harms vary with baseline care quality. The clinical rule that emerges: intensive targets earn their risk in younger, healthier, recently diagnosed patients with support systems; they pay it out in the frail, the elderly, and the long-diagnosed.

### Core Teaching Points
- Systematic review of intensive versus conventional glucose targets confirms microvascular benefit while showing macrovascular and mortality effects are context-dependent rather than uniformly favourable. (PMID 36718676)
- The ACCORD-era lesson stands: aggressive intensification in higher-risk, longer-diagnosed patients increased mortality and severe hypoglycaemia — patient selection, not the number itself, drives safety. (PMID 36718676, PMID 25944874)
- Meta-analysis across regions shows intensive-versus-standard effects vary with baseline control quality, implying diminishing returns where baseline care is already good. (PMID 25944874)
- Severe hypoglycaemia is the central harm of intensification — it drives cardiovascular events, falls, and cognitive decline — and its risk must be weighed against microvascular benefit in every target decision. (PMID 36718676)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether modern agents (GLP-1 RA, SGLT2i) change the risk-benefit of intensive targets independent of glycaemia is an active research area.
- Optimal de-intensification criteria for older patients are guideline-inferred rather than trial-proven.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Intensive versus standard: the consistent and variable effects.
- ACCORD: what went wrong and whom it warns about.
- Severe hypoglycaemia as the balancing harm.
- De-intensification: who, when, how.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 78-year-old with 20-year diabetes, CKD, and two prior hypos is targeted to HbA1c 6.5%; argue for de-intensification.
- A newly diagnosed motivated 50-year-old asks about tight control; balance microvascular benefit against hypoglycaemia risk.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence does not isolate ACCORD/ADVANCE/VADT primary reports; verify excess-mortality details against the primary publications before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36718676**: *Efficacy and safety of intensive versus conventional glucose targets in people with type 2 diabetes: a systematic review.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 25944874**: *Effect of Intensive Versus Standard Blood Glucose Control in Patients With Type 2 Diabetes Mellitus in Different Regions of the World.* (2015) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0955] intensive glycemic targets in type 2 diabetes

**Cluster**: glycemic targets | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The HbA1c number is now a shared decision, not an order: guidelines systematically individualise targets by life expectancy, comorbidity burden, hypoglycaemia risk, and patient preference. Younger, healthier patients aim for near-normal glycaemia (6.0-6.5%); healthy older adults relax modestly (7.0-7.5%); frailty, dementia, limited life expectancy, or severe hypoglycaemia history justify 8.0% or higher — where comfort and safety outrank the microvascular arithmetic. The systematic review of guideline recommendations shows this individualisation is now explicit and consistent across major societies; the craft is matching the target to the person, then matching the therapy's hypoglycaemia risk to the target.

### Core Teaching Points
- Major guidelines individualise HbA1c targets: approximately <6.5-7.0% for healthy younger patients, 7.0-7.5% for healthy older adults, and 8.0%+ for frail patients or limited life expectancy. (PMID 34379732, PMID 30833929)
- In older patients, intensive control's benefit-harm balance shifts: microvascular gains shrink while hypoglycaemia, falls, and treatment burden grow, driving relaxed targets with advancing frailty. (PMID 35614863, PMID 30833929)
- Guideline recommendations consistently frame targets around patient characteristics (life expectancy, comorbidity, hypoglycaemia risk, preference) rather than a single population number. (PMID 34379732)
- Therapy selection must match the target: agents with low hypoglycaemia risk (metformin, GLP-1 RA, SGLT2i, DPP-4i) support intensive targets safely; sulphonylureas and insulin require target relaxation or enhanced monitoring in vulnerable patients. (PMID 30833929, PMID 35614863)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Exact HbA1c cut-offs by frailty category vary between guidelines; local protocols differ.
- How to operationalise individualisation in busy clinics (frailty scoring uptake) is an implementation, not efficacy, question.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Target ranges by age, frailty, and life expectancy.
- Why older patients warrant relaxed targets.
- Agent selection to support intensive targets safely.
- Shared decision-making in target setting.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A healthy 45-year-old and a frail 84-year-old with identical HbA1c 7.4% need different targets; set both and justify.
- A sulphonylurea-treated older patient has recurrent hypos on an intensive target; restructure therapy and target together.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts summarise guidelines without the primary ADA/EASD target documents; verify exact ranges against current guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34379732**: *Individualisation of glycaemic management in older people with type 2 diabetes: a systematic review of clinical practice guidelines.* (2022) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 35614863**: *Intensive glycemic control and macrovascular, microvascular, hypoglycemia complications and mortality in older (age > 65 years) patients.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 30833929**: *Diabetes and Aging: From Treatment Goals to Pharmacologic Therapy.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0956] intensive LDL cholesterol lowering statin meta-analysis

**Cluster**: intensive LDL lowering | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The Cholesterol Treatment Trialists' meta-analyses established the foundational rule of lipid medicine: cardiovascular benefit is proportional to the absolute LDL-C reduction, in every group studied — men and women, primary and secondary prevention — and there is no threshold below which benefit stops. Intensive lowering therefore means driving LDL as low as practically achievable with tolerated therapy. The modern implementation question: whether moderate-intensity statin plus ezetimibe matches high-intensity statin alone — meta-analytic evidence now suggests comparable LDL lowering with better tolerability, reshaping the escalation ladder.

### Core Teaching Points
- CTT individual-participant meta-analysis shows statin benefit is proportional to absolute LDL-C reduction and is consistent across sexes and prevention settings, supporting intensive lowering targets. (PMID 25579834)
- Meta-analysis supports moderate-intensity statin plus ezetimibe as an alternative to high-intensity statin monotherapy, with comparable LDL-C lowering and improved tolerability in several analyses. (PMID 39550238)
- Intensive LDL targets in very-high-risk ASCVD (e.g. <1.4 mmol/L or <55 mg/dL per ESC) operationalise the proportional-benefit rule with combination therapy as needed. (PMID 25579834, PMID 39550238)
- Tolerability drives adherence: switching to combination moderate-intensity therapy is preferable to statin discontinuation when high-intensity doses cause adverse effects. (PMID 39550238)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether combination moderate-intensity therapy reduces hard cardiovascular outcomes identically to high-intensity monotherapy awaits dedicated outcome trials.
- Optimal LDL floors and very-long-term safety of very low LDL remain reassuring but observational.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The proportional-benefit rule from CTT meta-analysis.
- No threshold: interpreting very low LDL targets.
- Moderate-statin-plus-ezetimibe versus high-intensity monotherapy evidence.
- Tolerability-driven switching strategies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient cannot tolerate high-intensity atorvastatin due to myalgia; construct the combination alternative and its evidence base.
- A very-high-risk ASCVD patient asks 'how low should my cholesterol go'; answer with the proportional-benefit evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: The primary CTT 2010 meta-analysis and IMPROVE-IT are not among the retained abstracts (pre-2015 or filter-limited); verify effect magnitudes against the primary publications before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 25579834**: *Efficacy and safety of LDL-lowering therapy among men and women: meta-analysis of individual data from 174,000 participants in 27 randomised trials (CTT).* (2015) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 39550238**: *Safety and efficacy of moderate-intensity statin plus ezetimibe versus high-intensity statin monotherapy.* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0957] Intermediate-risk pulmonary embolism reperfusion

**Cluster**: intermediate-risk PE | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intermediate-risk (submassive) pulmonary embolism — haemodynamically stable but with RV dysfunction or troponin elevation — is the discipline's judgement call: anticoagulation alone has a low but real early mortality, while systemic thrombolysis prevents haemodynamic collapse but at a cost of major bleeding and intracranial haemorrhage. PEITHO quantified the trade: thrombolysis averted deterioration but with roughly 2% intracranial bleeding. Meta-analyses frame the modern answer: anticoagulation for most, rescue thrombolysis for the 5-10% who deteriorate, and catheter-directed therapy as the evolving middle path — promising lower bleeding, still awaiting definitive outcome trials.

### Core Teaching Points
- Meta-analysis of intermediate-risk PE shows thrombolysis reduces early clinical deterioration and recurrent PE compared with anticoagulation alone, at the cost of significantly more major bleeding. (PMID 26384442)
- The PEITHO-era trade-off defines practice: most intermediate-risk patients are managed with anticoagulation plus close monitoring, with rescue thrombolysis reserved for haemodynamic deterioration. (PMID 26384442, PMID 37855802)
- Network and pairwise meta-analyses position catheter-directed thrombolysis between systemic lysis and anticoagulation — lower bleeding than systemic lysis, but without definitive outcome superiority. (PMID 37855802, PMID 37336568)
- Risk markers (RV dysfunction on echo/CT, elevated troponin/BNP, PESI class) identify the intermediate-risk category but do not yet reliably identify who benefits from upfront reperfusion. (PMID 37855802, PMID 26384442)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether catheter-directed therapy improves hard outcomes versus anticoagulation alone is the subject of ongoing trials.
- Optimal selection of intermediate-high-risk patients ('the grey zone') for upfront reperfusion is unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Defining intermediate-risk PE: RV dysfunction plus biomarkers.
- The PEITHO trade: benefit versus intracranial haemorrhage.
- Anticoagulation-plus-rescue versus upfront lysis.
- Catheter-directed therapy positioning.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A stable PE patient has RV dilation on CT and troponin elevation; defend the anticoagulation-plus-monitoring strategy and define rescue criteria.
- An intermediate-risk PE patient deteriorates at hour 18 with rising lactate; outline rescue reperfusion.

**Reviewer Gap**: The PEITHO primary report (NEJM 2014) is not among the retained abstracts; verify bleeding and deterioration rates against the primary publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26384442**: *Thrombolysis for acute intermediate-risk pulmonary embolism: A meta-analysis.* (2015) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 37855802**: *Efficacy and Safety of Anticoagulation, Catheter-Directed Thrombolysis, or Systemic Thrombolysis in Acute Pulmonary Embolism.* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 37336568**: *Catheter-directed thrombolysis compared with systemic thrombolysis and anticoagulation in patients with intermediate- or high-risk pulmonary embolism.* (2023) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0958] Intermittent Haemodialysis: Principles and Adequacy

**Cluster**: haemodialysis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intermittent haemodialysis works by the physics of diffusion and convection: blood and dialysate flow past a semipermeable membrane in counter-current, clearing small solutes in proportion to time, surface area, and concentration gradient. Adequacy is measured — Kt/V (clearance x time over volume) targeting around 1.2-1.4 per session thrice-weekly, supplemented by the uraemic-solute view that phosphate and middle molecules clear differently from urea. The modern refinement is individualisation: incremental regimens for residual renal function and session tolerance, recognising that the number is a guide, not a verdict. And the commonest session-level complication, intradialytic hypotension, reflects the fine balance between ultrafiltration demand and cardiovascular compensation.

### Core Teaching Points
- IHD adequacy is quantified by Kt/V (spKt/V target ≥1.2 per session, with minimum delivered dose-equivalent of 1.4 by single-pool measures), supplemented by assessment of phosphate, fluid, and middle-molecule clearance. (PMID 34595991, PMID 27578429)
- Uremic solute kinetics differ by size and protein-binding: small solutes like urea clear readily, while phosphate and larger 'middle molecules' need longer or more frequent sessions — the rationale for individualised prescriptions. (PMID 27578429)
- Incremental haemodialysis — starting with fewer/shorter sessions when residual kidney function remains — is supported as an individualised adequacy strategy rather than a fixed thrice-weekly default. (PMID 34595991)
- Intradialytic hypotension results from the interaction of ultrafiltration rate, refilling capacity, and autonomic/vascular compensation; it associates with adverse long-term outcomes and requires active prevention (dialysate temperature, UF profiling, sodium modelling, midodrine). (PMID 33391741)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether higher session frequency (short daily) improves hard outcomes beyond adequacy numbers is debated.
- Optimal incremental-dialysis protocols and their long-term safety are under study.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Kt/V: calculation logic, targets, and pitfalls.
- Solute-size classes and their clearance implications.
- Incremental dialysis rationale.
- Intradialytic hypotension: mechanisms and countermeasures.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient's spKt/V is 1.05 thrice-weekly; diagnose the adequacy shortfall and redesign the prescription.
- A patient repeatedly cramps and hypotends mid-session with high interdialytic gains; apply the IDH prevention toolkit.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts are narrative reviews; verify Kt/V targets and dialysis-complication management against KDOQI/ERBP adequacy guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34595991**: *Toward an individualized determination of dialysis adequacy: a narrative review with special emphasis on incremental haemodialysis.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 27578429**: *Effect of Treatment Duration and Frequency on Uremic Solute Kinetics, Clearances and Concentrations.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 33391741**: *An update review of intradialytic hypotension: concept, risk factors, clinical implications and management.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0959] Intermittent haemodialysis vs CRRT: selection criteria in critically ill

**Cluster**: RRT modality | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The IHD-versus-CRRT debate has matured from ideology to physiology: meta-analyses show no mortality difference between modalities in critically ill AKI, so the choice rests on the patient's haemodynamic tolerance, metabolic burden, and practical constraints. CRRT offers slow, continuous solute and fluid removal — kinder to unstable circuits, better for severe catabolism and raised intracranial pressure, but resource-heavy and anticoagulation-hungry. IHD is efficient, available, and frees the patient between sessions, but tolerates less instability. Hybrid extended-daily dialysis splits the difference, and meta-analysis finds it comparable to CRRT — a legitimate middle path.

### Core Teaching Points
- No mortality difference between IHD and CRRT has been shown in meta-analyses of critically ill AKI; modality selection is therefore physiological and practical, not evidence-dictated. (PMID 31695978, PMID 36723298)
- CRRT is favoured for haemodynamic instability, severe fluid overload needing gentle removal, high catabolic states, and raised intracranial pressure; IHD suits stable patients and resource-constrained settings. (PMID 36723298, PMID 31695978)
- Extended daily (hybrid) dialysis shows comparable outcomes to CRRT in meta-analysis, offering slower clearance with intermittent logistics — a legitimate alternative where expertise exists. (PMID 25843704)
- Practical factors — anticoagulation needs, nursing ratio, vascular access, cost, and the ability to interrupt for procedures — legitimately shape modality choice alongside physiology. (PMID 36723298, PMID 31695978)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether CRRT's haemodynamic gentleness translates to renal-recovery benefit is unproven.
- Optimal modality-switching protocols during a single AKI episode are not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The no-mortality-difference evidence base.
- Physiological selection criteria for each modality.
- Hybrid/extended-daily dialysis positioning.
- Practical constraints in modality choice.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A septic shock patient with AKI, on high-dose noradrenaline, needs RRT; select and justify the modality.
- A stable post-surgical AKI patient needs RRT in a unit without CRRT capacity; assess IHD candidacy and precautions.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is review/meta-analysis level; verify selection-criteria details against the current KDIGO AKI guideline before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31695978**: *Single-best Choice Between Intermittent Versus Continuous Renal Replacement Therapy: A Review.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 36723298**: *Debate: Intermittent Hemodialysis versus Continuous Kidney Replacement Therapy in the Critically Ill Patient.* (2023) [debate-review] - None
- **PMID 25843704**: *Extended Daily Dialysis Versus Continuous Renal Replacement Therapy for Acute Kidney Injury: A Meta-analysis.* (2015) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0960] interstitial lung disease

**Cluster**: ILD | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Interstitial lung disease is an umbrella, not a diagnosis: the idiopathic interstitial pneumonias, connective-tissue-disease-related ILD, hypersensitivity pneumonitis, and drug-induced disease share radiological patterns but diverge in treatment and prognosis. The modern craft is pattern recognition followed by an aetiology hunt — exposures, autoimmunity, drugs — because the HRCT pattern organises everything: UIP suggests IPF after cause exclusion, while NSIP, organising pneumonia, and HP patterns point to specific treatable entities. Two contemporary frames matter: interstitial lung abnormalities on incidental CT, common and mostly indolent but a marker of future progression; and interstitial pneumonia with autoimmune features, the research category for patients who fall between rheumatology and respiratory clinics.

### Core Teaching Points
- ILD requires a systematic aetiology work-up — exposures (hypersensitivity pneumonitis), connective tissue disease serology, drug history, and family history — because treatment is cause-specific. (PMID 30695649, PMID 32649920)
- HRCT patterns organise ILD diagnosis: UIP pattern strongly suggests IPF (after cause exclusion), while NSIP, organising pneumonia, and hypersensitivity patterns point to specific treatable entities. (PMID 37505195, PMID 40358445)
- The progressive-pulmonary-fibrosis concept unites fibrosing ILDs by trajectory rather than label: worsening symptoms, physiology, or imaging within a year on appropriate therapy triggers antifibrotic treatment (see topic 909). (PMID 37156412)
- Non-IPF ILDs with inflammatory phenotypes — CTD-ILD and chronic hypersensitivity pneumonitis — have immunomodulatory options (mycophenolate, azathioprine, cyclophosphamide, rituximab) that IPF lacks. (PMID 35938712)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Surveillance intervals and treatment thresholds for ILAs are not standardised.
- Whether IPAF should be treated as inflammatory or fibrotic disease (or both sequentially) is unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ILD aetiology work-up sequence.
- HRCT pattern recognition and diagnostic weight.
- Antifibrotic versus immunosuppressive selection by ILD type.
- ILA recognition and IPAF classification.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An incidental CT shows subpleural reticulation in a 70-year-old; distinguish ILA from clinical ILD and plan surveillance.
- A patient has UIP-pattern ILD with positive ANA but no CTD criteria; discuss the IPAF framework and treatment options.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is heterogeneous (position paper, research classification, imaging and treatment reviews); verify ILD-specific guideline management against ATS/ERS documents before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32649920**: *Interstitial lung abnormalities detected incidentally on CT: a Position Paper from the Fleischner Society.* (2020) [position-paper] - None
- **PMID 30695649**: *Interstitial Pneumonia with Autoimmune Features.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 37156412**: *Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 40358445**: *Advances in Concept and Imaging of Interstitial Lung Disease.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 37505195**: *Imaging Features of Idiopathic Interstitial Lung Diseases.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0961] Interstitial Nephritis: Acute and Chronic

**Cluster**: interstitial nephritis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute interstitial nephritis is the great mimic of AKI: an idiosyncratic drug hypersensitivity reaction arriving 7-10 days after exposure to the culprit — antibiotics, PPIs, NSAIDs the commonest — with a sterile pyuria, modest proteinuria, and often systemic allergic features. The diagnostic trap is that the classic triad (fever, rash, eosinophilia) is frequently absent; eosinophiluria has fallen from favour, and biopsy remains the reference standard when suspicion is high and improvement does not follow drug withdrawal. Chronic tubulointerstitial nephritis is its insidious counterpart: progressive fibrosis from persistent insults — including long-term PPI exposure, analgesics, or aristolochic acid — presenting with concentrating defects and slowly progressive CKD.

### Core Teaching Points
- Drug-induced AIN is a delayed hypersensitivity reaction appearing 7-10 days after culprit exposure; drugs account for the overwhelming majority of AIN cases, with antibiotics, PPIs, and NSAIDs the leading causes. (PMID 28284381)
- The classic allergic triad is frequently absent; diagnosis rests on clinical suspicion (AKI after a new drug, sterile pyuria, modest non-nephrotic proteinuria), and biopsy is the reference standard when suspicion persists. (PMID 28284381, PMID 30544782)
- First-line treatment is withdrawal of the culprit drug with corticosteroids for persistent or biopsy-severe disease; early steroid responsiveness is the rule and delayed treatment risks incomplete recovery. (PMID 28284381, PMID 27072818)
- Chronic tubulointerstitial nephritis — from prolonged PPI use, analgesics, toxins, or previous AIN — presents with tubular dysfunction (concentrating defects, acidosis, salt wasting) and progressive CKD requiring culprit withdrawal and supportive care. (PMID 27072818, PMID 30544782)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal steroid dose, duration, and whether early biopsy-guided therapy improves renal recovery lacks randomised evidence.
- The causal contribution of PPIs to CKD progression at population level remains debated despite consistent observational signals.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Culprit-drug timeline and the absent-triad trap.
- Diagnostic work-up: when to biopsy.
- Steroid indications and response expectations.
- Chronic TIN versus glomerular CKD patterns.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient on a new antibiotic develops AKI with sterile pyuria but no rash or fever; assess for AIN and plan management.
- A long-term PPI user has slowly rising creatinine and bland sediment; evaluate chronic TIN and counsel on deprescribing.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is mechanism-focused; verify steroid protocols and biopsy thresholds against nephrology guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 28284381**: *Mechanisms of Drug-Induced Interstitial Nephritis.* (2017) [review] - None

## [0962] Intestinal Lymphoma

**Cluster**: intestinal lymphoma | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The small bowel is the longest segment of the gut and the rarest site for cancer — but primary gastrointestinal lymphoma is the exception, the GI tract being the commonest extranodal site of non-Hodgkin lymphoma. The subtypes each carry a signature: diffuse large B-cell lymphoma as the dominant aggressive form; MALT lymphoma linked to Helicobacter in the stomach; enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma (now MEITL) as the feared complication of coeliac disease; and Burkitt-type disease in the immunocompromised. Presentation is non-specific — pain, obstruction, bleeding, weight loss — so the diagnostic route runs through CT enterography, capsule endoscopy, and deep biopsy, with specialised cross-sectional imaging for staging.

### Core Teaching Points
- The GI tract is the most common extranodal site of non-Hodgkin lymphoma, though primary GI lymphoma remains a minority of all NHL; small-bowel primaries are disproportionately lymphomatous among small-bowel malignancies. (PMID 31414733, PMID 37652650)
- Principal subtypes: DLBCL (dominant aggressive form), MALT (gastric, H. pylori-associated), enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma (coeliac-linked), and immunodeficiency-associated lymphomas. (PMID 31414733)
- Diagnosis requires endoscopic or surgical deep biopsy with immunophenotyping; cross-sectional imaging (CT/PET) and bone-marrow assessment stage the disease. (PMID 37652650, PMID 31414733)
- Treatment is subtype- and stage-directed: H. pylori eradication for early gastric MALT, chemoimmunotherapy (R-CHOP-type) for DLBCL, and surgery reserved for complications (perforation, obstruction, bleeding) rather than routine therapy. (PMID 31414733, PMID 37652650)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal management of refractory/relapsed enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma is unsatisfactory and individualised.
- The role of surgery versus endoscopic management for small-bowel obstruction in lymphoma is case-based.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- GI lymphoma subtypes and their associations.
- Diagnostic pathway: enterography, capsule, deep biopsy.
- Staging essentials.
- Subtype-directed therapy including H. pylori eradication for MALT.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A coeliac patient on a strict diet presents with weight loss and obstructive symptoms; evaluate for enteropathy-associated T-cell lymphoma.
- A gastric MALT lymphoma patient asks whether chemotherapy is needed; apply the H. pylori-eradication paradigm.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE: only two sources, both reviews. The retained abstracts lack subtype-specific treatment outcome series; verify therapy selection against lymphoma guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31414733**: *Gastrointestinal non-Hodgkin lymphomas.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 37652650**: *Updates in the diagnosis and management of small-bowel tumors.* (2023) [review] - None

## [0963] Intestinal Pseudo-obstruction

**Cluster**: intestinal pseudo-obstruction | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Chronic intestinal pseudo-obstruction is dysmotility masquerading as obstruction: coordinated propulsive activity fails without a mechanical blockage, producing recurrent distension, pain, vomiting, and malnutrition. Diagnosis is one of exclusion — imaging to rule out obstruction, then manometry, scintigraphy, and full-thickness biopsy when available to characterise the neuromuscular pathology. Secondary causes (scleroderma, amyloid, opioids, paraneoplastic) must be hunted. Management combines nutritional support, prokinetic trials, and small-bowel transplantation for intestinal failure; pyridostigmine's systematic-review evidence makes it a first-line pharmacological option.

### Core Teaching Points
- CIPO is a severe gastrointestinal dysmotility mimicking mechanical obstruction; diagnosis requires exclusion of mechanical causes followed by physiological testing (manometry, transit studies) and consideration of full-thickness biopsy. (PMID 32073506, PMID 29877952)
- Aetiology is primary (visceral myopathy or neuropathy) or secondary — scleroderma, amyloidosis, endocrine disease, drugs (opioids, anticholinergics), paraneoplastic syndromes; identifying secondary causes redirects therapy. (PMID 29877952, PMID 32073506)
- Management is multimodal: dietary modification and enteral/parenteral nutrition, prokinetic agents (pyridostigmine supported by systematic review), treatment of secondary causes, and decompressive or transplant surgery for intestinal failure. (PMID 37132128, PMID 32073506)
- Pyridostigmine, an acetylcholinesterase inhibitor enhancing cholinergic drive, has systematic-review support as a treatment option for CIPO directed at improving colonic and gut motility. (PMID 37132128)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The evidence base for most prokinetics and novel agents (prucalopride, mosapride) in CIPO is small and heterogeneous.
- Optimal timing and candidacy for intestinal transplantation in CIPO-related intestinal failure is individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic sequence: exclusion then physiology.
- Primary versus secondary causes.
- Prokinetic options and pyridostigmine evidence.
- Nutrition and transplantation escalation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient has recurrent distension and vomiting with repeatedly 'normal' CTs; structure the CIPO work-up.
- A scleroderma patient develops CIPO; identify the secondary mechanism and adjust management.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include manometry-interpretation or transplant-candidacy criteria; verify against neurogastroenterology guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32073506**: *Update on chronic intestinal pseudo-obstruction.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 29877952**: *Pathophysiology, Diagnosis, and Management of Chronic Intestinal Pseudo-Obstruction.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 37132128**: *Pyridostigmine in chronic intestinal pseudo-obstruction - a systematic review.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None

## [0964] Intestinal Tuberculosis

**Cluster**: intestinal tuberculosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intestinal tuberculosis returns wherever tuberculosis returns: the ileocaecal region is its favoured site, and the challenge is twofold — diagnosing a disease whose presentation (chronic abdominal pain, weight loss, ileocaecal ulceration, strictures) is near-identical to Crohn's disease, and treating it with a regimen proven for pulmonary TB but applied to sites where drug penetration and duration questions persist. The diagnostic modernisation is molecular and endoscopic: tissue PCR, biopsy culture, and endoscopic-ultrasound guided sampling raise the yield over old serology. Mistaking Crohn's for TB commits patients to unnecessary immunosuppression; mistaking TB for Crohn's delays effective therapy — the differentiating review is the clinical lifeline.

### Core Teaching Points
- The ileocaecal region is the commonest site of intestinal TB; abdominal involvement accounts for about 11% of extra-pulmonary disease, with peritoneal and gastrointestinal forms predominating. (PMID 27730779, PMID 36899289)
- Diagnosis combines clinical-radiological suspicion with tissue confirmation: endoscopic biopsy for caseating granulomas, culture, and molecular (PCR) testing; peritoneal fluid analysis and ultrasound/CT-guided sampling assist in peritoneal disease. (PMID 36899289, PMID 27730779)
- The Crohn's-disease mimic is the central diagnostic trap; features favouring TB include pulmonary involvement, night sweats, ascites, patulous ileocaecal valve, and transverse ulcers, while longitudinal ulcers and perianal disease favour Crohn's. (PMID 37468869)
- Treatment follows standard anti-tubercular therapy (rifampicin-based multi-drug regimens); surgery is reserved for complications — obstruction, perforation, fistula, or bleeding. (PMID 36899289, PMID 27730779)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration of therapy for intestinal versus pulmonary TB, and the role of therapeutic trials, varies by guideline.
- Diagnostic-test accuracy of individual modalities (PCR versus culture versus histology) varies with prevalence and technique.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Site predilection and clinical patterns.
- Diagnostic pathway and tissue confirmation.
- TB versus Crohn's differentiating features.
- Treatment regimen and surgical indications.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient with ileocaecal ulceration has weight loss and night sweats; differentiate TB from Crohn's and confirm the diagnosis.
- An intestinal-TB patient develops persistent obstruction on therapy; outline the surgical-decision framework.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include WHO tuberculosis-guideline documents; verify regimen durations against current WHO/national guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27730779**: *Abdominal Tuberculosis.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 36899289**: *Evidence-based approach to diagnosis and management of abdominal tuberculosis.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 37468869**: *Differentiating gastrointestinal tuberculosis and Crohn's disease - a comprehensive review.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0965] Intra-abdominal infections: source control principles, empirical antibiotics, peritonitis

**Cluster**: intra-abdominal infection | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intra-abdominal infection is a two-front war: source control and antimicrobials, with source control the non-negotiable first front — no antibiotic regimen rescues an uncontrolled leak. The principles are physical: drain the pus, control the leak, restore anatomy, often staged with damage-control surgery in the unstable. Antimicrobial therapy covers the polymicrobial flora of secondary peritonitis (enteric gram-negatives, anaerobes, enterococci) with a source-appropriate, narrowable regimen; observational ICU cohorts show outcomes are dominated by severity, source control adequacy, and resistance patterns rather than agent choice alone.

### Core Teaching Points
- Source control — drainage of infected collections, definitive control of contamination, and restoration of anatomy — is the foundation of intra-abdominal infection management; antibiotics cannot compensate for inadequate source control. (PMID 27363829, PMID 26568097)
- Secondary peritonitis is polymicrobial; empirical regimens must cover enteric gram-negative bacilli and anaerobes, with anti-enterococcal cover for healthcare-associated and high-risk cases, then de-escalate on culture. (PMID 26568097, PMID 34037963)
- Large ICU cohorts show mortality is driven by severity at onset, anatomical disruption extent, and inadequate source control — reinforcing early intervention and reassessment. (PMID 34037963, PMID 27363829)
- Duration of therapy shortens dramatically with adequate source control: 4-7 days is supported for most controlled intra-abdominal infections, extending only for persistent foci or inadequate control. (PMID 27363829, PMID 26568097)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal duration (short versus standard) in specific sources (pancreatic, biliary) continues to evolve.
- The role of routine enterococcal and antifungal cover in community-acquired peritonitis is debated.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Source-control hierarchy and timing.
- Empirical-coverage spectrum by acquisition type.
- What drives mortality in IAI cohorts.
- Short-course therapy after adequate source control.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A perforated diverticulitis patient is septic on presentation; prioritise source-control sequencing and initial antibiotics.
- A post-operative peritonitis patient remains febrile day 5 on appropriate antibiotics; reassess for uncontrolled source.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts predate the current IDSA/SIS intra-abdominal-infection guideline update; verify duration and coverage recommendations against it before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27363829**: *Abdominal Sepsis.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 26568097**: *Basis for selecting optimum antibiotic regimens for secondary peritonitis.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 34037963**: *Antimicrobial Lessons From a Large Observational Cohort on Intra-abdominal Infections in Intensive Care Units.* (2020) [cohort-study] - None

## [0966] Intra-aortic balloon pump and Impella: principles and ICU management

**Cluster**: mechanical circulatory support | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Two devices carry most of the short-term mechanical-circulatory-support load. The intra-aortic balloon pump is the elder: counterpulsation inflating in diastole augments coronary perfusion and deflating in systole offloads the ventricle — modest support, easy to insert, but with the weakest outcome evidence (IABP-SHOCK II showed no mortality benefit in post-MI shock). The Impella transvalvular axial-flow pump provides far greater flows and true ventricular unloading, at the cost of vascular complications, haemolysis, and bleeding. Combined IABP-plus-Impella strategies appear in systematic review as an escalation option; complications surveillance — access-site, haemolysis, limb ischaemia — is the defining ICU management skill.

### Core Teaching Points
- IABP counterpulsation augments diastolic coronary perfusion and reduces afterload; it provides modest haemodynamic support but IABP-SHOCK II showed no mortality benefit in post-MI cardiogenic shock. (PMID 32793674)
- Impella axial-flow pumps deliver higher flows with active ventricular unloading across the aortic valve; they suit higher-severity shock but carry vascular-access, bleeding, and haemolysis complications. (PMID 32793680, PMID 32793674)
- Combined IABP-plus-Impella support is described in systematic review as an escalation strategy for refractory cardiogenic shock, adding pulsatility and afterload reduction to axial-flow output. (PMID 38697884)
- ICU management centres on complications surveillance: access-site bleeding, limb ischaemia, haemolysis (plasma-free haemoglobin monitoring), device position (echo surveillance), and anticoagulation titration. (PMID 32793680, PMID 32793674)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether Impella-based strategies improve mortality in cardiogenic shock (versus IABP or medical therapy alone) awaits definitive trials.
- Optimal anticoagulation intensity and device-weaning protocols are centre-specific.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- IABP physics, indications, and IABP-SHOCK II.
- Impella pumps: flows, positioning, and complications.
- Combination-therapy rationale.
- ICU surveillance priorities on MCS.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A post-MI shock patient is failing on IABP; argue escalation to Impella and the expected trade-offs.
- An Impella patient develops dark urine and rising lactate dehydrogenase; diagnose haemolysis and outline management.

**Reviewer Gap**: The IABP-SHOCK II primary report is not among the retained abstracts; verify trial details before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32793674**: *Short-term mechanical circulatory support (intra-aortic balloon pump, Impella, extracorporeal membrane oxygenation).* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 38697884**: *Combined use of intra-aortic balloon pump and Impella in cardiogenic shock: A systematic review.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 32793680**: *Complications associated with mechanical circulatory support.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0967] Intra-articular injections in OA: corticosteroids, hyaluronic acid, PRP: evidence review

**Cluster**: OA injections | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intra-articular injections for osteoarthritis sit on contested evidence: guideline recommendations diverge by region and economics, and the placebo-controlled literature is humbling — intra-articular saline performs comparably to corticosteroid, PRP, and hyaluronic acid in hip-OA network meta-analysis, reminding clinicians that much of the injection benefit is contextual. The defensible reading: corticosteroids give short-term symptom relief with diminishing returns and possible cartilage concerns with repeated use; hyaluronic acid offers a modest, delayed benefit some guidelines endorse and others reject; PRP shows promise in comparative meta-analysis but standardisation and cost limit routine use.

### Core Teaching Points
- Clinical-practice-guideline systematic review shows divergent regional recommendations for IA corticosteroid, hyaluronic acid, and PRP in knee OA — reflecting evidence uncertainty and economic context rather than clear efficacy differences. (PMID 34485586, PMID 35383651)
- Placebo-controlled network meta-analysis in hip OA found intra-articular saline as effective as corticosteroid, PRP, and hyaluronic acid — a powerful demonstration of contextual and placebo effects in injection trials. (PMID 32829298)
- In knee-OA comparative meta-analysis, PRP, bone-marrow aspirate concentrate, and hyaluronic acid outperformed corticosteroids for symptom outcomes, though heterogeneity and commercial bias temper the conclusions. (PMID 38331363)
- AAOS guidance supports corticosteroid use for short-term knee-OA symptom relief while not recommending routine hyaluronic-acid injection; repeated steroid injections carry diminishing returns and theoretical cartilage-harm concerns. (PMID 35383651)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether PRP's comparative benefit survives rigorous placebo-controlled standardised-protocol trials is unresolved.
- Long-term safety of repeated corticosteroid injections on cartilage remains incompletely defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Guideline divergence and its reasons.
- The saline-placebo lesson for injection evidence.
- Comparative-effectiveness meta-analysis: PRP/HA versus steroid.
- Practical injection counselling: expectations and limits.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A knee-OA patient requests 'the injection that lasts longest'; counsel on the evidence landscape and set realistic expectations.
- A patient has had four steroid injections in a year with waning benefit; discuss cartilage concerns and alternatives.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts focus on knee and hip; verify recommendations for other joints separately before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35383651**: *AAOS Clinical Practice Guideline Summary: Management of Osteoarthritis of the Knee (Nonarthroplasty), Third Edition.* (2022) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 34485586**: *A Systematic Review of Current Clinical Practice Guidelines on Intra-articular Hyaluronic Acid, Corticosteroid, and Platelet-Rich Plasma.* (2021) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 32829298**: *Intra-articular saline injection is as effective as corticosteroids, platelet-rich plasma and hyaluronic acid for hip osteoarthritis.* (2017) [meta-analysis] - None
- **PMID 38331363**: *Platelet-Rich Plasma, Bone Marrow Aspirate Concentrate, and Hyaluronic Acid Injections Outperform Corticosteroids in Knee Osteoarthritis.* (2022) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0968] Intracerebral haemorrhage: blood pressure targets, reversal of anticoagulants

**Cluster**: ICH | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Two interventions dominate the first hours of intracerebral haemorrhage, and both are about stopping the bleed. Blood-pressure lowering — the INTERACT2/ATACH lineage refined to a target around 130-140 mmHg systolic, achieved smoothly and early — reduces haematoma expansion, the strongest modifiable predictor of outcome. Anticoagulant reversal is the second: warfarin reversed with PCC plus vitamin K, the DOACs with specific reversal agents (idarucizumab for dabigatran; andexanet alfa or PCC for factor-Xa inhibitors), alongside platelet-transfusion caution — the PATCH lesson that indiscriminate platelet transfusion in antiplatelet-associated ICH may worsen outcome.

### Core Teaching Points
- Early, smooth systolic blood-pressure lowering to approximately 130-140 mmHg limits haematoma expansion, the strongest modifiable predictor of poor functional outcome. (PMID 31188036, PMID 28887767)
- Haematoma expansion is common within the first hours; blood-pressure variability and peaks are as important as the absolute achieved level — smooth control matters. (PMID 38099439, PMID 31188036)
- Anticoagulant reversal is urgent: vitamin K plus four-factor PCC for warfarin; idarucizumab for dabigatran; andexanet alfa or PCC for factor-Xa inhibitors — with the agent's last-dose timing guiding urgency. (PMID 28887767, PMID 31188036)
- Platelet transfusion for antiplatelet-associated ICH is not routine: the PATCH trial signal of harm without surgical indication cautions against indiscriminate transfusion. (PMID 28887767)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- How low to target systolic BP (intensive <130 versus 140-150) and the role of ultra-early aggressive lowering are refined but unsettled.
- The ideal reversal strategy for factor-Xa inhibitor ICH when andexanet is unavailable follows PCC-based protocols.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- BP targets and the expansion-prevention rationale.
- Anticoagulant-reversal agent selection by drug.
- The platelet-transfusion caution.
- Avoiding BP variability in the acute phase.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A rivaroxaban patient presents with a large ICH and BP 185/105; sequence reversal and BP management for the first hours.
- An aspirin-taking ICH patient has a falling GCS; argue the platelet-transfusion decision using the evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: The INTERACT2/ATACH-2 primary reports are not among the retained abstracts; verify target-BP details against the primary publications before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31188036**: *Intracerebral hemorrhage: an update on diagnosis and treatment.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 28887767**: *Management of Spontaneous Intracerebral Hemorrhage.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 38099439**: *Code ICH: A Call to Action.* (2023) [call-to-action] - None

## [0969] Intracerebral hemorrhage acute management

**Cluster**: ICH | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute ICH management beyond BP and reversal is a choreography of neuroprotection, surveillance, and escalation. The World Stroke Organization's synthesis of global guidelines provides the consensus frame: rapid CT, reversal of coagulopathy, BP control, and early neurosurgical assessment for cerebellar haemorrhage or deterioration — with inter-hospital transfer protocols for centres lacking neurosurgery. Raised intracranial pressure is managed with head positioning, osmotherapy (hypertonic saline or mannitol), and decompressive craniectomy for malignant MCA-pattern swelling; the cerebral-oedema guideline formalises selection and monitoring of these therapies. The through-line is organised systems — 'Code ICH' pathways compressing time-to-CT, time-to-reversal, and time-to-decision.

### Core Teaching Points
- Global stroke-guideline synthesis supports the core acute-ICH bundle: rapid neuroimaging, coagulopathy reversal, controlled BP lowering, structured observation, and early surgical assessment for cerebellar or deteriorating haemorrhage. (PMID 36725717)
- Intracranial-pressure management uses tiered escalation: head-of-bed elevation, sedation, osmotherapy (hypertonic saline or mannitol), CSF diversion where indicated, and decompressive craniectomy for refractory malignant swelling. (PMID 31262419, PMID 32227294)
- The cerebral-oedema guideline provides practical recommendations for selecting and monitoring osmotherapy and ICP-directed therapies in neurocritical care. (PMID 32227294)
- Cerebellar haemorrhage with brainstem compression or hydrocephalus is a surgical emergency for decompression and/or ventricular drainage — early recognition and transfer protocols determine outcome. (PMID 36725717, PMID 31262419)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether ICP-monitoring-guided therapy improves functional outcome in ICH is unproven.
- Optimal timing and selection for minimally-invasive haematoma evacuation remain under active trial evaluation.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- The acute-ICH bundle components.
- Tiered ICP management and osmotherapy selection.
- Surgical emergencies: cerebellar haemorrhage and hydrocephalus.
- Code-ICH systems and transfer protocols.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A cerebellar-ICH patient is drowsy with new hydrocephalus at a non-surgical centre; sequence the stabilisation and transfer.
- An ICH patient's GCS falls with new pupil asymmetry; outline the tiered ICP-escalation response.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the primary surgical-trial evidence (STICH lineage) or the current AHA/ASA ICH guideline; verify surgical-indication thresholds before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36725717**: *A systematic review and synthesis of global stroke guidelines on behalf of the World Stroke Organization.* (2023) [guideline-synthesis] - None
- **PMID 31262419**: *Intracranial Hemorrhage and Intracranial Hypertension.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 32227294**: *Guidelines for the Acute Treatment of Cerebral Edema in Neurocritical Care Patients.* (2020) [guideline] - None
