## [0970] Intravenous fluid prescribing and fluid balance management

**Cluster**: IV fluid prescribing | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intravenous-fluid prescribing is the commonest inpatient intervention and among the least audited: fluids are drugs with indications, contraindications, and toxicity — yet they are often prescribed 'to keep the vein open' by the most junior team member. The modern evidence frame is the four Ds and the harm narrative: the evidence base for exactly when and how much resuscitation fluid remains thin in multimorbid acute medicine, while harm accrues quietly through fluid accumulation — positive balance drives oedema, AKI, and delayed recovery even when each bolus looked reasonable. Perioperative and thoracic-surgery data anchor the optimisation principle: goal-directed, moderate-volume strategies beat both liberal flooding and dogmatic restriction.

### Core Teaching Points
- Fluid prescribing should follow drug-prescribing logic: assess need (hypovolaemia versus euvolaemia), choose fluid type deliberately (crystalloid default), define volume and endpoint, and review daily with a stop date. (PMID 29858432, PMID 34006046)
- The evidence base for exactly when and how much resuscitation fluid to give in acute medicine is limited; uncertainty should translate into small aliquots with reassessment rather than large empirical boluses. (PMID 29858432)
- Cumulative positive fluid balance is an independent harm driver: 'zero fluid accumulation' is the therapeutic goal in critical illness, with deresuscitation (fluid removal) a deliberate phase of care. (PMID 34006046)
- Perioperative evidence supports optimised, goal-directed moderate-fluid strategies over liberal administration; thoracic-surgery data show excess fluid drives respiratory complications. (PMID 34764839)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which dynamic measures (passive leg raise, stroke-volume variation, echo) best guide fluid responsiveness at the bedside remains practice-variable.
- Optimal deresuscitation timing and triggers in established positive balance are not standardised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Fluid-as-drug prescribing framework.
- Fluid responsiveness assessment.
- The harm of cumulative positive balance.
- Goal-directed versus liberal strategies perioperatively.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A septic patient has received 6 litres by day 2 with rising oxygen requirements and positive 5-litre balance; design the reassessment and deresuscitation.
- A post-operative patient is on 'maintenance fluids' day 4 without review; audit the prescription against prescribing principles.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained abstracts do not include the current consensus definitions of fluid stewardship or the crystalloid-versus-colloid trial literature; verify fluid-type guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34006046**: *Aiming for zero fluid accumulation: First, do no harm.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 29858432**: *What is the evidence base for fluid resuscitation in acute medicine?* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 34764839**: *Restricted, optimized or liberal fluid strategy in thoracic surgery: A narrative review.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0971] Intravenous inotropes: dobutamine, milrinone, levosimendan in acute heart failure

**Cluster**: inotropes in heart failure | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
In acute decompensated heart failure with a low-output state — hypoperfusion, rising lactate, cool extremities despite adequate filling pressures — intravenous inotropes are the temporary scaffold while the cause is treated. Dobutamine, the beta-agonist workhorse, increases contractility and output at the price of tachyarrhythmia and myocardial oxygen demand; milrinone, a phosphodiesterase-3 inhibitor, adds vasodilation and lusitropy but accumulates in renal dysfunction and causes hypotension; levosimendan, the calcium-sensitising agent with active metabolites, offers catecholamine-independent inotropy suited to the beta-blocked patient. None improve hard outcomes — they are a bridge to recovery, device, or decision.

### Core Teaching Points
- Inotropes in acute heart failure are indicated for the hypoperfused low-output state (end-organ dysfunction with low cardiac output), not for routine decongestion; they are a bridge to definitive therapy. (PMID 26690127)
- Dobutamine provides inotropy and mild vasodilation through beta-agonism, with tachyarrhythmia and increased oxygen demand as principal limitations. (PMID 26690127)
- Milrinone offers inotropy plus vasodilation and is useful in pulmonary hypertension contexts, but causes hypotension and requires renal dose adjustment; levosimendan provides calcium-sensitising, catecholamine-independent inotropy with utility in beta-blocked patients. (PMID 26690127)
- No inotrope has improved survival in acute heart failure; therapy is temporising, with arrhythmia, ischaemia, and hypotension as the costs that must be monitored. (PMID 26690127, PMID 34447992)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether levosimendan improves outcomes over dobutamine in specific phenotypes (beta-blocked, septic-cardiomyopathy) is unproven.
- Optimal weaning sequences and inotrope-free intervals are protocolised rather than evidence-defined.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Low-output-state recognition and inotrope indications.
- Agent selection by phenotype and renal function.
- The no-mortality-benefit principle.
- Monitoring: arrhythmia, ischaemia, hypotension.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A decompensated-HF patient is cold, confused, and lactate-rising on optimal diuretics; decide on inotrope initiation and agent selection.
- A beta-blocked low-output patient needs inotropy; argue for levosimendan over escalating dobutamine.

**Reviewer Gap**: THIN EVIDENCE BASE: only one source. The retained abstract lacks the comparative-trial literature (LIDO, SURVIVE, LEVO-CTS); verify agent-comparison data before approval. Cross-reference topic 947 for the shock-specific inotrope evidence.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26690127**: *Use of Inotropic Agents in Treatment of Systolic Heart Failure.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 34447992**: *2021 ESC Guidelines for the diagnosis and treatment of acute and chronic heart failure.* (2021) [guideline] -

## [0972] intrinsic acute kidney injury

**Cluster**: intrinsic AKI | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intrinsic acute kidney injury is parenchymal failure: the kidney itself is injured, whether by ischaemia-reperfusion (the ischaemic ATN of shock and sepsis), nephrotoxins (aminoglycosides, contrast, cisplatin), pigment (rhabdomyolysis, haemolysis), or interstitial inflammation (drug AIN). It is distinguished from pre-renal azotaemia by its failure to respond to volume and by urinary sediment — muddy-brown casts the classic signature of ischaemic ATN. Management is largely supportive: haemodynamic optimisation without fluid overload, nephrotoxin withdrawal, dose adjustment, and the recognition that recovery, when it comes, takes weeks.

### Core Teaching Points
- AKI is defined by rising creatinine or falling urine output over hours to days; categorising the cause as pre-renal, intrinsic, or post-renal structures the diagnostic work-up. (PMID 33121629)
- Intrinsic AKI subtypes include ischaemic/toxic acute tubular necrosis, pigment nephropathy, and interstitial nephritis; the urinary sediment (muddy-brown granular casts in ATN) and failure to respond to volume help distinguish intrinsic from pre-renal disease. (PMID 33121629, PMID 26804946)
- Management is supportive: treat the cause, withdraw nephrotoxins, adjust drug doses, optimise haemodynamics while avoiding fluid overload, and institute RRT only for absolute indications. (PMID 26804946, PMID 33121629)
- Recovery from intrinsic AKI is slow — tubular regeneration takes weeks — and incomplete recovery leaves CKD, the rationale for nephrotoxin vigilance and follow-up. (PMID 26804946)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- No pharmacological therapy (dopamine, fenoldopam, mannitol) has improved outcomes in intrinsic AKI.
- Biomarker-guided early differentiation of pre-renal from intrinsic AKI is promising but not practice-changing.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Pre-renal, intrinsic, post-renal categorisation.
- Sediment and diagnostic clues by subtype.
- Supportive management and nephrotoxin withdrawal.
- Recovery timeline and CKD transition.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A septic patient has AKI unresponsive to 48 hours of fluid optimisation with muddy-brown casts; classify and manage.
- A crush-injury patient has dark urine and rising creatinine; outline the pigment-nephropathy pathway and prevention.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is AKI-general; verify intrinsic-subtype specifics (pigment, AIN, contrast) against dedicated reviews — cross-reference topics 918 and 961 for interstitial causes.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 33121629**: *Acute Kidney Injury.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 26804946**: *Acute kidney injury: current concepts and new insights.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0973] invasive aspergillosis antifungal therapy

**Cluster**: invasive aspergillosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Antifungal therapy for invasive aspergillosis is anchored by the ESCMID-ECMM-ERS guideline: voriconazole is first-line for most invasive disease, with isavuconazole an equal-alternative and liposomal amphotericin B the alternative when azoles fail or are contraindicated. The pharmacology makes or breaks outcomes: voriconazole's nonlinear kinetics, CYP2C19 genetic variability, and narrow therapeutic window demand therapeutic drug monitoring; posaconazole and itraconazole absorption is erratic without formulations optimised. Resistance surveillance — azole-resistant A. fumigatus — is now a first-order concern shaping empiric choices and requiring susceptibility data.

### Core Teaching Points
- Voriconazole is first-line therapy for invasive aspergillosis per ESCMID-ECMM-ERS guidance, with isavuconazole an equal alternative and liposomal amphotericin B reserved for intolerance, failure, or resistance. (PMID 29544767, PMID 34016284)
- Therapeutic drug monitoring is essential for voriconazole (nonlinear kinetics, CYP2C19 variability, neurotoxicity at high levels) and recommended for posaconazole/itraconazole; trough-guided dosing improves exposure-target attainment. (PMID 28702763, PMID 29544767)
- Combination antifungal therapy (voriconazole plus an echinocandin) is considered in selected high-risk or refractory cases but lacks definitive outcome superiority. (PMID 29544767, PMID 34016284)
- Azole resistance in A. fumigatus is an escalating threat; susceptibility testing and local-resistance patterns should inform empiric and salvage therapy. (PMID 34016284, PMID 29544767)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The optimal role and timing of combination antifungal therapy remains unresolved.
- Whether newer agents (rezafungin, olorofim) displace current first-line therapy awaits comparative outcome data.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line agent selection by guideline.
- TDM indications and target troughs.
- Combination-therapy positioning.
- Azole resistance and susceptibility-guided therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An immunocompromised patient with probable IA has CYP2C19 ultrarapid-metaboliser status; adjust voriconazole management.
- An IA patient fails voriconazole therapy on adequate troughs; design salvage therapy using the guideline ladder.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained guideline summary is 2017-era; verify against the current ESCMID-ECMM-ERS update before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 29544767**: *Diagnosis and management of Aspergillus diseases: executive summary of the 2017 ESCMID-ECMM-ERS guideline.* (2018) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 34016284**: *Aspergillosis: Epidemiology, Diagnosis, and Treatment.* (2019) [review] - None
- **PMID 28702763**: *Pharmacokinetics of antifungal drugs: practical implications for optimized treatment of patients.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0974] invasive candidiasis and candidemia antifungal therapy

**Cluster**: invasive candidiasis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Candidemia is the commonest invasive fungal disease in hospitals, and its management has two non-negotiables: an echinocandin is first-line empiric therapy (fluconazole reserved for stable, susceptible isolates), and central-line removal plus fundoscopic examination are part of the treatment, not adjuncts to it. The IDSA 2016 update codifies therapy by syndrome — candidemia, candidaemia with neutropenia, and organ-specific disease each have distinct durations and agents. The challenges review frames the modern threats: non-albicans species with reduced azole susceptibility, echninocandin resistance emerging in C. glabrata, and the difficulty of diagnosing deep-seated foci without candidemia.

### Core Teaching Points
- An echinocandin (caspofungin, micafungin, anidulafungin) is recommended first-line empiric therapy for candidemia; fluconazole is an option for stable patients with susceptible isolates, and step-down to oral fluconazole follows susceptibility. (PMID 26679628, PMID 26739610)
- Source control is integral: central-venous-catheter removal where feasible, and ophthalmological examination to exclude endophthalmitis, are parts of standard candidemia management. (PMID 26679628, PMID 37220664)
- Non-albicans species (C. glabrata, C. parapsilosis, C. tropicalis) now dominate many series with reduced azole susceptibility, driving species-level therapy selection and susceptibility testing. (PMID 37220664, PMID 26739610)
- Treatment duration is typically 14 days from first negative blood culture for candidemia without metastatic foci; longer with endophthalmitis, osteoarticular, or other deep-seated disease. (PMID 26679628)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The optimal role of rapid species-identification and resistance-marker testing in directing early therapy is evolving.
- Management of persistent candidemia despite therapy (source exclusion, resistance, immune reconstitution) is individualised.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-line agent selection and step-down rules.
- Source control: line removal and fundoscopy.
- Species-level susceptibility patterns.
- Duration by syndrome.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A candidaemic ICU patient has a central line and new visual floaters; sequence therapy, line removal, and ophthalmology.
- A stable patient grows fluconazole-susceptible C. albicans from blood; justify step-down to oral therapy and duration.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained IDSA guideline is the 2016 update; verify against any newer revision before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26679628**: *Clinical Practice Guideline for the Management of Candidiasis: 2016 Update by the Infectious Diseases Society of America.* (2016) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 26739610**: *Invasive Candidiasis.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 37220664**: *Invasive candidiasis: current clinical challenges and unmet needs in adult populations.* (2022) [review] - None

## [0975] Invasive fungal infection in immunocompromised: aspergillosis, candida, galactomannan, voriconazole

**Cluster**: invasive fungal infection | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
In the immunocompromised host, invasive fungal infection is a race between diagnostic certainty and disease progression — and the modern strategy treats early. Serum and bronchoalveolar galactomannan, beta-D-glucan, and high-resolution CT's halo sign provide pre-culture evidence; the host's net state of immunosuppression (neutropenia duration, corticosteroid dose, transplant type) sets the pre-test probability. The ICU adds a diagnostic wrinkle: invasive aspergillosis now occurs in non-neutropenic patients — COPD on steroids, cirrhosis, post-influenza — where the classic frameworks fit poorly. Therapy is empiric-to-preemptive, anchored on voriconazole with the echinocandins and the newer agents filling defined niches.

### Core Teaching Points
- Host-immunosuppression assessment (neutropenia, corticosteroid dose, transplant, haematologic malignancy) drives fungal-risk stratification and the threshold for empiric/preemptive therapy. (PMID 26165502, PMID 28355466)
- Non-culture diagnostics — galactomannan (serum and BAL), beta-D-glucan, and CT halo/infarct patterns — enable earlier diagnosis and preemptive therapy before culture or histology. (PMID 26165502, PMID 24994691)
- Invasive aspergillosis increasingly affects non-neutropenic ICU patients (COPD on steroids, post-influenza, liver failure), challenging classical diagnostic frameworks built on neutropenia. (PMID 28355466)
- Voriconazole anchors therapy; echinocandins serve as first-line in selected candidiasis contexts and salvage in aspergillosis, with newer agents expanding the armamentarium. (PMID 24994691, PMID 26165502)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal thresholds for galactomannan and beta-D-glucan positivity vary by host and specimen.
- Whether preemptive (diagnostics-triggered) strategies outperform empiric antifungal therapy in outcomes is incompletely tested.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Risk stratification by immunosuppression type.
- Galactomannan and beta-D-glucan interpretation.
- Non-neutropenic invasive aspergillosis recognition.
- Empiric versus preemptive therapy strategies.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A neutropenic patient has fever, a halo sign on CT, and a rising serum galactomannan; outline the preemptive-therapy decision.
- A COPD patient on high-dose steroids deteriorates with putative bacterial pneumonia unresponsive to antibiotics; consider non-neutropenic invasive aspergillosis.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained reviews are 2015-2016 era; verify diagnostics thresholds and the newer-agent landscape against current ECMM guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 26165502**: *Fungal infections in the ICU: advances in treatment and diagnosis.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 28355466**: *Invasive mould infections in the ICU setting: complexities and solutions.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 24994691**: *Current and future therapies for invasive aspergillosis.* (2015) [review] - None

## [0976] Invasive Pulmonary Aspergillosis

**Cluster**: invasive aspergillosis | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Invasive pulmonary aspergillosis is the clinical entity — fever, cough, pleuritic pain, and progressive infiltrates in an immunocompromised host — that the antifungal guidelines (topic 973) treat. Diagnosis is probabilistic, staged from possible to probable to proven on host factors, radiology, and mycology; the halo sign on early CT is the radiological signature, later giving way to the air-crescent of recovering lesions. Refractory disease — failure on adequate voriconazole — demands systematic reassessment: adherence and troughs first, resistance and imaging progression next, then salvage with a lipid amphotericin, isavuconazole, or combination approaches, with surgical resection considered for localised disease in fit patients.

### Core Teaching Points
- IPA diagnosis is staged (possible/probable/proven) combining host factors, radiological features (halo sign, infarct-shaped consolidation), and mycological evidence (galactomannan, culture, BAL microscopy). (PMID 38714471, PMID 32034433)
- Invasive aspergillosis increasingly involves non-fumigatus species with variable antifungal susceptibility; identification to species level and susceptibility testing inform management. (PMID 38714471)
- Refractory IPA management follows a sequence: confirm exposure (adherence, trough levels), assess resistance and progression, then salvage with alternative agents (liposomal amphotericin B, isavuconazole), combination therapy, or surgical resection for localised disease. (PMID 38286175)
- Severe respiratory infection in the immunocompromised demands systematic identification of the cause — invasive diagnostics (BAL with galactomannan, multiplex PCR) improve targeted therapy in hypoxaemic respiratory failure. (PMID 32034433)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The contribution of surgical resection to outcomes in localised IPA is based on case series rather than trials.
- Optimal salvage-therapy sequencing for refractory disease is expert-guided.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic staging and the halo sign.
- Species identification and susceptibility.
- Refractory-disease work-up sequence.
- Role of invasive diagnostics in the immunocompromised.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A neutropenic patient on voriconazole for probable IPA worsens; work through the refractory-disease sequence.
- An immunocompromised patient has hypoxaemic respiratory failure with a negative routine work-up; justify BAL with galactomannan and PCR.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is review-level; verify diagnostic-staging criteria against the current ECMM/ERS consensus definitions before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38714471**: *Invasive aspergillosis: A comprehensive review.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 38286175**: *How do I manage refractory invasive pulmonary aspergillosis.* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 32034433**: *Diagnosis of severe respiratory infections in immunocompromised patients.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0977] investigating suspected stable angina in women

**Cluster**: stable angina in women | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Suspected stable angina in women runs on a different track: women present later, with atypical and microvascular-predominant disease, and carry a higher burden of angina with non-obstructive coronary arteries — ANOCA/INOCA affecting nearly half of women undergoing angiography for suspected ischemia. The 2021 AHA/ACC chest-pain guideline explicitly addresses sex-specific pathways: functional testing and CT-angiography selection perform differently by sex, and a negative anatomical test does not end the work-up when microvascular disease is plausible. The modern skill is resisting both errors: dismissing women's chest pain as non-cardiac, and stop investigating once the epicardial arteries look clean.

### Core Teaching Points
- Women with suspected CAD present with atypical symptoms more often, develop more nonobstructive and microvascular disease, and are under-investigated relative to men — the 2021 AHA/ACC guideline codifies sex-specific diagnostic pathways. (PMID 36921653)
- ANOCA/INOCA — angina with non-obstructive coronary arteries — may affect nearly half of patients undergoing invasive angiography for suspected ischemia and encompasses coronary microvascular dysfunction and vasospasm. (PMID 37704315, PMID 36921653)
- Diagnostic-test performance differs by sex; CTA subgroup analysis informs appropriate-use, and a clean epicardial angiogram should prompt functional/microvascular assessment (stress imaging, CFR/IMR where available) rather than reassurance alone. (PMID 31189617, PMID 37704315)
- Investigating women requires the dual framework: exclude obstructive disease with appropriate testing, then actively pursue microvascular and vasospastic mechanisms when symptoms persist. (PMID 37704315, PMID 36921653)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal diagnostic thresholds and test-selection algorithms for women remain refined by ongoing sex-specific outcome data.
- Treatment of confirmed microvascular angina improves symptoms more consistently than outcomes.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Sex differences in presentation and disease pattern.
- ANOCA/INOCA definitions and population frequency.
- Test selection by sex from the AHA/ACC guideline.
- Next steps after a nonobstructive angiogram.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 58-year-old woman has exertional chest discomfort, a normal resting ECG, and intermediate pre-test probability; design the guideline-concordant investigation.
- A woman's angiogram shows nonobstructive disease but disabling symptoms continue; outline the microvascular work-up.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is guideline-review level; verify test-performance numbers against the primary AHA/ACC guideline and PROMISE/SCOT-HEART sex-subgroup analyses before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 36921653**: *Chest Pain in Women: Considerations From the 2021 AHA/ACC Chest Pain Guideline.* (2023) [guideline-review] - None
- **PMID 37704315**: *Comprehensive Management of ANOCA, Part 1 - Definition, Patient Population, and Diagnosis (JACC State-of-the-Art Review).* (2023) [state-of-the-art] - None
- **PMID 31189617**: *Diagnosis of obstructive coronary artery disease using computed tomography angiography in patients with stable chest pain (subgroup analysis).* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0978] iron deficiency anaemia

**Cluster**: iron deficiency anaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Iron deficiency anaemia is a sign before it is a diagnosis: in men and postmenopausal women the mandate is finding the blood loss, which is gastrointestinal until proven otherwise. The BSG guideline structures the work-up — coeliac serology with the ferritin, faecal occult blood and endoscopic evaluation by risk, and small-bowel investigation when upper-and-lower endoscopy is negative — while ferritin thresholds (with transferrin saturation where inflammation blurs the picture) anchor the laboratory diagnosis. Treatment restores the deficit (oral first for most, intravenous when oral fails or urgency dictates — see topic 979) but never substitutes for finding the cause.

### Core Teaching Points
- In adults without inflammation, ferritin below 45 ng/mL is diagnostic of iron deficiency; ferritin 46-99 ng/mL with transferrin saturation below 20% also confirms deficiency — inflammation raises ferritin and masks deficiency. (PMID 41252836)
- Men and postmenopausal women with IDA require gastrointestinal evaluation for occult blood loss; coeliac serology is part of the first-line work-up, and small-bowel investigation follows negative bidirectional endoscopy in ongoing-deficiency cases. (PMID 34497146, PMID 33362380)
- Oral iron is first-line for most stable patients; intravenous iron is reserved for intolerance, malabsorption, ongoing losses exceeding oral absorption, or clinical urgency. (PMID 34497146, PMID 41252836)
- Response is monitored by reticulocyte haemoglobin and haemoglobin rise at 2-4 weeks; failure to respond prompts re-evaluation of adherence, absorption, continued loss, and the original diagnosis. (PMID 41252836, PMID 34497146)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal small-bowel-imaging pathways (capsule versus MR-enterography versus device-assisted enteroscopy) follow local expertise.
- The threshold for IV-first strategies in non-urgent IDA is evolving (see topic 979).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Ferritin/TSAT diagnostic thresholds and inflammation caveats.
- GI work-up sequence by sex and risk.
- Oral-versus-IV first-line selection.
- Response monitoring and non-response work-up.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 65-year-old man has ferritin 30 ng/mL and microcytic anaemia; structure the cause-seeking work-up.
- An IDA patient's haemoglobin fails to rise after 4 weeks of oral iron; evaluate adherence, absorption, and continued loss.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is adult-focused; pregnancy and paediatric IDA follow separate guidance — verify scope before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 34497146**: *British Society of Gastroenterology guidelines for the management of iron deficiency anaemia in adults.* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 41252836**: *Iron Deficiency Anemia: Evaluation and Management.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 33362380**: *Diagnosis and treatment of iron-deficiency anemia in gastrointestinal bleeding: A systematic review.* (2019) [systematic-review] - None

## [0979] Iron deficiency anaemia: intravenous iron therapy

**Cluster**: iron deficiency anaemia | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Intravenous iron has moved from rescue to routine: modern formulations (ferric carboxymaltose, iron derisomaltose, ferumoxytol) deliver a gram of iron in 15-60 minutes with reaction rates that no longer justify inpatient observation. The indications are intolerance or non-response to oral iron, malabsorption, ongoing losses outpacing oral absorption, and urgency — but increasingly also convenience and guaranteed delivery. The distinctive modern safety signal is hypophosphatemia: FCM's FGF-23-mediated phosphate wasting is common, usually transient and subclinical, but symptomatic cases with osteomalacia risk warrant monitoring in high-risk and repeated-dose patients.

### Core Teaching Points
- IV iron indications: oral intolerance or non-response, malabsorption (IBD, bariatric, coeliac), ongoing losses exceeding oral replacement, and clinical urgency; modern formulations allow rapid high-dose repletion in single or split doses. (PMID 40603803)
- Oral iron remains first-line for stable, tolerant patients; IV iron achieves faster and more complete repletion and is increasingly used first-line where speed, certainty, or tolerance favour it. (PMID 40603803)
- Ferric carboxymaltose is associated with hypophosphatemia through FGF-23-mediated renal phosphate wasting; systematic review quantifies the risk as frequent, usually transient, but occasionally symptomatic. (PMID 39935027, PMID 34534708)
- Hypophosphatemia risk is higher with repeated doses, low baseline phosphate, and certain formulations; monitoring phosphate in at-risk patients and choosing lower-risk formulations (e.g. derisomaltose) are practical mitigations. (PMID 34534708, PMID 39935027)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term clinical consequences of repeated FCM-associated hypophosphatemia (bone health) are incompletely defined.
- Optimal IV-first versus oral-first strategies by phenotype and health-system cost are evolving.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- IV-iron indications and modern formulations.
- Oral-versus-IV positioning.
- FCM hypophosphatemia mechanism and risk factors.
- Monitoring and formulation-selection mitigation.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An IBD patient with ongoing intestinal losses needs iron repletion; compare oral versus IV strategies and select a formulation.
- A patient receiving repeated FCM doses has fatigue and a low phosphate; recognise FCM-associated hypophosphatemia and adjust.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is general-adult; condition-specific IV-iron evidence (heart failure, CKD) follows separate guidance — verify scope before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40603803**: *Oral and Intravenous Iron Therapy.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 39935027**: *Ferric Carboxymaltose (FCM)-Associated Hypophosphatemia (HPP): A Systematic Review.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 34534708**: *Hypophosphatemia after intravenous iron therapy: Comprehensive review of clinical findings and recommendations.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0980] Irradiated, Leukoreduced and Washed Blood Products

**Cluster**: blood product modification | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Blood-component modifications exist because transfusions can harm in specific hosts: irradiation prevents the rare and fatal transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease in recipients whose immune systems cannot clear donor lymphocytes — congenital immunodeficiency, haematologic malignancy, stem-cell transplant, directed donations from relatives, and HLA-matched platelets. Leukoreduction reduces febrile non-haemolytic reactions, CMV transmission risk, and HLA-alloimmunisation — near-universal in many systems. Washing removes plasma for the rare severe allergic or IgA-deficiency recipient. Each modification adds cost and delay, so the discipline is matching modification to indication, not applying them by default.

### Core Teaching Points
- Irradiated cellular components prevent transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease (TA-GVHD), a rare but near-universally fatal complication in recipients who cannot reject donor lymphocytes. (PMID 25931584, PMID 37753040)
- Irradiation indications include congenital cellular immunodeficiency, haematologic malignancy and its treatment, haematopoietic stem-cell transplantation, directed donations from blood relatives, and HLA-matched platelets. (PMID 37753040, PMID 27113002)
- Leukoreduction reduces febrile non-haemolytic transfusion reactions, CMV transmission risk, and HLA-alloimmunisation; many jurisdictions apply it universally while others target by indication. (PMID 27113002)
- Washed components (removing plasma) are reserved for severe allergic transfusion reactions and IgA-deficiency with anti-IgA — the modification must match a defined indication because each adds cost and delay. (PMID 27113002, PMID 25931584)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether pathogen-reduction technology will consolidate or replace component-modification strategies is system-dependent.
- The precise threshold of immunosuppression warranting irradiation in non-classical indications follows expert consensus.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- TA-GVHD pathophysiology and fatality.
- The irradiation indication list.
- Leukoreduction benefits by mechanism.
- Washed-component indications.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A stem-cell-transplant recipient needs platelets from an HLA-matched sibling donor; specify the required modification and why.
- A patient has recurrent febrile reactions to transfusion; explain the role and mechanism of leukoreduction.

**Reviewer Gap**: The retained evidence is indication-focused; verify modification policies against national haemovigilance and transfusion-service guidelines before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27113002**: *Modifications to Blood Components: When to Use them and What is the Evidence?* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 25931584**: *A systematic review of transfusion-associated graft-versus-host disease.* (2016) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 37753040**: *Transfusion-Associated Graft-Versus-Host Disease in Adults.* (2020) [review] - None

## [0981] irritable bowel syndrome

**Cluster**: IBS general management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Irritable bowel syndrome affects roughly 4-10% of the population (estimates range up to 21% depending on criteria) and is defined by abdominal pain associated with altered bowel habits rather than being a diagnosis of exclusion. Modern management is positive and subtype-directed: classify as IBS-C, IBS-D, or mixed, then step through diet and lifestyle measures, fibre and probiotics, targeted pharmacotherapy by subtype, and gut-directed psychological therapy for refractory symptoms. This page anchors the general strategy; the paired diet/microbiome topic (982) details dietary intervention.

### Core Teaching Points
- IBS is a positive clinical diagnosis based on symptom criteria (Rome IV): recurrent abdominal pain related to defecation with altered stool frequency or form; subtypes are IBS-C, IBS-D, and mixed. (PMID 40789179, PMID 25734736)
- Probiotics may benefit some IBS patients, but heterogeneity of species, strains, and endpoints has prevented specific strain-level recommendations. (PMID 37541528)
- Dietary therapy - soluble fibre and fermentable-carbohydrate restriction - is a first-line treatment pillar alongside lifestyle advice, chosen by subtype. (PMID 35419092)
- IBS substantially reduces quality of life and work productivity; practical stepwise treatment recommendations exist for generalists and specialists. (PMID 25734736, PMID 40789179)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Which specific probiotic strains (if any) have reproducible efficacy in IBS subtypes remains unresolved.
- The durability of dietary restriction beyond short symptom-control periods is uncertain.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Rome IV diagnostic criteria and subtype classification.
- Evidence limits of probiotics and why no specific strain is recommended.
- Stepwise management ladder by IBS subtype.
- Red flags that should divert the work-up away from an IBS label.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 28-year-old woman has 6 months of bloating and pain relieved by defecation with alternating stool habits; structure the positive diagnosis and subtype.
- An IBS-D patient asks whether a probiotic will cure symptoms; frame an honest evidence answer and subtype-appropriate alternatives.

**Reviewer Gap**: Diet/fibers evidence retained is a narrative review; the dietary-interventions network meta-analysis (40258374) was assigned to topic 982 for scope differentiation.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40789179**: *Irritable Bowel Syndrome.* (2025) [review] - None
- **PMID 37541528**: *Efficacy of Probiotics in Irritable Bowel Syndrome: Systematic Review and Meta-analysis.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 35419092**: *Diet, fibers, and probiotics for irritable bowel syndrome.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 25734736**: *Irritable bowel syndrome: a clinical review.* (2015) [review] - None

## [0982] Irritable bowel syndrome diet and microbiome-directed therapy

**Cluster**: IBS dietary and microbiome therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Diet is the most requested and most misunderstood lever in IBS. The low-FODMAP diet - restricting fermentable oligosaccharides, disaccharides, monosaccharides, and polyols - is now an internationally accepted strategy: MRI studies show short-chain fermentable carbohydrates expand small-bowel water and colonic gas, provoking symptoms in patients with visceral hypersensitivity. The diet works through a three-phase restriction-reintroduction-personalisation structure under dietetic supervision. Microbiome-directed therapy (probiotics, and emerging prebiotic/psychobiotic approaches) rests on the reframing of IBS as a disturbance of the microbiota-gut-brain axis, though long-term, large randomised evidence remains thin.

### Core Teaching Points
- MRI studies demonstrate that short-chain fermentable carbohydrates increase small-intestinal water and colonic gas, producing symptoms in patients with visceral hypersensitivity - the mechanistic basis of the low-FODMAP diet. (PMID 28592442)
- The low-FODMAP diet is an internationally accepted dietary management strategy for IBS with demonstrated symptom efficacy in randomised trials. (PMID 38337655, PMID 28244669)
- Implementation follows three phases: short restriction, structured reintroduction to identify triggers, then personalisation for long-term maintenance - ideally under dietetic guidance. (PMID 28244669)
- IBS is increasingly conceptualised as a disturbance of the microbiota-gut-brain axis, providing the rationale for microbiome-directed (probiotic and psychobiotic) therapies. (PMID 35125827)
- A network meta-analysis compares the relative efficacy of available dietary interventions for IBS, informing diet selection when multiple options are proposed. (PMID 40258374)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term, large randomised controlled trials of the low-FODMAP diet are still lacking, and effects on microbiome composition versus symptoms need disentangling.
- Which microbiome-directed interventions (which strains, which targets) confer durable benefit is unresolved.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- FODMAP acronym and the fermentable carbohydrates involved.
- Three-phase low-FODMAP implementation and reintroduction logic.
- Mechanistic evidence (luminal distension, visceral hypersensitivity) behind dietary therapy.
- Microbiota-gut-brain axis as rationale for microbiome-directed therapy.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An IBS-C patient asks for dietary options before medication; outline the dietitian-supervised low-FODMAP pathway and alternatives.
- A patient restricted FODMAPs for a year and never reintroduced; correct the phase structure and micronutrient risks.

**Reviewer Gap**: Evidence base for long-term dietary and microbiome-directed therapy remains limited; statement-level citations come from reviews and one network meta-analysis rather than large durable-outcome trials.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 38337655**: *The Role of the FODMAP Diet in IBS.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 28592442**: *The low FODMAP diet: recent advances in understanding its mechanisms and efficacy in IBS.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 28244669**: *How to institute the low-FODMAP diet.* (2015) [review] - None
- **PMID 35125827**: *Gut bless you: The microbiota-gut-brain axis in irritable bowel syndrome.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 40258374**: *Efficacy of dietary interventions in irritable bowel syndrome: a systematic review and network meta-analysis.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None

## [0983] ischaemic heart disease

**Cluster**: chronic coronary disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ischaemic heart disease spans stable chronic coronary disease through acute occlusive events. The 2023 AHA/ACC chronic coronary disease guideline consolidated a decade of evidence since the 2012 stable ischaemic heart disease guideline: guideline-directed medical therapy for secondary prevention, symptom-directed antianginal therapy, and selective revascularisation. At the acute extreme, STEMI is complete coronary occlusion and a time-critical emergency. Antiplatelet choice for long-term secondary prevention is an active question - an individual patient-level meta-analysis now directly compares clopidogrel with aspirin monotherapy in established coronary disease.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2023 AHA/ACC chronic coronary disease guideline updates and consolidates evidence for managing stable ischaemic heart disease, replacing the 2012 stable IHD guideline framework. (PMID 37471501)
- STEMI represents complete coronary artery occlusion leading to myocardial ischaemia and necrosis; it remains a leading cause of morbidity and mortality and demands rapid reperfusion. (PMID 37904413)
- An individual patient-level meta-analysis compares clopidogrel versus aspirin monotherapy for secondary prevention in established coronary disease, most of whom had prior PCI or ACS. (PMID 40902613)
- Chronic coronary disease management rests on secondary-prevention risk-factor control alongside symptom-directed antianginal therapy, with revascularisation reserved for prognostic or refractory-symptom indications. (PMID 37471501)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal long-term antiplatelet agent and intensity after PCI or ACS is still being refined (clopidogrel vs aspirin head-to-head evidence is young).
- Which stable patients benefit prognostically from revascularisation beyond left-main/proximal-LAD anatomy.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Components of guideline-directed medical therapy in chronic coronary disease.
- Time-critical pathophysiology of STEMI and reperfusion goals.
- Antiplatelet selection for secondary prevention after PCI/ACS.
- Indications for revascularisation in stable disease.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 62-year-old with exertional angina and multivessel disease on optimal medical therapy; weigh revascularisation indications.
- A post-MI patient asks why clopidogrel rather than lifelong aspirin; summarise the comparative-evidence landscape.

**Reviewer Gap**: The clopidogrel-versus-aspirin meta-analysis abstract was truncated mid-results in retrieval; verify effect estimates and confidence intervals against the full text before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 37471501**: *2023 AHA/ACC/ACCP/ASPC/NLA/PCNA Guideline for the Management of Patients With Chronic Coronary Disease.* (2023) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37904413**: *Comprehensive review of ST-segment elevation myocardial infarction: Understanding pathophysiology, diagnosis, and management.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40902613**: *Clopidogrel versus aspirin for secondary prevention of coronary artery disease: a systematic review and individual patient-level meta-analysis.* (2025) [systematic-review] - None

## [0984] Ischaemic heart disease and acute coronary syndromes

**Cluster**: ACS management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute coronary syndromes sit at the emergency end of ischaemic heart disease, and their guidance has just been rewritten: the 2025 ACC/AHA/ACEP/NAEMSP/SCAI ACS guideline incorporates evidence since the 2013 STEMI and 2014 NSTE-ACS documents. Two companion frames complete the picture: the 2021 chest pain guideline standardises front-door evaluation and risk stratification, and the 2026 ACC scientific statement on antiplatelet therapy recalibrates aspirin - no longer routine for primary prevention, reserved for selected high-risk patients, while secondary-prevention therapy remains foundational.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2025 ACC/AHA/ACEP/NAEMSP/SCAI ACS guideline replaces the 2013 STEMI and 2014 NSTE-ACS guidelines, incorporating new evidence across the ACS spectrum. (PMID 40014670)
- The 2026 ACC scientific statement on antiplatelet therapy: recent data do not support routine aspirin for primary prevention of ASCVD in unselected populations; use is reserved for selected patients. (PMID 42377293)
- The 2021 chest pain guideline provides recommendations and algorithms for evaluating chest pain in adults, emphasising structured risk assessment at first contact. (PMID 34709879)
- Antiplatelet therapy remains fundamental to ASCVD management; recommendations have evolved over the past decade and should be applied selectively by prevention context. (PMID 42377293)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Timing and selection of an invasive strategy in intermediate-risk NSTE-ACS continues to evolve.
- Optimal de-escalation and duration of dual antiplatelet therapy balancing ischaemia and bleeding.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- First-contact evaluation of chest pain using the 2021 guideline framework.
- Primary versus secondary prevention aspirin decisions.
- Evolution of ACS guidelines and what changed in 2025.
- Risk stratification driving invasive-strategy timing.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An ED patient with 2 hours of pressure-like chest pain; walk the triage, ECG, and pathway decision algorithm.
- A stable ASCVD patient asks whether he should restart daily aspirin after stopping it; apply the 2026 statement logic.

**Reviewer Gap**: Antiplatelet statement is a scientific statement rather than formal guideline; verify class-of-recommendation wording against the full document before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 40014670**: *2025 ACC/AHA/ACEP/NAEMSP/SCAI Guideline for the Management of Patients With Acute Coronary Syndromes.* (2025) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 42377293**: *Antiplatelet Therapy in the Management of Atherosclerotic Cardiovascular Disease: 2026 ACC Scientific Statement.* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 34709879**: *2021 AHA/ACC/ASE/CHEST/SAEM/SCCT/SCMR Guideline for the Evaluation and Diagnosis of Chest Pain.* (2021) [guideline] - None

## [0985] Ischaemic optic neuropathy: arteritic (GCA) vs non-arteritic (NAION) differentiation

**Cluster**: ischaemic optic neuropathy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Anterior ischaemic optic neuropathy is a leading cause of sudden, painless monocular vision loss after age 50, and the first clinical task is separating its two forms because everything downstream differs. The arteritic form, usually driven by giant cell arteritis, is a medical and ophthalmological emergency: corticosteroids must start immediately to protect the fellow eye, and GCA may present with minimal systemic features. The non-arteritic form (NAION) - associated with vascular risk factors, often a small cup-to-disc ratio - has no proven acute treatment, so management is risk-factor control. Posterior forms (PION) complicate the picture and require the same urgent discrimination.

### Core Teaching Points
- Two forms of AION carry clearly different clinical, diagnostic, and therapeutic implications: arteritic (usually GCA, an emergency) versus non-arteritic; posterior forms (PION) also exist and complicate diagnosis. (PMID 31952875, PMID 42710617)
- Suspected arteritic AION demands immediate corticosteroid therapy because the fellow eye is threatened; GCA may present with few or no systemic features, so inflammatory markers and temporal artery assessment support - not replace - clinical judgement. (PMID 33892603, PMID 42710617)
- AAION is usually due to giant cell arteritis; NAION, by contrast, is associated with vascular risk factors and small optic discs, and has no proven acute therapy. (PMID 31952875, PMID 33892603)
- Ischemic optic neuropathies are among the leading causes of severe visual loss over age 50 and are clinically, aetiologically, and therapeutically distinct entities - anatomical (anterior vs posterior) and aetiological (arteritic vs non-arteritic) classification both matter. (PMID 31952875)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- No acute therapy has proven efficacy for NAION; the role of aspirin remains debated.
- Optimal biopsy timing and steroid-taper strategy after visual-stabilisation in GCA-associated AAION.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Differentiating arteritic from non-arteritic AION at the bedside (disc appearance, age, systemic features).
- Immediate management steps when GCA is suspected, and protecting the fellow eye.
- Anatomical classification: AION versus PION.
- What can and cannot be offered for NAION.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 72-year-old reports sudden painless visual loss with jaw claudication; sequence steroids, inflammatory markers, and biopsy without delaying treatment.
- A 55-year-old hypertensive has painless morning visual loss with a crowded disc; explain NAION prognosis and risk-factor management.

**Reviewer Gap**: Evidence base is narrative reviews and a position paper; no recent randomised treatment evidence for either form is retrievable under the pipeline filter.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 31952875**: *Acute ischemic optic nerve disease: Pathophysiology, clinical features and management.* (2018) [review] - None
- **PMID 33892603**: *Arteritic anterior ischaemic optic neuropathy: An update.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 42710617**: *Joint position paper on anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (AION). Vascular Risk Group and Systemic Autoimmune Disease Group.* (2025) [position-paper] - None

## [0986] ischaemic stroke

**Cluster**: acute ischaemic stroke management | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Acute ischaemic stroke - vascular occlusion causing cerebral infarction - is the fifth most common cause of death in the United States, with incidence projected to rise. Its management has been recast by reperfusion: intravenous thrombolysis, now shifting from alteplase to tenecteplase, plus mechanical thrombectomy for large-vessel occlusion have expanded the treatment window to 24 hours in selected patients. The 2026 AHA/ASA guideline replaces the 2018 guideline and its 2019 update with a comprehensive prehospital-to-discharge framework. Everything in the pathway keys off one datum: time since last-known-well.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2026 AHA/ASA guideline for early management of acute ischaemic stroke replaces the 2018 guideline and 2019 update, providing comprehensive evidence-based recommendations from prehospital care onward. (PMID 41582814)
- Timely reperfusion is the cornerstone of stroke care and requires early symptom recognition by the public and first responders, triage to an appropriate stroke centre, and efficient assessment. (PMID 32507747)
- Intravenous thrombolysis (first alteplase, now increasingly tenecteplase) remains a cornerstone of acute management; in large-vessel occlusion, endovascular thrombectomy added to IV thrombolysis has expanded the therapeutic window to 24 hours. (PMID 39496213)
- Treatment decisions require establishing a high probability of ischaemic stroke and confirming the time since last-known-well, which anchors eligibility for both thrombolysis and thrombectomy. (PMID 39496213)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Comparative effectiveness of tenecteplase versus alteplase is being refined by ongoing trials.
- Patient-selection criteria for thrombectomy in the extended (up to 24-hour) window continue to evolve.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Thrombolysis eligibility, contraindications, and time windows.
- Large-vessel occlusion recognition and thrombectomy window.
- The decisive role of last-known-well time in eligibility decisions.
- Prehospital recognition and stroke-centre triage.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A patient wakes with aphasia found at 07:00; determine thrombolysis/thrombectomy eligibility from last-known-well.
- A transfer decision for a suspected large-vessel occlusion 90 minutes from a thrombectomy centre; justify the pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: Pivotal thrombectomy trials (e.g., MR CLEAN, DAWN, DEFUSE-3) predate the pipeline's 2015+ filter and are cited here through guideline and review summaries; verify against primary publications.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41582814**: *2026 Guideline for the Early Management of Patients With Acute Ischemic Stroke.* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 32507747**: *Diagnosis and management of acute ischaemic stroke.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 39496213**: *Contemporary Management of Acute Ischemic Stroke.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0987] Ivabradine in heart failure with reduced ejection fraction

**Cluster**: ivabradine in HFrEF | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Ivabradine is the selective funny-current (If) blocker that reduces heart rate without touching contractility or vascular tone - a pharmacological niche in HFrEF. It was tested in the landmark SHIFT trial in stable chronic heart failure, low ejection fraction, sinus rhythm, on contemporary background therapy. Its licensed niche is precise: sinus rhythm, resting heart rate at or above threshold, despite maximally tolerated beta-blockade - or when beta-blockers cannot be used at all. Meta-analyses now quantify its clinical and haemodynamic effects on top of, and apart from, beta-blockade.

### Core Teaching Points
- Ivabradine selectively blocks funny-current (If) channels in sinoatrial node cells, producing pure heart-rate reduction with no direct effect on contractility or vessels. (PMID 27752846)
- Ivabradine is indicated for HFrEF patients in sinus rhythm with heart rate >=70 bpm (licensing varies by region) on a maximum tolerated beta-blocker dose, or when beta-blockers are contraindicated. (PMID 38729854, PMID 27752846)
- Ivabradine was tested in a large outcome trial (SHIFT) in stable chronic heart failure with low ejection fraction and sinus rhythm on contemporary background therapy. (PMID 27752846)
- A systematic review and meta-analysis examined ivabradine's additional effect, apart from beta-blockers, on cardiovascular death, all-cause mortality, and hospitalisation in HFrEF. (PMID 30173307)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether ivabradine's benefit is fully independent of beta-blockade, and its net effect when added to contemporary quadruple therapy.
- The balance of haemodynamic versus hard clinical benefit in real-world populations outside trial enrolment criteria.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- If-channel mechanism and why it preserves contractility.
- SHIFT trial population and entry criteria.
- Licensed niche: sinus rhythm, heart-rate threshold, beta-blocker maximised or contraindicated.
- When ivabradine is NOT appropriate (AF, pacing, low-normal HR).

### Case Generation Hooks
- An HFrEF patient remains at 82 bpm sinus rhythm on maximally tolerated beta-blocker; assess ivabradine candidacy.
- A beta-blocker-intolerant HFrEF patient in sinus rhythm asks about rate control options; position ivabradine.

**Reviewer Gap**: SHIFT (2010) and the pivotal outcome evidence predate the pipeline's 2015+ filter and are cited via review summaries; verify doses and effect sizes against the primary publication before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27752846**: *Ivabradine.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 30173307**: *The effect of ivabradine therapy on heart failure patients with reduced ejection fraction: a systematic review and meta-analysis.* (2019) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 38729854**: *A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomised Controlled Trials Assessing Clinical and Haemodynamic Outcomes of Ivabradine.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None

## [0988] JAK inhibitors in RA: tofacitinib, baricitinib, upadacitinib: efficacy and cardiovascular safety

**Cluster**: JAK inhibitors in RA | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The JAK inhibitors transformed RA treatment with oral, rapid, targeted efficacy - and then the class acquired a safety shadow. After the regulatory wake-up call from the tofacitinib cardiovascular outcomes programme, prescribing is now risk-stratified: older patients with cardiovascular risk factors need particular counselling before a JAK inhibitor, and TNF inhibitors remain the usual comparator benchmark. This page focuses on RA-specific evidence: meta-analyses of randomised trials quantifying cardiovascular event risk, network meta-analyses comparing class safety, and the upadacitinib-specific cardiovascular data.

### Core Teaching Points
- A systematic review of phase 2-4 randomised trials compares major adverse cardiovascular events and all-cause death for JAK inhibitors or tocilizumab versus TNF inhibitors in RA - the head-to-head safety evidence regulators now expect. (PMID 39383617)
- A network meta-analysis meticulously assesses and compares the cardiovascular safety of JAK inhibitors in RA, supporting class-level risk comparison across agents. (PMID 37614310)
- For upadacitinib specifically, a systematic review and meta-analysis of cardiovascular adverse events found current evidence does not provide a definitive conclusion - counsel with calibrated uncertainty. (PMID 39513305)
- Meta-analysis of randomised controlled trials found the overall impact of JAK inhibitors on cardiovascular event risk in RA requires subgroup interpretation, particularly in older patients with cardiovascular risk factors. (PMID 31088790)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Absolute cardiovascular risk of individual JAK inhibitors in older RA patients with baseline risk factors.
- Whether safety differences exist between JAK1-selective and pan-JAK agents at the class level.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Implications of the tofacitinib cardiovascular outcomes programme for prescribing.
- MACE definition and which patients warrant heightened surveillance.
- When to prefer a TNF inhibitor over a JAK inhibitor on safety grounds.
- Interpreting non-definitive upadacitinib cardiovascular evidence for consent discussions.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 68-year-old smoker with RA failing a TNF inhibitor asks for tofacitinib; risk-stratify the conversation.
- A patient on upadacitinib with a new VTE asks whether the drug caused it; summarise what the meta-analytic evidence does and does not show.

**Reviewer Gap**: The pivotal tofacitinib cardiovascular outcome trial (ORAL Surveillance) predates the pipeline's 2015+ filter and is cited via review summaries; verify absolute risk figures against the primary publication.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39383617**: *Cardiovascular safety of the class of JAK inhibitors or tocilizumab compared with TNF inhibitors in patients with RA.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 37614310**: *Cardiovascular safety of Janus kinase inhibitors in patients with rheumatoid arthritis: systematic review and network meta-analysis.* (2023) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 39513305**: *Upadacitinib and Cardiovascular Adverse Events in Rheumatoid Arthritis: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None
- **PMID 31088790**: *Impact of Janus kinase inhibitors on risk of cardiovascular events in patients with rheumatoid arthritis: meta-analysis of RCTs.* (2019) [meta-analysis] - None

## [0989] JAK Inhibitors in Rheumatology

**Cluster**: JAK inhibitors across rheumatic disease | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
JAK-STAT signalling sits downstream of the cytokine receptors that drive rheumatoid arthritis, which is why JAK inhibitors work - and why their biology spans rheumatic disease more broadly. The 2023 EULAR RA recommendations position these agents within treat-to-target sequencing, after conventional synthetic DMARDs and usually after or instead of a TNF inhibitor, with risk-factor caveats inherited from the class's cardiovascular surveillance. Upadacitinib, the JAK1-selective exemplar, is approved across rheumatic, dermatologic, and gastrointestinal indications, giving rheumatologists a cross-specialty familiarity with its mechanism and trial programme.

### Core Teaching Points
- The 2023 EULAR update on synthetic and biological DMARDs for RA provides the current recommendations on where JAK inhibitors sit in the treatment sequence, based on systematic literature research and international task-force consensus. (PMID 41826212)
- JAK-STAT signalling contributes to RA pathogenesis through cytokine-receptor pathways, providing the mechanistic rationale for JAK inhibition as targeted oral therapy. (PMID 38240831, PMID 39125897)
- Upadacitinib is a selective JAK1 inhibitor approved by the FDA, EMA, and other agencies for chronic inflammatory diseases spanning rheumatic, dermatologic, and gastrointestinal conditions. (PMID 37984057)
- A systematic review and meta-analysis of randomised trials characterises upadacitinib's efficacy and safety across immune-mediated inflammatory diseases, informing its benefit-risk profile. (PMID 40666511)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Long-term class safety of JAK inhibitors relative to established biologic DMARDs beyond cardiovascular endpoints (malignancy, serious infection).
- Optimal positioning of JAK1-selective agents versus IL-6 pathway blockade after TNF-inhibitor failure.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- JAK selectivity: what JAK1-selective inhibition means clinically versus pan-JAK agents.
- EULAR treat-to-target sequencing of JAK inhibitors after csDMARDs.
- Mechanistic basis of JAK-STAT blockade in RA cytokine signalling.
- Cross-indication familiarity: where else upadacitinib is approved.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An RA patient has failed methotrexate and one TNF inhibitor; apply EULAR sequencing logic to choosing a JAK inhibitor versus an alternative bDMARD.
- A patient asks how upadacitinib differs from tofacitinib; explain selectivity, indications, and the safety caveats that apply to both.

**Reviewer Gap**: Comparative-effectiveness evidence between individual JAK inhibitors remains indirect; cardiovascular primary-trial data predate the 2015+ pipeline filter and are cited via review summaries.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 41826212**: *EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs: 2023 update.* (2026) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 38240831**: *Rheumatoid arthritis (Lancet Seminar).* (2023) [review] - None
- **PMID 37984057**: *Upadacitinib: Mechanism of action, clinical, and translational science.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 39125897**: *JAK Inhibitors in Rheumatoid Arthritis: Immunomodulatory Properties and Clinical Efficacy.* (2024) [review] - None
- **PMID 40666511**: *Efficacy and safety of upadacitinib for patients with immune-mediated inflammatory diseases.* (2025) [systematic-review] - None

## [0990] Juvenile idiopathic arthritis biologic strategy

**Cluster**: JIA biologic therapy | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Juvenile idiopathic arthritis management aims for remission - control of articular and systemic inflammation before joint damage produces disability and growth restriction. Methotrexate remains the conventional anchor, but the biologic era has transformed the escalation ladder: TNF inhibitors (etanercept, adalimumab), abatacept, tocilizumab, and IL-1 blockade each answer a different phenotype. Comparative-effectiveness evidence - including health-technology evaluation of abatacept, adalimumab, etanercept, and tocilizumab - supports choosing among them, while the infection signal associated with biologics demands disciplined screening and vigilance in a population where the disease itself already raises bacterial-infection risk.

### Core Teaching Points
- JIA management combines pharmacological, physical, and psychosocial therapies with the goal of inducing remission by controlling articular and systemic inflammation; biologics now anchor escalation beyond methotrexate. (PMID 32116038)
- Clinical-effectiveness and cost-effectiveness evidence directly compares abatacept, adalimumab, etanercept, and tocilizumab for JIA, informing biologic selection. (PMID 27135404)
- Biologic-associated infection is the principal safety concern in paediatric rheumatology; the bacterial-infection risk appears increased in JIA patients from the disease itself, compounding agent-related immunosuppression. (PMID 26385753)
- Biologic choice is phenotype-driven: TNF inhibitors for polyarticular disease, IL-1/IL-6 pathways for systemic phenotypes - matching mechanism to presentation. (PMID 32116038, PMID 27135404)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal biologic sequencing after first-agent failure and how to choose the second biologic.
- Long-term safety and growth effects of early biologic exposure; cost-effectiveness thresholds vary by health system.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Biologic classes and their pathways (TNF, CTLA4-Ig, IL-6R, IL-1).
- Phenotype-driven biologic selection in JIA subtypes.
- Pre-biologic infection screening and vaccination review.
- Treat-to-target and remission goals specific to paediatric populations.

### Case Generation Hooks
- An 8-year-old with polyarticular JIA has inadequate response to methotrexate; construct the biologic-selection reasoning.
- A child on etanercept presents with fever; outline the infection work-up and whether the biologic should be held.

**Reviewer Gap**: WEAK EVIDENCE BASE: only three retained sources, one a 2015 technology appraisal; no recent international JIA biologic guideline was retrievable under the pipeline filter - verify against the current ACR/EULAR paediatric guidance before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 32116038**: *Current and emerging biologics for the treatment of juvenile idiopathic arthritis.* (2020) [review] - None
- **PMID 27135404**: *The clinical effectiveness and cost-effectiveness of abatacept, adalimumab, etanercept and tocilizumab for JIA.* (2015) [technology-appraisal] - None
- **PMID 26385753**: *Biologic-associated infections in pediatric rheumatology.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0991] Juvenile idiopathic arthritis JIA: ILAR categories, oligoarticular, polyarticular, systemic

**Cluster**: JIA classification | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Juvenile idiopathic arthritis is the umbrella term for arthritis of unknown origin lasting more than six weeks with onset before age 16 - the most common chronic inflammatory rheumatic disease of childhood. Classification drives everything: the ILAR framework defines seven mutually exclusive categories (oligoarticular, polyarticular RF-positive and RF-negative, systemic, enthesitis-related, psoriatic, undifferentiated), and each category carries its own treatment pathway, uveitis risk, and prognosis. The 2021 ACR guideline translates the classification into pharmacologic management for oligoarthritis, temporomandibular joint arthritis, and systemic JIA with and without macrophage activation syndrome.

### Core Teaching Points
- JIA is defined as arthritis of unknown origin lasting >6 weeks with onset before age 16; the ILAR classification recognises seven mutually exclusive categories including oligoarticular, polyarticular (RF-positive and RF-negative), and systemic forms. (PMID 35087087)
- Subtype classification - by number of joints affected, extra-articular manifestations, systemic symptoms, serology, and genetics - determines treatment pathway and prognosis. (PMID 34425842)
- The 2021 ACR guideline provides pharmacologic-treatment recommendations for JIA organised by category: oligoarthritis, temporomandibular joint arthritis, and systemic JIA with and without macrophage activation syndrome. (PMID 35233986)
- The guideline also addresses tapering and discontinuing treatment in inactive disease - an active-management question in every JIA category. (PMID 35233986)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- The ILAR categories imperfectly map to molecular phenotypes; reclassification efforts continue.
- When and how aggressively to taper therapy in sustained inactive disease remains debated by category.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- ILAR category definitions and the seven-category framework.
- Category-specific treatment pathways per the 2021 ACR guideline.
- Uveitis surveillance differences across categories.
- Tapering and withdrawal criteria in inactive disease.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 4-year-old girl with a single swollen knee and positive ANA; classify and set surveillance for category-specific uveitis risk.
- A 10-year-old with five active joints; distinguish polyarticular from extended oligoarticular and apply the guideline pathway.

**Reviewer Gap**: Evidence is classification reviews and the 2021 ACR guideline; no updated international JIA classification revision is retrievable under the pipeline filter.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 35233986**: *2021 American College of Rheumatology Guideline for the Treatment of Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis.* (2021) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 35087087**: *Juvenile idiopathic arthritis.* (2022) [review] - None
- **PMID 34425842**: *Juvenile idiopathic arthritis: from aetiopathogenesis to therapeutic approaches.* (2021) [review] - None

## [0992] Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis subtypes

**Cluster**: systemic JIA and MAS | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Among JIA subtypes, systemic JIA stands apart: severe systemic inflammation with arthritis that may be absent at presentation, sitting on a continuum with adult-onset Still's disease. The first joint EULAR/PReS recommendations for Still's disease give diagnosis and management a unified frame. The subtype's defining danger is macrophage activation syndrome - a potentially life-threatening hyperinflammatory complication that has acquired new classification criteria and pathophysiological understanding in recent years. Treating the systemic phenotype increasingly means treating the innate-inflammatory drive early, before MAS supervenes.

### Core Teaching Points
- Systemic JIA and adult-onset Still's disease are considered the same disease; EULAR and PReS convened a joint task force, producing the first shared recommendations for diagnosis and management. (PMID 39317417)
- sJIA is hallmarked by severe systemic inflammation that may accompany or precede arthritis; contemporary understanding places it on a continuum with AOSD. (PMID 37923864)
- Macrophage activation syndrome is a potentially life-threatening complication occurring most commonly in systemic JIA; recent advances include new classification criteria and deeper pathophysiological understanding. (PMID 26461152)
- Patients with sJIA are particularly prone to MAS, making early recognition of the systemic phenotype - fever pattern, ferritin, systemic inflammation markers - a core clinical skill. (PMID 37923864, PMID 26461152)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal early biologic timing in sJIA to prevent MAS versus cost and infection trade-offs.
- How the joint EULAR/PReS Still's recommendations should be adapted where paediatric and adult services differ.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Distinguishing sJIA from other febrile illnesses at presentation.
- MAS clinical and laboratory recognition (ferritin, fibrinogen pattern, cytopenias).
- The sJIA-AOSD continuum and the joint EULAR/PReS recommendations.
- Early aggressive therapy rationale in the systemic phenotype.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 6-year-old with daily spiking fevers, evanescent rash, and ferritin rising; evaluate for sJIA and impending MAS.
- An sJIA patient on IL-1 blockade develops cytopenias and falling fibrinogen; recognise MAS and escalate.

**Reviewer Gap**: Subtype coverage is concentrated on the systemic phenotype; evidence for enthesitis-related and psoriatic subtype management was not retrievable under the pipeline filter.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39317417**: *EULAR/PReS recommendations for the diagnosis and management of Still's disease, comprising systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis and adult-onset Still's disease.* (2024) [guideline] - None
- **PMID 37923864**: *Treatment of systemic juvenile idiopathic arthritis.* (2021) [review] - None
- **PMID 26461152**: *Macrophage Activation Syndrome.* (2016) [review] - None

## [0993] Kawasaki Disease

**Cluster**: Kawasaki disease overview | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
Kawasaki disease - acute self-limited febrile vasculitis of childhood, primarily affecting children under five - is the leading cause of acquired heart disease in developed countries. Untreated, roughly a quarter of children develop coronary artery aneurysms; treatment within the window reduces that risk several-fold. Two AHA scientific statements frame modern care: the 2017 statement on diagnosis, treatment, and long-term management, and the 2024 update summarising recent advances. The clinical challenge is recognition - fever with mucocutaneous features in an irritable child - because treatment urgency is measured in days.

### Core Teaching Points
- Kawasaki disease is an acute self-limited febrile illness primarily affecting children under 5 and is the leading cause of acquired heart disease in developed countries; coronary artery dilation/aneurysms develop in about 25% of untreated patients. (PMID 39534969, PMID 28356445)
- The 2024 AHA scientific statement updates diagnosis and management, summarising recent evidence advances since the 2017 statement. (PMID 39534969)
- The 2017 AHA statement provides the framework for diagnosis, treatment, and long-term management, including serial echocardiographic surveillance of coronary arteries. (PMID 28356445)
- Classic presentation combines fever, rash, conjunctivitis, mucositis, lymphadenopathy, and extremity changes; coronary aneurysms occur in approximately 20% without treatment, with giant aneurysms the most feared outcome. (PMID 39299749)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Optimal management of refractory or IVIG-resistant disease and risk-stratified intensification.
- Long-term cardiovascular surveillance intervals for patients with regressed aneurysms.

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- Diagnostic criteria and incomplete/atypical Kawasaki presentations.
- Coronary artery aneurysm risk and surveillance echocardiography schedules.
- Treatment window urgency and what changes it.
- Long-term management per the AHA statements.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A 3-year-old with 6 days of fever, bilateral conjunctival injection, and strawberry tongue; assemble the diagnosis and treatment timeline.
- A child treated on day 4 has persisting fever at 36 hours post-IVIG; define IVIG resistance and next steps.

**Reviewer Gap**: Evidence is AHA scientific statements and a contemporary review; primary IVIG trials predate the pipeline filter and are cited through the statements.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 39534969**: *Update on Diagnosis and Management of Kawasaki Disease: A Scientific Statement From the American Heart Association.* (2024) [guideline] - None

## [0994] Kawasaki disease: diagnostic criteria, IVIG, aspirin, coronary artery aneurysm

**Cluster**: Kawasaki acute treatment specifics | **Status**: published

### Clinical Mentor Synopsis
The acute treatment trio of Kawasaki disease - intravenous immunoglobulin, aspirin, and vigilant coronary surveillance - rests on precise criteria and timing. Without IVIG, one child in five develops a coronary artery aneurysm; treatment within ten days of fever onset reduces that risk roughly five-fold. The standard regimen is IVIG 2 g/kg over 10-12 hours with antiplatelet-dose aspirin, but the aspirin component has been quietly debated: a 2024 systematic review and meta-analysis directly compares no/low-dose with high-dose aspirin across coronary outcomes, IVIG resistance, and hospital course.

### Core Teaching Points
- If not treated early with high-dose IVIG, 1 in 5 children with Kawasaki disease develop coronary artery aneurysms; IVIG administered within the treatment window reduces this risk about 5-fold. (PMID 27056781)
- All patients with acute Kawasaki disease should receive IVIG 2 g/kg, generally administered over 10-12 hours; medical therapy aims to reduce aneurysm prevalence, suppress systemic inflammation, and prevent coronary thrombosis. (PMID 28580631)
- A 2024 systematic review and meta-analysis assesses no/low-dose versus high-dose aspirin for coronary artery aneurysm incidence, IVIG resistance, hospital stay, and fever duration in acute Kawasaki disease - informing whether high-dose aspirin remains necessary adjunct therapy. (PMID 39051299)
- Diagnostic criteria remain clinical - fever plus mucocutaneous features - with echocardiography defining coronary artery aneurysm status and serial imaging tracking evolution. (PMID 27056781, PMID 28580631)

### Key Controversies & Uncertainties
- Whether high-dose aspirin adds benefit over low/no-dose aspirin atop modern IVIG therapy is being actively re-examined.
- Optimal intensification for patients at highest aneurysm risk (young age, delayed presentation).

### High-Yield MCQ Angles
- IVIG dose, administration, and the ten-day treatment window.
- Aspirin dosing debate and what the 2024 meta-analysis examined.
- Defining and grading coronary artery aneurysms.
- IVIG resistance and retreatment criteria.

### Case Generation Hooks
- A child presents on day 9 of fever meeting full criteria; confirm eligibility urgency and write the IVIG/aspirin orders.
- A newly diagnosed child has a parent asking why aspirin is needed when the IVIG 'does the work'; summarise the adjunct-therapy evidence.

**Reviewer Gap**: The aspirin meta-analysis abstract was truncated in retrieval; verify pooled effect estimates against the full text before approval.

### Evidence Sources
- **PMID 27056781**: *Kawasaki Disease.* (2016) [review] - None
- **PMID 28580631**: *Kawasaki disease: Medical therapies.* (2017) [review] - None
- **PMID 39051299**: *The Effectiveness of No or Low-Dose versus High-Dose Aspirin in Treating Acute Kawasaki Disease: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.* (2024) [systematic-review] - None
